--- K-12 Education Investment Narrative 01272020.docx ---
K-12 Education 2020 Strategy Review Investment Narrative
We invest across solutions, delivery, and enabling conditions and categorize our activities in the investment plan accordingly. Each group breaks down into finer categories. 
There are two significant proposed changes to our financials from the previous year, both in Solutions: Existing & Emerging Solutions: (1) increase Coherent Instructional Systems by $165M, and (2) increase Teacher Preparation by $34M. 
Coherent Instructional Systems: We can advance quality, coherence, and more equitable outcomes for Black, Latinx, and low-income students by investing an additional $165M over four years. We hypothesize that increased investments can transform the curricula marketplace and increase opportunities for Black, Latinx, and low-income students. Note that all draft plans below are actively being refined based on foundation leadership feedback.  
Note: We estimate that $16.5M of these dollars would specifically be invested toward policy & advocacy priorities. 
Approximately $86M of the $165M proposed increase can be covered within existing US Program resources. In 2019, we ceased investments in two bodies of work: Principal Preparation and School Management & Operations. The majority of those funds, as well as some from within the US Program Office of the President’s budget, would be reallocated to support this work. The remaining $79M have been submitted as part of our 2020 annual plan. 
Teacher Preparation: The budget approved during the 2017 strategy refresh provided enough funds to grow the number of preparation programs we support via transformation centers through 2021 (when the 3-year grants made in 2018 will end). Given our desire to go deeper in Teacher Preparation in California and Texas, we are proposing investing an additional $33.8M over four years. The funds to cover this increase will come from the now closed Principal Preparation body of work. 
These increased funds would specifically support the following initiatives, with a priority focus on California and Texas, with the requirement that tools and models that are developed would be shared as public goods within and beyond those two states: 
Support the transformation of additional preparation programs via our current Teacher Prep Transformation Centers, targeted technical assistance, and stakeholder engagement 
Support new transformation centers focused on alternative certification pathways  
Support technical assistance for K-12 school systems to implement human capital strategies 
Other Budget Adjustments: Additionally, we propose the following budget adjustments – all which can be funded through reallocations from the previously mentioned closed bodies of work and through depleting the K-12 Reserve within our existing budget.   
Increase Social Emotional Learning by $13.0M over four years. This increase would specifically support the validation and codification of solutions focused on improving high-quality integrated school support solution. This proposed increase would be covered by the funds reallocated from the Principal Preparation and School Management & Operations bodies of work. 
Increase Advancing Actionable Knowledge by $13M over four years to create new collaborations with research clearinghouses and increase our work in translating content into useful and usable formats. This increase would be covered by funds from the R&D Reserve within Solutions: R&D for New Solutions. 


--- K-12 Education Investment Narrative 03042021.docx ---
K-12 Education 2021 Strategy Review Investment Narrative
We invest across solutions, delivery, and enabling conditions and categorize our activities in the investment plan accordingly. 
As part of the US Program Strategic Opportunity process K-12 reallocated a total of $217.7M over four years. $157.7M was contributed to the Strategic Opportunities Fund. In addition to this, due to the size of the K-12 Strategic Opportunities (the fund and Algebra 1), we made the decision to further reallocate an additional $60M over four years bringing our total to $217.7M of strategic shifts. The major drivers of both the reductions and increases are as follows:
Delivery:
Networks for School Improvement will be reduced by $126M over four years. This reduction comes from unallocated funds that were being reserved for future cohorts. This means we will not fund any new cohorts of networks until the pending body of work decision in late 2024, and will focus on codifying network assets, maintaining an effective community of practice, scaling network assets, and executing an effective evaluation plan. 
Students with Disabilities will be reduced by $4.5M over two years (2021-22) and will no longer proceed with adding more networks into their cohort, including the planned expansion into Washington State. 

Solutions:
As an offset to the reductions outlined in other areas of the strategy, K-12 will receive ~$111M across three strategic opportunities related to accelerating next generation digital learning: $50M will go toward driving adoption of high-quality products, and $60.5M will go toward driving Algebra 1 outcomes with data and R&D.
A new body of work – Assessments – will be funded for a total of $13.5M over four years through an internal reallocation of funding from other bodies of work.
The team will reallocate $40M that remained in the K-12 R&D Reserve intended to fund emerging “big bet” ideas. Having launched R&D work in math and writing, and with the development of a large, advanced education R&D fund, we will allocate remaining funds to the new opportunities identified across the division.
Enabling Conditions:
A new body of work – COVID-19 Focus State Recovery Support – will be funded for a total of $1.1M in 2021 to continue support of commitments made in 2020.  
Global Policy & Advocacy:
In accordance with the reallocations within the K-12 budget over four years, we’ve reduced the Policy, Advocacy, and Communications envelope by a proportional amount ($16M).
Additionally, we are transferring 6 percent of the K-12 Policy, Advocacy, and Communications envelope to the Pathways team to fund their policy and advocacy. 


--- K-12 Education Investment Narrative 10242023.docx ---
Investment Plan Narrative
When the K-12 strategy was developed, bodies of work were organized by their content-focus, e.g., instructional materials, teachers and teaching, coherent systems, etc. As implementation plans were developed, it no longer made sense to segment work that way because investments often cut across multiple content areas. We have now reorganized our bodies of work to follow our strategy theory of action more closely – availability, adoption, use (enactment), and cross-cutting supports. Despite this organizational change, spending priorities remain consistent – our focus remains on increasing the quality of instructional materials.  
By Body of Work
Comparisons to Prior Bodies of Work
Includes: Instructional Materials R&D, Teachers & Teaching R&D.
Includes: Instructional Materials R&D, Teachers & Teaching R&D, Scale Quality Products & Aligned Services, Demand & Adoption, Professional Networks.
Reduction in annual allocation to NSIs is now included in additional support for instructional materials availability & adoption.    
Reduction in annual allocation to Teacher Preparation is now included in additional support for enactment in classrooms.
By Strategy Taxonomy
Notes: 
The Assessments body of work will move out of K-12 Education and into the US Program Assessment initiative as of 2024, a decrease of $11M in the 2024 K-12 budget allocation. This change is already reflected in the numbers provided here. 
Due to slower than expected expenditures, we are proposing rollover amounts of ($4.1M) for 2024 and ($1.5M) for 2025. These amounts will cover adjusted payment schedules for Networks for School Improvement as well as the Advanced Education R&D Fund (AERDF).   


--- K-12 Education Investment Narrative 10092025.docx ---
K-12 Education 2025 AP Investment Plan Narrative
The K-12 Education Strategy focuses on mathematics. To do this we focus investments on three targeted strategies 1) Improve the quality of high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) and teacher supports, 2) Increase the awareness and adoption of high-quality materials (HQIM), 3) Support the effective use of high-quality materials. In addition to these investment strategies, we also invest in continued support for learning dissemination from the Networks for School Improvement portfolio, Cross-Cutting Supports to enable the work across the three strategies, and Global Policy, and Advocacy.  
Over the past four years, the K-12 Education team has made significant trade-offs and shifted away from the previous strategy of delivery focused portfolios (e.g. Networks for School Improvement) towards investing in innovation, including R&D and digital products and curriculum which now represents 47% of our portfolio being invested directly in these categories. In addition, we have a focus on increasing the awareness and adoption of high-quality instructional materials represented by an additional 10% of the K-12 portfolio.
2026-29 budget allocation shifts
The K-12 team is proposing minor budget adjustments from the previous annual budget to support aligning our professional learning provider availability work into a new cross-cutting supports body of work and adding additional funding to continue to ramp down and finalize the support of the Networks for School Improvement body of work through the National Improvement Hub. 
As part of our annual planning process, and continued over the next six months, the team will review out-year allocations in alignment with our projected impact goals. Through this process we will continue to assess our resource allocation levels across the team, and in total compared to our ambitious goals.  Given the ever-evolving landscape for digital product development, especially in the evolving AI products in education, there may be a recommendation for increased investment in this area. The team will continue to assess trade-offs, but this could result in a request for incremental resources to support AI product development and implementation.  Below is a summary of our key portfolios and their 4YO projections (next page).
Improving the Quality of Instructional Materials and Teacher Supports: ($7.3M) decrease
Scale Quality Products & Aligned Services: decrease by $7.3M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning & Provider Availability across the four focus states.
Increase the Awareness & Adoption of High-Quality Materials: ($12.5M) decrease
Demand Aggregation & Adoption: decrease by $7M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning & Provider Availability across the four focus states.
Florida Enactment: decrease by $5.5M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning and Provider Availability across the four focus states.
Support the Effective Use of High-Quality Materials: ($7.2M) decrease
California Enactment: decrease by $0.9M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning & Provider Availability across the four focus states.
New York Enactment: decrease by $4.5M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning & Provider Availability across the four focus states.
Texas Enactment: decrease by $0.8M to support a new Cross-Cutting Supports body of work for Professional Learning & Provider Availability across the four focus states.
Networks for School Improvement: $7M increase
Networks for School Improvement: work continues to focus on learning dissemination and will increase by $7M in 2026 to support the National Improvement Hub, with funds reallocated from the bodies of work California Enactment, Florida Enactment, and Texas Enactment.
Cross-Cutting Supports: $20M increase

--- K-12 Education Strategy Memo 10242023.docx ---
Memo
Our K-12 Education strategy seeks to ensure that all students – particularly Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds (“priority students”) – graduate high school skilled in the math they need to be successful in higher education, workforce, and life. Strategically, this means:
We hypothesize that to improve achievement, students must experience math instruction that is (a) effectively personalized; (b) focused on student voice, communication, and interaction to test knowledge and deep math skills; and (c) focused not only on procedures and concepts, but also the application of math to solve real world, complex problems that illustrate why math matters. Our strategy thesis is that if we can shift district and school adoption to more engaging, relevant, usable, and effective digital materials, enacted with aligned professional learning for teachers, we can create these classroom experiences and improve student achievement. We do this by investing in: 
R&D to improve the quality and reach of instructional materials and supports teachers receive. 
The improvement and adoption of select high-quality instructional materials to drive scale across the U.S., particularly within our focus states. The upcoming state curriculum adoptions in California (2025), Texas (2025), and Florida (2027), together with New York (which does not have a state-set curriculum adoption process) will reach 32 percent (or 16 million) of all U.S. students. 
Market influencing through the creation and amplification of definitions of quality so that more teachers, district purchasers, and others know where to go to access information on what works.
Networks of teachers, curriculum leads and coaches, and others to provide feedback to product developers, as well as raise awareness among them about quality products and offerings. 
Networks of districts to support the enactment of high-quality instruction.
Strategy Context
Post COVID, US math scores across the U.S. are the lowest they’ve been since 1990. This impact will not be easily remedied in the near term. Over the long-term, however, the shift to digital products over the course of the pandemic, together with recent developments in AI, promise to transform how students are taught and the supports teachers receive. Approximately 25 percent of the materials offered now are digital and that is estimated to double by 2025. Increasingly sophisticated digital materials are complemented by the widespread recognition that math instruction must change – the demand for more engaging materials among teachers remains high. Parents of all political persuasions recognize that math must be modernized. In a 2022 nationally representative survey, 71 percent of math teachers ranked engagement as one of their top five priorities in selecting instructional tasks. Yet headlines out of Florida and Texas, among other states, continue to seize on fears about the “wokeness” of education – with pushes to make instruction more relevant often being misconstrued as and caught up in the debate around critical race theory. 
Market conditions to support the adoption of instructional materials are mixed. Across the $1 billion math market, incumbents maintain outsized market share with the “Big 3” (Savvas, McGraw-Hill, and Houghton Mifflin Harcourt) collectively taking 70 percent of the core curriculum market. These three are all owned by private equity firms. And although there are exceptions to the rule, private equity-backed companies have reduced R&D and product spend and instead emphasized sales activities and maximizing cash flow. There are signs that demand is shifting towards new, potentially disruptive players who continue to invest in product. These “next gen” publishers (many of whom are our partners) have already captured 25 percent of the market as of 2022, up from 16 percent in 2016 and regulatory tailwinds have the potential to accelerate that shift. Further, EdReports ratings have drawn incumbent


--- K-12 Education Strategy Memo 10092025.docx ---
Dear Bill and Mark,
Every year, millions of students show up to math class excited to learn, but they quickly disengage - confused by unclear lessons, unsupported by outdated tools, and unseen in the examples used. Schools do little to accelerate learning, a key goal of our strategy. TNTP’s 2025 report, The Opportunity Makers, surveyed approximately 28,000 elementary and middle schools where students begin below grade level and found that only about 1,300 schools (5%) consistently help the average student gain ≥1.3 grade levels per year—enough to catch up over time. These high-impact schools share several key characteristics:
Instructional Coherence – aligned curriculum, professional learning (PL), instruction, and targeted interventions at the skill and subskill level; 
Consistency – sustained, school-wide delivery of strong instruction anchored in high-quality curricula, reinforced year over year through aligned systems across classrooms, schools, and districts; and
An Ethos of Belonging – via instruction and curriculum that are contextually relevant and foster student motivation, engagement, and persistence, together known as CMEP.
Our strategy advances a vision of high-impact schools supported by AI-enabled solutions capable of ensuring engaging and motivating access to grade-level content; offering personalized opportunities for below-grade-level students to accelerate; and supporting consistent, high-quality teaching practice within and across classrooms. We envision these solutions coming together coherently on the market and in school systems and classrooms—which are, to date, highly fractured—to advance our goal of state-level growth in math elementary and middle school instruction to enable more students to be prepared for and pass Algebra 1 no later than ninth grade. Across 19 Bodies of Work, we are funding 3 strategies in CA, TX, FL and NY:
Improving Quality of Products and Teacher Supports: Developing, testing, and commercializing high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) and associated teacher supports, including four critical AI use cases: tutoring, grading and student work analysis, lesson and instructional planning, and adaptive practice and feedback (see slide 11). 
Shaping the Product and Service Markets: Driving district adoption of these materials (with the goal of cumulatively reaching more than 50% of students across CA, FL, NY, and TX) and associated PL packages, where initially paper products serve as a vehicle for digital up-sales as our partners attempt to change purchasing behavior and scale HQIM. 
Demonstrating and Scaling District-Level Adoption and Use: Developing proof points and incenting effective and consistent use to increase target state Alg 1 proficiency rates by 10 percentage points, ultimately ensuring that students maintain their joy, interest and commitment to taking the advanced math necessary to succeed in a shifting, AI enabled, STEM focused marketplace. 
Our Adoption and nascent Algebra Impact Models (currently completed for CA) will inform tradeoffs and prioritization in each state on the path to our 2032 student impact goals (see slides 8-9 for targets and actuals on key strategy measures). 
At strategy review, we will focus on how these three strategies optimize the existing school structures and systems to affect millions of students now. Against a decade+ stagnation in student math scores, our efforts to see 10 percentage point gains in number of students reaching proficiency, affecting potentially 1.7 million students by 2032, are themselves transformative, and we are already seeing demonstration of the impact our solutions could have in testing and early adoption.  At the same time, we know AI frontier labs and innovators are accelerating at a pace that outstrips the field’s present capacity to absorb it. Yet it must. Bill’s visit to Newark’s First Avenue Academy underscores both the urgency and opportunity to harness AI now. At our 90-minute October 31 learning session, w


--- K-12 Education Strategy Review Memo 03042019.docx ---
K-12 Education 2019 Strategy Review Memo
Over one year into our revised K-12 Education strategy, we have successfully operationalized key parts of our strategy, and aligned to the goal of significantly increasing the number of Black, Latinx, and low-income students who earn a high school diploma, enroll in a postsecondary institution, and are on track in the first year to obtain a credential with labor-market value. At the same time, we have recognized that some of our early thinking around potential investment strategies has evolved and requires revision. Here, we briefly summarize progress in our Networks for School Improvement, Solutions, R&D, and Charters portfolios. We then seek your guidance on four bodies of work within our Solutions portfolio – Teacher Preparation, Principal Preparation, School Management & Operations, and Social Emotional Learning – where we believe our investment strategies should expand or stop. We outline preliminary thoughts about a “catalog” or “playbook” strategy to validate and disseminate emerging lessons arising from our investments. We conclude by describing key 2019 priorities and expected decisions. 
Summary of Progress
Networks for School Improvement 
While it’s too early to see any impact on student outcomes, we met all execution milestones in 2018 (see Appendix: Body of Work Progress & Priorities). We continue to be optimistic about this work’s potential to improve outcomes for students served within our funded networks, generate significant learning for the field, and enable us to codify models of school improvement by 2022 for potential scaling. We selected our first cohort of networks primarily via an RFP last Fall: 21 intermediaries serving approximately 250K students in 298 schools across 13 states. Each network works with secondary school-based teams to use data to identify a problem, select a strategy and evidence-based interventions to address the problem, set a target for improvement, iterate to make the approach more effective, and improve specific outcomes that are predictive of high school graduation and postsecondary readiness. Seven organizations received 3-5-year grants to pilot or expand their networks, and 14 received grants to strengthen their capacity to support future networks through subsequent RFP applications. 
Many network grantees are in the process of completing key operational benchmarks: 
Because most (15 of 21) intermediaries chose to focus on improving middle school and 9th grade indicators, we issued a second RFP to identify up to five additional networks to focus on high school to postsecondary transitions to balance our portfolio. We will identify our next full cohort of networks through another national RFP later this year.
In 2018, we launched a formative evaluation of the Networks for School Improvement. Columbia University’s Center for Public Research and Leadership will examine how the initial cohort of intermediaries are implementing the strategy and identify characteristics of effective networks. The Center for Public Research and Leadership will study a randomly selected eight-member sub-sample of grantees to more deeply understand their operations and impact. And in 2019, we will design and launch a series of quasi-experimental and experimental summative evaluations, supplemented by process and qualitative data, soliciting participation by individuals with general expertise in randomized control trials (Tom Kane and Ed Glaser, Harvard), Bayesian statistical methods (Avi Feller, Berkeley), continuous improvement within networks (Jennifer Russell, Pittsburgh; Tony Bryk, Carnegie), and targeted problems of practice (Terrance Green at UT Austin; Jenny Nagaoka, University of Chicago). Selected partners will prioritize providing regular feedback of intermediate results as well as longer, multi-year work.

--- 11162020 K-12 Education Roundtable - Memo.docx ---
K-12 Education Roundtable: Memo 
November 16, 2020 
The impact of COVID-19 on K-12 education has been catastrophic. States, districts, and charter networks have confronted a myriad of operational, budgetary, and policy challenges to address the learning, social-emotional, and racial impact of this pandemic. The goal of this learning session is to give you an understanding of how educators have navigated these challenges. While many continue to struggle, and the equity impact on Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty remains profound, we are seeing bright spots and durable lessons learned that are worthy of further study and attention. In this memo, we provide detail on the current state of affairs, emerging bright spots, and opportunities for us and others to seize this moment to ensure that all students – but particularly Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty – receive the high-quality education they deserve. Key questions we will explore with our three guests include:
Enduring District and State Challenges: How did districts navigate the pandemic in the first months after school closure? What problems persist today? Where are there bright spots that should be copied or replicated?
Impact on a Best-In-Class Digital Platform: What emerging lessons are digital curricula companies, like Curriculum Associates, learning from their data? What are key products features that have proven most valuable? What are the challenges ahead for the market?
Emerging Opportunities: What are the emerging opportunities, particularly for Black and Latino Students and students experiencing poverty? What is the ongoing impact of the digital divide? What is the ongoing impact of budget cuts? What will happen to assessment and accountability?
1: The Current State of K-12 Education
School Re-Openings: Nearly half of the nation's districts began the 2020-21 school year with remote or hybrid learning, but some that started fully remote are starting to bring students back in-person and vice versa. As of November, in a scan of 12,400 districts, 58 percent are hybrid, 23 percent are remote, and 19 percent are in-person.
School Enrollment: Enrollment is declining in public schools across the country. While comprehensive national data are not yet available, a sample of 100 districts saw average kindergarten enrollment drop 16 percent.
Shortened School Day: Due to the pandemic, official district policies will lead to children missing so much instructional time that they would be considered chronically absent in any other year. Seven of the 10 largest school districts — Los Angeles, Clark County, Wake County, New York City, Montgomery County, Fairfax County, and Chicago – are all planning to deliver far less instructional time to students than normal.
Learning Loss: While there has been no national or representative assessment of learning loss during COVID (“learning loss” not only refers to the months out of school, but also to the loss of knowledge retained from one school year to the next), McKinsey & Company estimate that learning loss will be an average of seven months, but greater for Black students (10.3 months) and Latino students (9.2 months). These estimates are built off a statistical model that uses academic studies of the effectiveness of remote learning relative to traditional classroom instruction and evaluates them against three epidemiological scenarios that have schools reopening at different times.
Trauma: Research points to students dealing with numerous types of trauma during school closures, including death of a family member, mental health challenges, alcohol and drug abuse, child abuse, and hunger. Approximately 14 million children in the U.S. are not getting enough to eat, with many previously relying on school meal programs. About three in ten Black households with children and one in four Latino households with children did not have sufficient food due to a lack of resources in June 2020, whil


--- 02192021 K-12 Education Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies Memo .docx ---
K-12 Education: Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies Memo
Successful completion of Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade is a critical milestone for all students, but particularly for Black and Latino students and those experiencing poverty. Completing Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade is a key predictor of on-time high school graduation, postsecondary enrollment and persistence, and opens up a range of careers that depend on math skills. Given the importance of Algebra 1 and the strength of the assets we’ve created that relate to math, we propose shifting our resources so that by 2023, we allocate approximately 70% of new investments towards supporting more students to pass Algebra 1 by the end of the 9th grade. This would require coordinated policy, public goods, and delivery strategies and would also bolster efforts across other US Program teams around building new high school math pathways post Algebra 1, including data science and statistics, to bridge to meaningful postsecondary and work opportunities.  
We are not pushing the reset button on the K-12 strategy, but propose exploring the interplay among policy, advocacy, and communications, R&D, solutions development and dissemination, and delivery more tightly around a specific subject matter. This proposed shift towards greater integration builds on existing work, particularly our work in Middle Years Math, the continued shift toward digital, instructional materials (including curricula, assessments, and other interventions), and our overall Coherent Instructional Systems work. Our current Networks for School Improvement and Coherent Instructional Systems delivery work would continue unaffected through 2025 and will remain focused on codifying emerging solutions and effective implementation strategies. And we expect that a lot of what we learn from those efforts will inform the next stage of our math-focused delivery work.  
As the foundation has in Global Health, we propose focusing our strategy on a set of specific, consequential, clear, and measurable targets and bring the full force of the foundation across program, advocacy, and research to this issue. By creating a coherent message, consistently communicated, and iterating based on our learning, we increase the likelihood that we have a greater impact on student achievement. We are seeking your thought partnership, early agreement with this focus, and permission to proceed to build out a more developed strategy. 
This memo covers several key issues:1) an early vision and potential goal, 2) the consequential nature of math and the advantages of using it to study student learning, 3) how this shift towards math builds on our historic and current investments, 4) early thinking about what a strategy could look like by 2023-24, and 5) some operational next steps.  
A Draft 10 Year Vision to Guide Our Work
A cornerstone of this strategy is to clearly articulate an evergreen set of interrelated short and long-term goals to guide investments, government relations, communications, and advocacy for the next decade. These goals would drive internal execution decisions and trade-offs over the next 5-10 years and would have key interim progress indicators to ensure we remain on track. These goals would be guided by the following vision: raise passage rates of Algebra 1 for all students, but particularly Black and Latino students and those experiencing poverty, ensure that 50 percent of instructional materials align with multiple dimensions of quality beyond standards alignment. Success includes increasing students’ interest, motivation, and confidence to pursue math after Algebra 1 and use of quantitative reasoning in daily life as an adult. Early thinking on potential short and long-term goals is outlined on slides 14-16.

Math is in many ways unique in K-12 education – it has a straightforward body of knowledge that must be mastered to gain pr


--- 06022021 K-12 Fund Memo.docx ---
K-12 Fund: Memo
Driving Market Share of High-Quality Instructional Products
Central idea: Achieve the K-12 fund’s original goal of improving K-12 instructional product quality and scale by (a) investing in select providers (primarily math) to build a “full stack” of digital products and reach scale; and (b) supporting states and districts to adopt and implement those products. This opportunity will bolster existing work including the Coherent Instructional Systems and Middle Years Math bodies of work. New investments will prioritize math while also supporting opportunistic interventions to steer near-term English Language Arts purchases toward quality.
Market developments have affirmed the need for high quality, full stack products.
Interviews with dozens of leaders, investors, providers, and experts about the (partial) return to school and impact of the $190B in federal recovery dollars going to K-12 schools have affirmed five trends:
Recovery buying focused on addressing learning loss: District procurement will focus on intervention, assessment, and independent practice products over the next 1-2 years given the focus of federal recovery funding and the urgent need to remediate learning loss, followed by expanded core purchases (California math adoption is in 2023).
Importance of digital products: We expect continued use of digital products as teachers adapt delivery models to be more personalized, respond to learning loss (particularly in math), and the fact that many teachers have embraced the use of tech in the classroom and see it as enduring.
Desire for fewer products: The “panic buying” that took place in 2020 has resulted in districts having an overwhelming number of products in their stack, leading to a significant desire to consolidate both products and vendors; likely favoring incumbents that can offer a “one stop shop” and have deep relationships at district level.
Availability of investor capital: The influx of federal funds makes the edtech market particularly attractive to private investors, with valuations moving from 4-5x to 5-10x. We expect to see continued private equity and special purpose acquisition company (SPAC) activity in this market (which is already 65%+ owned by private equity).
Interest in OER: There is growing interest in OER material given the lower cost and a growing awareness of its quality, but OER offerings remain largely print and operate at limited scale.
Informed by these trends, our baseline expectation of the future instructional products market includes five characteristics, all of which impact our investment thesis:
Near-term demand for supplemental products may adversely affect instructional coherence: Buyers’ immediate need to address learning loss will likely result in increased purchasing of intervention products aligned to specific learning gaps, further reducing instructional coherence that was already exacerbated from the 2020 buying spree. We hope to address that risk by partnering with supplemental providers to build aligned core products with a focus on integrated content.
Providers will continue to consolidate to offer a full suite of products, but we do not expect sufficient integration: We expect most market players to meet buyers’ need for a “one stop shop” by building offerings across the full stack; unfortunately, we do not expect sufficient integration of those. As supplemental players expand to core and vice versa, we have the opportunity to improve the quality and integration of the underlying offerings – areas we expect providers to fall short.
OER usage is expanding despite an unclear business model and variable quality of derivative work: Large publishers have already recognized the brand value and low-cost of OER core products and are increasingly taking advantage of open licenses. However, derivative products often vary in quality, e.g., McGraw Hill’s poor user interface for their Illustrative Math offering. We expect more publishers to subsidize their product


--- 08102021 K-12 Math Update Memo.docx ---
K-12 Math Update: Memo
August 10, 2021
Mastering elementary and middle school math, culminating in the successful completion of Algebra 1 by the end of the 9th grade, is a crucial set of steps on students’ academic journeys, marking the transition from concrete to conceptual math and laying the groundwork to take advanced math courses, graduate from high school, enroll in college, and succeed in college. Yet despite a range of efforts over the past several decades, a disproportionate number of students of color and students experiencing poverty receive inadequate instruction, or are demoralized by the experience and cease to pursue appropriate, higher-level math. 
Building on our curricula and place-based work, we propose focusing our strategy on a set of specific and measurable goals that aim to (1) improve the market presence and adoption of high-quality instructional materials nationally, but also specifically in California, Florida, New York, Texas, and Washington, and (2) increase the percentage of Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty who are prepared for and complete Algebra 1 by the end of the 9th grade in select communities within those same states and progress into higher level math with positive math identities and mindsets. Approximately 60-70 percent of our investments would be focused on a value chain bridging from innovation to solutions to delivery in math, with an attention to scaling public goods that emerge from each of these investment areas. The remaining share of investments will be devoted to continuing to build on assets emerging from our current strategy, including: selective investments in increasing the availability of English Language Arts and science materials given momentum in the market, supporting further investment in building the evidence base around social emotional learning, including how insights can practically be included in products and services; supporting market mechanisms that make the quality of materials visible on the supply and demand side; work on the teacher pipeline agnostic of subject; and delivery work anchored on on-track outcomes that are broader than math.   
With preliminary information – as well as through conversations with experts, practitioners, and other stakeholders – we have developed a theory of change (see slide 5) that identifies several high-leverage drivers. And based on our review of the research, as well as our comparative advantage and existing strategy assets, we have developed a draft theory of action that places high-quality instructional materials as well as the supports for schools, teachers, and students to use them in their respective contexts as the main drivers of our strategy (see slides 8-9). 
We hypothesize that an overall investment strategy containing several key elements will aid in transforming the math instructional materials and supports landscape over the next 10 years: 
1: Data
At the national level, data like the National Assessment of Education Progress (NAEP) underscore that student math achievement is low, stagnant, or decreasing over time, and is vastly different when viewed by race and income. While NAEP provides an incomplete picture of Algebra 1 achievement (algebraic concepts are estimated to only account for 30% of the total math assessment in 8th grade), SAT and ACT tests and other international measures confirm this view. And while national data collected out of the US Department of Education’s Office of Civil Rights provide Algebra 1 course taking and passage percentages by grade, they do not include the overall numbers of students in each grade, thereby obscuring actual student performance. For example, Tennessee reports to the Office of Civil Rights that 89 percent of students pass Algebra 1 in 8th grade and 73 percent pass it in 9th grade. --- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Memo.docx ---
K-12 Assessment 
Memo
Current State Summary
Since the 1990s, the predominant paradigm for education policy – at both the state and federal levels – has been rooted in the notion of standards-based accountability. Federal policy, first enacted in No Child Left Behind (NCLB), was built on the hypothesis that if we set high academic standards for all students, assess students’ progress toward meeting standards, transparently disaggregate assessment data by subgroups (including by race), hold schools accountable for these results, and target resources and interventions to schools and districts that are the most behind, we will close any subgroup gaps over time. Unfortunately, research suggests that student improvements have been mixed, varying by state, subject area, and grade level, and by having a marginal impact overall, particularly on Black and Latino students, and students experiencing poverty. Despite bright spots (DC, Tennessee, Massachusetts), racial and socioeconomic achievement gaps have not narrowed, as evidenced by NAEP scores that have remained stagnant nationally for almost all students over the last 10 years (see slide 5 in the presentation). States and districts’ threats of punitive action have not been accompanied by strong technical assistance or resources of sufficient power to increase math and reading test scores. Standards-based reform has also had a mixed impact on non-test metrics (e.g., while some schools saw increases in attendance and high school graduation rates, school accountability pressures have also been shown to negatively affect student behavior, resulting in an increasing number of school discipline offenses). The current law’s incentive structure and evidence of impact is summarized in Appendix A. 
As a result, critics of testing maintain that far from creating incentives for growth, standards-based accountability has led to a proliferation of time-consuming and counterproductive testing that has not yielded meaningful gains in student achievement (see slide 8 of the presentation, which offers an example of the array of tests taken by students in Dallas). While dissatisfaction around the role of assessments has been growing for some time, the disruptions to annual testing required by the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) caused by COVID have spurred calls for innovation from actors across the political spectrum.  While advocates and policymakers are reluctant to abandon the transparency and comparability of standards-based accountability assessments, others contend that the federally-mandated accountability system does not provide actionable tools for educators, parents, and students to improve achievement. Moreover, they argue that the current system leaves little room for districts and schools to value broader markers of youth development, such as factors important to social emotional learning that are increasingly shown to foster academic growth. 
While ESSA reauthorization is likely years away, it is widely expected that the federal government is likely to be less activist than in prior iterations of federal policymaking like NCLB or Race to the Top. Nevertheless, in the short term, other forces are pushing on and placing the current system at risk (e.g., there may be another round of testing waivers this spring; state and local activism to eliminate or overhaul high-stakes testing). At present, we remain committed to advocating through partners for the preservation of the current law’s provisions, as summarized in Appendix B. But over the long-term, given the current context, we believe that the foundation and its partners should begin exploring feasible strategies that are socialized and supported by a broad, diverse range of partners. Together with other foundations (Walton, Shusterman, Chan Zuckerberg), we propose investing in a set of programmatic and policy ideas for improving the current assessment and accountability system at the state, and potentially federal, levels.
Future State


--- K-12 Education Strategy Review Memo 03042021.docx ---
K-12 Education 2021 Strategy Review Memo 
COVID-19 and the resulting school closures have disrupted U.S. K-12 schools and exacerbated learning gaps in academic performance and well-being among all students, but disproportionately significant for Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty. Even now, 15-16 million public school students across all states are caught in the digital divide. This could lead to an average of 7-14 months of lost learning for disconnected students and an additional 232,000 high school students dropping out. Most of these students are Black, Latino, or Native American, or reside in rural communities. 
In this memo, we detail progress within each of our portfolios, outline the adjustments we have made – based on what we’ve learned and because of COVID – and preview our 2021 priorities. Throughout, we highlight the work we’ve done over the past three years focused on improving outcomes and opportunities for Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty. Most notably, COVID-19 forced our delivery grantees – Networks for School Improvement and charter schools focused on more effective models to support Students with Disabilities – to make significant adjustments because of school closures. Despite strong efforts by our partners, we rate our overall strategy progress over the last year as being slightly off track due primarily to the effects of the pandemic.
During our time with you, we are eager to discuss our progress in 2020, any additional follow up you have regarding the Algebra 1 strategy direction we’ll discuss with you on Feb. 19, and issues related to assessment and accountability (see Appendix A). The Federal government has eliminated state testing requirements for the 2019-20 and (likely, though yet to be confirmed) for the 2020-21 school years. And other student on-track outcome data are compromised or unavailable. We believe there is an opportunity to use these developments to create systems that get parents, educators, and policymakers the information they need to understand the progress of individual students, for schools to meet the needs of those students, and direct funding flows toward evidence-based supports to help students improve. We look forward to the discussion of these topics, as well as our strategy progress.
I. Summary of Progress 
Solutions: R&D of New Solutions 
Through our R&D efforts, we aim to invest in higher risk strategies that generate dramatic improvements for Black and Latino students and those experiencing poverty. Our work has centered in middle years math and secondary writing. Further, we have developed the Advanced Education R&D Fund (AERDF) to spur innovation in the K-12 R&D pipeline that can be commercialized and respond to market demand for our priority students. Our R&D efforts are on-track. 
Middle Years Math: Due to COVID-19, we accelerated investments in promising math interventions in high dosage tutoring and online micro-tutoring. Working with Saga Education, we supported the development and testing of an adapted tutoring model that supplemented daily in-person tutoring with work in an online program on alternate days. This modification reduced costs from $3,100 to $1,800 per student. Initial evidence from an evaluation in Chicago showed the tutoring produced large increases in math knowledge with an average impact of 0.26 standard deviations on end-of-year math test scores (which is a large effect size and translates to about 1.6 years of additional growth in high school) and 0.37 standard deviations on math grades (equivalent to 0.5 grade points higher). Based on these findings, we supported Saga to tutor 2,000 New York City 9th graders in math in the wake of COVID while simultaneously attempting to reduce the price point again by about 50 percent while innovating on the model to go from blended tutoring to online tutoring. Citadel Foundation has invested $3.3M in a parallel Saga pilot in Chicago Public Schools and Broward


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Memo.docx ---
Memo
Math and numeric thinking are important skills for US citizenship and employment. And yet very little progress has been made to raise student achievement over the last two decades. US student outcome gains have been stagnant, with persistent gaps among student sub-groups based on race, ethnicity, and income. Dramatic action is merited, and the foundation can – through our work with partners – break this impasse and transform math education. 
Our optimism is fueled by the knowledge and assets the foundation has garnered over the last 20 years. Our strategy builds on prior investments we have made in the Common Core State Standards, the Math Design Collaborative lesson units, and our Networks for School Improvement work, and seeks to take advantage of advances in technology, particularly around strengthening instructional materials and assessments. We intend to use these and other tools, practices, and policies to strengthen and transform math education to enable more students to complete Algebra 1, persist into higher level math classes, and enter adulthood with math competence and confidence. We will do this by working across the areas of R&D, commercialization, and scale of improvement products and services, while also supporting their effective enactment via delivery. 
Change of this magnitude will not be quick or easy. Given the complexity of US education governance, finance, and markets, change will require time to prove impact and to create systemic conditions needed to scale it. We will need to shift our investment strategies to initially focus on product innovation and development, then aggressively drive it to market and ensure that it is enacted in schools with integrity. And we must be clear-eyed about political obstacles, as well as teacher and leader behaviors. To realize impact, we must eschew quick wins and silver bullets for a longer-term approach partnered with students, educators, product designers, and communities in the field.  
Our strategy takes a phased approach for investing, assessing progress and shifting priorities over this longer timeframe. We assert that by concentrating in one area (math generally, Algebra 1 specifically) and maintaining a longer-term focus, we can improve student outcomes for Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds (“priority students”). For our priority students to graduate skilled in the math they need to succeed in higher education, the workforce, and their lives, they need: 1) high-quality instruction that supports mastery of algebraic concepts and builds positive mindsets; and 2) access to higher-level math courses that offer multiple pathways for further developing their math skills. Our strategy outlines the first 10 years of our plan for improving math outcomes at scale. By 2032, we aim to: 
Demonstrably improve the quality of digital instructional materials beyond standards-alignment, focused primarily on increasing student academic outcomes, engagement, and motivation 
Demonstrably improve the quality of teaching supports through both pre-service and in-service training around the use of high-quality instructional materials. 
Validate the impact and identify measures for integrating quality instructional materials and quality teaching supports in coherent systems to support instruction while laying the groundwork for systemic changes at scale state-wide.
With success in these areas, we will lay the foundation to scale reform efforts state-wide in focus states (California, Florida, New York, and Texas). And while we intend to be opportunistic as appropriate, we are organizing our work and monitoring our progress over the next decade in three phases, where we will adjust priorities and resources as we reach key milestones related to the efficacy and impact we’re seeing. 
Phase 1 (years 1-4): define, innovate around, and validate quality dimensions for instructional materials and product features, focused primarily on dimensions that support


--- 04162024 K-12 Courseware Feature Map Memo.docx ---
Memo
Strategy Context
Over the next decade, our focus is to increase the academic success of all students – particularly Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds – to demonstrate proficiency in Algebra 1 no later than the end of the 9th grade and proceed into higher level math classes. Improving the quality of instructional materials is a key lever to achieving this goal. 
High-quality instructional materials are more than just digital textbooks – they offer an opportunity to shift the status quo for students and teachers by making math concepts and materials more engaging, more applicable to real world challenges, all while being embedded with ongoing assessments that help redirect and reinforce concepts for students who are each learning at an individualized pace. Further, digital tools can support teachers by saving time and reducing cognitive load – providing them with real-time data on student progress and tools that help them differentiate instruction for students, as well as leveraging AI and video to connect them to best practices from our nation’s top teachers. 
Our vision is that instruction in every classroom:
Is personalized to the different needs of students. Few students receive the level of personalization necessary to succeed in math in the traditional classroom. We know that teachers are facing very real constraints on time and capacity that limit their ability to meaningfully personalize instruction for students. Forty-three percent of teachers cite challenges with classrooms that are too large and require too much differentiation. When compounded with middle school teachers teaching roughly 90 students over five periods, teachers do not have the time and capacity they need to personalize instruction for students. 
Interactive with opportunities to engage with other students in math discourse and problem solving. Talking about math is a critical part of learning math. Interaction and communication foster student voice and collaboration that increase self-direction and confidence. Students learn to share and compare their problem-solving approaches and arguments, to ask questions and give feedback, and to learn from each other’s perspectives. But here, too, teacher time is a barrier to creating effective learning environments. Teachers may encourage group work or opportunities for collaboration, but without clear data to pair students based on their knowledge or challenges, group work is rarely intentionally designed to optimize student learning. 
Offers opportunities to apply math that uses procedures and concepts to solve multi-step, real world problems. Math materials and instruction typically focus on discrete procedural and conceptual knowledge, as opposed to applying math to complex, real-world problems. For too many students, the result is that learning math often feels like a series of monotonous and repetitive tasks that seem meaningless, leading to feelings of apathy and disconnection.  But by bringing in other disciplines like science and statistics, students can have more rewarding experiences that align with their interests and underscore the importance of math in meaningful ways. 
To achieve this vision of the math classroom, we are working to address these problems by improving the quality of instructional materials to make them more engaging and motivating for students that result in increased student outcomes and 2) supporting the effective use of those high-quality instructional materials in schools in California, Florida, New York, and Texas (states that serve 32 percent of the US student population) to establish a beachhead for broader national impact. 
Product Quality Framework
Over the last several months, we’ve clarified our position on specific features within products that we believe are most connected to improving student motivation and engagement in ways that lead to improved student outcomes. This working list of features is called our Product Quality Frame


--- 103125 K-12 Education Learning Session Memo.docx ---
10/31 K-12 Learning Session
The Challenge
Stagnant Growth and Widening Gaps: Why We Can’t Get to Proficient in the Current System
Schools presently have little capacity to accelerate student learning. Achievement gaps persist, and for some students, widen over their career. Before COVID-19, U.S. achievement gains had effectively stalled. NAEP shows a decade of stagnation, with 13-year-old scores slipping from 2012 to 2020, evidence that the system struggles to improve even in stable times.  The pandemic only further exposed the problem; the 2022 NAEP recorded the largest drops on record. In math, eighth graders fell 8 points (back to roughly 2000 levels) and fourth graders fell 5 (back to about 2003); 38% of eighth graders scored below Basic, up from 31% in 2019. Third and eighth grade reading scores also fell 3 points, erasing three decades of slow gains and returning scores to their 1992 levels (NCES, 2023).
When students fall behind, the system rarely helps them accelerate fast enough to catch up. TNTP’s analysis of 28,000 schools identifies roughly 1,300 “trajectory-changing” schools—the top 5%—where students learn about 1.3 years per year, or roughly 0.3 years toward grade level annually. Even there, the timelines are sobering: at that pace, a student 1 year behind needs about 3.3 years to catch up; 3 years behind needs a decade. Scaling today’s best performers, on their current model, won’t move millions of students to proficiency quickly enough (TNTP, 2024). Closing the gap requires solutions beyond those used by even the best schools, AI and a deeper understanding of how we can transform school systems could be the key to closing the gap.  Redesigning school systems and deploying high-dosage, precision interventions capable of delivering extraordinary growth, fast.
Gaps in Belonging and Mental Health Supports Are Driving Student Disengagement
Evidence points to a widening mismatch between students’ needs and what schools have the capacity to provide. Understaffed and overworked adults in the system undermines well-being and nonacademic support: in the CDC’s Youth Risk Behavior Survey, the share of high schoolers reporting persistent sadness or hopelessness rose from 28% in 2011 to 42% in 2021, and 22% seriously considered attempting suicide (CDC, 2023) Engagement is similarly fragile. A 2023 Gallup poll found only 47% of Gen Z students “thriving,” and pre-pandemic Yale research showed that nearly three-quarters of high schoolers’ school-related emotions were negative—most commonly boredom and stress—signaling curricula and pedagogy that are not perceived as relevant or meaningful (Walton Family Foundation & Gallup, 2024; Belli, 2020). Consistent with these trends, the National Center for Education Statistics reports that 80% of public schools saw pandemic-related declines in student behavior and social-emotional development (NCES, 2022). To be sure, these factors have been accelerated by COVID and emerging research on social media.  At the same time, we believe they are baked into the system.  Class sizes of 25-35 students in middle and high schools, and teacher overall student loads of 90- 150 students per day, create enormous variation in the school classroom.  Students sometimes are operating at six different grade levels in a classroom.  Students need more human interaction with adults and their peers. Teacher support for students' emotional and cognitive needs is critical to fostering behavioral engagement and mitigating disengagement difficulties.  Supportive teacher interactions are associated with fewer overall mental health problems—including reduced conduct and anxiety issues—in students, with positive effects shown to last into young adulthood. 
AI is Presently Not Being Deployed to Solve These Challenges and Risks Backlash if Not Quickly Corrected
AI use in U.S. K–12 grew sharply in 2024–25. 54% of students and 53% of ELA/math/science teachers reported using AI for school, yet dominant uses center on effici


--- 022426 K-12 Education Memo.docx ---
One-third of students—disproportionately Black, Latino, and low-income—are at least a year below grade level in math, and only 5% of schools nationally produce even 1.3 years of annual growth. Incremental gains will not close this gap; students who are behind require true acceleration. Our goal going forward is clear: to develop and validate AI tutoring—accessible both in and out of school—that enables students below grade level to achieve 1.6 years of growth in a single year. At the same time, we will ensure that schools continue to have the foundational high-quality instructional materials (HQIM) necessary for AI tutoring to work in school, aiming to reach 7.4m elementary and 4.4m middle school students through HQIM adoption by 2030. This memo outlines the strategic pivots required to make that ambition real: (1) refining what we invest in, with AI tutoring at the center; (2) redefining how we conduct R&D, connecting upstream models to downstream implementation; and (3) clarifying why this focus positions us to drive impact before scaling responsibly through 2030. Based on feedback from our conversation, we will come back in Q3 with a fuller investment and measurement plan. These shifts require us to shift ~20% of our budget to support intensified product investments in AI tutoring and supply and demand shaping work focused on AI-enablement; we will trade off those resources from scaled implementation (~10%) and focus state-specific networks and distribution partners (~10%). In addition to these budget shifts across portfolios, we are adjusting how we invest the baseline ~$600M (57%) of product and market resources towards more of a focus on investments in AI tutoring, teacher supports, and coherence, and are shifting from an AI-informed investment strategy, to one that is AI-centric. Slide 16 details these shifts.
Background: Lessons from Prior Work
From 2009 to 2017, the Gates Foundation invested nearly $300M to catalyze technology-enabled personalized learning across school models, digital content, and enabling infrastructure, including early support for Summit Public Schools. The evidence shows that meaningful gains are possible, particularly for lower-performing students, but depend on sustained, coherent implementation. RAND's 2015 study of 62 personalized learning schools found significant gains (0.27 SD in math, 0.19 SD in reading), with the lowest-performing students consistently outpacing their comparison peers, and K-5 students gaining up to 13 percentile points in math (Pane et al., 2015). Similarly, evaluations of Teach to One, New Classrooms' personalized math model, found that consistently-enrolled students, predominantly low-income, Black, and Latino, grew 23% more in math over three years than a national comparison group (Margolis, 2019). These patterns suggest that impact may be greater for students who haven't been sufficiently engaged in school. Impact also appears linked to sustained implementation: RAND's 2015 study found that growth continued to accumulate in schools with three years of implementation, with two-year effects of 0.27 SD in math across 62 schools and larger effects in the subset of schools with longer implementation histories (Pane et al., 2015). By contrast, evidence on gamification, often seen as a strategy for engaging disengaged learners, shows small to moderate effects on motivation that decrease as novelty wears off, with mixed impacts: one meta-analysis reports moderately positive effects on learning outcomes, while a high-quality U.S. randomized trial finds no statistically significant gains (Huang et al., 2020; Serdiouk et al., 2019).
RAND's 2017 follow-up found much smaller gains (0.09 in math, 0.07 in reading) in a newer cohort, reflecting instructional patterns that consistently erode impact: 1) Fragmentation: Across 40 schools, researchers documented 62 different digital curricula and assessments with little commonality from site to site. 2) Shallow technology use: Technology


--- 022526 Lessons for Personalization in K-12 Memo.docx ---
Bill and Mark,
On February 25, we have an hour scheduled with you to share key learnings from the foundation’s past investments in personalized learning and to discuss how those lessons could inform future strategy in the context of rapidly advancing AI.
From 2009–2017, the foundation pursued a nearly $300M strategy to catalyze technology-enabled personalized learning, investing across school models, digital content, and enabling infrastructure. The results were meaningful but uneven. The strongest and most consistent gains appeared in mathematics and among lower-performing students, particularly when students experienced sustained exposure and when implementation was coherent and well supported (Pane et al., 2015; Pane et al., 2017). 
What limited impact was not the idea of personalization; it was the practical difficulty of delivering it consistently at scale. In practice, implementation ran into the same constraints repeatedly: uneven quality and alignment of digital materials; heavy teacher cognitive load to plan, group, monitor, and respond and clarity/ skill around the skills need to implement personalization; data privacy and governance concerns; organizational and regulatory barriers to competency-based structures and protected mentoring time; and basic interoperability/data-plumbing failures (i.e., identity management, rostering, standards alignment). Summit Learning illustrates this pattern: strong design vision, high implementation load, and persistent scaling headwinds. 
We believe that the thoughtful deployment of AI holds promise to overcome many of these hurdles: delayed or low-quality signals about students’ real-time academic understanding, the time and capacity required to plan and manage differentiation, and inconsistent alignment between materials, standards and school conditions for successful implementation. Grounded in clear instructional and pedagogical objectives and guardrails, AI can make diagnosis, grouping, pacing adjustments, and task selection more reliable and less dependent on teacher time and expertise, permitting the redeployment of financial and human resources to support student acceleration.
Lessons from our past investments are relevant to the shifts we outlined in our February 24 materials. On February 25, two former partners—Diane Tavenner, former CEO of Summit Schools, and Joel Rose, co-founder and CEO of New Classrooms—will join us to share firsthand perspectives on what worked, what constrained impact, and what may be different now as AI capabilities mature.
What We’ve Learned About Personalized Learning
Across the evidence base, personalized learning has shown promising but not definitive effects on student achievement, with the most consistent results in mathematics. The strongest multi-site evidence comes from RAND’s studies of personalized learning schools. In the 2015 RAND study commissioned by the Gates Foundation, students in 62 personalized learning schools outperformed a matched comparison group, with estimated gains of 0.27 standard deviations in math and 0.19 in reading, roughly 11 and 8 percentile points, respectively. Effects were largest in elementary and middle schools where personalized learning practices were more fully integrated into core instruction. Because the design was quasi-experimental rather than randomized, the findings are not strictly causal and could partly reflect broader school-level differences rather than personalized learning itself (Pane et al., 2015). RAND’s 2017 follow-up, examining a newer cohort, found smaller impacts, 0.09 in math and 0.07 in reading, with only math statistically significant. This pattern is consistent with early-stage implementation challenges and underscores the importance of sustained investment in system redesign and instructional coherence (Pane et al., 2017).
A consistent pattern across studies is that impacts appear to grow when implementation is sustained and coherent over multiple years. RAND found that in a subset o

Across our strategy, we seek to significantly increase the number of Black, Latinx, and low-income students who earn a high school diploma, enroll in a postsecondary institution, and are on-track in their first year to obtain a credential with labor market value. We aim to accomplish this by investing in work that has both direct and indirect impact on student outcomes through: (1) catalyzing innovation, widespread availability, and adoption of high-quality solutions, (2) strengthening the capacity of delivery systems to directly improve student outcomes, and (3) enabling conditions and platforms. Solutions
