--- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Cover Note.docx ---
K-12 Assessment
Cover Note
COVID-19’s disruption to the education system has galvanized critics of existing state and federal assessment and accountability systems. Many across the political spectrum are calling for significant reform, if not elimination, of standards-based accountability. This memo explores the history and impact of the present system, considers possible alternatives, and proposes options for the foundation as this debate unfolds. In our conversation on December 2, we seek to align with you on an initial vision for an improved system, explore the foundation’s options for near-term action, and discuss criteria for future stock-takes on this work in an ever-changing education and political context. 
The existing assessment and accountability system primarily provides information to policymakers and a national audience. The system has not given teachers, parents, or students the information they need to improve their own performance. We propose that this system be reweighted to continue addressing policymakers’ concerns (transparency, progress monitoring), while also providing information (including academic and nonacademic measures, measures of school and district conditions) to drive policy and school-based actions that are more responsive to student need. Based on our conversations with the field, we assert that this new system should: 
Maintain transparency into student achievement and expand to require or incentivize the collection of more actionable data, inclusive of measures of school and district conditions (e.g., access to advanced coursework, student-counselor ratios, etc.). 
Ensure that measures and metrics are credible and useful to students, families, teachers, and principals by including their perspectives in the design of the system.
Support actors to intervene in low-performing schools to support students furthest from benchmarks by addressing the conditions preventing their achievement.
To this end, we believe that the foundation should support partners to 1) convene key stakeholders, including parents and communities, to redesign and implement new forms of state and federal accountability with greater credibility across the system, 2) support technical innovation around summative assessment to decrease administrative burden and time dedicated to the upkeep system while maintaining disaggregation of data, and 3) elevate new, emerging district models of measurement that bring together academic, nonacademic, and school measures that enable teachers, principals, district leaders, and school communities to monitor and improve student success. 
These are consequential steps, and they would set us on a clear path to engage in an important, but highly contentious political context when the policy window opens. Any foundation action will likely invite criticism due to the controversial nature of assessment generally – we’ll need to resource our efforts accordingly and will incorporate that recommendation into our broader K-12 strategy discussion next year. Nevertheless, we believe it is essential to participate in this debate, however heated, because of assessment and accountability’s impact on all parts of the foundation’s education portfolio and aspirations for Black and Latino students and students of all races experiencing poverty. Accomplishing the objectives above will create tailwinds for critical priorities in US Program, particularly for our K-12 and Pathways work. While these proposed actions build on two of the foundation’s comparative advantages, our technical expertise and our convening power, moving the field to a new system will not be easy. 


--- 103125 K-12 Education Learning Session Cover Note.docx ---
AI is already reshaping public education. A widely read Atlantic essay captured one student’s view: AI makes it too easy to cheat, procrastinate, and cut corners (Rosario, 2025). Early adoption data are equally mixed. Six in ten K–12 teachers used AI last year, and regular users saved nearly six hours a week. Yet fewer than half say the tools make material more engaging (Walton Family Foundation, 2025), and teachers’ and parents’ worries about eroding critical thinking persist (Doss et al., 2025). Adoption of AI tools are inequitable, clustering in wealthier districts while under-resourced schools lag behind (Laird et al., 2025). Students are split on how they view AI in the classroom: roughly half of Gen Z wants AI taught as essential, even as more than half worry about its impact on critical thinking (Walton Family Foundation, 2025; Doss, 2025). All of this is unfolding in a policy vacuum, only a third of teachers report having school AI guidance, resulting in implementation that is largely unguided and inconsistent across classrooms (Doss et al., 2025).
Our K-12 strategy is making significant progress with AI-powered tools for curricular materials, tutoring, and practices to increase and accelerate student performance in mathematics (See appendix B). Investing in these platforms is a foundational step that allows us to generate immediate gains, refine new instructional approaches, and build an evidence base. We are laying out the groundwork for more ambitious future efforts through adoption and enactment investments. However, as discussed at our October 9 Strategy review, we must be bolder.  Most EdTech implementations have merely focused on digitalizing traditional approaches, replacing textbooks with PDFs or paper worksheets with digital ones—while working within the outdated constructs like the rigid 50-minute period, seven-period day, and conventional staffing models.  Modern schools still reflect a factory-era design that prioritizes uniformity over individuality—constraining personalization and perpetuating achievement gaps. This industrial model remains deeply entrenched, locked in place by three forces: community demand for the familiar, a limited supply of scalable programs, and a K-12 landscape designed to optimize the current paradigm.
We risk gaining incremental efficiency or learning gains without transformation—small, short-term improvements for some and widening gaps for the students who need the most support. In learning sessions beginning on October 31, we will explore how leaders in the field are developing new approaches to exploit AI to create disruptive change based on the intensive study of the system’s existing shortfalls and possibilities emerging in cutting edge tools and platforms. At this learning session, we want to interrogate the proposition that AI provides an opportunity to explore multiple models of AI schools that shift from optimizing the existing school system to imagine recreating schools to better accelerate student learning, provide new forms of social support via human connection, and build students’ agency and metacognitive skills to increasingly navigate a world where information is ubiquitous.  We initially conceptualize the models as a continuum, where technology evolves over time as teachers and students build the capacity to effectively lean into radically different ways of working, teaching, learning and developing.  We are in the early stages of exploring the role the foundation could play in supporting this transformation in K-12 education. As part of this exploration, we will introduce you to foundational thinkers in this field on October 31:  
Sarah Johnson, the CEO of Relay School of Education and Teaching Lab, will describe their work studying the use of AI in New York City classrooms. Her teams focus on how AI tools can provide real-time coaching for teachers and personalized learning paths for students. Johnson will share key findings on the strengths of current AI, suc


--- 022426 K-12 Education Cover Note (1).docx ---
Nationally, only 28% of 8th graders are proficient in math (NAEP, 2024), and roughly one in three students in grades 3–8 are more than a full grade level behind (Reardon et al., 2024). These students are overwhelmingly Black, Latino, and low-income students who, without significant intervention, have little chance of reaching Algebra I proficiency by the end of 9th grade.  A recent TNTP study found that only 5% of schools achieve even 1.3 grade levels of annual growth, underscoring how rare true acceleration remains (TNTP, 2024). While the field has made progress—55% of math classrooms now use high-quality instructional materials (HQIM), a necessary foundation for grade-level learning—this has not yet translated into sufficient impact (Kaufman et al., 2024). 31% of teachers report that HQIM is too challenging for their students, fewer than 15% of students use digital supplements at recommended dosages, and just 8% receive high-dose tutoring, despite strong evidence of its effectiveness (Doan et al., 2023). At the same time, AI use is rapidly expanding—over 85% of teachers and students report using AI tools (though too few receive consistent guidance on how to use it effectively) and early studies show potential benefits in productivity and learning—creating a moment of both opportunity and risk (see slide 4 for more details on headwinds and tailwinds CDT, 2025). Taken together, these data highlight a system where need is acute, core instructional foundations are incomplete, and existing supports are not reaching students at the scale or intensity required—while new AI capabilities, if deliberately designed and integrated, offer a plausible path to strengthening acceleration rather than further fragmenting it.
We hypothesize that achieving meaningful acceleration—defined as 1.6 grade levels of growth in a single year for students below grade level—requires a fundamental shift in how instructional products and implementation strategies are designed, integrated, and supported. This memo and session are designed to explain how we will deliver AI tutoring, as a standalone tool and as observed in school sites, that achieves 1.6 years of growth for students below grade level. TNTP's 2024 report, The Opportunity Makers, provides an empirical foundation. Analyzing thousands of elementary and middle schools, TNTP identified 1,300 "trajectory-changing" schools (5% of those studied) that averaged 1.3 years of student learning annually. These schools shared three qualities: belonging (emotional climate that activates excellence), consistency (reliably good teaching and grade-level content across classrooms), and coherence (unified instructional program). Internationally, the Foundation's Global Education portfolio has demonstrated that acceleration of 1.6 years of learning is achievable not only in pilot initiatives but at national scale when these conditions are met. Programs in Kenya, Uttar Pradesh, El Salvador, Ghana, and Senegal have delivered effect sizes ranging from 0.32 to 0.70 standard deviations on foundational literacy and numeracy assessments—equivalent to or exceeding the 1.6-year acceleration target (see Appendix B for more information on Global Education’s work).  In the next phase of our strategy (2026-2030), we propose a shift to our approach to product and R&D that will help make these qualities more broadly accessible to schools.
We believe student acceleration requires a continued focus on R&D on motivation, engagement, and persistence (see 2025 SR discussion of CMEP, page 3). In addition, we will continue scaling HQIM to reach at least 7.4 million elementary and 4.4 million middle school students—this remains foundational for acceleration in schools. However, we will significantly intensify efforts to develop AI-centric instructional tools, particularly in partnership with frontier AI labs (e.g., Google, OpenAI), with a focus on independently accessed tutoring available through general-purpose AI tools (e.g., ChatGPT)

--- 022526 Lessons for Personalization in K-12 Cover Note.docx ---
On February 25, we have one hour scheduled with you to share key learnings from the foundation's past investments in personalized learning and to discuss how those lessons should inform future strategy as AI capabilities rapidly advance. This conversation connects directly to the K-12 team's proposed direction, which we will walk you through on February 24, including the goal of achieving 1.6 acceleration for students below grade level. That goal rests on delivering personalized learning at scale, something the foundation has pursued before with meaningful but incomplete results.
Between 2009 and 2017, the foundation invested nearly $300 million to catalyze technology-enabled personalized learning across school models, digital content, and enabling infrastructure. The attached memo reviews what the evidence shows about where personalized learning worked and for whom, examines two detailed case studies (Summit Learning and Teach to One) that illustrate both the promise and the practical constraints of scaling these models, and identifies four areas where AI may reduce the implementation burdens that limited earlier efforts.
The core takeaway from our past work is straightforward: what constrained impact was not the idea of personalization itself but the difficulty of delivering it reliably and at scale. Teachers faced heavy cognitive loads, digital materials were fragmented and misaligned, data infrastructure was inadequate, and accountability systems often worked against programs designed to meet students where they were. We believe AI is now positioned to address many of these specific constraints, but only if it is integrated with coherent instructional materials and aligned to the real conditions of schools and districts.
Two former partners, Diane Tavenner (Summit Public Schools) and Joel Rose (New Classrooms), will join us on February 25 to anchor the discussion in practitioner experience. Both led pioneering models supported by the foundation and bring candid perspectives on what limited earlier efforts and what has meaningfully shifted since then. You’ve engaged with Diane and Joel previously, including the conversation in which you described Teach to One as “the future of math.” Their insights make them especially valuable thought partners as we consider what it would take to realize this work effectively in an AI-enabled environment.
Session Objectives
Make sense of lessons from past personalized learning efforts, clarify why they did not scale as anticipated, and identify relevant insights in an AI-enabled context.
Hear from leaders in the field on what is possible now with AI and consider the foundation’s role in pushing work forward.
Session Approach (60 mins)
Meeting Leader & Note-Taker Names
Meeting Leader: Bob Hughes, Director of K-12 Education
Note-Taker: Charlotte Brake, SPM Officer


--- 06022021 K-12 Fund Cover Letter.docx ---
Dear Bill,
We look forward to meeting with you on June 2, 2021 to update you on our work to drive market share of quality products, referred to originally as “the fund.” Our thesis was that we could drive quality in the marketplace by pooling patient capital and – replicating plays made by private equity firms – “buy and build” a full product stack through majority investments in for-profit players. While we do not see a viable route to $1B in capital sourced from mission-aligned investors, we can still achieve the K-12 fund’s original goal of improving K-12 instructional product quality and scale by (a) investing in select providers (primarily math) to build a “full stack” of digital products and reach scale; and (b) supporting states and districts to adopt and implement those products. 
In our pre-read materials, we share with you the latest updates on our work and our proposed investment areas to increase quality in the instructional product market and improve outcomes for Black and Latino students and those experiencing poverty.    
We look forward to hearing your feedback.

--- 08102021 K-12 Math Update Cover Letter.docx ---
K-12 Math Update: Cover Letter
August 10, 2021
We look forward to our time with you on August 10th to discuss our hypotheses for how we improve math achievement in the US. In the enclosed materials, we describe our emerging strategy focus – across a coherent value chain – to (1) improve the market presence and adoption of high-quality instructional materials nationally, but also specifically in California, Florida, New York, Texas, and Washington, and to (2) increase the percentage of students who are prepared for, complete, and reach proficiency in Algebra 1 by the end of the 9th grade in those same states and progress into higher level math with positive math identities and mindsets. If successful, these efforts would reach 6 million (50 percent of) Black and Latino students in grades K-9 in the US but have a much broader impact on all students. We outline our strategy goals as well as seven preliminary investment strategies: 
Data: build stronger data systems that enable our focus states to track students’ developmental progress and academic outcomes culminating in proficiency in Algebra 1 and persistence in math through high school and into college; push for national and state systems of data collection around resource inputs and student outcomes. 
Research and Innovation: explore students’ developmental and academic learning progressions to better understand the barriers they experience in learning foundational concepts and skills necessary to obtain mastery in key math areas; push for rapid commercialization of these insights together with a more comprehensive synthesis of existing research about emerging innovation around math programs and interventions. 
Instructional Materials Efficacy & Improvement: invest in the evaluation and improvement of several full course titles in K-9 math, aligned supplemental supports, and interim assessments; create prototypes for broader evaluation of materials across the market. 
Supply of High-Quality Materials & Digital Integration: support efforts to digitize supplemental and full-course curricula and complementary supports into coherent packages that can be adopted across the country; establish back-end infrastructure to these titles to understand use and improve products over time.
Demand Aggregation for Quality Materials and Professional Learning: aggregate demand around high-quality products and services to improve their market share over the next ten years, including supporting teacher networks to advocate for and support local collaboration around high-quality resources; disseminate research-based tools directly to principals and educators.
Implementation Pilots and Scaling Initiatives: building on the emerging lessons from our existing work (e.g., Networks for School Improvement) invest in district- and intermediary-led sites that use solutions, networking, and broader student on-track metrics to improve math achievement; scale lessons learned via policy and advocacy within focus states.
Targeted Communications Engagement: invest in targeted communications efforts to support advocacy for the adoption of high-quality math curriculum and instructional materials and foster greater understanding among key audiences around the importance of math and the essential ingredients for all students’ success. 
In these materials, we do not address math pathways after Algebra 1. Working with the US Program Pathways team, we will come back to you on this topic later in 2022. 
We look forward to your ongoing partnership as we develop this work. With your feedback, we will finalize development of these plans and progress metrics and targets to present back to you in Q1 2022. We will also be reserving 15 minutes at the end of the meeting to update you on our team restructuring to ensure we have the right expertise on staff to execute this vision.


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Cover Letter.docx ---
Cover Letter 
Mastering elementary and middle school math, culminating in the successful completion of Algebra 1 by the end of the 9th grade, are crucial steps in a student’s academic journey toward adulthood. Students who pass Algebra 1 by 9th grade are twice as likely to graduate high school and are more likely to enroll and graduate with a bachelor’s degree and go on to well-paid careers. Yet the US’s track record at scale for all students – but particularly Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds – remains dismal, with few significant gains in over two decades. With focus, resources, and time, this can be changed.
This type of intractable problem plays to the foundation’s unique technical strengths. Considerable evidence shows this massive level of failure is not inevitable, and, with focused, long-term efforts, the transformation of math instruction is possible. One study has found that students using higher-quality curricula in consecutive years can accumulate several years’ worth of additional learning relative to their peers. Likewise, students taught by math teachers who have received curriculum-aligned professional learning can accumulate additional learning of at least 0.2 standard deviations per year. To realize these findings at scale, the foundation must mobilize educators around effective instructional strategies, engage researchers and developers to develop stronger notions of quality, and equip policymakers and the public with knowledge to scale emerging innovations to shift the future for millions of students. 
We are excited to propose a strategy that can catalyze the transformation of math instruction. Our strategy focuses on three key areas that we believe can dramatically improve instruction: 
Strengthen and scale the quality, efficacy, and usability of digital instructional materials to improve student motivation, engagement, and proficiency.
Increase the number of teachers who are prepared and supported through strong professional learning throughout their careers to provide high-quality instruction. 
Validate the impact districts and schools can have when coherent systems are implemented to support math instruction.
We will also invest in critical enablers across these priority areas, including strengthening the research infrastructure to more rapidly identify and test needed improvements and on raising awareness for key decision makers around definitions of quality to help drive impact. 
And even as we work to optimize the existing education system, we know that longer-term impact will require fundamentally redesigning math instruction. As such, we will also invest to transform math instruction more radically, including efforts to reimagine content, pedagogy, technology, and implementation to increase student engagement, personalization, and agency.
The enclosed materials detail our proposed goals, key progress milestones (i.e., stage gates to assess continued viability of our hypotheses), investment priorities, and measurement plan. We are excited about the transformative potential of a focused, long-term strategy and look forward to our time with you on September 7 to seek your approval and feedback. 
Meeting Name: K-12 Education Strategy Approval
Meeting Leader: Bob Hughes
Meeting Notetaker: Janet Diggs
Acronym List
Key Terms & Definitions


--- 04162024 K-12 Courseware Feature Map Cover Letter.docx ---
We are looking forward to our time with you on April 16, 2024. We are following up from our 2023 strategy review discussion to share our Product Quality Framework that captures the features we believe are needed to make an instructional product high-quality. During the meeting, we will showcase key features in the framework across multiple products to illustrate its use. We believe this framework – with our partners’ products mapped against it – will illustrate how digital products can transform educational experiences for students and teachers in measurable ways that lead to improved student outcomes and saved time for teachers. 
Our product demonstrations will focus primarily on Carnegie Learning, a digital product that provides a fully aligned suite of products – core curriculum (digital and print), supplemental materials to use for student practice (including homework), and assessment – all on a digital platform with dashboards for teachers, students, and parents. Steve Ritter, Co-Founder and Chief Scientist of Carnegie Learning, will join us to discuss how key features, aligned to our Product Quality Framework, have evolved on their platform, based on ongoing research and feedback from students and teachers, and are knit together through a platform that allows students to learn together and individually. Specifically, we will look closely at how adaptive feedback and assessment, multimedia, and AI tutoring are driving personalization for students. 
Members from our K-12 Education team will then briefly showcase two other products – Math Nation and Kiddom – focusing on how they give students choice to select personalized assistance based on where they’re struggling and offer teachers opportunities to personalize instruction. 
At the end of the meeting, we’ll return to the Product Quality Framework to review how our anchor partners’ products are mapped against it. This framework is driving our thinking and understanding of our partners and the wider marketplace, and, while it will continue to evolve, it's informing how we see our unique role as a foundation to drive instructional materials quality, adoption, use, and impact. 
We look forward to your feedback on the framework and hope to solicit your thoughts on future product demos around other feature categories to highlight trends in our partners’ products and across the industry.  


--- 11162020 K-12 Education Roundtable - Meeting Notes.docx ---
K-12 Education Roundtable 
Co-Chair Meeting Notes 
November 16, 2020 
Attendees: Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, Allan Golston, Andy Cook, Bob Hughes, Henry Hipps, Jennifer Engle, Patrick Methvin, Sara Allan, Dan Kauffman, Ankur Vora, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Sam White, June Davis (notetaker). 
External Guests: Mike McGee (Chiefs for Change), John King (Education Trust), Rob Waldron (Curriculum Associates). 
I. Follow ups 
Assessments: Follow up with assessment work underway, including challenges faced by districts around the validity of assessments, to solicit input and feedback.  Share upcoming recommendations from Mike Magee/Chiefs for Change (CFC) on communication.
Digital divide/access: K-12 and North America Team are partnering with Walton Foundation to understand the landscape and progress of access to digital devices and connectivity in the K-12 and postsecondary space, assess ongoing legislative efforts to address it and determine an appropriate role for philanthropy.
II. Key takeaways 
Current state of education field in response to COVID-19
Operational challenges: Mike Magee discussed importance of supporting schools and districts on operational challenges through “day in the life” scenario planning tools before they could shift to academics and grapple with the challenges of virtual environments. 
Damaging racial/class impact of the Digital Divide: Creative efforts by schools and districts to help students get connected, including hotspots on school buses.  Head of Ed Reports Green Curricula Associates, Rob Waldron shared K-8 data showing 1/3 of students are in school, 2/3 are out of school.  Students in low-income communities were farther behind even before COVID crisis.  When students in these communities had access to iReady, they used it more (30% more than in higher income neighborhoods).  John King shared data about disproportionate impact on Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty: 79% of White families had reliable internet access, while 66% of Black families and 61% of Latino families had reliable internet access, with corresponding deeper learning loss toll for those students.
Noise in the assessment data: Students are getting “assistance” from parents or peers, creating noise in the testing data.  In grade K-2, parents are taking diagnostics for their children.  In grades 6-8, peers are helping each other.  
Importance of management: In districts that nailed spring learning loss, leadership actions by the principal and teachers made a difference and was reflected in higher percentage of people using iReady and other digital tools in an integrated fashion.  Baltimore was cited as an exemplar.
Need for policy response at state and federal level: Attempts to push for more of a federal response.  Opportunity for states to step up, with some like Ohio identifying ways to make cuts in ways that were equity advancing or protecting.
Emerging and longer-term opportunities created during the COVID-19 pandemic: 
New structures and redesigned roles of teachers/adults: Redesigned roles of adults in systems to maximize capacity of the best teachers, banked video lessons from master teachers, creation of mentor groups staffed by an adult, a district practice of calling every high school student every day.
Breaking down building barriers: Places like Northern Virginia Community College began offering courses to 11th and 12th graders in the region, breaking the notion that the only class you can take is at the school you attend.
Schools and districts see digital divide as a continuing challenge to virtual/blended learning: Mike Magee identified this as critical to ensuring that digital instruction is equitable for all students and endures post the pandemic.   The group discussed the problem as being very solvable.  This year, districts were competing against each other to get the last Chromebook from suppliers.  If budgets forced personnel reductions, CFC could see innovative responses to that constraint with

--- 02192021 K-12 Education Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies Meeting Notes.docx ---
Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies
Meeting Notes
Meeting Participants: Adam Tucker, Allan Golston, Bill Gates, Bill Tucker, Bob Hughes, Dan Kaufman, Henry Hipps, Jamie McKee, Josh Edelman, June Davis, Larry Cohen, Mariana Preciado, Marie Gibson, Mark Suzman, Mary Alice Heuschel, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Rob Nabors, Sara Allan, Shweta Suresh
Follow Up Needed: 
Develop a strategy-wide theory of change and theory of action that capture how the individual pieces fit together and what levers we hypothesize are integral for advancing Algebra 1 outcomes. 
Detail how prior and current strategy assets and priorities will be leveraged in a strategy focused on math.
Conduct a detailed landscape of US math instruction nationally as well as within USP focus states. This will require us to look at variations in math outcomes, inputs, the dissemination of high-quality materials (or lack thereof), teacher labor markets and preparation around math, postsecondary remediation rates, etc. 
Conduct extensive modeling on what strong research-based math program grades 3-9 could look like when implemented. This is a hypothesis and can help us make sense of variation we see between high and low performers. In addition, it gives us a sense of quality that we can use to foster conversation with the field. 
Work with the USP Pathways team to ensure tight integration with the math pathways work.
Discussion Themes: 
Need for coherence and integration: A focus in math should help us integrate the most impactful work we’ve done over the last 20 years and we need to determine what and how to integrate it into the strategy. What value do we continue to place on teacher observations, human capital management systems, previous tools we’ve funded, etc.? We need to model what we think it takes to support the goals we set and then detail what pieces of that system we now fund and why.  
Importance of growth mindset and identity formation in math instruction and learning: A stronger focus in math provides not just the opportunity to strengthen academic skillsets, but also attends to other parts of a student’s identity formation and social emotional well-being. We hear that you also see the importance of these and will continue to build out how to incorporate this more strongly in our work. 
Account for our comparative advantages and recognize the advantage others possess: As we move toward more strategic coherence, it will be important that we develop work in ways that align with the aspects of other work we fund to integrate with the work of others. And as we learn more, it may be critical for us to take a firmer point of view on ensuring that coherence, e.g., developing tools that align with each other or more strongly advocating for high-quality materials.  
Ensuring we have the right expertise and skillsets on the team: As we develop the strategy, it will also be important that we build the team so that we have the expertise and skillsets we need. Particularly, it will be important to ensure we have strategic and analytic individuals that can make sense of the whole system, not just those with deep expertise in specific parts.


--- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Meeting Notes.docx ---
K-12 Assessment
Meeting Notes
December 2, 2021
Attendees: Adam Tucker, Allan Golston, Angela Jones, Akur Vora, Becky Taylor, Bill Gates, Bob Hughes, Brandee Tate, Carina Wong, Carolyn Ainslie, Dan Kaufman, Gabriella Gomez, Gargee Ghosh, Hein Tran, Jamie McKee, Jennifer Engle, June Davis, Mark Suzman, Melinda French Gates, Michele Meredith, Olivia Polius, Patrick Methvin, Rachel Leifer, Raul Gonzalez, Rhett-Dornbach-Bender, Rob Nabors, Sam White, Sara Allan, Tamika Tasby, Vivian Mihalakis
Follow-Ups:
We agreed to come back as our view becomes crisper regarding a set of technical options for innovation within the assessment system that both meet our values and are politically viable. 
We agreed to keep leadership informed as we are seeing promising practices in measurement in place and as we develop advocates for this work on the front lines. 
Discussion Themes: 
Risks & Messaging: We discussed the need for two tracks in our messaging given the current political climate. We clarified that in the near term, there is little risk to the foundation as we continue to stand behind our partners and message the need for maintaining key assessment and accountability provisions. The risks and challenges arise as we consider alternatives to the current system. 
Limitations of the Current System: There is widespread agreement among our partners and among stakeholders on the ground that the system could benefit from improvement. While we do need to maintain the ability to provide school comparability and transparency through access to data, we also discussed unmet needs by the current system, namely actionability at the school and student level to drive improvement. We also noted a particular need for improvement in the high school assessment space.
Input Measures vs. Output Measures: We discussed the need to separate and distinguish between input and output measures. We agreed that school success should not be determined by input measures. We noted that input measures allow stakeholders making improvement decisions access to additional data to understand where to focus their efforts; similarly, parents and caregivers find input measures critical to their ability to advocate for improvement in schools. 
Timeline: A key milestone for this strategy is the reauthorization of ESSA. However, because the political context is uncertain, there may be opportunities beyond ESSA reauthorization where having a few options for innovation could prove valuable. We have an opportunity to work closely with stakeholders in the near term to understand their needs while looking across states to see where promising practices are emerging.

--- K-12 Education Appendix A 01272020.docx ---
Appendix A: Considering a Focus on Math
Over the last two decades, our education investments have been directed at broadly improving the educational system with the goal of producing national, measurable growth in student outcomes. But results have been mixed. In 2017, based on these lessons learned and a continuing commitment to realize the promise of the Common Core State Standards, we adopted a strategy focused on innovation, solutions, and targeted delivery to improve the success of Black, Latinx, and low-income students graduating in high school and succeeding in the first year of a postsecondary program. We continue to believe that this mix of efforts is promising and will provide valuable proofs of concept. This work is on-track.
Our ambition remains to catalyze national improvements in student outcomes. We also think that now is an opportune time to reflect and consider what achieving broader impact will require, even as our current work plays out. We see opportunities to accelerate impact and would like to consider shifts in parts of our strategy both now and when a majority of our bodies of work reach decision points in 2022. We would like to discuss three preliminary ideas with you in our Strategy Review meeting, anticipating presenting you with more detailed, concrete recommendations by Q4 2020.
Focus: Should we double down on a single area of focus – we suggest mathematics – enabling us to deepen the field’s understanding of change around a particular consequential outcome? 
Place: Should we focus our existing investments in a more limited number of places enabling greater concentration and coordination of our programmatic and policy efforts? 
Time Horizon: Should we commit to and measure progress toward a strategy-wide solutions and delivery impact target for 10+ years, enabling us to understand more practically how investing across innovation, solutions, and delivery can yield impact over a longer time horizon? 
The Importance of Focus.
In our initial decision to anchor our place-based delivery strategy on a set of outcome measures widely used in the field at the middle and high school levels, we shifted the public view of the foundation to a much more collaborative partner seeking to learn alongside the field. And more importantly, we are learning important lessons about what it takes for schools to improve predictive outcomes that are consequential to student success. 
The consequence of this decision from an impact perspective is that, in our effort to codify a highly generalizable model, this approach will not yield impact in any concentrated outcome area. We see the potential value of a focus on a much tighter set of student outcomes to gain a deeper understanding of school- or district-based problems. We have begun to see the importance of strong curricula as a critical component in our Networks for School Improvement, both in efforts to improve the outcome indicator itself, but also with the interaction of academics with other essential school-level systems. 
By intentional design and intensive field interest, math has emerged as a focus of many of our current bodies of work and constitutes an emerging through-line in significant parts of our strategy. This is not surprising. Despite significant efforts to improve mathematics learning in the US dating back to A Nation at Risk, student achievement in mathematics across the country remains far below where it needs to be. Algebra remains the single biggest barrier to on-time high school graduation. In the US, only about 1/4 of students take Algebra in the 8th grade. This trend continues in student participation in higher level mathematics, statistics, and data science in high school and college. Earnings premiums are strongest among those who are both good at math and engaged in activities that demand strong communication skills. The failure to address persistent achievement gaps has a disparate racial and gender impact in both postsecondary success and larg

--- 08102021 K-12 Math Update Appendix A Measurement.docx ---
Appendix A: Preliminary Measurement Plans
August 10, 2021
In this appendix, we outline preliminary measurement plans for capturing the two key components of the proposed math strategy goal: (1) the state-level math educational landscape in our target states and (2) student outcomes in communities. A full draft of the strategy measurement plan will include leading indicators for both areas that will inform our ongoing decision-making will be shared with you in Q2 2022. 
We are moving to a strategy-wide measurement, learning, and evaluation plan. This is a significant shift. Previously, our strategy measurement and learning focused at the body of work-level, e.g., what impact are Networks for School Improvement having, what impact are we seeing within Coherent Instructional Systems. While we will still measure specific areas of the strategy and investments and their activities, we are moving to a cumulative measurement approach that looks at our entire strategy. This means that in future strategy reviews, we will report on progress and impact on state-level math educational landscape in California, Florida, New York, Texas, and Washington and on student outcomes in priority communities to meet our draft goals. 
To support these efforts – and to support our target setting – we are in the process of developing a strategy-wide impact model. The model is currently being developed in collaboration with Mathematica and is intended to inform decision-making by the K-12 leadership team and program officers in relation to math strategy investments. Using the existing evidence base, we will use Bayesian statistical methods to model the potential impact of our strategy. In short, we will generate an impact model that assigns confidence to our hypotheses as inputs to predicted impact based on the “weight” of the evidence for that hypothesis. As more is added to the evidence base – through our work as well as others’ – we will update the model to better understand our potential for long-term impact. We will share a draft of the model with the IDM team for their feedback and look forward to sharing more about it with you in Q2 2022. 
State-Level Math Educational Landscape
Our working definition of “math educational landscape” in our draft math strategy goal is the availability, adoption, and use of high-quality instructional materials and supports. The evidence base and our interviews with district leaders, advocates, educators, families, and students revealed many factors in the math educational landscape that contribute to the student math outcomes we seek to improve. However, there are a concrete set of areas where we are best positioned to have an impact at the state-level, especially with regards to the scaled availability, adoption, and use of high-quality instructional resources and related supports. Specifically, we will measure Availability, Adoption, and Quality Math Instruction (inclusive of use of high-quality instructional materials) as the key outcome areas that track progress and success in our state-level work. Likely leading indicators of these factors include the quality of enabling infrastructure and conditions, market health and information transparency, job-embedded teacher supports for use of instructional materials, and developer and adoption decision-maker beliefs, attitudes, and behaviors.
We will obtain data to measure these factors with a mixed methods approach of self-reported survey data from actors in the district and school systems (using the representative panel infrastructure we’ve already built with the RAND Corporation), qualitative interviews with partners, and publicly available data on resources, purchases, and policies. We will use baseline data to further refine the questions asked on surveys and in interviews and determine what quantitative data collection could add more detail and nuance to the publicly available data. Our measurement work will align with efforts to build out high-quality math data sy


--- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Appendix A.docx ---
Appendix A: Overview of the Current Assessment and Accountability System
The current assessment and accountability regime grew out of the standards movement of the 1990s. Before the 1990s, every state had different approaches to determining proficiency; norm-referenced tests were widely used. Some states, like Kentucky, Texas, and Massachusetts had begun to experiment with policies that would lay the groundwork for a federal system of standards and accountability. Bipartisan coalitions began to coalesce around the “soft bigotry of low expectations” and advocate for higher standards and assessments for students at the national level. Signed into law in 2002, No Child Left Behind (NCLB) ushered in the test-based accountability systems that largely persist today. To pass this legislation, a big-tent coalition joining conservatives and liberals came together in ways we haven’t seen since.
NCLB was succeeded by the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) in 2015 to address NCLB’s many shortcomings, particularly its narrow emphasis on using standardized tests to measure school performance and prescriptive consequences for poor performance – including mandatory school closures. Its deficit-based approach was politically challenging for local actors and had significant unintended consequences. In its first six years, NCLB decreased time on non-tested subjects (e.g., art, music, and physical education) by one-third. Under pressure to perform, teachers reported burnout and turnover, and reports of unethical practices such as manipulating subgroup reporting and cheating were widespread. 
ESSA provides more flexibility in holding schools and educators accountable and intervening in low-performing schools based on evidence. Among other changes, it replaced mandatory consequences based narrowly on measures of academic achievement with a broader set of measures to be used by local stakeholders to advocate for change and determine locally what evidence-based improvement strategies would be appropriate to drive achievement. It also required stakeholders (e.g., parent groups) to be consulted in the process of developing state plans, in efforts to garner support from local communities. 
ESSA requires the reporting of four academic measures and one additional indicator in state accountability frameworks. Note that academic achievement remained the highest weighted indicator, leaving the experience of the system among schools and districts largely unchanged: 
Academic achievement in reading and math (proficiency rates from state standardized summative assessments with 95% participation, annually in grades 3-8 and once in high school)
Another academic indicator, such as student growth in reading and math
Four-year high school graduation rates with the option to include extended year rates
Progress towards English language proficiency for English Language Learners
At least one measure of school quality or student success (e.g., attendance, college and career readiness), known as the “fifth indicator”
Other than English language proficiency, these indicators must be disaggregated by student groups to provide transparency into inequities across subgroups.
In 2017, states were required to submit plans to the U.S. Department of Education detailing how they would implement ESSA before gaining access to any federal funds. In addition, all states had to spend 7 percent of their Title I funds (funds allocated based on the percentage of low-income families) to schools identified as falling into improvement categories. While ESSA did broaden the measures states were required to collect, summative assessment scores still drive state accountability systems and are perceived (as were NCLB tests) to be high-burden and low-value to students. Maintaining ESSA-mandated assessments has been in danger since spring 2020 of the COVID pandemic and eliminating federal testing requirements will be on the table until it is settled. It is far from settled: This year’s spring asses


--- K-12 Education Optional Appendix A 03042021.docx ---
Appendix A: Assessments & Accountability Systems
Over the course of its history, the foundation has strongly supported standards-based reform, a policy strategy aimed at ensuring outcomes for students characterized as: regular summative assessments aligned with clearly articulated standards of learning with results disaggregated by student subgroup to monitor student progress over time and compare student and school results across jurisdictions. This policy regime was pioneered by the states in the early 1990s, but then elevated in Federal policy through No Child Left Behind (and now through the Every Student Succeeds Act). Various studies identify a positive relationship between accountability policies and student achievement. Hanushek and Raymond find a positive relationship between the implementation of accountability policy and student achievement gains from 1992 to 2002 across 42 states. Studies in states such as Texas, North Carolina, Connecticut, and Arkansas have found student achievement gains on both state assessments and NAEP, the National Assessment of Education Progress. 
After early gains, however, NAEP has remained stagnant for the last decade, leading some to wonder whether this strategy can continue to produce gains. COVID-19 has accelerated the conversation about the ongoing viability of this approach on two fronts. First, due to COVID, school district and state officials found themselves unable to administer tests in a manner that met the reliability thresholds required by Federal policy. As a response, in the Spring of 2020, the U.S. Department of Education granted testing waivers to all 50 states and D.C., eliminating mass standardized testing for the 2019-20 school year. In the next few months, policymakers will be forced to address data collection and 2021 testing: 23 states have publicly stated that assessment data will be critical to see where students stand, identify gaps, and allocate resources, and at least another nine have released details of their actual plans for spring assessments in-person. At the same time, federal policymakers are also providing greater flexibility to districts to administer these tests safely or shortening assessments to reduce testing time, though plans are likely to change over the next few months. On a second front, some parents and community members who find little practical utility in the assessments themselves believe they drive greater inequity into the school system, perceiving that test results are used to further segregate students and deny them opportunity – exactly the opposite of what the policy regime intends are increasingly asking for larger systemic change. 
More broadly, there is widespread awareness that the present system is fragmented and may no longer drive student achievement as conceived. We, other foundations, and national education and civil rights organizations have yet to arrive at a consensus about appropriate next steps. While there is a desire to resume data collection around student performance as soon as possible, there is heightened awareness of the problems of the current, highly fractured assessment and accountability regime. These problems run much deeper than whether to administer tests or whether a shift in type of assessment would greatly improve student outcomes. Among these are:
There is a consensus that end-of-year tests do not provide actionable information to teachers in time to inform classroom instruction and fully evaluate the impact of programmatic and school-wide interventions. A strongly held hypothesis is that a richer set of measures, including end-of-year tests to maintain comparability but extending beyond that, are needed to support systematic improvement. We need to move from a system that relies mainly on a single test to a coherent system of measures.
Some argue that annual testing may be inconsistent with growth as revealed in math curricula and learning management systems because it does not sufficiently measure s

--- K-12 Education Appendix B 01272020.docx ---
Appendix B: Middle Years Math Deep Dive
Our investments to improve math learning are illustrative of the overall approach we are taking in education R&D. We are investing in rigorous research, development, and evaluation of promising interventions and on discovery of new ones. We are making focused bets on education technology as a key mechanism to shift teaching practice and learning experiences at scale. We hypothesize that effective interventions can ultimately be integrated into existing instructional systems (digital and non-digital) to ensure coherence and to access existing delivery channels to schools. Through our partnership with Mathematica, we are supporting grantees to systematically build evidence of impact and scalability that will inform what moves forward into a translation and commercialization phase.
Today’s Challenges: We believe that Common Core State Standards, aligned curriculum, and effective professional development are necessary but insufficient for overcoming specific teaching and learning challenges that constrain math outcomes for many students: 
Cultural and societal narratives held and transmitted by teachers and parents negatively influence math identity for specific groups, particularly students of color and girls. 
Common mathematical teaching practices reinforce bias and lack of belonging.
Low teacher pedagogical content knowledge and specific mathematics content knowledge lead to rigid, direct instruction that focuses on procedures and prevents teachers from seeing and addressing common misconceptions.
Most students get inadequate exposure to problem solving to build foundational number sense and make explicit connections – even with good curricula.
Many students have underdeveloped executive functioning skills (e.g. self-control, working memory, cognitive flexibility) that constrain their ability to engage productively.
The effect of these challenges is that most students do not engage deeply enough in doing hard mathematics, and do not develop positive mindsets and identity relative to mathematics, which in turn depresses engagement and perpetuates low attainment in math.
Understanding Success: We aim to codify a set of interventions and ultimately broader solution models that put many more students on-track for success in high school math by the end of 8th grade. The math field consensus defines five strands of mathematical proficiency as:
Conceptual Understanding – comprehension of math concepts, operations, and relations
Procedural Fluency – skill in carrying out procedures flexibly, accurately, and efficiently 
Strategic Competence – ability to formulate, represent, and solve mathematical problems
Adaptive Reasoning – capacity for logical thought, explanation, and justification
Productive Disposition and Mindset – habitual inclination to see math as sensible, useful, and worthwhile, coupled with a belief in diligence and one’s own efficacy.
Practically, we will measure impact through standardized assessments of learning growth and proficiency, student self-report of math mindsets and engagement, and attitudes towards pursuing challenging mathematics opportunities. The threshold for evaluating an individual intervention varies by its scope and focus; however, we are using 2+ years of academic growth in a single year, and proficiency on summative 8th grade assessments for strands #1-4 above.
Investment Hypotheses: Our initial portfolio is designed to test intervention hypotheses across several domains. Most of these projects are 1-2-year development and initial evidence building grants, with several in later stage validation trials.

Lastly, it covers teacher biases, low expectations, capacity, training and ability to make real-world connections in the algebra classroom are barriers to access and success for students.

--- 02192021 K-12 Education Advancing Math Outcomes in Target Geographies Appendix B.docx ---
Appendix B: The Importance of Algebra 1
Successful completion of Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade is a critical milestone for all students, but particularly for Black and Latino students and those experiencing poverty. Completing Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade is a key predictor of on-time high school graduation, postsecondary enrollment and persistence, and opens up a range of careers that depend on math skills. Note: We are neutral on whether students should take the course in 8th or 9th grade, as the ideal time will differ by student. Research studies differ in whether they focus on 8th or 9th grade; data from both levels are included below.
There are four reasons that we propose a focus on Algebra 1.
Algebra 1 is a critical foundational “language” and gateway course to higher level STEM courses in high school. Algebra 1 is considered a gateway, or for many students a gatekeeper, to higher-level math, because it represents the transition from concrete numerical math in earlier grades, through to deeper, conceptual, and abstract math (e.g., structure of equations, generalizing patterns).
Students who pass Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade are more likely to take advanced math courses, graduate from high school, enroll in college, and succeed in college. 
Few students recover from failing algebra and failing the subject in 8th or 9th grade disrupts their progress. Only ~20 percent of students who fail Algebra 1 in 8th grade and repeat it in 9th grade achieve proficiency by the end of 9th grade.
Algebra 1 is a requirement for graduation in many states yet is a hurdle for many students. 
A California study found that students who passed Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade graduated at twice the rate of those who didn’t (70 percent versus 35 percent).
In 2013, 42 states required successful completion of at least 3 years of math, with 16 of those requiring four years; Algebra 1 is explicitly stated as a graduation requirement in 23 of these states, but even in the states where it’s not, the course is a critical step in meeting the graduation requirement for math because students may not be able to take the more advanced math courses without passing Algebra 1. Failing Algebra 1 puts students at significant risk of not completing high school, and Algebra 1 has the highest failure rate of any high school course.
Completing Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade allows students sufficient time to complete next level courses to ensure they are adequately prepared for college. 
Students who take Algebra 1 or higher in 8th grade are significantly more likely to be college ready than students who do not.
Mastery of algebra is a critical step to enrollment and success in a college preparatory math sequence (can include trigonometry, pre-calculus, and calculus). Completion of these courses in high school strongly predicts how well students are prepared for postsecondary-level math.
Math, and more broadly STEM, skills are the future of the workforce, and thus advancement is this area is critical to students’ future career options and earning potential. 
Success in advanced high school math courses predicts postsecondary success and careers in STEM (Adelman, 1999).
STEM skills are in high demand but also short supply across the economy. For individuals lacking these skills, they face intense competition for jobs and potentially lower long-term salaries. 
This is significant, as we know the importance and need for STEM skills will only continue to grow in the future.
Therefore, to increase enrollment and successful completion of Algebra 1 by the end of 9th grade among priority students, we need to ensure students not only enroll, but are equipped with the skills required from earlier grades to pass Algebra 1. As a result, we are exploring focusing more of our work on grades 3 through 9. We propose a start with 3rd grade because it’s when students start to move from mastering the "basics" of math to applying them in more conceptual ways (includin


--- 08102021 K-12 Math Update Appendix B Learning Progressions.docx ---
Appendix B: Learning Progressions
August 10, 2021
One potential area for research is around learning progressions and learning trajectories and their codification, validation, use, and role in curricula. Research shows that when children learn math, there is an inherent progression of increased complexity and a set of predictable obstacles; however, multiple paths to the same learning goal are possible. To date, learning progressions work has made valuable contributions to the field (e.g., the Common Core State Standards) as it has created a deep understanding of developmental progressions in discrete math domains. Learning progressions and learning trajectories (which we refer to as “learning progressions” hereafter) are based on development; they “represent hypotheses about how students’ understanding develops and may not be tied to specific ages or grades.” 
State of the Literature
There are approximately 75 distinct learning progressions which are supported by rich and relevant research, but they are largely inaccessible as the insights are buried in academic papers which are not actionable for teachers. Additionally, fewer than 20 percent use formal psychometric models to determine the reliability of the learning progression. Reliability testing of this nature likely decreases further when learning progressions developed outside of academia are considered, although these tend to be more accessible to users. Further, learning progressions are concentrated primarily in elementary and middle school content (91 percent plus). The distribution of topics covered by the learning progressions is irregular with 45 percent focused on number, contrasted with just 8 percent on geometry. More research would be required to understand why this discrepancy exists. It may be in part because number sense is generally considered a cornerstone of math, making it a natural topic of study. Whereas we teach less geometry which may make for less compelling research. 
Lastly, most of the learning progression participants have been white, rural or suburban, middle-class students with little representation across various groups or achievement levels. They have not been tested with different subgroups who may have different factors affecting motivation and engagement.
There are four key curricular approaches to learning progressions. Of them, the Traditional Learning Progression is the most common, while the Adaptive Learning Progression is the most nascent. The role of tech in the Adaptive Learning Progression is not to make teachers redundant, but rather to leverage artificial intelligence to identify mastered and missing skills to keep a student on track, so teachers can focus on addressing misunderstandings and nuanced concepts. 
Approach 1: Traditional Learning Progression 
The Traditional Learning Progression directs students on a linear path where all students move through the same sequence at the same pace to achieve a fixed learning goal. Rooted in curricular sequences and activities mapped in the Common Core State Standards, this is the most common learning progression that we tend to see in practice today. 
A helpful visual metaphor for the Traditional Learning Progression is a staircase, which shows a typical classroom approach in which a teacher provides lessons and activities intended to move students through each developmental step to attain a specific goal within the same timeframe. While the appeal of the Traditional Learning Progression lies in its ability to standardize instruction and assessment, it does not accurately reflect how children naturally learn, and it is criticized for its one-size-fits-all approach and role in exacerbating equity and opportunity gaps. 
Approach 2: Asynchronous Learning Progression
Like the Traditional Learning Progression, the Asynchronous Learning Progression directs all students on the same linear path, but the delivery and assessment mechanisms enable individuals to work at their own pace to achie


--- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Appendix B.docx ---
Appendix B: Near-Term Assessment Strategy
Key Points
Lingering instability within the education system due to COVID creates opportunities for opponents of assessments and accountability to advocate for waiving spring 2022 summative assessments altogether and related accountability requirements.
There is strong interest among funders for new policy ideas around assessments and accountability, but we have yet to develop our own clear positions on what a good system would look like, and there is limited appetite among our grantees and key Hill leaders for any changes to current law soon.
We will focus over the next several months on opposing blanket testing and accountability waivers to preserve the primary guardrails of the current system.
Current Policy and Political Landscape
Driven by the pandemic, K-12 public schools across the country closed their doors in the spring of 2020. Since then, maintaining ESSA-mandated summative assessments has been at risk. The issue is far from settled: the spring 2021 assessments were a patchwork across states due to COVID and the U.S. Department of Education’s granting of waivers from ESSA requirements, and there currently is no specific solution or agreed-upon pathway for future assessments. Driven by the Delta variant, over 7,000 schools have already opened and quickly closed due to COVID outbreaks, creating further momentum for opponents of testing and accountability.  
Over the past year, we have met with other funders regularly to discuss new policy ideas around assessments and accountability. In defining problems with the current system, funders have identified the need for tests that can help teachers and schools address COVID-related learning loss as a key issue, as well as maintain data comparability as a “system check” for equity. Among our partners, there is growing acknowledgement that the current system is not achieving intended results. Many believe the current accountability policy is not effectively addressing the needs of students attending the lowest-performing schools. At the same time, however, they fear moving too quickly to new solutions would place current law at risk, making future policy improvements more difficult. 
Meanwhile, teachers’ unions have been vigorously advocating on the Hill and with the Administration to end federal testing mandates, while others on the opposite side of the political spectrum (e.g., Florida Gov. De Santis and Georgia Gov. Kemp) are pushing for changes in their states or at the federal level designed to decrease the role of summative assessments. Like key grantees, the Democratic education committee staff on the Hill has some interest in developing new and innovative ideas that can make tests more useful to teachers and parents but worry about rocking the boat now as they fear losing a fight with the unions on assessments.  
Near-Term Approach: Fighting to Maintain the Status Quo
Because pushing to resume state assessments and school improvement requirements would require no changes in law, and because our key partners have already been engaged in these efforts over the last year, maintaining current requirements will not require grantees to make major advocacy strategy changes. Our grantees are united in support of current law, even though they privately question its efficacy; their main concern is action that might weaken the law without having a replacement on hand. As state education agencies get closer to a steady state, system leader groups such as the Council of Chief State School Officers will likely support current law to avoid further disruption to their members, while the teachers’ unions will continue to advocate against current law until reauthorization. 
We plan to push over the next 3-4 months – through partners such as the Education Trust and other civil rights groups focused on education, plus other respected and influential partners like the Alliance for Excellence in Education, Center for American Progress, and Data Qua


--- K-12 Education Appendix B 10242023.docx ---
Appendix B: Networks for School Improvement
Since August 2018, the foundation has supported 24 organizations supporting 38 networks of middle and high schools serving almost 700,000 students of which 20 percent identify as Black, 60 percent identify as Latino, and 66 percent are from low-income backgrounds. Networks for School Improvement grantees (“networks”) continue to converge and embrace common solutions (e.g., interventions, tools, measures, practices, etc.) thereby reducing variation within and across networks. In a recent social network analysis (January 2023), intermediaries reported strong connections to an average of nine other intermediaries, and more than half of those connections were formed through the Networks for School Improvement Community of Practice. Intermediaries such as High Tech High, Bank Street, Connecticut Rise, and New Visions Public Schools have emerged as key information brokers across the overall network. Surveys of district-based network members show they have positive perceptions of the network work. Nearly 85 percent would recommend the network to a colleague and 75 percent believe their network is making a difference for the students.   
Though overall on-track rates remain low, this year we found that 45 percent of schools have increased in the number of on-track students from baseline, varying by their networks’ focus. Gains were highest in Well-Matched Postsecondary schools (72 percent), followed by College Ready On-Track (39 percent), 8th Grade On-Track (37 percent) and 9th Grade On-Track (26 percent). Eighth and 9th grade networks’ outcomes are depressed by the heavy role of attendance in the metric. A set of promising practices are connected to increases in individual components of the on-track metric: 
Network Design: Networks with a narrower focus (e.g., select improvement strategies, specific populations of students) are more likely to see greater change in their focal indicators. For example, New Vision for Public School saw large effects for GPA and has one intervention: advisory. On the other hand, Texas NSI showed declines and has eight interventions.  
Measurement Capacity: Networks with systems to collect and use progress metrics, measurement tools, and strong analytic skills are more likely to see greater change in their indicators. For example, CORE, Teaching Matters, and New Visions for Public Schools all have aligned performance measures, predictive data, protocols for action, and strong analytic partners. City Year and Texas NSI, on the other hand, lack proximate measures and access to data thereby limiting their ability to track progress and adjust strategies.   
Content Differences: Networks focused on English Language Arts saw more change in their indicators than math networks. This could be due to differences in implementation focus: most English Language Arts networks implement practices that are consequential to comprehending texts, a core skill for literacy learning. Math-focused networks, on the other hand, focus on pedagogical practices and inclusive learning cultures that are divorced from specific math content such as fractions.  
Maturity: Longer-established networks show larger changes in their focal indicators. Of the 11 networks that are showing large effects on focal indicators, only three – Teaching Matters, Teach Plus, and New Visions for Public Schools – are from our most recent cohort of networks.
We are working with networks to establish long-term sustainability plans (e.g., New Visions, CORE, CT Rise to scale data systems, PERTS – a student survey organization – to adapt and scale student surveys to elementary schools and math, WestEd to further develop proximate measures for classrooms). We are also exploring creating an enduring technical assistance hub at High Tech High or the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching. We will follow up with more information in Q4 2024.

Leveraging Advanced LLMs for Teacher and Student Supports ($71M) : Pilot and scale the use of emerging, multi-modal Large Language Models (LLMs) capable of supporting complex learning across multiple content areas. These tools move beyond specialized tutors to offer integrated support for the interdisciplinary work and personalized learning playlists that characterize Model 2.

AI assistance must be contextually aware of the curriculum and appropriate pedagogy to be truly effective.

This shift in human capital is essential for the teacher-as-facilitator role.
Proof Points ($56M): Build real-world examples with summer school, after-school, and other regulated spaces that integrate multiple skill sets—e.g., vocational training, youth development, mastery-based student exploration, and community service—into a coherent delivery model.


--- 022426 K-12 Education Appendix B.docx ---
Appendix B
Acceleration Evidence from Global Education
Global Education’s research base suggests that K-12’s ambition to accelerate outcomes by 1.6 years of learning is backed by considerable evidence. This appendix makes four points: 1) It is possible to estimate 1.6 years of acceleration across countries by converting learning impacts to effect sizes; 2) The global education space has many examples of large scale programs that have hit or exceeded that 1.6 year acceleration level; 3) These effective global programs overwhelmingly focus on the instructional core and include a small number of common elements (i.e., aligned student learning materials and structured teacher materials, on-going teacher support, and system integration). Increasingly, we are seeing effective applications of AI solutions targeting specific use cases, with potential for greater acceleration of impacts; 4) Directly correlating the size of these impacts in the US space requires caution, given the low starting levels of students in many global studies, which impacts distributions and has implications on effect size calculations. 
A Global Benchmark for Accelerating Learning
Ambition 2045 argues that acceleration impacts of 1.6 years of learning is possible in the US. Based on a range of studies from the US and across low- and middle-income countries, Global Education has defined an additional year of schooling as between 30 and 40% of a standard deviation. Therefore, an additional .6 years of learning equates to approximately a .2SD effect size. Global Education’s strategy targets 15 million additional children supported by programs with a 0.20 SD effect size by 2028. To support stronger comparable measurements across contexts, we are currently supporting JPAL to adapt the cross-country Global Proficiency Framework into an item bank that could be used by researchers across Latin America, Africa, South Asia and Southeast Asia. 
Global Education’s Accelerating Learning Impacts
Global Education’s evidence base shows that acceleration equivalent to 1.6 years of learning is possible not only in pilot initiatives but also at large scale, when particular conditions are met. In Kenya, the Tusome structured pedagogy program delivered 0.70 SD effects , while doubling the percentage of children hitting English and Kiswahili benchmarks in one year. Tusome was implemented in 23,000 primary schools across the country, reaching 7.2M children. At the state level, Uttar Pradesh increased the percentage of children reaching numeracy benchmarks by 3x in 2024, with literacy progress doubling in the same time period (across 137,000 government schools). A national numeracy program in El Salvador (ESMATE) improved learning outcomes by .49 SD in Grade 2, using high quality textbooks with a focus on math problem solving. Ghana’s Learning program, used a structured pedagogy bilingual program focused on local language materials and teacher instructional support, saw a 0.52 SD effect size . Finally, recent Gates funded interventions in Senegal focused on foundational learning remediation showed a 0.32 SD effect . Given the magnitude of the effects Global Ed has consistently seen, our ambitious goals continue to expect impacts of 1.6 years of learning, at national scale. 
Acceleration is Possible When Focused on the Instructional Core
Across the Global Education examples noted above, the consistent pattern is instructional coherence. This is an analogue to the ‘HQIM + support for effective use’ work in K-12. Specifically, effective large scale global education programs change what teachers and students do in the classroom on a daily basis. Nearly universally, the common characteristics of these programs include structured instructional tools (scripted or semi-scripted lessons), integrated materials (teachers’ guides and student learning materials that align with one another, as well as the curriculum), ongoing targeted teacher support (ideally coaching, but also teacher communities


--- 08102021 K-12 Math Update Appendix C Curricula Efficacy.docx ---
Appendix C: Curricula Efficacy Studies Methodology
August 10, 2021
Study Purpose
We have partnered with Mathematica to conduct an analysis of middle school math systems. This research study will deepen understanding of which middle school math curricula positively affect student learning experiences for Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty and how professional learning supports instructional enactment of those curricula.
Study Goals
Assess the efficacy of EdReports "green" compared to "non-green" curricula on student outcomes
Understand how and why curricula transform in teachers’ hands
Understand the relationship between quality of instructional enactment and curricular efficacy
Understand the contribution of high-quality professional learning supports on curricular enactment and efficacy
Conceptual Framework for the Study
Key Study Details
This study uses a quasi-experimental approach to understand each studied title’s comparative impact versus the others to increase student learning experience and achievement. The study sample includes four districts and ~48 schools across those districts. Instructional contexts, curricular transformation, and the quality of curricular enactment will be measured through a combination of surveys, interviews, and observational data. Student learning experience will be measured through student surveys, and student achievement will be measured through a combination of formative assessment, benchmark assessment, and summative assessment.
Curricula
Green rated curricula: Eureka Math, Illustrative Math, Into Math
Non-green rated curricula: Big Ideas, Glencoe Math (California’s version, McGraw Hill), Key Elements of Mathematics Success
Study Districts
Cleveland Metropolitan School District, Los Angeles Unified School District, New York City Department of Education, and San Diego Unified School District
Professional Learning Providers 
Teaching Lab, Houghton Mifflin Harcourt, and Partnership for Los Angeles Schools
Key Information About the Analyses

--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix C Strategy Look Back.docx ---
Appendix C: Strategy Look Back
For the last 20 years, the foundation’s education work has been guided by the same vision: that all students, particularly students of color and students from low-income backgrounds, graduate high school ready to succeed in higher education, the workforce, and life. Though our strategy foci have adjusted throughout the years, they were all grounded in a theory around what system elements are essential for improving student outcomes. The information below summarizes those systems elements.
