--- K-12 Education Appendix C 10242023.docx ---
Appendix C: Measurement, Learning & Evaluation Progress
In 2022, we shared our plan for monitoring the broader state-level outcomes to which our work contributes and the more proximate changes to which we expect our investments will directly lead. This appendix offers an update on our progress against that plan and how we are using the information to inform strategy and decision-making. 
We are on-track with regards to the data we anticipate using to assess student academic outcomes and experience in the math classroom across our focus states. For academic data, we have partnered with the National Center for Analysis of Longitudinal Data in Education Research and the Stanford Education Data Archive to support access to state-level data alongside the ability to make comparisons nationally. For the math classroom experience, we are triangulating across multiple sources of data, including student surveys (obtained through a new partnership with RAND to obtain nationally and state representative student data); teacher, school, and district leader surveys to understand curriculum access, teacher supports, and instructional quality; and market data to track formally adopted curriculum alongside survey reported product use. Looking ahead, we are exploring multiple approaches to improve the quality of our data. We are piloting an approach in New York City Public Schools to use AI to extract meaningful data on annual education plans, including curriculum adoption. We also anticipate deepening our ability to monitor instructional quality at the district-level by supporting district-researcher partnerships within our focus states.
We are also on-track with our impact model development. We partnered with Mathematica to quantitatively model the range of possible 10-year impacts on 9th grade Algebra 1 pass rates as a function of the share of students who are exposed to high-quality instructional materials and effective educators, as amplified by effective district instructional systems. Based on meta-analytic scans of the evidence base with a high level of ambition around potential impact, the model uses a mean effect size of 0.25 for instructional content, and 0.63 for effective educators. We used the first version of the model to help set internal expectations for portfolio-level impact and target setting. 
The team is currently developing version 2 of the model based on refinements to the strategy and from recommendations from the Institute for Disease Modeling team. The revised model will leverage new data to improve its accuracy, predict Algebra proficiency rather than passing rates, and explore opportunities to incorporate cohorts to model improvements over time as students experience high-quality math instruction across their school experience. We anticipate the completion of the next version by July 2024. The revised model will be used to adjust strategy targets and inform state plans. 
We are monitoring an important potential challenge to our measurement work, especially as we work with districts on research and data collection. Post-COVID-19, our partners have begun reporting to us that districts are very reticent to approve research and data collection partnerships, especially if such efforts do not align with their immediate priorities. To mitigate this risk, we are prioritizing data collection approaches that do not require district approval or resources and making special effort to align our data priorities with those of our partner districts. 


--- 04162024 K-12 Courseware Feature Map Appendix C Carnegie Learning Detailed Overview.docx ---
Appendix C: Additional Detail on Carnegie Learning
Market Potential and scale 
Over the last five years, Carnegie Learning has grown to over $100+ million in revenue, generating average operating profit margins of 5 percent while increasing investment in product development. This last point is salient: Carnegie Learning is majority-owned and controlled by Madison Dearborn Partners, a middle-market private equity fund that has raised over $31 billion. Madison Dearborn Partners acquired an unlevered controlling stake in Carnegie Learning in 2020. With the support of their sponsor, we see Carnegie Learning continue to make significant investments in product while maintaining profitability; this profile stands in contrast to larger market incumbents which are typically saddled with high debt service requirements. 
As of 2023, five percent of middle school teachers nationally report using Carnegie Learning in their classrooms, a number that varies by state. 
We expect this to grow in the next few years with upcoming state adoptions. 
Carnegie Learning has developed a strong presence in Texas (10 percent of the national market), a foundation priority state, where it was selected as a white-labeled, freely available math program from the state agency for grades 6-12 in the state. As part of this selection, the Texas Education Association used federal funding to provide Carnegie Learning with ~$20M in upfront funding for state-specific modifications and a perpetual license to its content (they provided similar funding and support to Great Minds for K-5 math and Amplify for a literacy content). School districts in Texas can choose to adopt a state specific version of Carnegie Learning’s textbook (the Carnegie Learning Texas Math Solution) at no direct cost. This has supported strong recent growth in Texas for Carnegie Learning: early market data indicates that Carnegie Learning has increased its market share 150 percent from 8 percent in 2020-21 through 2022-23 to 19 percent in 2023-24, which aligns with 20 percent of teachers currently reporting using Carnegie Learning. With newly-enacted legislation (House Bill 1605), the state is further increasing incentives to adopt high-quality materials broadly and its white-labeled curricula in particular. Districts will now receive an incremental $40 per student per year to adopt products approved by the state board of education, and a further $20 to print the white-labeled curricula. Consequently, a district selecting the Texas Carnegie Math Solution can receive printed textbooks (and potentially practice workbooks) at no out-of-pocket cost and can repurpose the $40 to purchase other instructional materials, including digital supports like MATHia and/or professional learning for teachers. We expect these state-led incentives will bolster further growth in Texas for Carnegie Learning as well as foundation co travelers Great Minds and Amplify (they are committed to quality) in the coming years. 
Carnegie Learning will submit proposals to be included on the official state curriculum adoption lists in California (2026) and Texas (2027). 
They are also considering partnering with the foundation to develop a Florida-specific version of their product (to meet the Florida-specific state standards) for their upcoming curriculum adoption in 2027.
Focus State Market Projections
Evidence of impact 
 In 2014, The RAND Corporation, funded by the U.S. Department of Education, conducted a randomized control trial of Carnegie Learning’s blended core Algebra 1 course in 2014. The study compared schools that were given Carnegie Learning’s blended core solution for Algebra 1 to schools that continued to use the curriculum they had in place. Each school in the study participated in the experiment for two years, leading to two cohorts of Algebra I students. The McGraw-Hill Acuity series was used for pre- and post-tests, and results are based on scores on that exam.

--- 103125 K-12 Education Learning Session Appendix C.docx ---
The State of AI in K-12 Schools
Overview: AI use in U.S. K–12 education is accelerating rapidly. While adoption has potentially brought new opportunities for efficiency and personalization, it has also introduced challenges related to equity, data privacy, and concerns around the development of critical thinking skills. Training and school policies for students and teachers on how to use AI lags far behind AI usage rates in schools.
Artificial Intelligence Market Data
By July 2025, nearly 10% of the global population reported using ChatGPT on a weekly basis. Nearly half of all messages from adults were generated by users under age 26, though age disparities have narrowed over time. Three conversation categories together account for roughly 80% of total ChatGPT usage: Practical Guidance, Seeking Information, and Writing (Chatterji et al, 2025).
As ChatGPT usage continues to expand, non-work-related messages have risen from about 53% to over 70% of all activity. This shift indicates that ChatGPT, and AI more broadly, is becoming an increasingly routine feature of people’s everyday lives (Chatterji et al, 2025).
Data from Anthropic show that education and science usage shares are on the rise. While the use of Claude for coding continues to dominate the total sample at 36%, educational tasks surged from 9.3% to 12.4%. In particular, tasks related to developing instructional materials increased by 1.3 percentage points, from a base of 0.2 percent to 1.5 percent—a more than six-fold increase that may signal growing adoption among educators (Anthropic, 2025).
AI Adoption and Usage Trends
The 2024–2025 school year marked rapid growth in AI use in schools. In 2025, 54% of students and 53% of English language arts, math, and science teachers reported using AI for school—an increase of more than 15 percentage points compared with surveys in the past 1-2 years (Doss et al., 2025).
More high school than middle school students reported using AI for school, and progressively higher percentages of elementary, middle, and high school teachers said that they used AI during the school year (Doss et al., 2025).
Parents with higher incomes or who live in urban and suburban areas report higher rates of AI use for themselves and their children (Laird et al., 2025).
56% of students said they have conversations with AI at least once a week, using it for a variety of academic and non-academic purposes (Laird et al., 2025).
Among teens who use generative AI, the most commonly reported activities are using gen AI for homework help (53%), to stave off boredom (42%), and for translation (41%) (Common Sense Media, 2024).
The most commonly used AI tools in class and for homework are ChatGPT (45% students), Google Translate (15%), and Google Gemini (13%). 45% of Black students reported not using any of these tools, compared with 40% of White students, Hispanic students, and all respondents overall (RAND AYP, 2025).
Instructional & Operational Impacts
Districts are using AI primarily to reduce teacher workload and improve productivity, with limited exploration of how AI might drive deeper instructional redesign or systemic change (Dusseault et al., 2025).
Teachers who use AI weekly for at least one task report saving nearly six hours per week—adding up to almost six weeks saved over the course of a school year. The most common uses include lesson planning and creating assignments (Walton Family Foundation, 2025).
Beyond improving efficiency, most teachers say AI enhances the quality and accessibility of their materials. However, fewer than half believe it will make materials more engaging (Walton Family Foundation, 2025).
About half of Gen Z students believe schools should both permit AI use in class and teach students how to use it effectively. This may be because most Gen Z students believe they will need AI skills for their postgraduation futures (Walton Family Foundation, 2025).
More than half of licensed special education teachers use AI to assist with IE

K-12 Education Roundtable: Appendix 
November 16, 2020 
Organization Info & Investment History 
Chiefs for Change
Mike Magee
CEO, Chiefs of Change
Mike Magee is CEO of Chiefs for Change. Previously, he co-founded and was CEO of Rhode Island Mayoral Academies. As CEO of Rhode Island Mayoral Academies, he built a statewide network of regional, diverse-by-design public charter schools while successfully advocating for sweeping changes to state education policy. Before starting Rhode Island Mayoral Academies, for a dozen years, Magee taught American literature and philosophy at Haverford College, Wheaton College, and the Rhode Island School of Design. In 2004, his book, Emancipating Pragmatism, won the Elizabeth Agee Prize in American Studies. He is a 2013 Pahara-Aspen Education Fellow and Pahara Fellowship moderator. Magee holds a Ph.D. in English from the University of Pennsylvania and a bachelor’s in political science and English from the College of the Holy Cross.
Organization Overview 
Chiefs for Change is a nonprofit, bipartisan organization whose mission is to build a diverse community of state and local education system leaders dedicated to the shared vision that every public school prepares students for today’s world and tomorrow’s, and support them in ways that maximize the success of their efforts. Chiefs for Change includes 41 education commissioners, state superintendents, and district superintendents serving over 7 million students, 454,000 teachers, and 14,000 schools. Chiefs for Change spent the last five years building out and implementing a strategy for impact on public education across the nation. We have created synergy between our three areas of work – member support, leadership development and advocacy – by aligning that work around a clear and coherent set of beliefs: Children deserve safe and welcoming schools, access to an excellent education, fully prepared teachers with the support they need to do their jobs well, high-quality instructional materials, and reliable and affordable pathways to college and meaningful careers. Our north star is a system in which all students, irrespective of geography or economic means, have fair access to all schools and equitable, flexible, and affordable pathways to college and meaningful careers.

John B. King Jr. is the president and CEO of The Education Trust, a national nonprofit organization that seeks to identify and close educational opportunity and achievement gaps. King served as U.S. Secretary of Education in the Obama administration. Prior to that role, King carried out the duties of Deputy Secretary, overseeing policies and programs related to P-12 education, English learners, special education, innovation, and agency operations. He holds an Ed.D. from Columbia University, a JD from Yale, and B.A. from Harvard.
Organization Overview
The Education Trust is a national nonprofit that works to close opportunity gaps that disproportionately affect students of color and students from low-income families. Through research and advocacy, Ed Trust supports efforts that expand excellence and equity in education from preschool through college, increase college access and completion particularly for historically underserved students, engage diverse communities dedicated to education equity, and increase political and public will to act on equity issues.

Appendix D: Networks for School Improvement Lessons on Math
August 10, 2021
The Networks for School Improvement portfolio set out to codify a delivery model(s) that supports school teams to solve school-based problems that affect student achievement by using data to engage in rapid improvement cycles. We are three years into the process of testing and validating an improvement approach so that a model can be codified and replicated and have a growing set of data, resources, tools, and insights to inform and support both the conceptualization and the execution of the new math strategy:
What’s Next
Understanding Student and Systems Outcomes: What are we learning about the tension and work required to bridge the gap between the development of locally driven measures that support rapid-cycle improvement in classrooms, and the need to collect consistent data across the portfolio to validate and refine the delivery model? 
Continuing to Build Intermediary Capacity: The lever for change through Networks for School Improvement is the intermediary organization, and a goal of this work is to “codify an improvement model” for intermediaries. 
Planning for Sustainability and Scale: Emerging evidence about the importance of depth, ownership, and other aspects of early planning for sustainability beyond the horizon of the grant.

August 10, 2021
As we develop our strategy, it is crucial we include the perspectives of math experts, other education experts, practitioners, students, and parents. --- 12022021 K-12 Assessment Appendix D.docx ---
Appendix D: Risk Analysis Around Assessments and Accountability 
Below, we summarize a few scenarios for foundation and partner exploration on the assessment and accountability system:
Option 1: Continue fighting to maintain current law through our key partners while taking no position on a new vision for the system (see Appendix B for more detail): Because pushing to resume state assessments and school improvement requirements set by federal law would require no changes in law, and our partners have been engaged in this over the last year, it would not require them to make major advocacy strategy changes or the foundation to allocate new, significant resources. 

Pros: Current law provides comparable data. Maintaining it would be led by partners, with little need for foundation engagement or additional resources, though publicly supporting transparent data on student outcomes would underscore the foundation’s commitment to racial equity. Thus, there is little-to-no brand risk for the foundation among our traditional allies on this issue.

Cons: Current law does not provide actionable data. In addition, given that the current school year has already faced disruptions in a growing number of districts, regardless of fighting to maintain current law we could easily see another patchwork year with weakened requirements (most likely waivers of some accountability requirements such as the 95 percent participation rule and new schools being identified for improvement), which would delay the development of obtaining better assessments and accountability policies (though it would not necessarily be fatal for pursuing those policies in the future if we chose to). Alternatively, success in defending a system that is widely perceived even among its proponents as flawed without proposing any alternatives could paradoxically create even greater pressure longer-term for tossing out the whole system.
Option 2: Continue fighting to maintain current law while accelerating innovation through a learning agenda: Updates that require changes to the law will very likely take years to come to fruition. As noted in Appendix B, based on everything we have heard from our key advocacy grantees and Hill allies, and given what we know about the many other first-order priorities of state and district leaders in the context of school closures, unfinished learning, social and emotional trauma, and ongoing pandemic-related health and safety issues, there is currently little chance that an advocacy push towards a specific vision of a new assessment and accountability system will make headway. There is also a high level of risk that it could provoke backlash against the foundation from proponents and/or opponents of the current system. Most importantly, we are collectively just beginning the process with our grantees and other key stakeholders of developing and testing out alternative models for what a better system would look like. As a result, rather than advocating for specific solutions at this time, the foundation could instead focus on learning which assessment and accountability policies will lead to the values we have identified – actionable, comparable data and effective accountability – through grantee convenings, commissioning papers, etc. and our analysis of this past year’s assessments as well as next year’s assessments (in whatever shape they take). 

Pros: Accelerating innovation would not require a change in law but could require some administrative advocacy to encourage the Department of Education to use its authority to support state and local education agency pilots and demonstrations. Specifically, the Department of Education could shape its learning and research agenda to pursue areas of inquiry that align with the types of assessments and accountability systems we are seeking. It would provide high value (generating new ideas for actionable and comparable data and more effective accountability) in the long run. Other funders are strongly su

Appendix D: Feedback from External Strategy Advisors
Beginning in fall 2021, we engaged nine strategy advisors to provide feedback on our strategy. We met with them three times and recently shared with them our strategy memo and asked that they provide written feedback on the strengths and risks of our approach. Their feedback has been synthesized here. Full feedback from any of the advisors can be provided upon request. 
*Participated in advisor meetings but has not yet provided written feedback on strategy.
Themes from Feedback 
Advisors commended the strategy’s focus on Algebra 1 and the way it builds on the foundation’s strengths in influencing curriculum markets.
“I applaud the major choices reflected in the strategy: the ten-year timeframe that builds on prior investments, the specificity of Algebra 1 as the key outcome, the emphasis on ongoing research, the bet on digital tools…and the prominence of the instructional triangle (student, teacher, subject) as embedded in a complex environment of states, districts, schools, and families.” ~ Jason Zimba 
“The proposed strategy is ambitious and forward-looking…and seems to rest on the compelling and evidence-based argument that teachers and instructional materials are the fulcrums of education.” ~ Shanette Porter 
Advisors agreed that the foundation should continue to invest in improving curriculum for teacher use and in preparing teachers to use high-quality curriculum effectively. 
“More work is always needed to upgrade designs to improve the teacher experience and practice without compromising too much on the mathematics and learning.” ~ Phil Daro
“When developing the product quality dimensions, the foundation should include publishers at the table to ensure the resulting criteria are fully embraced and actionable…. The foundation may wish to pay special attention to innovations that support students with scaffolds and supports to do grade-level work, rather than retreating into remediation.” ~ Jason Zimba 
“It may be worthwhile to explicitly discuss the need to build demand [for high-quality curriculum] – to create stories/memes for teachers, parents, principals, purchasers, and suppliers that highlight how important evidence-grounded approaches to learning, motivation, and identity really are.” ~ Bror Saxberg
Advisors expressed support for the strategy’s professional learning investments and cautioned that particular attention is needed when it comes to teacher mindsets. 
“The focus on quality teacher professional development makes sense to me and uses an approach that builds on partnerships with districts and providers…Assessments of teacher effectiveness (that go beyond measures of their impact on student growth and include timepoints beyond initial teacher training) could be useful for understanding more about recruitment and retention as well as understanding how to pivot and adjust your work in order to adapt to the needs of students.” ~ Shanette Porter
“The strategy could be improved with more discussion of how professional development can help teachers make modifications to curricula and interventions that are more likely to succeed than not…the key is to design for teaching variability…and how to make changes without losing the likely assistance from the active ingredients.” ~ Bror Saxberg
“We have found that changing teacher ideas about student ability was critical to the teachers’ implementing evidence-based teaching practices. Many math teachers believe that students either have a math brain or do not, and research shows that low student expectations correlate with low achievement. My concern is that good curriculum will not bring the gains you want, while teachers are still forming expectations about student ability based on race, income, gender....” ~ Jo Boaler

--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix E Measurement Plan.docx ---
Appendix E: Measurement Plan
Our measurement, learning, and evaluation plan will monitor the broader state-level outcomes to which we seek to contribute and the more proximate changes to which we expect our investments will directly lead. It will also lay out a plan for obtaining the evidence we need to test critical strategy assumptions and stay abreast of shifts in the field. We will use this information to inform strategic decisions and ensure we are optimizing use of resources to contribute to our goals. 
Monitoring Progress
 As shown in Figure 1 below, we hold ourselves directly accountable for our strategy milestones – the direct changes connected to our investments. We will set these expected milestones and associated targets and track their progress. Jointly, these milestones articulate our hypotheses about how we think our investments can most strongly contribute to the connected downstream outcomes in our strategy. We will be able to assess whether our strategy is on or off track against our current hypotheses by assessing these milestones.
Figure 1: Measurement Architecture
As we move to the right in Figure 1, we articulate hypotheses about why we think our strategy milestones are important as they connect to an increased likelihood of downstream changes in systems, adults, and, ultimately, students. We also know that other factors outside of our investments contribute to these outcomes, and we intend to monitor many of those, as well. By monitoring the full suite of these connected but decreasingly attributable aspects of our hypotheses, we can use the information to make smarter decisions about how to allocate resources and, if necessary, shift our milestones in consultation with foundation leadership. 
Data Sources & Targets
To do this monitoring work will require us to collect significant data on both student outcomes (academic and non-academic) and system factors, and we are identifying key data sources to support this data collection and to identify gaps we will need to fill. Our plan is to rely on smart proxies wherever possible, triangulate among multiple data sources to generate insights and balance strengths and weaknesses of data sources, and invest in improving the quality of data over time (see Table 1 for illustrative examples below). 
Table 1: Illustrative Examples of Data Sourcing Approach
To use these data, we need to have expectations of change over time – targets – against which to assess progress. For our milestones, these targets are specific and timebound based on our expectations of what our investments will produce. For our strategy goals and downstream measures, we will set directional targets based on historical data, associated milestones, and broader understanding of the evidence base and context. These inputs are captured in a quantitative impact model we have developed with Mathematica Policy Research. We are using this model to set expected trajectories for improvements in systems and student outcomes across focus states over 10+ years, and we will update those trajectories with actuals over time. The relationship between the prediction and actuals will allow us to reassess our hypotheses and make shifts. See Figure 2 for early draft 10-year targets that we will refine over the next year and come back to you with updated targets in our 2023 Strategy Review.
Figure 2. Draft 10-Year State-Level Outcome Targets.
Tests of our Hypotheses
There are critical assumptions that we must test to feel confident in our investment against our hypotheses. We are proposing an ongoing developmental evaluation (an evaluation that acts as a support to decision-makers in the implementation of interventions), which will include discrete tests of impact (e.g., efficacy of particular instructional resources), as well as a correlational (but not causal) understanding of the long-term impacts of the strategy on the key student outcomes of interest, including Algebra 1 proficiency, math mindsets, and future math


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix F Scaling Hypotheses.docx ---
Appendix F: Scaling Hypothesis
The first 10 years of our strategy are focused on demonstrably improving the quality of instructional materials and teaching supports. We will also validate the impact of bringing those together in coherent systems that support instruction for our priority students. With improved quality and availability of materials and teaching supports, we will focus scaling these efforts across our focus states. We anticipate those efforts begin in earnest in the early 2030s.
To see high-quality instructional materials, teacher preparation programs, and professional learning services adopted and used well at scale, we aim to drive incentives for school district decisionmakers to adopt these “as the path of least resistance.”  We will do this by:
Making high-quality materials, professional learning services, and teacher preparation programs widely available in the market within our focus states
Strengthening networks of district decisionmakers to make it more likely that peers point them to quality frameworks and products that meet quality thresholds. This is important given what we know from decisionmakers about the importance of peer validation
Influencing funding streams and policy incentives in focus states to make adoption of quality materials and services the best supported pathway  
These hypotheses are built on the following assumptions and assertions: 
The foundation is best positioned to target states as the unit of scale for our K-12 work given that the ultimate responsibility for K-12 education falls to the individual states.  
Given large number of public resources that already flow to support curriculum adoption and professional learning, sustainability for our priorities requires redirection of these dollars towards better definitions of efficacy and quality.
District decisionmakers tasked with purchasing and implementation need support (access, incentives, and accountability) to change their behavior. We must engage them in ways that reflect how they make decisions in addition to driving them to the evidence.
Illustrative State Scaling Pathways
In tandem with our efforts to increase the availability and adoption of high-quality instructional materials and teacher preparation and learning supports, we will pursue state-specific scaling pathways that will be customized for context in each of our focus states. Below will illustrate a non-state-specific scaling pathway and the signals we’ll look for to understand progress.


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix G Policy and Advocacy.docx ---
Appendix G: Policy & Advocacy Priorities
The following is a phased approach for policy development and advocacy in support of the K-12 Education strategy, followed by the general messaging we will be using to launch the strategy and build awareness. Following strategy approval, by Q1 2023, we will develop a comprehensive and detailed, fully integrated (policy development, advocacy and policymaker engagement, and communications and research) strategic advocacy plan with more specific goals, tactics, and outcome measures that follows the three phases of programmatic work over the next decade. What follows builds on our current and ongoing effort with grantees to provide the underlying information needed to develop the initial phases listed below.
Policy Development and Advocacy 
Meeting the K-12 Education strategy’s goals requires building policy and advocacy grantee capacity over multiple phases anchored in the foundation’s Theory of Influence. We are taking this multi-phased approach based on our experience in the field, most recently in California with our deliberate approach to building a coalition in support of high-quality instructional materials. Without establishing this base of support with partners, we risk failing to make advancements in our goal to support the overall K-12 math strategy. 
Now to Q1 2024
Building Awareness: Discussions with policy and advocacy grantees and other stakeholders make clear that they are not currently focused on math or are not focused on the same solutions or key milestones (like Algebra 1) as is the foundation. Specifically, grantees have not delved as deeply into math as a solution; their focus has been primarily on “enabling conditions” for achieving math equity at scale – access to rigorous coursework and qualified teachers – rather than scaling up a specific math strategy or policy. We will aim to address this by building initial awareness among grantees of math as an equity-focused solution. This is anchored in our overarching messaging mentioned above for a range of audiences. 
Coalition Building: There are currently no state or local-level “advocacy coalitions” prepared to support the K-12 math work, beyond the coalition we painstakingly built in California over the last 3-4 years. In each of the other three focus states, we will essentially be starting from scratch or leveraging a small number of partners, i.e., Impact Florida, Ed Trust NY, and TX 2036, who we hope will work with to build a broader coalition. This means that in each geography we will need to align our goals with an initial set of partners and invest in building their math advocacy and communication capacities, while also helping them become deeper math content experts. This will all happen as we continue to monitor the political environment for windows of opportunity. Over the next several years, before we advocate proactively for adoption of any state and district policies, we will need to: 1) identify and connect with partners in “listening mode”; 2) build awareness about the importance of math and high-quality math materials and instructional supports; 3) establish common goals and advocacy agendas, and 4) build the research, policy, advocacy, and communications capacities of our partners.
Policy Analysis and Development: Parallel to the building of the coalitions on the ground, we plan on going deeper into policy research, analysis, and development of state and federal-level policies that support or incentivize improving quality across the different elements of the K-12 strategy. We will identify and disseminate through grantees exemplar policies incentivizing adoption of high-quality instructional materials as well as other areas including: 1) aligned professional learning through changes in Title II of the Elementary and Secondary Education Act and applicable state-level policies; 2) assessments that better support instruction while maintaining comparability across geographies; 3) teacher licensure, certifi


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix H Communications Plan.docx ---
Appendix H: Communications Plan
In August of 2021, we notified K-12 grantees and partners of our intention to focus our work more directly on math. At that time, the details were not shared, as they were still being developed (this announcement was timed to align with the major K-12 structural changes that occurred last year). With an approved strategy, we will announce the strategy to key stakeholders, including state policymakers, district and school leaders, education influencers and educators, as well as current grantees, partners, and other funders in the space.
The new strategy comes at a critical and complex time: 
COVID-era student outcomes and media coverage have consistently underscored the need to address unfinished learning resulting from the pandemic – which has been especially acute in math and exacerbated opportunity and outcome gaps. 
There is a demonstrated and unmet demand in the workforce for math skills, including real-world problem solving, reasoning and critical thinking skills
The pandemic has also highlighted the need for more high-quality, aligned, and tech-enabled instructional materials and platforms and the need to embrace the availability of technology because of gaps exposed by the pandemic and recovery investments. 
Still, debates are waging in states such as California and Oregon about which course sequences in math best prepare students for future success while also addressing inequitable access to accelerated math pathways that unfairly leave out many Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds.
At the same time, curriculum and instructional materials have emerged as political issues within the broader “culture wars.” A prime example of this is Florida’s recent move to exclude textbooks and curricular materials that included “critical race theory” and “social emotional learning” from the list of approved resources for districts. 
Conservative media outlets continue to reframe inclusive and culturally relevant math instruction as “woke math” to stoke concern and anger.
Layered within the broader contextual dynamics mentioned above are the challenges we expect to encounter that come with any pivots to the foundation’s focus in K-12 education – specifically the critiques that we are too quick to abandon previous strategies and that our strategies don’t address more of the immediate needs and concerns of schools, educators, and parents. It will be critical to clearly address these arguments in the rollout by explaining our thinking and tying past investments and insights to our increased focus on mathematics, professional learning, R&D, and other key strategy anchors. 
PRINCIPLES FOR ANNOUNCING THE STRATEGY
Against this backdrop, our plan to build awareness and support for the new K-12 Education strategy will adhere to several key principles:
Frame the strategy within the context of unfinished learning in math and the need to not only catch students up, but to also seize this opportunity to think differently about how we can better engage students in math and better prepare them for a dynamic, modern world that requires fundamental math skills.
Draw clear connections between our past investments – including our investments in Networks for School Improvement and the Algebra 1 Grand Challenge – and how they inform our current strategy and thinking. This should be seen as a deepening of focus building on several years of investments rather than as an entirely new direction, while our strategy vision for student success remains essentially the same.
Highlight specific investments with key grantees (e.g., Zearn, Illustrative Math, a district or school partner, among others) that show concrete examples of which partners we are funding and what they are doing. This will help increase the likelihood media coverage will not solely home in on the shift and instead highlight our investments and why.
Seize opportunities to frame this work as nonpartisan and focused on identifying and bu


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix I RD Partnerships.docx ---
Appendix I: R&D Infrastructure Partnerships Overview
With the massive switch to digital curricula and cloud-based platforms in 2020, the data available in digital math tools and learning management systems can enable more nuanced R&D on math solution efficacy at scale. In 2022, we funded partnerships to develop durable research tools with large edtech platforms and are developing more in 2023. These partners aim to study key drivers of quality.
In addition to their individual projects, teams across the portfolio are working in a community of practice to: 1) translate learning from their work to share with others; 2) document best practices for working in developer-researcher-practitioner partnerships; 3) understand what culturally responsive math education can and does look like; and 4) develop shared practices for working with data across partners and across teams in the community (e.g., data structures, analysis approaches, etc.). 
Illustrative Research Questions Partners are Exploring
Measuring Motivation, Engagement, Persistence, and Learning in Digital Learning Platforms
There are two assumptions built into our measurement approach: 
It requires that we characterize what we believe represents a valid and repeatable chain of behaviors and events that occur when a student is “motivated”, “engaged”, “persisting”, and “learning,” which has yet to do be done. 
It requires us to believe that data collected through student interactions within digital learning platforms produce information that is valid test of those ideas. 
We have outlined definitions of each concept, along with measurement constructs and tools we could use to measure student behaviors and classroom experiences. 


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix J Teacher Preparation.docx ---
Appendix J: Teacher Preparation Overview
Research shows that teachers are the most important in-school factor in student success, but that schools serving Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds are disproportionately staffed by underprepared and inexperienced teachers who do not reflect the racial demographics of their students. Addressing this inequity is critical to our overall strategy.
Eight years ago, we developed a framework for quality teacher preparation and started providing technical assistance to help programs align to the framework components. Since that time, we successfully spread the adoption of this quality framework and learned what it takes to stand up quality teacher prep programs in a variety of contexts. In addition, we supported numerous universities and their K-12 partners to pilot and launch sustainable, scalable residencies. Though residencies are proven to be high-quality, they have historically been boutique, grant-funded initiatives incapable of operating once grants end and with a business model that made it impossible to scale to meet the hiring demands of districts. 
In 2020, we shifted from a national strategy to one focused deeply in two states: California and Texas. While similar in terms of student demographics, size, and teacher shortages, these two states presented unique political contexts and opportunities to learn. Across both states, they support over 11 million students with >50 percent in each identifying as Latino or Hispanic. In 2019-20 (the most recent year for which data are available), these states prepared more than 34,000 teachers representing more than 22 percent of the teachers prepared nationwide. Thanks to the work of our transformation center grantees, today approximately 15 percent of California teachers and 23 percent of Texas teachers are prepared by institutions that have committed to implementing a high-quality model that aligns to our framework. 
Our existing strategy focuses on building the capacity of in-state technical assistance providers who have local expertise and credibility that positions them well to support additional programs in each state. We are also building relationships with state agency staff and are starting to see success in getting state leaders to adopt parts of our quality definition. For example, last year, the Texas Education Agency launched a teacher residency initiative that aligns with our framework and established a goal to scale sustainable residencies to 65 percent of all programs statewide by 2035. And California included “sustainability” as a required component in their most recent round of residency grants and cited our grantees as exemplars.
Our Proposed Strategy
We will continue to build on the momentum we established in California and Texas by leveraging the relationships we have built with other funders, key state leaders, and advocacy partners to strengthen and diversify the teaching workforce with a goal of increasing the percent of teachers prepared by programs that commit to a high-quality model by at least 20 percentage points from current baselines over the next ten years. 
Building on the successes we are seeing and the lessons we learned, we propose expanding the teacher preparation strategy to New York and Florida. Specifically, we aim to support: 
Technical assistance to expand high-quality, sustainable teacher preparation models, including residency models
Targeted investments to strengthen novice teachers’ pedagogical content knowledge in math and their ability to effectively use high-quality instructional materials
Strategic investments in organizations with potential for national scale to align with our quality framework. 
Technical assistance to K-12 school districts to implement strategic staffing models that contribute to the affordability and sustainability of residency models
States use a patchwork of measures to assess the quality of teacher preparation programs. There is significan


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix K Math Pathways.docx ---
Appendix K: Math Pathways Overview
High schools must diversify math courses to offer pathways that align with students’ postsecondary programs of study and career goals. However, high schools continue to guide students to an algebra-heavy curriculum aligned to a perceived singular higher education calculus pathway, even though the labor market increasingly requires skills such as statistics, data science, and quantitative analysis. Consistent with our approach to partner initially with content developers to help improve math instruction in elementary and middle grades, we also plan to develop 3-4 new, open-source, digital, advanced math courses that reflect best-in-class product features to enhance student motivation, engagement, and persistence and help students transition successfully to their chosen postsecondary program of study. 
Approximately 51 percent of high school math teachers report using materials they create themselves at least weekly, compared to 42 percent of K-8 teachers nationally. The market is dominated by a three-year series that tends to be sold to districts as a package: Algebra 1 / geometry / Algebra 2 and less so by the follow-on advanced and elective math courses. For example, courses in the 3-year series enroll 3 to 4 times more students than the advanced electives (an estimated 2.3M enrollees in Algebra 2 compared to 800,000 in calculus). Working with Illustrative Math, which already offers the three-year series, we will develop an exemplar modernized Algebra 2 course. The course will offer more thorough coverage of non-algebraic concepts, such as statistics and data literacy, but also ensure that college-bound students likely to major in a discipline that requires calculus are on track. 
Given the comparatively nascent market for advanced courses past the three-year series, such as high school data science and statistics courses, we will invest in partnerships between exemplar K-12 curriculum development partners like Zearn and Great Minds, and leading data science and statistics education scholars, to create digital curricula for fourth-year high school math courses in data science, statistics, and – depending on market readiness – potentially quantitative reasoning. One added complexity is that these courses must be designed with an understanding of how the high school courses sequence into and/or fulfill the requirements for similarly titled college courses.  
This would shift from a traditional singular pathway in K-12, which just 16 percent of students (and 6.5 percent of Black students and 10 percent of Latino students) complete.
From:
To: 
Strategy Alignment
High school pathways course development will require attending to student motivation and mindsets, since many students have disengaged from math by high school. As we approach our investments in developing and scaling these four program-aligned math courses we will: 
Prototype and then commercialize key product features of student motivation, engagement, and persistence
Invest in R&D partnerships between exemplar product developers, education researchers, and practitioners to build out coherent high-quality instructional materials specifically with our priority students in mind 
Improve and accelerate R&D on new course content and products, such as instrumenting curricula and building platform infrastructure to facilitate research and foster research partnerships between developers and users to understand impacts on math identity and mindset 
Supplement our future math visioning work by offering lessons on how to develop and implement approaches that challenge traditional math instruction, including interdisciplinary approaches that center data and related real-world projects. 
Data Collection
Finally, we will address gaps in the availability on student performance in math across K-12 and postsecondary systems. The lack of consistent data about high school and postsecondary math course offerings, enrollment, and passing, impedes our abil


--- 09072022 K-12 Education Strategy Approval Appendix L Modernizing Math.docx ---
Appendix L: Modernizing Math Overview
We are reserving up to 10 percent of our budget to generate a radically different future design for math education in the US. We have launched a closed RFP that is intended to help shape our vision, generate investment ideas, identify advisors, and create stakeholder buy-in. This initial phase is being scoped as a 12-month design project with awards of $500K-1M each. We have invited Substantial, Intentional Futures, Stanford D. School, MIT Open Learning Initiative, and IDEO to apply. Selected partners would be expected to bring math, technology, and learning science expertise as well as stakeholder voice to the table. Working with multiple orgs in parallel is intended to yield different perspectives and spur greater creativity. Depending on the results, we may select one for exploration or synthesize the strongest components of each. 
Excerpt from External Facing Language from RFP
Below is language from the RFP that we recently provided to organizations we invited to apply for this project:
Project Overview 
The Gates Foundation U.S. K-12 Education team is developing a potential grantmaking strategy to tackle this challenge over the next 10+ years. As part of this process, we seek to develop a long-range vision of a different design for math education where our priority students are supported to reach their full learning potential in ways that are identity affirming, engaging, relevant, and useful for career and life. We are soliciting preliminary proposals to organize and facilitate a process that can generate this design. We intend to use this design for a variety of purposes, including as a vision to drive our grantmaking, to identify visionary people that we may not have previously met, and potentially to share with the education field. We humbly consider this a huge opportunity to shape the direction of a large and long-term philanthropic effort to tackle one of our society’s most intractable and important challenges.
Potential Criteria for a Math Education Design
We are open to radical ideas and seek to meet new people who hold them, including ideas that draw inspiration from models of math education from around the world. That said, our research and grantmaking to date has led us to a set of hypotheses for potential criteria for a future math education design. We share these only to provide a sense for the types of ideas that may be generated by process:
Demonstrate specifically why and how it can achieve quantifiable significant impact when implemented, through a research basis and a set of hypotheses.
be grounded in our best understanding of how students learn math and the conditions necessary to support that learning. Where the design exceeds present knowledge, designers should identify gaps in existing research or technology, it should outline research to increase its feasibility  
be scalable assuming a relatively constant workforce in terms of interest, prep, and abilities. 
be developmentally appropriate for students and have an empirical basis in the relevant mathematics learning progressions and identity formation.
rely on research-based understandings of why today’s system is leading to students failing in math, paying particular attention to Black and Latino students, and low-income students
link math as it exists to other critical disciplines, including computer science, the sciences, and social sciences. It should empower students to apply existing understandings to new contexts. 
propose pedagogies that build on understandings in learning science, cognitive psychology, etc., 
maximize technology (including potential advances in technology over the next 10 years) to support both students and teachers in the delivery of content, pedagogy, assessment in more personalized, engaging, and collaborative ways. 
overcome barriers in the present system. Examples could include labor shortages and the inherent challenges in teaching groups of students who have varying levels of ma

--- K-12 Education Appendix 1_AI to Disrupt Stagnant School Models _10092025.docx ---
Appendix: A Dual Strategy for the Future of Learning: Optimizing the Classroom and Reinventing the School
Our K-12 strategy is making significant progress, with AI-powered tools for tutoring, feedback, and practice showing clear promise for improving instruction within today's schools. We must continue this vital work. Investing in these platforms is a foundational step, allowing us to generate immediate gains, refine new instructional approaches, and build an evidence base for what works. However, modern schools still reflect a factory-era design that prioritizes uniformity over individuality. Students move through age-based cohorts on rigid timetables, while teachers are expected to be content experts, assessors, and personalizers all at once. This structure sidelines the deeper experiences that foster purpose, belonging, and well-being, and it sustains achievement gaps by constraining agency and personalization.  
We believe that artificial intelligence has the potential to disrupt this outdated model, simultaneously challenging schools’ role as knowledge gatekeepers and offering the chance to create more dynamic, student-centered ecosystems. But the risks are real: AI can erode attention, displace jobs, and amplify inequities if not guided by a clear, human-centered vision that strengthens educators’ roles and prioritizes student well-being. Simply, layering AI’s increasingly sophisticated tools atop outdated school structures will fail to meet our collective ambition to ensure that all students obtain the skills necessary to thrive in our rapidly evolving economic and social environment. To achieve a breakthrough, we must pursue a dual strategy: continue to augment the current system while simultaneously beginning to architect its successor:
From Uniformity to True Personalization: Today’s one-size-fits-all instruction leaves many students bored or behind. AI can power a move toward mastery-based pathways, where persistent AI agents understand each learner’s unique strengths and needs. This new learning "operating system" must credential skills wherever they are learned—in classrooms, internships, or community programs—finally breaking free from rigid, age-based progression.
From Isolation to Dynamic Collaboration: Schooling is too often an individual endeavor. AI can make collaboration the norm, dynamically grouping students to solve problems collectively while receiving tailored support. This model extends beyond the classroom, building networks of peers, mentors, and community partners to reinforce that learning is a social, relational act.
From Siloed Subjects to Integrated Problems: Traditional schooling fragments knowledge. The future of learning lies in organizing education around real-world, interdisciplinary challenges. AI can serve as the connective tissue, helping students and educators design customized, project-based learning paths where success is measured by validated competencies, not seat time.
From Teacher-as-Broadcaster to Teacher-as-Architect: AI can automate administrative and instructional tasks, freeing educators to focus on what humans do best: designing rich learning experiences, mentoring students, and fostering deep human connection. This elevates the profession, expanding roles to include coaches, mentors, and other specialists who guide learners alongside AI agents.
A Three-Stage Pathway to Transformation
We envision this transition occurring across a continuum of three models, each requiring deeper organizational change.
Model 1: AI-Assisted (Augmenting the Traditional School) This model reflects our current investment strategy. It maintains the traditional school structure—bell schedules, subject silos, and age-based cohorts—while using AI to augment existing practices. Teachers use AI for administrative support and planning, while students use adaptive software for targeted practice. Here, AI is a supplementary tool that drives efficiency and incremental improvement.
Model 2: AI-Integrated

--- K-12 Education Notes 030419.docx ---
Strategy Review: K-12 Education
Meeting Notes 
March 4, 2019
Attendees: Adam Tucker, Allan Golston, Andy Cook, Bill Gates, Bob Hughes, Bryan Richardson, Carina Wong, Carolyn Ainslie, Chris Elias, Connie Collingsworth, David Bley, Don Shalvey, Greg Ferrante, Henry Hipps, Jamie McKee, Jean-Claude Brizard, Jill Nishi, Josh Edelman, Katie Ford, Larry Cohen, Lynn Eisenhart, Marie Gibson, Mark Suzman, Mary Alice Heuschel, Michelle Rojas, Patrick Methvin, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Rob Nabors, Rodger Voorhies, Sam White, Sara Allan, Steven Rice, Sue Desmond-Hellman; Trevor Mundel
Data Privacy: The issue of data privacy remains a key concern throughout the K-12 Education strategy. Some districts are reluctant to share de-identified student-level data with the foundation’s Networks for School Improvement data partner. Data privacy concerns among different stakeholders constitute a significant political barrier to creating more data transparency, interoperability, and use. Working across the US Program and with the US Policy, Advocacy & Communications team, we will follow up with you by the end of Q2 2019 with information on the key challenges around data privacy.  
Scale and Impact of Key Solutions: Our plans for scale and impact of key solutions with momentum behind them are not bold enough. Specifically, 1) in high-quality curriculum, we plan to increase support in implementation of high-quality curriculum and instructional systems; and 2) in teacher preparation, we need to assess the scale potential of teacher preparation programs that implement components of clinical residency models. We will follow up with you by Q4 2019 with clear targets for what impact at scale looks like over the next 3-5 years for Curriculum and Teacher Preparation, including cost analyses, detail on the policy environments in key states, and other factors.
Philanthropic Leverage: Our strategy to engage other funders more broadly in our work and in education system improvement is unclear and is viewed as insufficient. We need better ways of communicating aspects of the strategy, a clearer plan of engagement with national and local funders, and a defined framework for judging progress and success around other funders’ engagement. In partnership with the US Policy, Advocacy, and Communications team and Rob Rosen’s team, we will develop a strategy for leveraging local funders where appropriate for our Solutions and Networks for School Improvement portfolios. We will develop a communication strategy for funder engagement around our portfolios that will be responsive to our scale goals, share existing partnerships leveraged in this work, and create a clear design for how we will assess progress against the plan. We will come back to you by the end of Q4 2019 with our thinking and plan. 
Social Emotional Learning: With a stronger evidence base around Social Emotional Learning that we originally hypothesized, we are exploring ways for us to better leverage the momentum in the field. We will come back to you by the end of Q4 2019 we more information on the evidence base, as well as potential investment opportunities.  
Pathways: We did not have enough time to discuss our pathways work in the meeting and will instead follow up with you by the end of Q4 2019 with more information on the US Program’s work supporting pathways from high school to postsecondary to the workforce. 
Advanced Education R&D Portfolio: We had the opportunity to update Bill on our Advanced Education R&D Portfolio at the end of 2018 but have not been able to update Sue. We will follow up with Sue by the end of Q4 2019 to ensure alignment on this work.

K-12 Education Roundtable: Participant Biographies 	
November 16, 2020  
FOUNDATION PARTICIPANTs
Allan Golston 
President, U.S. Program
Allan C. Golston, president of the United States Program, leads the foundation’s efforts to help all students graduate from high school, get to and through college, and get a good job. Originally joining the foundation in 2000 as the chief financial officer, Allan felt a deep connection to the U.S. Program work—which spans early learning, K-12, postsecondary, and education pathways into the workforce, as well as economic mobility and opportunity—and began serving as president in 2006. 
Allan believes education provides a bridge to opportunity unlike any other in America, and he is committed to ensuring all young people have the education and opportunity they deserve. This includes a deep focus on equity in education and using data to get to better outcomes for students of all backgrounds.
Allan’s professional background is in consulting, public accounting, consumer packaged goods, software development, finance, healthcare, and education. He holds an MBA from Seattle University and a bachelor’s degree in accounting from the University of Colorado. Allan is an active community volunteer and serves on the boards of a number of regional and national organizations, including Charter Schools Growth Fund, Gates Philanthropy Partners, and Stryker Corporation. 
Bob Hughes 
Director, K-12 Education, U.S. Program
Robert L. Hughes, director of K-12 Education in the United States Program oversees the work to ensure that we prepare all students for success in college and career.
Before joining the foundation, Hughes was president of New Visions for Public Schools, an New York City school network of 70 district schools serving approximately 45,000 students. Under his leadership, New Visions created 99 district and 7 charter public schools, provided mentoring services to hundreds of new principals, developed school-based certification programs for teachers and principals, and created suites of data and system tools to streamline school operations and track student progress toward graduation and college.
Bob serves on the Board of the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching. Prior board affiliations include Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching, Advocates for Children of New York, Fund For Teachers Board of Directors and Projects in Education, the nonprofit publisher of Education Week.
Dan Kaufman 
Director, USP Policy & Communication 
Dan Kaufman leads the teams responsible for developing policy and using strategic communications in support of the foundation’s programmatic work to strengthen U.S. education and economic mobility. 
Dan brings 20 years of mission-driven strategic communications and policy experience in the nonprofit, for-profit and public sector to the foundation, with more than 15 years in K-12 and higher education. During the last decade, he was a managing director at Finn Partners leading the firm’s Pre-K through 12th grade communications practice and served as a member of the Prince George's County Board of Education (2013-15).
Previously, Dan worked in communications at the National Education Association and its Maryland affiliate, leading successful integrated campaigns around No Child Left Behind, school funding and teacher pensions, and at the American Federation of State, County and Municipal Employees, where he focused on federal and state policy around education, health care, tax reform, and Social Security. 
Dan’s experience in education began as a bilingual/ESL teacher in the Atlanta Public Schools. He has taught college- and graduate-level courses at American University, Georgetown University, George Washington University and the Catholic University of Chile, where he served as a Fulbright scholar.
Dan earned his Ph.D. and M.A. in Political Science from the University of California, San Diego, and B.A. in Political Science from Swarthmore College.

FOUNDATION PARTICIPANTs
Akilah Allen 
Program Officer, K-12 Education 
Akilah Allen is a Program Officer on the K-12 Education Team. Prior to joining the foundation, Akilah worked at the Office of the State Superintendent of Education in Washington, DC, where she led the measurement and evaluation agenda for improved health and academic outcomes. Overseas, Akilah has worked in USAID’s education offices in Indonesia and South Africa, first driving strategy development and later locally leading President Obama’s flagship global leadership development program.
Akilah began her career on Wall Street at Goldman Sachs before transitioning to education. She has served as a New York City public school teacher and worked at the district level for DC Public Schools. She earned her undergraduate degree from Spelman College, Master in Education from CCNY, and Master in Public Policy from the Harvard Kennedy School of Government.
Bob Hughes 
Director, K-12 Education
Robert L. Hughes, director of K-12 Education in the United States Program oversees the work to ensure that we prepare all students for success in college and career.
Before joining the foundation, Hughes was president of New Visions for Public Schools, an New York City school network of 70 district schools serving approximately 45,000 students. Under his leadership, New Visions created 99 district and 7 charter public schools, provided mentoring services to hundreds of new principals, developed school-based certification programs for teachers and principals, and created suites of data and system tools to streamline school operations and track student progress toward graduation and college.
Bob serves on the Board of the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching. Prior board affiliations include Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching, Advocates for Children of New York, Fund For Teachers Board of Directors and Projects in Education, the nonprofit publisher of Education Week.
David Cumberbatch 
Senior Advisor, K-12 Education 
David is a Senior Advisor on the K-12 Education team. He brings over 20 years of experience in the Technology and Education sectors with the last 15 years of his career focused on education technology innovation and businesses. David most recently has served as a Senior Advisor to Vistria Private Equity, New Markets Venture Partners, Village Capital, and a few start-ups in workforce and education technology. He was Founder, President and CEO of ECS Learning Systems, a supplementary education solutions provider. Today ECS serves over 500,000 students annually in multiple states (over 70% students of color and underserved). ECS holds a strong market position in the state of Texas and is partnered with the TEA on its open education resources efforts.
David served as the Chief Strategy Officer and Executive Vice President at K 12 Inc. Prior to that, he also served as the Chief Strategy and Marketing Officer of ACT. At ACT, he was involved in the ACT test's digitization efforts, the launch of ACT Aspire for 3rd to 9th-grade students, the development of ACT Profile, and led the creation of the ACT’s corporate venturing department.
David earned a BA in Computing and Economics from the University of Kent at Canterbury and an MPhil in Management Studies from the University of Oxford, where he attended as a Rhodes Scholar. Out of Business school David also held product management and marketing roles at P&G in Europe and Latin America (6 years) and Microsoft in Redmond (6 years). He began his working life in a gap-year as an Assistant Teacher in his native Trinidad and Tobago's public school system and remains passionate about the potential for technology to transform global education for underserved students and students of color.

August 10, 2021
Adam Tucker 
Deputy Director, K-12 Education, Networks for School Improvement
Adam Tucker is a Deputy Director on the K-12 Education team and helps lead the foundation’s place-based delivery strategy which seeks to test, codify, and scale school improvement models that significantly increase the number of Black, Latino, and students experiencing poverty, who earn a high school diploma, enroll in a post-secondary institution and are on track in their first year to obtain a credential with labor market value. Prior to joining the foundation, Adam led the communications & advocacy division of Edison Schools. 
Before Edison, he served as a vice president of LearnNow, another education management organization. In his current role at the foundation, Adam also draws upon his experience with the Coalition of Essential Schools where he led its school partnerships, public engagement, and advocacy program, and Children Now, a nationally recognized children's advocacy group. He started his education career as a high school history teacher and program administrator in New England. Adam is a graduate of Brown University.
Bob Hughes 
Director, K-12 Education
Bob Hughes, director of K-12 Education in the United States Program, oversees the work to ensure that we prepare all students for success in college and career. Dan Kaufman 
Director, US Program Policy & Communications 
Dan Kaufman leads the teams responsible for developing policy and using strategic communications in support of the foundation’s programmatic work to strengthen U.S. education and economic mobility. 
Dan brings 20 years of mission-driven strategic communications and policy experience in the nonprofit, for-profit and public sector to the foundation, with more than 15 years in K-12 and higher education. During the last decade, he was a managing director at Finn Partners leading the firm’s Pre-K through 12th grade communications practice and served as a member of the Prince George's County Board of Education (2013-15).
Previously, Dan worked in communications at the National Education Association and its Maryland affiliate, leading successful integrated campaigns around No Child Left Behind, school funding and teacher pensions, and at the American Federation of State, County and Municipal Employees, where he focused on federal and state policy around education, health care, tax reform, and Social Security. 
Dan’s experience in education began as a bilingual/ESL teacher in the Atlanta Public Schools. He has taught college- and graduate-level courses at American University, Georgetown University, George Washington University, and the Catholic University of Chile, where he served as a Fulbright scholar.
Dan earned his Ph.D. and M.A. in Political Science from the University of California, San Diego, and B.A. in Political Science from Swarthmore College.

February 25, 2026 | 12:30-1:30 PM PST
External Participants
Diane Tavenner
Co-Founder, Summit Public Schools
For 20 years, Diane was the co-founder and CEO of summit public schools, a nationally-recognized nonprofit school system that earned many accolades and distinctions, most notably America’s best high schools from US News and World Report, America’s most challenging high schools by the Washington Post and one of the world’s top 10 most innovative companies in education from Fast Company. Diane is the author of the best-selling book Prepared: What Kids Need for a Fulfilled Life and she co-hosts the podcast, Class Disrupted, with Michael Horn. She serves as the board chairperson for the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching and Learning, the Pahara Institute, and Minerva University. She is a member of the Aspen Global Leadership Network (AGLN) moderator corp. Diane holds a bachelor’s degree in psychology and sociology from the University of Southern California and has a master’s degree in administration and policy analysis from Stanford University.
Joel Rose
Co-Founder & CEO, New Classrooms
As a 5th-grade teacher, Joel Rose was committed to providing all of his students with an education they needed and deserved. Despite long hours and extensive training, he struggled with how best to organize daily instruction around the unique needs and strengths of each of his students. To address this challenge, Joel co-founded New Classrooms Innovation Partners as an independent, non-profit organization in order to bring innovative learning products and models to more students. Joel has published articles about personalized learning, innovation, and school models in The Atlantic, The 74, and Education Next. He has spoken at numerous convenings, including the Aspen Institute Ideas Festival; NBC’s Education Nation; and the annual conferences for the American Federation of Teachers, National School Boards Association, and National Association of Independent Schools. Joel holds a B.A. in political science from Tufts University and a J.D. from the University of Miami School of Law; and is also a Pahara-Aspen Education Fellow at the Aspen Institute.

President, Intentional Futures
Rich was the Education Practice lead before becoming President of Intentional Futures. Rich studied mathematics at Colgate University and has an MBA from Stanford University. He enjoys working with leaders to help them to realize their big ideas in pursuit of meaningful impact.

--- K-12 Strategy Meeting Follow Up Summary.docx ---
K-12 Strategy Review
Co-Chair Meeting Notes 
February 1, 2016
Attendees: Allan Golston, Bill Gates, Bill Tucker, Carina Wong, Chris Elias, Dan Greenstein, Don Shalvey, Gavin Payne, Irvin Scott, Jill Nishi, Jim Bromley, Larry Cohen, Leigh Morgan, Lynn Olson, Mary Alice Heuschel, Marie Gibson, Mark Suzman, Melinda Gates, Miguel Viega-Pestana, Nirosha Pour-Moezzi, Rae Ann Knopf, Sam White, Sara Allan, Sarah Buhayar, Steven Rice, Thomas Mitchell, Todd Pierce, Tom Stritikus
Follow-Ups
By June 2016:
The Achievement Gap: We will have completed an achievement gap analysis (what is the current knowledge base about what works) and will have a set of recommendations related to our strategy.
Principals: We will have clarified our strategy for engaging principals and other school leaders and provide additional details on that plan. 
Evaluation: We will provide for you a state-by-state assessment of the teacher feedback and evaluation environment, whether there are states or large districts tying their evaluations to personnel decisions (e.g. DCPS), or whether they more strongly connect them to professional growth (e.g. Kentucky), or both.
Assessments: (Target by the end of Q2) We will have more detail for you on our plan in regards to assessments – including our work with the consortia, as well as with ACT and SAT.
Curriculum: (Target by the end of Q2) We will have a plan for obtaining 80% market share for Common Core-aligned curriculum and by what year we can get there. As part of this, we will provide a state-by-state overview of what’s happening, a sense of what is happening in the critical districts in those states, and our analysis of what teachers are actually doing in their classrooms – irrespective of what states and districts are mandating and buying. 
Tools for Teachers: We will confirm our list of core tools for teachers, adding additional tools as needed (e.g. observation rubrics, video feedback, peer observation), and detail the current use, hurdles for further adoption, and 2019 goals for each. In addition, we will provide further detail as follows:
Student Surveys: We need to make a stronger, clearer case for using student surveys. We will provide you with a list of the places that are using student surveys effectively and seeing positive effects associated with them.
Observation Feedback Tools: We will assess the current quality of supply and barriers to demand for video based feedback and professional development tools and solutions, and develop specific goals and implementation strategies to increase use.
Professional Development: We will come back to you with a clear strategy for deepening our professional development work. 
Key Takeaways from Co-Chairs
We need to continue to work with the places that serve the most students. So while we should pursue work through networks of districts, we heard from you that we should also clarify how our strategy will also support and/or move the large, urban districts, where appropriate, particularly in cases where they are unable or unwilling to participate in networks.
You pushed us to think more about our interventions as possible “bottoms up” plays – direct to the consumer, instead of chiefly as “top down” – through the system (e.g. online class for leaders to improve their skills on observing and coaching on instructional practice).  
We need to engage in additional discussion with our advocacy and policy team about the framework we use (e.g. opt in vs. opt out).
Other Discussion Items
Understanding the capacities the BOCES and other education service providers have in order to advance the work with districts across the states and SREB will be critical to our success – as well as to clarify our advocacy strategy regarding the role/capacity of educational service providers.
We need to use quicker metrics and be really hard on ourselves. We know student achievement indicators lag. So it’s important to make shorter bets and put systems and surveys in place to measure progress and en
