--- Postsecondary Success Investment Narrative 03042021.docx ---
Postsecondary Success 2021 Investment Plan Narrative
FY21-24 Proposed Investment Plan Context
The Postsecondary Success team is proposing to make strategic changes to several existing bodies of work, create three new ones, and sunset one body of work, resulting in a net increase to our portfolio of $46.6M over 2021-24. The reallocations shift some of our portfolio away from Delivery, PAC, and Reserve to Solutions, Transformation, and Enabling Context. The most notable changes are described below.
Solutions. Our Solutions portfolio will experience the most change, resulting in a net increase of $68.6M over the four-year period (increasing from 15% of total funding to 27%).
Significant expansion to Develop Courseware Solutions, ($62.4M, $52M funded through the USP Strategic Reallocation). This includes a more comprehensive courseware R&D strategy to develop and scale high-quality courseware and ensure that high-quality courseware enables faculty (largely adjuncts) to shift their instructional practice and adopt evidence-based teaching practices (EBTs).
New body of work: Digital Learning Infrastructure, at $27.5M, surfaced by exploring strategic opportunities and funded by the USP Strategic Reallocation and foundation’s Racial Equity Initiative. The work will focus on catalyzing a low-cost, tech-enabled digital learning infrastructure to help HBCUs and other institutions primarily serving racially minoritized students.
Sunsetting Explore PS Financial Solutions ($9.6M) for several reasons: closure of this body of work will not have an impact on strategy coherence (as no part of the value chain is currently relying on actors in this area); limited bandwidth on the team to execute this work; and a strong hypothesis that policy is ultimately going to have the strongest impact on this (aside from fully comprehensive institutional business model changes, which are not a “solution-only” endeavor).
Transformation. Transformation portfolio is also being reshaped by two new bodies of work (National Scale Enterprises and Campus Climate Tools), support for credit mobility, and additional internal trade-offs.
New body of work: Shape Growth of National Scale Enterprises, at $22.5M, funded by the USP Strategic Reallocation. Over the past several years, we have been exploring what role the foundation should play in shaping the growth of the highest potential national scale enterprises to produce more equitable access and value for students of color and low-income students. This new body of work is an outgrowth of the Forecast Industry Structure and Determine Our Role body of work and is designed to steer existing (and potentially new) NSEs to serve populations and geographies that traditional institutions can’t or won’t and incubate and deploy critical R&D that closes equity gaps. Student outcomes from this BoW will also partially offset decreases in our strategy target (due to budget decreases in Delivery).
New body of work: Campus Climate Tools & Resources, at $7M, funded by reallocations within PS budget. This body of work was identified via existing transformation and DEI efforts and committed to as part of the foundation’s Racial Equity Initiative (as part of the reallocation of $50M existing resources).
Scaling down Development, Testing, and Practice Functions by $11.5M, to enable funding of Campus Climate Tools & Resources and other work.  While the new Campus Climate Tools & Resources BoW was one of our hypothesized ideas that would have emerged from the Dev, Test, Practice budget, this action accelerates our work on Campus Climate Tools by specifically setting aside resources for this topic. The strength of our evidence to be able to produce a lasting Dev, Test, Practice center is low, and the core strategy should not be impacted by this over the 3-4 year period. The reduction will leave $12.5M for our Transformation R&D activities which should be sufficient to produce and test several additional “blockbuster” public good tools/ resourc


--- 110920 Postsecondary EdTech Roundtable Memo.docx ---
November 9, 2020 EdTech Roundtable Discussion Prep Memo
Bill and Mark,
We are excited for you to have time on November 9th for a roundtable discussion on the topic of Education Technologies with three chief executives from USP partners:  Jeff Livingston from Ed Solutions (an EdTech market consulting firm), Shalinee Sharma from Zearn (a nonprofit K-12 curriculum publisher and software developer) and Kim Thanos from Lumen learning (a PS gateway courseware and faculty professional development provider).  Our objective for this session is to provide you with the opportunity to discuss product innovation and market trends for education technologies (specifically related to digital learning technologies) with leaders who span our strategies.
As you know, US education has been significantly disrupted with over 50% of K12 districts and over 65% of higher ed students learning primarily or fully remotely.  This is creating learning challenges as many students are not accessing education at all and learning gains are projected to continue to lag due to sub-optimal learning environments and modalities.  This will be further exacerbated by budget cuts at state and district levels, which will have profound impacts on educational resource sales cycles and purchasing patterns.  Major publishers, already laden with PE debt, are taking a significant hit as OER-related offerings are gaining steam.  From an opportunistic perspective, significant teacher/ faculty attention is now focused on educational technology.  Innovation is accelerating, including significant investment capital flowing into the space (with recent raises by Vistria and Quad Partners) and whole categories (like Learning Management Systems) morphing and seeing rapid growth by offerings meeting immediate needs (Google Classroom, etc).  Given the amount of change in the learning software space alone, this conversation will not plan to veer into other EdTech offerings (e.g. we will cover PS Advising Technology in a December conversation). 
We’ve selected the participants due to their collective broad view of the digital learning market and deep experience in issues critical to our respective digital learning strategies (e.g. breakthroughs on using courseware to help change faculty behavior, success in moving the needle for our focus populations of Black, Latino and low income students, experience with the intersection of OER and for-profit business models, etc).  While we expect a wide-ranging discussion where you will be able to pursue areas of interest to you, we offer the following potential “starter questions” related to the EdTech markets and product offerings.  The attached powerpoint also includes details about each of the participant’s respective offerings that may prompt questions from you as well.
Products
There seems to be a disconnect between the quality of digital learning offerings and what the market rewards.  How do you explain that?   Which elements of quality are starting to be rewarded and which aren’t?
How do you believe quality digital learning tools can actually help reshape how teachers/faculty spend their time (both the nature of their role and movement to evidence-based teaching practices)?  How is that playing out with your offerings and what opportunities do you see in the future?
What do you currently consider as state-of-the-art in terms of designing digital learning tools for more equitable outcomes?  Which aspects of that have you made progress on and where would you like to go further?
The Market
We understand many of the issues regarding educating our target population of Black, Latino and low-income students in this environment.  How do you see the digital learning market serving them?  What are you doing to serve them?
What has happened with Ed Tech adoption over the past 6 months?  Are there specific tools and technologies growing faster than others?  How is this impacting your offerings in particular?

--- 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Memo.docx ---
Postsecondary Value Commission Memo
The purpose of today’s meeting is to share the recommendations from the Postsecondary Value Commission, seek guidance on the next phase of proposed work, and discuss potential broader impact for a USP division-wide orientation toward equitable value creation and economic mobility.
Why We Launched the Value Commission
In an effort to steer the postsecondary education reform movement toward greater equity and to push institutions to play a stronger role in promoting economic mobility, the Postsecondary Success team began work in 2018 to extend its strategy goal from a focus on completion to focus on value. Why? As income and wealth inequalities continue to grow in this country, the rising cost of college coupled with systemic barriers to completion are putting the benefits of postsecondary education out of reach for too many students. We cannot create a skilled and credentialed workforce, thriving economy, and inclusive democracy without providing more students – especially students of color, low-income students, and women – with a postsecondary credential that leads to economic mobility and security and the competencies to advance a more just society. 
We know that a student born into poverty is five times more likely to achieve economic mobility by going to college than a student who does not enroll. In recent years, the foundation’s Economic Mobility and Opportunity team has brought a clear focus on mobility to the US Program, while the new Pathways Functional team is pushing the division to examine inequities in the education-to-career pipeline and the new USP Data Functional team is working to measure education-to-workforce outcomes at scale. In Postsecondary Success, we know that Black, Latinx, and low-income students still do not have equal opportunities to earn college credentials compared to their white and high-income peers. While half of white adults have an associate’s degree or higher, only 26 percent of Latinx, and 36 percent of Black individuals reach the same level of attainment. These attainment gaps are even greater across income groups. Further, we are learning from our value work that these disparities continue into the labor market such that our postsecondary system is not fully serving as an engine of economic mobility for students of color and low-income students, even when they do overcome obstacles to earning credentials. In our view, the emerging availability of workforce and education data linkages in the field requires applying an explicit equity lens to ensure earnings and employment data do not merely become new indicators in the rankings race to the top for elite schools, but rather help identify and address gaps in post-college outcomes. We see this as a critical juncture to steer the next 10 years of the higher education reform movement, analogous to how the Commission on the Future of Higher Education (“The Spellings Commission”) in 2006 heralded in the “completion movement.” As the field is seizing the moment to lean into racial and socioeconomic equity, we must use this opportunity to extend the higher education reform movement toward equitable completions of value that lead to economic mobility for our target populations. 
What We Are Learning from the Commission
The Postsecondary Value Commission, comprised of thirty racially, professionally, politically, and geographically diverse experts (see Appendix G for detail), has developed a conceptual definition of value, a framework to measure value, and an action agenda to amplify the definition and framework with institutional leaders, federal and state policymakers, and students and families. While our original intent was to define value solely based on students’ economic return on investment given the context of growing income inequality in this country, the commission urged us to expand our focus to broader inclusion of non-economic benefits to society, as well as attention to their intersection with econom


--- 121020 Postsecondary Advising Memo.docx ---
PS Advising Update Memo – December 2020
Executive Summary
Implementation of high-quality advising technology is a critical solution as it follows the student throughout their entire journey at an institution and introduces unique opportunities to leverage technology to improve the student experience, reduce equity gaps, and improve institutional efficiency. We are not currently succeeding in the number and pacing of institutions with quality adoptions to reach our goals.  Key barriers include 1) technology barriers that do not enable the integration needed to create efficiencies and implement advising practices; 2) market barriers with over 100 players that fragment the market offerings, fail to invest in R&D to resolve interoperability, and provide little incentive to SIS incumbents to integrate; 3) institution barriers related to poor communication, limited capacity, and siloed departments that perpetuate a disjointed and confusing student experience. This memo and corresponding presentation provide an update on the postsecondary advising space (including institutional practices, technology products and market dynamics), our existing PS advising solution development strategy, progress to date, and path forward. Over the last 10 years, we have invested ~$52M in advising across our delivery (demand-side) and development (supply-side) bodies of work, which resulted in establishing the core advising product categories and producing clear understanding about the institutional practices and technology requirements for successful solution implementation. 
Best-in-class advising uses integrated technologies in four product categories to catalyze the institution’s ability to implement student-centered practices. These technology-enabled practices produce a seamless student experience that supports students with identifying their educational and professional goals and with following an optimal path to achievement. The visual below articulates the vision for a healthy advising ecosystem, which involves interaction between institutional practices, core advising technology, and the broader market.  Each part of the system (institutional practices, technology products, and market) reflect different aspects of a solution investment strategy. To date, our approach has focused on establishing the solution category (via investments pairing diverse institutions and innovative vendors to drive more mature offerings to build the evidence base and accelerate momentum in the four product categories) and building awareness and adoption of the implementation process needed to scale integrated technologies and institutional practices at our target institutions. To reach our strategy-wide student impact goals of increasing graduation rates and closing equity gaps at our 200-300 focus institutions, we will continue investing in the development and dissemination of institution-facing tools, resources, and services that support and scale implementation of high-quality advising practices and technology.  At the same time, we believe that the lack of technology integration is a persistent root cause issue in the advising ecosystem that requires intervention in order to address underlying barriers that prevent institutions from fully leveraging the technology and data required to scale high-quality advising practices. We intend to use this memo, presentation, and supporting appendices, which includes exemplars, market details, etc., to engage in thought partnership with you regarding our next steps and proposed approach.
Context: Advising Practices and Technology 
Advisors at postsecondary institutions perform three critical functions that guide and support students: (1) provide decision support across academic and non-academic matters that directly relate to student success (e.g., degree planning, course registration); (2) identify students who need support and proactively engage with them (e.g., advisor notifications to intervene, AI-powered behavioral nu


--- Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Memo 01272020.docx ---
Executive Summary 
The Postsecondary Success strategy is one year into executing against our refreshed plan approved by the Co-chairs in November 2018. While we are still roughly six months from refining our strategy’s north star goal (pending output from the Commission on the Value of Postsecondary Education), we pushed ahead this year on program and policy elements that will be necessary regardless. You may recall that while significant portions of the previous strategy were retained, we flagged a number of capability gaps to address in execution, which resulted in nearly full turn-over in the Leadership Team as well as ~25% change in the grant-making staff. 
With the leadership team in place as of July and the full team currently 90% staffed, we have made significant progress over the course of 2019, including:
Launching the Value Commission, which has developed a draft framework for defining value. The value concept is influencing the field including funders, organizations (e.g. National Academy of Sciences) and even the Federal government (with their recent release of program-level earnings and debt data through the College Scorecard).
Funding capacity-building for all key partners in our value chain, including our 3 solution network intermediaries as well as 12 organizations embarking on a 2-year effort to become one of our 4-6 Intermediaries for Scale. 
Scaling evidence-based DevEd reforms and related policies across 20+ states (per our Dec 1:1 discussion with Bill).
Refining our definition of institutional transformation and beginning to scale key tools that we believe can facilitate transformation, such as the National Student Clearinghouse data platform (nearing 200+ institutions) and our Institutional Transformation Assessment (300+ institutions using at least one module). 
Facilitated initial attempts to connect the supply of solutions and transformation resources to institutions, giving our partners the opportunity to learn about the value chain approach prior to all parts being established.
However, even with this progress, we rate the overall strategy as slightly behind where we hoped to be at this point. Internally, this is partially attributed to the time required to begin operationalizing the refreshed strategy, including introducing a more interdependent operating model and introducing a new leadership team and staff. Externally, progress lagged in initial efforts to deliver high quality Technical Assistance for our solutions and transformation resources due to still-developing partner maturity and execution capabilities; and in our solution innovation function given that program officer owners for the three solution areas joined the team this year. Key staff are now onboard, and we are ending the year with positive momentum and a set of strategy-wide priorities that we are confident will have us fully on-track by Q2 2020.
In our strategy discussion, we will: introduce the leadership team, reground you in the strategy and its emerging goals, share progress and lessons learned in 2019, and discuss priorities and key decisions for 2020. Throughout, we hope to address questions about our progress as well as how we are conceiving strategy impact over time through both “direct impact” (via institutions supported and students impacted through our emerging value chain) and “indirect impact” (via creation of public goods that will support change well beyond our direct partner ecosystem). 
Strategy Recap 
Context: As discussed in the 2018 refresh, US income and wealth inequality is growing at alarming rates. The wealth of White families relative to Black and Latinx families has grown to 13x and 10x respectively with recent increases during the Great Recession. Economic opportunity in the US is extremely difficult to attain today without postsecondary education: about 20% of individuals born into the lowest income quintile who attend college reach the top income quintile compared to only 4% of those not attending coll


--- Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Memo 03042021.docx ---
Intro and Objectives 
The Postsecondary Success strategy exists to create equitable value and economic mobility by increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps specifically for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students from low-income backgrounds. We do that through two overarching goals:
Goal 1: Demonstrate “proof of scale” that approximately 300 institutions can use our integrated solution approach to transformation (that we discussed with Bill in June) to increase postsecondary attainment rates and close opportunity gaps (by race and income) in degree completion and post-college outcomes (such as economic mobility).  As a reminder, when we focus on systemic reforms that close opportunity and outcome gaps, we concurrently see outcome improvements for all students.
Goal 2: Develop public goods (including tools, knowledge, and infrastructure) that will support many more institutions in their transformation efforts. 
We were in regular dialogue with the co-chairs on two fronts throughout 2020.  The first set clarified the strategy (i.e. What are our policy priorities?  Why do we need to work on transformation and how does that incorporate our evidence-based solutions?  What are our specific target student outcomes?  Where are we operating in the advising landscape?  Where did we arrive with the Value Commission and where is that taking us in the future?).  
The second set of conversations (identified in last year’s Strategy Review “Priorities and Key Decisions” section) highlighted opportunities in the evolving landscape and made recommendations for doubling down on areas of foundation strength.  These conversations resulted in strategic shifts and reallocations: a refresh of our courseware strategy, an additional R&D body of work on Digital Learning Infrastructure with HBCUs, and a new body of work leveraging National Scale Enterprises (e.g. ASU, etc) as a source of growth and R&D in this period when many institutions may fail.
The net result of our conversations was ultimately a 10% increase in the multiyear PS budget envelope, facilitated by significant reallocations within PS and USP, as well as strengthened co-chair alignment that has us poised for an impactful 2021 via a renewed focus on innovation in a number of key areas.  The objectives of this year’s strategy review are to:
Align on the state of the field and illustrate how our initial COVID-19 response investments, and tradeoffs over the course of the year, address emerging risks and opportunities, while also highlighting implications for our long-term strategy goals and timing. 
Provide a progress update on strategy goals and bodies of work, with a particular focus on our efforts related to Digital Learning, National Scale Enterprises, Guided Pathways Transformation framework, Service Design & Delivery (SD&D), the Value Movement, and Policy & Advocacy. 
2020 Environmental Context and Our Responses    
Given COVID-19, universities and colleges scrambled to pivot to online teaching approaches and even as recently as December, nearly two-thirds of U.S. colleges remained fully or primarily online (see Presentation slide 5). Amidst ongoing economic crisis, along with a national reckoning with longstanding racial inequity, we saw Fall 2020 enrollments decline overall (by 3.6% below 2019 level), with community colleges suffering the biggest declines at 10.1%, and with troubling disparities by income, race, and urbanicity.  A 22% drop in students attending college directly after high school signals issues for years to come. Due to loss of revenue resulting from enrollment declines and falling auxiliary revenues, institutions confront significant risks to their future economic viability, eliminating programs, departments, and staff. This crisis is far from over: institutions in most states will face severe state funding cuts in the coming year. At the same time, Black, Latino, Indigenous and students from low-income backgrounds continue to be dispropo


--- 062222 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update Memo.docx ---
Postsecondary Success
Memo   Note:  Please refer to appendix slides alongside the memo for data, dashboards and cases -studies that illustrate key concepts
When we refreshed the strategy 3 years ago, we agreed to focus on ensuring higher education is a vehicle for equitable economic mobility, and to help the field evolve from a focus on completion to one of equitable completion and value.  We are currently catalyzing the “Equitable Value” movement and recently provided an update memo showing significant progress.  In the meantime, PS graduation rates (a chief determinant in Equitable Value) which had inched up over the years prior to COVID (but had not significantly narrowed opportunity gaps by race or income- See Appendix 1 for grad rate progress) have shown signs of COVID-related stalling (first-year persistence rates dropped two percentage points in Fall 2020, its lowest since 2012).  Our role is to show that we can increase the slope of the grad rate increases while ensuring equity in these increases See Appendix 2 for exemplar growth.  For instance, over the past 10 years, Sinclair Community College (BMGF Completion by Design and Frontier Set member) has increased graduation rates by 16 pp (with double digit increases for all racial categories reported) compared to a 7 ppt overall increase for the Community College sector. We will do that for a meaningfully large set of institutions (which we consider ~250, or 4.5%+ of the Title IV institutions representing 13%+ of higher ed students and over 18% of our focus student populations) and then create a blueprint for even greater scale (which requires policy intervention from a resourcing perspective).  This is hard work.   There aren’t a lot of models for successfully scaling student success, and we have actually funded many of the versions that contribute to our thinking.  The following memo re-grounds us in that “Proof of Scale” approach, sharing the tasks accomplished and highlighting key lessons learned that give us confidence in the strategy as we prepare to launch; as well as expectations for the portfolio in the coming years.
Recap of Institutional Transformation concept
There are no silver bullets to achieve this desired performance, but since the initial days of the PS Strategy, we’ve innovated on impactful solutions (like digital learning, advising, and DevEd reform) that help address student loss and momentum points.  In the exemplars in Appendix 2 for instance, advising redesign features heavily in the GSU story and DevEd reform was critical for early gains at Sinclair Community College.   We’ve supported the maturity of these solutions (and related public goods) to the point of having promising efficacy and are making progress with national availability, awareness, and adoption (recognizing that we are seeing varying degrees of fidelity and efficacy as we go to broader scale).  (See Appendix 3-5 for solution recaps).  Given their criticality as components in exemplar institutional change journeys we continue to see these as key elements of the strategy moving forward, comprising 24% of our 2022-25 budget (up to 30% if we include solution implementation).  Our investments in solution innovation (which is not the main topic of today’s discussion) are meant to set new performance bars for these key inputs into institutional change efforts.  However, we learned from our research in exemplar solution implementers, that their KPI gains related to individual solutions (e.g. increases in gatekeeper course completion for digital learning exemplars) were not enough on their own to provide the dramatic graduation rate gains we seek, and that something more comprehensive was needed to ensure quality solution implementation, and broader student impact. --- 092922 Postsecondary Advising Memo.docx ---
PS Advising Update Memo – September 2022
Executive Summary
Intensive holistic advising is a pivotal driver of postsecondary student success. Holistic student advising provides comprehensive and personalized support to students to help them realize their academic, career, and personal goals and is delivered in a culturally relevant way, taking into account institutional context and mission (e.g., HBCUs, Tribal Colleges & Universities) and student context (e.g., staff trained to provide support that is aligned with the student’s identities). This type of advising, achieved through multi-modal student communication and support, can transform the student experience. Over the last few years, its impact on student success has become more overt: success has been demonstrated through multiple randomized control trials and intention-to-treat studies conducted throughout the United States, for both 2-year colleges and 4-year universities, in humanities and STEM fields. Most importantly, holistic advising has been shown to improve achievement, persistence, and completion for historically underserved students. There continues to be strong efficacy data demonstrating the positive impact of proactive, personalized holistic advising and coaching on retention (first-to-second semester) and completion from randomized control trials of intensive advising programs for sub-populations of students. 
CUNY’s Accelerated Study in Associate Programs (ASAP) - which employs several principles of holistic advising – showed an 11% increase in semester 1 to 2 retention scores and 17% increase in 3-year graduation rates. It also embarked on an ambitious expansion that included a focus on students in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) majors, with the goal to enroll, retain and graduate more STEM students. The results show that ASAP STEM students statistically significantly outperformed comparison group students on retention rate (9.4% increase) and STEM graduation rate (24.1% increase) compared to non-ASAP students. A similar study in Ohio also presents longer-term effects of CUNY ASAP in New York, showing that the program’s effect on Associates degree receipt persisted through 8 years and likely represents a permanent increase in degree receipt. An RCT at North Carolina Community Colleges showed that success coaching increases students’ longer-term retention by 4%, with especially notable impacts for full-time students (6% increase), male students (9% increase), and Black students (18% increase). 
While there is clear evidence that discrete holistic advising interventions work, and work well for our focus student populations (Black, Latino, Indigenous students, and students from low-income backgrounds), adoption barriers—particularly the lack of integration and interoperability, high implementation costs, lack of institutional capacity, and low advising product NPS—continue to impede efficacy measurements for holistic advising “at scale” within institutions. The field is at a crossroads, where it may continue to implement such interventions in a piecemeal fashion for subpopulations of students; or it can scale these efforts in a sustainable, highly coordinated manner to all students. Our strategy is predicated on the belief that scaling high quality holistic advising is only made possible through integrated technologies, which will require an intervention in the technology market.  The challenges of student to advisor ratios, disconnected tech stacks, and a market that does not serve the historically under-resourced institutions in any meaningful way, have only been exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. We believe that the use of technology can provide a scale-up mechanism for holistic advising, but we must solve the integration and interoperability challenges. With higher education in general becoming more technology-dependent as a result of the COVID-19 pandemic against the backdrop of a stagnant and consolidating market, the need to


--- Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Memo 10062022.docx ---
Intro and Objectives 
The Postsecondary Success strategy exists to create equitable value and economic mobility by increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps specifically for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students from low-income backgrounds. We do that through two overarching goals:
1: Demonstrate “proof of scale” that ~250 institutions can use an integrated solution approach to transformation to increase postsecondary attainment rates and close opportunity gaps in degree completions
2: Develop public goods, including solutions, tools, knowledge, and infrastructure, that will support many more institutions in their transformation efforts. 
As a reminder, when we focus on products and practices that help close opportunity and outcome gaps, we concurrently see outcome improvements for all students, and are creating a model for policy-enabled scale across the sector.
We currently assess Goal 1 as meeting expectations and on track to achieve our objectives by 2027. This is driven by meeting major milestones of reinvestment in six intermediaries for scale and three intermediaries for solution delivery, and increased network use of transformation tools and resources. 
We assess Goal 2 as slightly below expectations, driven by products and services that do not yet meet our high quality and equity thresholds. 
The objectives of this year’s strategy review are to:
Update you on the state of the field and how our portfolio shifts are responding in this dynamic environment 
Provide a progress update on strategy goals and key bodies of work
Align on the milestones, decisions, and potential engagement opportunities to advance the strategy in 2023
Environmental Context and Our Responses 
Postsecondary undergraduate enrollment continues to decline (down 9.4% compared to 2019). This is most acutely the case for institutions that serve our focus student populations. Variable government stimulus adds to economic uncertainty, distracting some student success efforts, while focusing others on the link between student retention and revenue. The racial justice movement continues to impact higher education in numerous and inconsistent ways. On one hand, federal relief funding is focused more on Minority Serving Institutions than in the past, and philanthropy has invested heavily in MSIs (particularly HBCUs). On the other hand, we are still confronting centuries of historic underfunding and racist targeting at MSIs (e.g., disruptive bomb threats at HBCUs), and state politicization of education has resulted in conservative governors policing class syllabi and causing Presidential turnover by inserting themselves in Board issues. 
Tailwinds for portfolio adjustments in 2020 (courseware, HBCU Digital Infrastructure, National Scale Enterprises, and the Equitable Value movement) continue as evidenced by increased institutional faculty participation in courseware and digital infrastructure, significant continued enrollment growth for National Scale Enterprises, and positive press coverage and significant philanthropic co-funding for Equitable Value. Detail on these efforts is found later in the memo. More recent adjustments such as research on Admissions Testing also seems promising given that we are one of the only funders in this space that is striving for unbiased research. 
Looking ahead, we expect the sector to be in a state of continued crisis. While we are seeing initial signs of recovery (i.e., Spring FAFSA completion is up 4.6%, although not back to 2019 levels), stimulus funding (which prevented a wave of closures and mergers) will taper off at a time when Black, Latino, Indigenous and students from low-income backgrounds continue to be disproportionally affected by the pandemic’s toll. Without additional Federal support in sight, we expect the pace of structural change to pick up, although the shape of that change is less predictable. Cost- (and debt-) related issues, along with questions about value, will remain ce


--- 040323 Postsecondary National Scale Enterprise Learning Session Memo.docx ---
Memo (from January 2023) re: National Scale Enterprise (NSE) Portfolio   
The Postsecondary Success strategy exists to create equitable value and economic mobility by increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps specifically for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students from low-income backgrounds.  In addition to innovating on public goods and solutions (e.g. digital learning, technology-supported advising, etc.) we have two paths to supporting institutions in achieving greater outcomes.  The first involves investments in Intermediaries who are supporting 200+ institutions on their transformational journeys.  The second approach is focused on directly investing in a small number of National Scale Enterprises (NSEs) that are leveraging innovations in technological infrastructure and business models to serve an increasingly higher percentage of students, including many who are not served by more traditional institutional types.
Given recent meetings between foundation leadership and ASU (Bill with Chris Howard; Mark and Allan with Michael Crow); as well as references to National Scale Enterprises (NSEs) in our June and July meetings with Bill and Melinda respectively; and the PS and US Pathways Strategy Reviews, we wanted to provide a brief, dedicated update on this portfolio. 
Overview
As previously reported, our findings suggest 5–10 national scale institutions (termed ‘enterprises’ to differentiate their organizational scope and scale) could capture >10% market share of undergraduate students within the next 10 years, if not sooner. NSEs also have the potential to exert strong secondary effects on the industry (both positively and negatively) through increased competition for students currently accessing higher education, and infrastructure that enables their business models to extend access to students not currently engaged by higher education (e.g. adult learners, etc). Because these enterprises are likely to continue their rapid growth with or without BMGF investment (for example, SNHU enrollment grew by ~30% over the past two years, despite the pandemic), the intent of this portfolio of investments is to:  a) directly influence and support the NSEs to achieve equitable outcomes for Black, Latino, and Indigenous students and students from low-income backgrounds at national scale, and b) facilitate additional R&D work that can support other USP BoWs (e.g. ASU role in developing exemplar Chemistry courseware).  
Our NSE work to date has three distinct objectives:
Objective 1: Increase graduation rates for focus population (Black, Latino, Indigenous, low-income) learners while continuously increasing overall success rates. 
Objective 2: Serve segments of students that other institutions cannot or will not serve with a goal of narrowing differences in degree attainment between our focus students and the overall population; including identifying opportunities to partner with NSEs on research and development that could accelerate broader USP strategies.
Objective 3: Evaluate and seed models, potentially resulting in NSE diversification that includes institutions built to serve specific groups of students (e.g., HBCU, TCU, etc.) which have the mission, deep experience, and strong track record serving our focus populations (e.g., equitable impact orientation).
Our investment in the NSE portfolio is complementary to the institutional transformation approach to scaling student success via intermediaries working with hundreds of colleges (our “transformation at scale” strategy). While contributing to our overall performance targets, this work differs in the approach: we are working directly with these institutions, rather than through an intermediary. In addition, the NSEs have capacity to conduct their own R&D, the scale to pilot innovations internally, and are tightly partnering with K-12 partners and employers to develop vertically integrated pathways. 
Progress to Date
The first focused year of investment


--- 022526 Postsecondary Strategy Update Memo.docx ---
Summary of Progress & State of the Field
The goal of the Postsecondary Success (PS) Strategy is to drive change that leads to more Americans earning postsecondary credentials of value—credentials that recognize their skills and provide measurable economic returns. Overall, we rate the PS strategy as meets expectations. This is a reflection of the progress we’ve made against our two strategic goals:
Goal 1 is to generate proof of transformation at a large enough scale to influence sector improvement efforts. We do this by supporting (200+) intermediary-supported institutions; shaping five (and growing) National Scale Enterprises (NSEs); and accelerating innovative higher education models. Collectively, the intermediary supported institutions and NSEs we work with represent 13.4% of enrollment nationally, between 15.3% (Latino) and 27.3% (Indigenous) enrollment for focus student populations. Our investments aim to generate nearly 180K more students earning credentials between 2026 and 2029, between a 1.5 and 3pp year over year (yoy) increase in completion rates. By Q3 2026, we will also invest in a first wave of innovative institutional models whose growth we think warrants accelerating. 
Goal 2 is to develop, share, and scale tech-enabled solutions that result in high quality experiences & high value outcomes for students. We accomplish this through (a) our AI-enabled Courseware (CW) products, which aim to improve gateway course completion rates; and (b) our Digital Holistic Student Supports (DHSS) effort, which funds a Unified Data & Technology System (UDTS) and a System of Engagement (SoE) to provide both the infrastructure and products necessary to improve student advising. 
The Courseware BoW has two workstreams—the Real Chem CW and the Learnvia effort. Our Real Chem CW Beta version achieved over 7pp increase in course success rates, and exceeded Academic Year (AY) 2024-25 adoption targets by over 50% for both faculty (121 compared to target of 80) and students (11,150 compared to target of 7200) and aims to reach 200K students and over 10% market share by AY 2030-31, beyond the end of our investment in 2028. Learnvia, a collaboration with Carnegie Mellon University, was launched, and piloted a Calculus Minimum Viable Product (MVP), and has already shown promise toward our 2030 goal of scaling courseware to reach 450 institutions (355K students) and pass rates averaging about 80% (or at least 10% improvement) with equity gaps cut in half. Our DHSS work is on target to launch MVP products with pilot institutions starting Q1 2026, the SoE now meeting thresholds in 6 (of 9) Target Product Profile (TPP) areas and incorporating a newly built enterprise AI infrastructure that leverages existing LLMs for institution-specific needs with appropriate controls. Furthermore, UDTS development is on target for a Q1 2026 release of a stable MVP. By 2028, we expect to see CSAT scores of at least 65%, at least 5pp gains in credit accumulation, and 10% declines in equity gaps for students enrolled at institutions that have adopted DHSS tools. 
Context
Over the last 25 years, postsecondary education has made meaningful progress as an engine of social and economic mobility, but significant challenges remain before its promise is fully realized for all learners. Nationally, the six-year completion rate reached 61.1% in 2025, up 7.4pp from 2013 and has held steady for the last four years; college graduates now earn 75% more than high school graduates; and public confidence in higher education saw a modest increase in 2025. Unfortunately, gaps persist: completion rates for Black and Latino students are below those for all students by -17.1pp and -10.6pp, respectively; completion rates for students in the lowest income quintile are -26pp below those in the highest quintile; the noted wage premium has stagnated since 2010; an estimated 42M Americans carry $1.8T in student debt; and 40M – 43M have some college but no credential, explaining why, desp

--- 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Cover Note.docx ---
Dear Bill, Melinda, and Mark:
We look forward to speaking with you about the Postsecondary Value Commission on November 18, 2020. In this meeting, we will share findings and recommendations from the commission, discuss our plans to launch a “value movement” in the field as the next phase of the work, and seek your guidance on a few key decisions related to the commission’s release early next year. 
This meeting follows our discussion in November 2018 where we aligned on the need to launch the Value Commission to steer the postsecondary education reform movement toward greater equity and to push institutions to play a stronger role in promoting economic mobility. The rationale was as clear then as it is now: As income and wealth inequalities continue to grow in this country, the rising cost of college coupled with systemic barriers to completion are putting the benefits of postsecondary education out of reach for too many Black, Latino, and low-income students. Yet, our hypothesis was – and remains – that institutions are not incented to move the needle on economic mobility and are not moving fast enough as a result. At that meeting, you gave us your support to proceed. 
Since then, we have nearly concluded the work of the commission, which officially launched in April 2019, with a group of thirty racially, professionally, politically, and geographically diverse experts in postsecondary education. The commission has completed its charge to develop a conceptual definition of value, a framework to measure value, and an action agenda for institutional leaders, federal and state policymakers, and students and families to promote equitable value. 
In the meeting, we will present the findings and recommendations of the commission by exploring the five most critical questions to answer by measuring value, demonstrating how commission research addressed those questions, and presenting a draft dashboard that shows how an example institution performs against these critical questions which include:  
Question 1: Are students earning enough to not only ensure minimum return on their investment, but to also put them on a path toward economic mobility and security as measured by the value thresholds?
Question 2: Are students of color, low-income students, and women earning enough to equitably meet the value thresholds in relation to their peers?
Question 3: Are institutions providing equitable access to help more students of color, low-income students, and women experience postsecondary value?
Question 4: Are institutions improving on key metrics that affect whether their students experience equitable postsecondary value?
Question 5: How much are institutions contributing to society by providing equitable value to students of color, low-income students, and women?
To guide our discussion, we recommend focusing your review of our documents on the memo, presentation deck, and appendices A (Value Framework), B (BMGF Value-Related Policy Positions), C (Public Perceptions on Value) and D (Commission Release Risks/Mitigations). The other appendices are optional but encouraged reading. 
We propose the following agenda:
Introduction and implications of this work for the US Program (5 minutes): Mark Suzman and Allan Golston
Share and discuss commission findings and recommendations (20 minutes): Patrick Methvin, Jennifer Engle, Jamey Rorison
Discuss the Value Movement and implications for our programmatic, data, and PAC work (20 minutes): Patrick Methvin, Jennifer Engle, Rob Nabors, Sue Desmond-Hellmann
Solicit guidance on next steps while discussing potential risks and mitigations (10 minutes): Patrick Methvin
We look forward to discussing our work and engaging with your feedback. In particular, we want to draw your attention to the discussion questions on slide 20 in the presentation deck, which focus on critical areas for alignment with you for the work moving forward. These areas include discussion of (1) focusing our near-term value work on race

--- 062222 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update Cover Note.docx ---
Cover Note:
The Postsecondary Success strategy exists to create equitable value and economic mobility by increasing postsecondary attainment rates and closing opportunity gaps specifically for Black, Latino, Indigenous, and students from low-income backgrounds. We do that through two overarching goals:
Goal 1: Demonstrate “proof of scale” that ~250 institutions can use an integrated solution approach to transformation (which we will revisit today) to increase postsecondary attainment rates and close opportunity gaps (by race and income) in degree completion and post-college outcomes at these institutions, while creating a model for policy-enabled scaling across the sector
Goal 2: Develop public goods (including solutions, tools, knowledge, and infrastructure) that will support many more institutions in their transformation efforts. 
Over the past two years, USP re-evaluated our strategies and resource allocations in light of significant strategic risks (as well as opportunities) presented by economic shocks, racial reckoning and COVID-related changes in educational and business practices.  In higher education, specific areas of note include:
The question of higher education value is taking center stage given continued increases to cost of attendance and less certain labor market outcomes than in the past
Overall enrollment declines (8% decrease in 2021 enrollment compared to 2019, disproportionately down for our focus populations).  While this enrollment decline rattled the institutional systems and resulted in numerous program closures and layoffs, catastrophic scenarios (10% + institutional closures) were avoided via Federal emergency relief, thus delaying (or slowing) more dramatic structural change in the industry
A few subsectors notably increased enrollment, like our National Scale Institutions: SNHU up 33% in overall headcount in Fall 2021 from Fall 2020 and ASU up 12% among first-year on-campus students in Fall 2021 from Fall 2020%) which has drawn the sector’s attention to the necessity of stronger digital infrastructure.
The COVID-related push toward remote learning has accelerated interest in (and adoption of) digital learning tools.  Similar trends are seen in the advising space (driven partially by advisor preference to work from home)
Federal resources are shifting more to our focus populations and institutions that serve them.  Additionally, policies proposing a first ever national retention and completion fund were written into draft legislation.  While these have occurred many times in K-12, this signals a likely new phase of scaling opportunities for the higher ed reform movement.
In the PS strategy, the above themes combined to present opportunities we addressed through 4-yr resource reallocations from delivery, institutional transformation, policy advocacy and PS reserve portfolios, toward increases in technology and data-enabled areas (Courseware +$52M, HBCU Digital Learning Infrastructure +$27M, National Scale Enterprises- ASU, WGU, SNHU) +$23M as well as ongoing exploratory work in a next wave of tech-enabled advising investments.  We have update memos and/or meetings planned for those four topics across the coming 6 months.
Alongside those reallocations, we analyzed ongoing enrollment and institutional viability trends, and determined that our strategy Goal 1,  launched in 2019, focused on supporting institutional transformation (leveraging evidence-based solutions as part of a realignment of an institution’s structures, culture, and business model to create a student experience that results in dramatic and equitable increases in educational outcomes), is still the most logical path to achieving our goal of improving educational system performance and achieving greater educational equity.  Today’s conversation will focus on our approach to scaling transformation across ~250 institutions.  In parallel, we continue monitoring our National Scale Enterprise partners, and are gauging their ability to hit equ

The goal of the Postsecondary Success (PS) Strategy is to drive change that leads to more Americans earning postsecondary credentials of value—credentials that recognize their skills and provide measurable economic returns. We have been monitoring trends impacting higher education and shared some of those themes with you (aligned to a Political, Economic, Societal, and Technological -PEST- dimensions) prior to a discussion of them with external guests this summer. Following that meeting, we committed to sharing how we are adjusting our work to account for opportunities and risks presented by these themes. For instance, the convergence of AI advancements, student affordability concerns, and political changes in the accreditation process have opened new opportunities for innovation in Postsecondary institutional models that may have the chance to grow in a similar fashion as our National Scale Enterprises. And dramatic advances in AI data and technology, are leading us to invest more in Horizontal AI infrastructure (data sets, benchmarks, data connectors, etc.) to strengthen AI Models alongside our courseware and advising products that sit on top of them.
During our meeting on February 25, we plan to:
Provide a strategy progress update and assessment (an abbreviated strategy review)
Share how PEST trends are impacting our key BoWs and the implications for their future efforts
Tee up additional proposed deep-dives this year (e.g., courseware update/demo, advising update/demo, institutional models update with showcase/demo of potential new models)

The presentation slides include performance dashboards for the strategy and key BoWs as well as more detail on how BoWs will adapt to major trends. Postsecondary National Scale Enterprises Roundtable on July 1, 2020
Co-chair Meeting Notes
Attendees
Scott Pulsipher (Western Governors University), Michael Crow (Arizona State University), Paul LeBlanc (Southern New Hampshire University), Melinda Gates, Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, Patrick Methvin, Allan Golston, Art Seavey (notetaker)
Follow-Ups
By December 2020, Postsecondary Success will provide a memo proposing a strategy and investment plan for national scale enterprises, including the portfolio’s relationship to our current theory of change and action. Two dimensions under development include: 
Who – Our most promising candidates include the three at the roundtable, however, we are monitoring others including Grand Canyon University, Purdue University, University of Maryland Global College, University of Phoenix, and Penn State University, as well as new partnerships between Paul Quinn College and Minerva and between University of Massachusetts and Brandman University.
Strategic Focus – As discussed in the roundtable, there are several roles for foundation investment: closing gaps in equitable outcomes as the enterprises grow, accelerating growth, expanding access for new student populations, strengthening industry infrastructure for things like credit and transfer, incubating new models including competency based approaches, and, R&D testing and distribution. We started with strengthening their business model and capabilities to produce equitable outcomes and focusing their growth to provide access for underserved students. When COVID-19 hit, we quickly invested to test the other roles and see promise in servicing and/or white-labeling R&D for at-risk institutions, supporting students at closing institutions, and strengthening credit and transfer systems. Monitoring progress of these investments along with monitoring the severity and duration of disruption to institutions and students from COVID-19 will help us recommend a portfolio balancing these multiple foci.
Key Takeaways
Co-chair interest (and potential funder collaboration) in R&D testing and distribution; philanthropy as a key source of innovation capital
Different approaches to serving students – WGU as digital first and only, ASU viewing research and high-end faculty as integral, SNHU connecting in hyper local partnerships
Intentional movement toward workforce alignment, micro- and stackable credentials, and student-centric credit evaluation policies and transfer pathways
Significant complement to and/or operation in K12:  largest teachers college in WGU (now run by BMGF alum Mark Milliron), charter school partnerships with SNHU as source of growth, ASU Digital Prep Academy and their own charter school network)
New Faculty models:  ASU master teacher+ teaching team, WGU faculty mentors, etc
Students require suite of support services and affordability is still a prominent barrier
Significant growth during the COVID crisis (including record staff hiring numbers)

Limits to Scale. We hear you on the skepticism of potential to scale. However, we do think each enterprise based on growth trajectory could grow to enrollments of 300K to 400K. With a precedent in University of Phoenix at its peak, the notion is not too far-fetched. Each institution takes a different approach in their rigor of understanding historical keys to success and where and how they will grow. WGU are the most top-down and target based. SNHU are closer to WGU in intentionality but invest across a number of growth channels. ASU are the most free-form and reluctant to engage in planning, instead, placing bets among multiple often competing leaders. We do not take Michael Crow’s “infinitely scalable” refrains at face value.
Equity. While each enterprise has made significant strides, they still have a long way to go, and their growth in a manner that could exacerbate inequality is still a risk. As Scott Pulsipher said, WGU are doing much better than peers, but that’s based on avera

EdTech Roundtable 
Co-Chair Meeting Notes 
November 9, 2020 
Attendees: Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, Allan Golston, Andy Cook, Bob Hughes, Henry Hipps, Patrick Methvin, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Sam White, June Davis, Rahim Rajan

Deeper learning about how courseware can change faculty practice: We will include a few examples of this during the January Demo Day 
Creating more coherent curriculum offerings: Interest in learning about how we could support Zearn (or other curriculum providers) in integrating the summative tests which people are paying too much for

II. Key takeaways 
Curriculum market
Historically, it has been important to frame K12 edtech less as a species of tech company, but more as a government procurement company
However, barriers are starting to come down – partially due to Common Core and EdReports.  This is leading to increasing share for innovators like Zearn
In higher ed, affordability is driving uptake of OER-based digital learning tools (so Community Colleges first, then open access 4-years)
In K12 when teachers first saw digital learning tools, they previously asked “what is my role” and now they are starting to see how to integrate them into their practice…  
In PS, faculty previously argued against DL Tools based on academic freedom, but are not resisting as much anymore (both due to necessity and because centralized decision-making (by administrators) is increasing)
Improving practice
Adaptivity and student passivity- We need student engagement to build metacognitive skills so you can’t rely exclusively on algorithmic adaptivity
Digital Learning can change faculty practice:
With Zearn, the ideal implementation leverages changes in classroom practice and use of different centers
With Lumen, student progress and engagement data is used to nudge faculty and offers them draft notes to send to students
Digital learning tools need to move more toward interactivity---less sage on the stage—  While high production quality lecturers is fine, you really need to intersperse it with student tasks (e.g. sociology experiments, etc)

 Melinda asked the team to develop a short list of 1-3 priorities that she and Bill should raise with the new administration in 2021. As we finalize the commission action agenda, we will circulate these priorities early next year as part of the broader foundation advocacy agenda.
II. Key takeaways from co-chairs
Accessing and scaling program-level data: Desire to understand the extent to which we can measure equitable value with currently available data. Recognition of strengths and limitations of College Scorecard and University of Texas System data sets, and the importance of amplifying them through a public tool to show what is currently possible, and to fuel critical conversations in the field. Preview of plans to recruit colleges and universities to voluntarily collect, analyze, and report data on equitable value through the forthcoming Value Data Collaborative, as well as to equip our North America Team partners to advocate for better data with federal and state policymakers.
Explaining earnings gaps: Discussion of substantial post-college earnings gaps by race, income, and gender. Citation of partner research that highlights several key factors that contribute to these gaps, including disparities in postsecondary access, completion, and social capital; systemic stratification of programs of study; and racial and gender bias in employment and wages. Further discussion about the institutional role in students’ program of study selection and opportunities for data sets to capture the aforementioned factors.
Political and reputational risks: Assessment of the risks related to the foundation driving this work and the need to ensure alignment and support across the field prior to public release. Emphasis on the Value Movement that we are building, led by key partners who we have engaged in the work throughout and are clearly aligned with the Value Commission’s mission and orientation. Mention of philanthropic partners who are also supportive of the work and are likely to co-invest with the foundation in future work.
III. Other discussion items
Policy implications and opportunities: Discussion of possible short- and long-term policy action from the incoming administration, with emphasis on maintaining realistic expectations given the current Republican control of Senate and the administration’s long list of non-education priorities. Forecast of potential topics that the administration may take up (e.g., free college and student loan reform).
Continued commission and field engagement: Reflection on commission’s role as a conduit of the Value Movement, and the sustained energy of commission members to remain actively engaged in amplifying the work. Preview of comprehensive rollout strategy to ensure that the work achieves maximum impact while remaining grounded in the nation’s current economic and cultural context.

Key Takeaways
We agreed that advising is a critical solution area and provides the “plumbing” for the student experience.  We also are aligned that lack of technology integration is a persistent root cause issue in the advising ecosystem that requires intervention in order to address underlying barriers that prevent institutions from fully leveraging the technology and data required to scale high-quality advising practice.
We discussed several possible levers to drive interoperability in the advising ecosystem (middleware, product standards, technical integration services, data architecture standardization, interoperability standards).

We intended to add this component as an additional focus area of our demonstration project.
In exploring the potential role of CRM, we will do additional analysis but we may de-prioritize interoperability standards based on what we learn – especially because we’re not convinced that standards alone can really shift these entrenched market dynamics.
We agreed that COVID-19 presents unique opportunities to build on the current adoption and innovation momentum in the field towards leveraging digital advising tools and increases the need to push for greater focus on integration and real-time data flow.
We are aligned on pursuing a 12-18 month demonstration project and learning agenda within the currently scoped bounds of our budget for this BoW to inform us on which levers are highest-impact (effective and scalable) for our target institutions.  These include:
Interoperability Demonstration Project ($2.3M): We are aligned on funding a partnership with several high profile advising tech providers and 3-5 resource-constrained institutions as well as a national scale exemplar to develop and test open or shared infrastructure to enable enterprise systems to be connected to corresponding advising tech tools for real-time data exchange.  Based on your suggestion, we will investigate both the use of middleware connectors between SIS and advising tech.  As part of this project, we will investigate much more deeply the potential  role the CRM can play in our future state as the data system from which to build connections and integrations across the advising tech stack.  We have increased the proposed budget estimate of this demonstration project in order to enhance the types of models we will test.
Development of Advising Product Quality Standards ($1.6M):  We agreed to fund the initial creation of advising product standards and market steering tool to help institutions credibly assess products across dimensions such as interoperability, implementation support, and UI/UX

--- 092922 Postsecondary Advising Learning Session Meeting Notes.docx ---
Postsecondary Advising Update Meeting on September 29, 2022
Co-chair Meeting Notes
Meeting Participants: Bill Gates, Mark Suzman, Allan Golston, Patrick Methvin, Rahim Rajan, Mei-Yen Ireland, Lydia Atangcho, Josh Baron, Jen Engle, Amin Qazi, Rachel Scherer
Follow-Ups
Continue with proposed 2023 activities (in conjunction with a $7M incremental request), including
Answering critical questions to inform a large strategic play (e.g., vendor alignment around a common advising data schema, research on how these solutions improve quality advising and create greater efficiency and reduce costs, exploration of demand aggregation and building institutional excitement for this play, cost model for open-source community and implementation support)
Seeding no-regrets investments to scale and test extensibility of ASSIST
Schedule a Q3 meeting with Bill to propose the path forward (incorporating insights from the aforementioned work)
Update BOW decision criteria to map questions we’re seeking to answer and associated milestones
Connect with Bill’s office on engagement with MSFT Azure team and additional cloud providers (in conjunction with USP Data)
Key Takeaways
We are aligned that the market is not currently meeting the needs of our focus institutions and that our strategic goal for this body of work is to find a way to motivate them to do it. We agreed that the ed tech, and specifically advising tech, market has pernicious broken dynamics (e.g., PE ownership, M&A that stifles innovation, chronically low NPS, lack of affordable, high-quality products for our target segment), which steers us away from pursuing a strategy focused on an existing comprehensive solution. 
We are also aligned that integrated technology via a single pane of glass is a key requirement in order to scale some of the high touch interventions that demonstrate strong impact on student outcomes (e.g., CUNY ASAP). We agreed to do some cost modeling to understand the saving potential of integrated technology as a part of our next phase of activities.
We discussed the potential to scale an application and data layer cake approach in which institutions ingest critical student data from legacy source systems using cloud services.  Data is then abstracted for consumption in an open-source application layer where advisors can access a unified, comprehensive view of a student through a single dashboard and institutions can unlock cutting edge AI/ML analytics.
We agreed that there are a set of levers and tactics for scaling this layer cake approach that we need to investigate before returning in Q3 2023 for the BOW strategy decision. These include:
Shared advising data schema and vendor buy-in 
Sustainable and innovative open-source community
Cost effective managed services to support implementation
Aggregation of demand-side signals, particularly for networks of institutions that could pilot and act as lighthouses for this play
We discussed the interconnections that we will leverage with USP Data Team’s Longitudinal Interoperable Record and relationships with major vendors as we work with the field to determine a shared advising data schema and appetite for this engagement among vendors.
We agreed to update BoW decision criteria and options based on alignment from this discussion.
We appreciate your offer of support as we connect with Microsoft and other large cloud vendors and will coordinate with your office as those opportunities take shape in the coming months. We are also sending you a “follow up memo” with more details about our ideal “asks” of those companies.

Bill encouraged PS to deepen our shared R&D work with NSEs across these spaces.  Results scaling things built in NSEs has been mixed to date though, so we will explore this question in greater depth and come back with perspectives 
Vertical integration—R&D efforts include ASU’s role in US Pathways “AA by 13” and credit mobility efforts, where NSEs are less mired in some of the “politics” than other institutions. NSEs can impact dual and concurrent enrollment and widen the pipeline for K12 students to see a pathway to college. They are experimenting in a diverse set of states (from a policy and context perspective)— Arizona, Texas, Washington—and will deepen relationships in these while spreading to more. SNHU is developing a student-centric data model in partnership with Purdue Global and Google that will allow a 360 degree, longitudinal view of a student through their student journey including with upstream (k12) and downstream (employer) partners.
Marketing strategies in this space differ from traditional institutions trying to convince already college-going students to choose their institution, versus trying to convince someone to choose to enroll in (or back in) college in the first place. Marketing budgets are necessarily larger for NSEs because of this. We estimate ASU (public) spends 5-7% of their budget on marketing whereas SNHU and WGU spend closer to 15%.  For comparison, for-profit universities spend between 25-30%.
Each of the NSEs deploy different curricular structures with distinct faculties driving the work, from competency-based courses developed and mentored by full-time faculty, but delivered by part-time coaches (WGU’s model) to seat-time based courses delivered by tenured faculty (ASU’s model). 
NSEs increase graduates by both including more students than just the traditional first-time college goers and through deep use of analytics (including natural language processing), developing better understanding of friction/hero points, and graduating higher percentages of the students attending. Growth has been high, with multipliers for our focus students. They’ve set targets and largely exceeded them. 
Some questions about how COVID has impacted preference for learning online. Growth seems to be consistent with pre-COVID trends, but we shall pay attention to this.

--- 040323 Postsecondary National Scale Enterprise Appendix A.docx ---
Appendix A:  Addendum to January 2023 Memo re: National Scale Enterprise (NSE) Portfolio  
This addendum adds new information to the memo you received on the NSE portfolio in January 2023. Specifically, this addendum includes:
Updated enrollment and early progress toward institutional targets of degrees awarded to Black, Latino, Indigenous, and low-income students
Projected impact over time in terms of degrees awarded as a proportion of all bachelor’s degrees awarded in the U.S.
Update on refinement of our definition of NSE and upcoming refreshed landscape scans, including the potential impact of adding institutions (e.g., we are providing planning grants to the University of Maryland Global Campus and Purdue Global)
Examples of the unique role that NSEs play in vertical integration from K12 to workforce
Discussion of questions to ask the NSE presidents during your dinner with them at ASU-GSV
Current Performance: Enrollment, Progress toward Targets, Financial Health
Enrollment
While undergraduate enrollment continued to shrink in 2022 (although at a 0.6% decline, it has stabilized compared to the 6% decline in the previous year), the NSEs continued their growth trajectory; with even higher increases for our focus student populations.  In 2022: 
ASU’s undergraduate enrollment grew by 4.4% to 112,171 with Latino enrollment growing at 5.6% (1479).  
SNHU’s overall undergraduate enrollment grew by 14.1% to 153,906 with Black (15.1%, 23,156), Latino (19.4%, 18,222) and low-income (16.2%, 59,506) outpacing overall growth.  
WGU’s undergraduate enrollment grew by 6.6% to 95,198 with disproportionate growth for Black (12.7%), Latino (14.0%), and low-income (24.7%) students.    
Changes in enrollment for 2022 and to date in 2023 give us confidence that the institutions continue to grow, and that the growth is driven heavily by our focus populations, in alignment with our goals.
Progress toward Degree Targets
In aggregate, the NSEs made positive progress toward the targets our investments helped them establish by slightly exceeding targets for degrees awarded goals for all focus groups in 2022.  The “as is” figures in the table below reflect a projected trend based on historical growth, and “target” figures were developed by the NSEs via our investments.  While it is too early to attribute graduation rate increasing to our initial investments, their 2022 progress is a positive sign en-route to seeing the impact of BMGF-funded institutional changes on graduation rates in 2024.  
Financial Health
Critical to seeing continued progress on enrollment and completion is the financial health of these institutions. All three NSEs are well capitalized and, alongside growing enrollment, have seen their net assets grow significantly in the last 10 years (ASU by ~$360M, SNHU by ~$910M, and WGU by ~$280M; see figure below). Net assets at the three NSEs increased by ~113% over the last 10 years, while total net assets at all Title IV institutions in the U.S. increased by 63% in that same period. While our investments in NSEs can help shape their agendas and prioritization, it is ultimately their capacity and capital that execute on the work. This strong financial position presents a potential significant source of field-led innovation capital.
Note: Net assets is either the total net assets (for institutions using FASB accounting standards) or net position (for public institutions using GASB accounting standards) as reported to IPEDS. Arizona State University reports its financials as one entity (for both Campus and Digital Immersion). For IPEDS reporting, ASU allocates 14.35% of its finances to Digital Immersion and the remainder to Campus Immersion.
Source: U.S. Department of Education, National Center for Education Statistics, Integrated Postsecondary Education Data System (IPEDS) Finance Survey Component

--- 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix B.docx ---
Introduction
In April of this year, the North America Team shared how we were adjusting our federal postsecondary priorities in response to the COVID-19 pandemic and offered specific points of view on numerous federal policies. We remain aligned with the Postsecondary Success team around prioritizing “money and measures” corresponding to the finance and data elements of our four policy pillars.  Several of our postsecondary policy priorities fall under the pathways pillar as well. As we noted at the time, we eagerly awaited the research output from the Postsecondary Value Commission and the opportunity to reevaluate our policy priorities through the lens of equitable student value.  NAT and Postsecondary Success decided to let the commission produce an action agenda separate from the foundation’s brand that would reflect the broader commission’s priorities, only some of which BMGF would take up. We expected much of our federal policy work would align with the concept of equitable value, and that there would be more opportunity to further develop our state policy agenda in line with the commission’s findings. 
With the Value Commission’s action agenda in front of us (see Appendix E), we have taken the opportunity to review and analyze the implications for our postsecondary policy priorities at the state and federal level. We would put the action items in three broad categories: 
Consistent with our current point of view and priorities: These are policies where we generally agree with the Value Commission, have points of view, and support partners who work on the issue. 
New policies or priorities: This could refer to a policy that we are initially open to and are interested in making a formal part of our priorities or could also refer to an opportunity to explore further based on the Value Commission’s analysis. 
Area of disagreement or not a priority: This is a case where we will either flag disagreement with the Value Commission, or a policy that we agree with but do not see as a likely priority for future research and grantmaking. 
The action agenda provides five thematic areas (access, affordability, completion, data, justice) each with a separate federal and state policy recommendation (in addition to institutional policy and practice recommendations) and with a specific focus on Black, Latinx, Indigenous, Asian American Pacific Islander (AAPI), low-income students and women. We will review all ten state and federal policy recommendations below, categorize them among the three areas above, provide our point of view, and recommend next steps. While this may appear to be a sharp inflection point, the process more closely resembles an ongoing evolution of our postsecondary policy priorities. We have closely followed and supported the Value Commission through message testing, survey research, and soliciting partner feedback, and have been thinking about the potential implications. Moreover, USP and NAT have spent the last several months on a joint prioritization process to identify policies across our four pillar areas most likely to advance shared objectives. The process has given us a formal venue to test our thinking and assumptions with the PS team about the Value Commission’s work.  As you will see below, our prioritization conversations heavily influence our recommended future priorities and next steps.
THEME #1:  Equalize Access to Increase Value
While college-going has risen across the board, college access remains unequal for Black and Latinx students, and students from low-income backgrounds. In 2016, 83 percent of recent high school graduates from high-income backgrounds enrolled in either two- or four-year colleges within a year of graduating, compared to just 67 percent of recent high school graduates from low-income backgrounds. Similar patterns persist by race. Further, our postsecondary system remains highly stratified, with too many institutions still providing limited access to the students for whom a college
