--- 111820 Postsecondary Value Commission Appendix C.docx ---
The Value Landscape: Public Perception, Conversation, and Implications for the Foundation
Overview
Advancing postsecondary value in policy and practice requires an understanding of whether and how the issue and the Postsecondary Value Commission’s work registers among key stakeholder audiences and in public conversation.
Over the past two years, we have conducted multi-modal opinion research of target audiences (i.e., institutional leaders, policymakers and other education influencers, students and families, engaged voters) regarding their perceptions of postsecondary value and the work of the commission. Additionally, we have analyzed (and continue to track) public conversation about value and the commission in traditional and digital/social media efforts in the field. This work yielded several topline insights; see below for more detailed discussion of the recommendations and implications.
Value/return on investment is emerging as an issue, but cost and debt are still “top of mind” for target audiences and drive the conversation. That focus is reflected in media coverage, though there are data suggesting it is possible to shift the conversation toward value with sustained effort, particularly among policymakers.
Recommendation: Start with investment and pivot immediately to return on that investment.
Overall, education after high school is seen as valuable. A significant portion of audiences agree that education after high school has become more valuable over the past decade. At the same time, COVID-19’s impact on colleges and universities and the economy are starting to figure into the value conversation and will require monitoring, particularly among younger adults.
Recommendation: Craft messages that acknowledge the current context.
Value is a “both/and” among target audiences, which means that individual economic benefits resonate, but so do non-economic personal benefits and societal benefits, and neither paints the full picture alone.
Recommendation: Emphasize that a better living leads to a better life in messaging, using data.
The need to focus on equity as part of the topic of value generates a divided response. Audiences are split on the need to emphasize racial disparities as part of the value conversation.
Recommendation: Despite mixed public perspectives on this topic, be assertive about equity. It is who we are: “All lives have equal value.” And lean on partners to field pushback and underscore how this issue connects to generational wealth gaps and other inequities.
The commission is generally seen as a good idea, but there is wariness about what it can and will accomplish, specifically, prompting action by institutional leaders and policymakers that will benefit students. Additionally, the commission faces critiques from conservative and progressive corners about its motives. Conservatives voice concerns about the focus on equity and attempting to “control” education; progressives voice concern about authenticity on the topic of equitable value.
Recommendation: Focus on action. We have made the action agenda concrete, specific, and evidence-based.
Including the Gates Foundation name/brand in postsecondary value messaging can help build support among education influencers but can be a drawback for Independents and Republicans.
Recommendation: Target foundation messages about the commission’s work to supportive audiences to build momentum and equip commission supporters to validate its findings.
Audience Perceptions
Working with the Glover Park Group and BlueLabs, we have conducted three phases of quantitative and qualitative research on target audience perceptions and attitudes about postsecondary value and the commission: 1) prior to the announcement of the commission (Spring 2019); 2) immediately following the announcement of the commission (Summer 2019), and 3) pre-release of the commission’s findings (Spring/Summer 2020). Several themes have emerged from this work, and the findings cited below are from the

--- 062222 Postsecondary Success Strategy Update Appendix Summary.docx ---
Summary for Appendix 
Our appendix document for this meeting is mean to be used as a “read-along” with the memo.  The memo references appendix pages (in yellow highlights) throughout the document where we have the opportunity to strengthen the narrative with data, examples or case studies.  The presentation materials that we will use during our discussion are essentially an excerpt of the appendix slides, but we would be happy to return to appendix slides during the conversation as needed.  General areas covered by the appendix include:
Sector-wide and exemplar grad rate progress by institution type and by student demographic
Recap of our three key solutions with implementation exemplars as well as BoW performance (across availability, awareness, adoption and efficacy)
Definition of institutional transformation along with examples (and student impacts) from various institutional types
What we view as the minimum elements of transformation, how we measure time and cost of transformation and examples of BMGF-funded public goods that can strengthen and accelerate the transformation process at scale
Overview of the conditions for scaling transformation that we are putting into place along with a case study of a previous investment (AACC Guided Pathways) that illustrates many of these conditions and gives us confidence in our approach going forward
Our approach to intermediary capacity building and profiles of the six intermediaries we have selected for our next stage of investments
Overview of our Solution Network Intermediaries, how they support quality solution implementation, and how we track this supply-chain of quality solution supports in our ecosystem
How our current policy priorities support the proof of scale strategy as well as approaches that we can pursue in the future to leverage policy-related resources to enable scale at 10X the level of our current approach 
Our Proof of Scale measurement frame, including key performance indicators for student outcomes, operational milestones and continuous learning
High-level view of what we commit to sharing in future board meetings (relating to strategy progress and learning)  over the next 5 years
Comparison between PS Intermediaries for Scale and K-12 Networks for School Improvement

--- Postsecondary Sucess Investment Plan Narrative 01272020.docx ---
Postsecondary Success 2020 Investment Plan Narrative
FY20-23 Proposed Investment Plan Context
The Postsecondary Success team is proposing several minor reallocations across our portfolios and bodies of work, mostly focused on timing shifts as we continue to refine our strategy. While we do not have incremental requests, we are requesting a pull forward of $3.8M from 2021 to 2020. The following table shows the timing shifts:
Delivery: reducing 2020 target by $1.6M as we pulled funding forward to 2019 to fund 12 newly selected Intermediaries for Scale for their 2-yr capacity building investments. This was balanced by modifying “Network scaling partners” body of work to include operational aspects of establishing our PS Value Chain (connecting supply of evidenced based solutions and transformational resources with demand from our network of institutions) and reallocating $1.5M per year for 2020 and 2021 to resource this change. We anticipate higher needs in the “Develop Intermediaries for Scale” portfolio in 2023, as intermediaries move from capacity building stage to working with institutions to transform. Overall, there is a $4.5M increase to this portfolio over 2020-23.
Enabling Context: increasing the 2020 target by $1.2M to fund continued work of the Commission on the Value of Postsecondary Education. Overall, there is a negligible change in funding to the Enabling Context Portfolio over 2020-23.
Global Policy & Advocacy: there are no shifts within this portfolio.
Institutional Transformation: reducing 2020 target by $3.9M as we front-loaded Frontier Set renewal payments in 2019 to better align with the academic year and more effective sub-granting to the institutions. In addition, we combined “Lab for Acceleration Toolkit for Development & Testing” and “R&D Transformation Tools and Supports” into a single “Development, Testing, and Practice Functions for Transformation” body of work. This reconfiguration resulted in a net decrease of about $5M over 2020-23 as we set out to focus on a development-test-practice cycle that could yield more public goods faster and help our intermediaries for scale understand how to use them.  This, in addition to smaller shifts in other bodies of work, results in a net decrease of $5.4M over 2020-23 within the overall Transformation Portfolio. 
Other Bodies of Work: this portfolio includes business support bodies of work, such as Business Operations, Reserve, and a new body of work – “Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion (DE&I) in Practice.”  We are increasing the 2020 target by $3.3M to fund the new DE&I body of work, slightly increase the Reserve, and account for costs related to PS-sponsored convenings that had been funded previously from individual bodies of work (to enable more transparency and cleaner tracking). Overall, the funding shifts result in an increase in funding to this Portfolio of $1.9M over 2020-23.
Solutions: increasing the 2020 target by $4.8M to accommodate delayed payments to Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education (“Deliver Digital Learning Solution” body of work) and provide additional funding for our “Develop Courseware Solution” and “Deliver DevEd Solution” bodies of work. Over 2020-2023, we are proposing a slight decrease ($1.2M) in the Solutions Portfolio as the strategy evolves to focus on Delivery at scale (and solution delivery dollars are increasingly captured in the Delivery portfolio).

--- Postsecondary Success Strategy Review Investment Plan Narrative 10062022.docx ---
Postsecondary Success 2022 Investment Plan Narrative
FY23-26 Proposed Investment Plan Context
The Postsecondary Success team is requesting a $7M additional funding for Develop Advising body work and potentially proposing to roll over ~$2.5M from 2022, resulting in a proposed net increase to our portfolio of $9.6M over 2023-26. 
Incremental request. Incremental budget request of $7M ($5M in 2023 and $2M in 2024) to pursue critical strategy development activities to scope a big bet play in 2024—will return with full strategy proposal in Q3 2023 (anticipate incremental ask of $25-$49M). The next phase of strategy development activities include (1) conducting market research and feasibility testing of the holistic student profile data layer play, including scalable human capital options (2) convening market players in order to facilitate cohesion around a holistic data layer play; (3) supporting the continued scaling and learning documentation of existing tech models (e.g., scaling Maricopa’s ASSIST platform adoption to all 10 institutions); (4) providing seed funds for data layer and implementation support collaborations and testing (e.g., test extensibility of ASSIST; HBCU-driven open-source community); (5) developing and testing an early MVP market steering decision support tool; (6) packaging insights and strategic plan, including performance thresholds, for the larger incremental ask in Q3 2023.
Potential rollover. There are a number of timing changes to PS investments, with some bodies of work spending more than budget in 2022 (e.g. larger than anticipated initial payments to Intermediaries for Scale and Solution Network reinvestments), and others spending less (pushing some investments into 2023). This may result in potential rollover request of ~$2.5M.

--- Pathways Investment Narrative 10062022.docx ---
Pathways Investment Plan Narrative
Current Budget Outlook: Our current approved strategy envelope of $132M runs from 2021-2024 and is allocated to eight bodies of work (excluding GPA) as follows: 
2021-2024 Pathways Budget
*Includes envelope of managed funds from the Postsecondary Success team which will cause this table to NOT tie out to the system generated Investment Plan report.
Strategic Shifts: In 2022, we shifted the Credit Mobility Platforms BoW to the Solutions group, due to the technology and innovation focus of the work and moved Pathways State Exemplar from the Exemplars group to Delivery, given its focus on delivery system strengthening. We are also proposing a Cross-Cutting Strategy Drivers and MLE Body of Work (see below). There were no shifts in allocation across bodies of work or portfolios.
Incremental Requests: Our revised baseline budget for 2023-2024 includes proposed incremental requests totaling $8.5M, with $4.0M in 2023 and $4.5M in 2024. This request includes the following:
$5M to supplement Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways Body of Work. The incremental $5M will enable the team to invest in pilot implementation and evaluation of 5-8 promising regional Accelerate[ED] models and support expansion of dual enrollment in Texas and California, where recent legislative wins are priming states for uptake of best practices and wide adoption. The existing allocation to this BoW will fund investment in additional national Accelerate[ED] models, a community of practice and technical assistance. 
$3.5M to ready the enabling conditions for scaled adoption in the next strategy period. This will fund a time limited Cross-cutting Strategy Drivers and MLE BoW that will cover additional MLE data collection, state and federal funding flow and unit cost analyses, coalition building and some targeted capacity building for key anchor partners that will play a role in the next phase. Using the Pathways System Framework as an anchor, we will assess the current state of Pathways learner supports and programs of study, coordinated delivery system structures, and enabling conditions and infrastructure in select states. These additional investments will inform decisions about place-based work and further hone the strategy’s theory of action in the next phase. 
Future Strategy Period: The current baseline budget for the next four-year strategy period (2025-28) appears lower than the current period (2021-2024) because the team benefitted from USP Strategic Opportunity funding that taper down over time. As we touched on briefly in the strategy review materials, we anticipate the current phase of the strategy will set up the potential to seek broader reach impact in the next stage, by shifting from development and validation of proof points and exemplars to a greater focus on scaling levers and student impact. 
We have set key decision points for our programmatic bodies of work (excluding Business Support) in Q4 2024. At that time, we will refresh the strategy. Our preliminary thinking is a recommendation to increase our scope, impact, and associated resources post 2024 to focus on three primary levers: expanding the reach and impact of existing solutions from innovation/early adoption stage to more widespread adoption (Advising models, Dual Enrollment, High School Math Pathways and Credit Mobility Platforms), creating a stronger and larger group of regional exemplars of integrated delivery of pathways solutions and structures via implementation of Accelerate[ED] models in states with supportive policy and funding environments, and increasing focus on state and federal policy and funding as critical enablers to scale more broadly. 
Over the next 12 months we will engage in preliminary strategy refreshment and costing of the next phase of work and will return in 2024 with a proposal for the next phase. We will preview our evolving thinking next fall during annual planning.


--- 090320 USP Pathways Roundtable Memo.docx ---
Maintaining Student Momentum in the High School to College Transition
Our labor market continues to reinforce that a postsecondary credential is a prerequisite for economic self-sufficiency. However, despite significant gains over the past decades, there are still large postsecondary enrollment differences by race and income. Further, enrollment gains are not matched by gains in postsecondary completion. According to a 2018 study by the Georgetown University Center on Education and the Workforce (CEW), even with elevated college-going rates, Black and Latinx students are only about half as likely as White peers to attain a bachelor’s degree. Over the past 35 years, as Black and Latinx college-going rates have climbed, the deficit in bachelor’s degree attainment between White and Black and Latinx students has increased from 15 to 21 percentage points. This diminishes the ability of young people to launch upwardly-mobile careers – and since 2008, we have seen the age of economic self-sufficiency, or the age by which a person earns at least the average wage of all workers, rise from the mid-twenties to the early thirties. 
A reason for the widening deficit: while White students inordinately attend selective four-year public colleges to pursue bachelor’s degrees, Black and Latinx students disproportionately attend open-access public colleges, most of them community colleges. Which postsecondary path students choose matters because of the staggering differences in resources and graduation rates. Regardless of test scores, a Black or Latinx student has a much greater chance of graduating from a selective college than an open-access one. For example, a Black or Latinx student who scores above average on the SAT (1000 or above) and enrolls at a selective college has an 81 percent chance of graduating but has only about a 46 percent chance of graduating at an open-access college.  
In fact, many low-income and students of color undermatch into less selective programs with lower completion rates than they are qualified to attend. Low-scoring, more-advantaged students are more likely to enroll in a bachelor’s program than high-scoring, less-advantaged students, with 41 percent of students – mostly from low-income families – undermatched. Only 23 percent of students in the bottom income quintile enroll in selective four-year BA programs, compared to 69 percent of students in the top quintile, despite highly selective colleges typically being more affordable for low-income students than non-selective public institutions. Finally, postsecondary credentials are not created equal: along with institution, choice of program is highly predictive of future income. The most lucrative majors lead to lifetime earnings more than $3 million higher than the least lucrative majors, yet information about careers and potential earnings is not typically made available to young people as they explore postsecondary options.
Students navigating a path to postsecondary face many barriers to enrollment and completion. Critical inputs on this pathway that are often missing for Black, Latinx, and low-income youth include access to information, support to complete complex process steps including financial aid applications, and academic and experiential preparation, including the social-emotional supports needed to see themselves as college goers and develop self-advocacy skills needed to become inoculated against often unwelcoming environments for first-generation students of color. 
The pandemic is exacerbating these navigational challenges, disrupting access for students to instructional and critical support resources like college advising. OneGoal found in some of the schools they serve, teachers lost all contact with close to 30% of students this past spring. We expect “summer melt” in postsecondary enrollment rates by recent high school graduates – typically 10-20% of accepted students do not enroll in the fall – to be significantly higher this year and stude


--- 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Memo.docx ---
Pathways Functional Strategy Proposal Memo
Executive Summary 
We know from research that Black and Latino students, and students experiencing poverty aspire to a thriving adult life encompassing financial stability, personal autonomy, and meaningful contribution to society. A postsecondary credential is critical to this aspiration, yet many lose momentum across key transitions between high school, college, and employment. Few high school and postsecondary systems have the structures or supports to help students make these transitions successfully. The consequences of these navigational challenges are clear: nearly 40% of Black and Latino high school graduates do not immediately transition to a postsecondary program, along with 30% of their White peers. Undermatching is a major issue, with 41% of enrollees, mostly from low-income families, attending less selective colleges than they are qualified for and could afford. This translates into low postsecondary attainment: only 38% and 46% of Black and Latino students earn a postsecondary degree or certification in six years. Even those who do attain credentials suffer from lagging labor market outcomes, with post-BA Whites earning a median of $70,000 a year compared to $53,000 and $57,000 among Black and Latino workers, respectively. The COVID-19 pandemic exacerbates challenges in the high school-to-postsecondary continuum, disrupting access to advising and learning opportunities. Among households with postsecondary plans this past fall, over 40% with incomes under $50,000 reported a household member canceled those plans, compared to 19% with incomes over $200,000. First-year postsecondary enrollment dropped by 13% overall and 19% at two-year institutions, with FAFSA completion rates for the class of 2021 down by 16% to date (Presentation Deck Slide 4).
To address these transition points more effectively, our proposed Pathways strategy brings together ongoing approved work in the K-12, Postsecondary, and Data strategies. It builds upon USP’s work on multiple pathways elements (e.g., early college high schools, postsecondary advising, community college guided pathways, see Appendix D Slide 1), evolving to refine our approach, expand promising areas, and integrate the work going forward. Our proposed focus is to create quality pathways by (1) providing personalized advising supports to Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty to equip them with the knowledge, skills, and relationships needed to navigate today’s disconnected systems across high school to postsecondary to workforce and (2) driving innovation to redesign grades 11-14 by shifting program structures (e.g., coherent career-aligned course of study spanning high school to postsecondary including early postsecondary learning in high school), delivery models (e.g., credit mobility platforms and high school/postsecondary partnerships), and incentives (policy, funding) toward integrated, easy-to-navigate programs of study that deliver better student outcomes. 
The proposed budget of $132.5M over four years (inclusive of previously approved funding and USP strategic opportunities funding; see Appendix B) will support uptake of effective advising solutions, establish proof points for redesign of programmatic structures and delivery models, and provide ongoing support for enabling policies, data, and narratives to drive greater integration across K-12 and postsecondary systems. Through a stronger focus in the COVID context on providing navigational supports to students now, while laying groundwork for systemic change, we can help mitigate the risk of the current generation of students struggling to get on postsecondary and upwardly mobile career paths.
A Quality Pathways System 
Quality pathways are integrated, intentionally designed structures that span high school and postsecondary systems and enable the delivery of credit-bearing courses and learning experiences, coupled with postsecondary and career advising 


--- Pathways Strategy Review Memo 10062022.docx ---
Introduction and Context
In 2020, the percent of high school graduates who transition to a postsecondary institution in the fall after graduation fell to 63%, with 43% in 4-year colleges and 20% in 2-year colleges. While COVID-19 was a key factor, this continued a decline from 70% that started three years earlier, a troubling trend when those who enroll immediately are more likely to persist and obtain a postsecondary credential. Enrollment differences by race deepened, with 67% of White students enrolled immediately after high school compared to 60% of Latino and 54% of Black students, and enrollment declines were 2.3 times higher for low-income high schools compared to higher income ones.
The Pathways strategy was approved in February 2021 to help reverse this trend, by addressing gaps across K-12 and postsecondary systems in providing experiences and supports necessary to help students, particularly Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds, successfully transition from high school to a postsecondary path leading to a credential of value. We do this by investing in “quality pathways,” intentionally designed programs of study that embed 1) early college coursework (e.g., dual enrollment (DE) and Advanced Placement (AP)), 2) career-connected learning, 3) aligned high school-to-postsecondary math course sequences, and 4) advising supports, (5) delivered through coordinated K-12/postsecondary/workforce structures. See Slide 4, Strategy Overview. Our vision is that these programs of study and navigational supports provide students with the knowledge, skills, and motivation that not only prepare them for higher education and beyond, but also enable them to make informed decisions about their choice of postsecondary institution and program of study. This will ultimately drive increases in immediate enrollment for the 1.4M annual high school graduates who currently do not transition to a postsecondary education path.
Implemented individually, these interventions have demonstrated strong evidence of impact on key “momentum metrics” such as FAFSA completion and completion of early college coursework that are predictive of or associated with postsecondary enrollment, persistence, and completion – yet relatively few of our priority students have access to quality pathways programs and transition supports (70% of U.S. high schools offer at least one DE course, but only 56% of the high schools with 75% or more students who qualify for free and reduced lunch do so).  While Early College High School (ECHS) models demonstrated how these elements in combination have powerful effects on postsecondary enrollment and completion, K-12 and higher education systems (with the exception of Texas) have yet to create mechanisms to incorporate them into the typical high school. Our strategy thus has a twofold approach during this first phase: (1) develop and adapt particular solutions (e.g., advising, credit mobility, dual enrollment) to improve equity of access and adoptability at scale while maintaining quality, and (2) develop and test mechanisms to integrate the delivery of these solutions in structured pathways that connect high school, college and workforce systems at regional and cross-state scale (e.g., Accelerate[ED] initiative and Texas-focused investments) . See Slide 5. We are investing $334 annually ($132M 2021-24) to achieve five objectives:
Increase demand for and expand reach of effective, affordable postsecondary advising models to >500,000 students;
Develop and test credit mobility platforms that enable students to make informed decisions about which postsecondary institutions will maximize credit for relevant prior coursework, to save students time and cost to credential attainment and save institutions time and resources in the transfer process (with PS and USP Data teams)
Support 15 states to adopt new high school math course frameworks that align to postsecondary admissions requirements and programs of stu


--- 031323 USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation Memo.docx ---
USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation
Background
The current system of American mathematics education fails to meet the needs of a tech- and data-driven economy and a complex democracy. For too many students, the way we teach math isn’t engaging and doesn’t feel relevant, and they don’t see themselves as “math people.” As a result, many students neither acquire the foundational math knowledge by eighth grade nor excel in high school math: Recent NAEP scores show that only 26% of all eighth-grade students and 9% of Black and 14% of Hispanic students, respectively, performed at or above NAEP Proficient. In high school, NAEP estimates that only 37% of twelfth-grade students were academically prepared for mathematics in college. 
Moreover, the types of math needed to succeed in economic and civic life are changing. New fields of work center on data and statistical and quantitative analysis and reasoning and existing middle-wage fields such as nursing are increasingly relying on data-driven analysis. Colleges and universities are shifting away from college algebra as a universal gateway course requirement to introductory math courses with content that reflects the analytical requirements of distinct programs of study. Thus, high school students require an updated and more relevant mix of what math to learn, which includes data science, decision science, and statistics, as options alongside improvements in the traditional calculus-focused pathway, which remains essential for many STEM-focused professions.
Similarly, with the emergence of technology and digital materials and interventions, as well as our improved understanding of learning progressions and the imperative to focus on the transfer of procedural and conceptual understandings of math to real world problems, there is an equally important need to significantly change how K-12 students are taught mathematics and engage in math problem solving, e.g., away from pencil and paper toward use of computational and modeling tools and access to large datasets. These changes in the what and the how of math, along with advancements in machine learning and artificial intelligence (AI), offer promising opportunities to create more engaging, relevant math experiences that increase student motivation, engagement, persistence and learning from elementary, through high school pathways, and on to postsecondary and vocational opportunities. 
This memo provides an overview of the topics that the six math experts (see Participant Bios) will cover during the March 13 math pathways and instructional transformation roundtable with you. It also provides background information on the products that they will discuss.
Advances in What Math is Taught
We will discuss shifts in the math content that is the focus of both high school and higher education programs and how states’ K-12 and higher education systems are responding to these changes. This discussion builds upon the June 2022 math pathways meeting with you when we discussed (1) recent changes in the postsecondary math landscape, (2) the current structure of high school math course offerings, (3) lessons learned from the first phase of high school math pathways work, (4) market structure for curricula for new math pathways offerings, and (5) the next phase of the Pathways team’s work to build demand and alignment for new math pathways and the K-12 team’s work to create a supply of quality curriculum for these new pathways. See Appendix A for the June 2022 memo.
This section will be led by Dr. Dave Kung, director of policy at the Dana Center and Pam Burdman, founder and executive director of Just Equations. The Dana Center leads the Launch Years Initiative, which is working with twenty states to scale mathematics pathways from high school through postsecondary education and into the workplace. Just Equations engages decision-makers and influencers with evidence to build shared understanding of access problems and potential s


--- 102824 Pathways Strategy Update Memo.docx ---
2024 Pathways Strategy Update Memo 
Executive Summary: Context, Progress, and Future Plans
Over the past decade, both High School (HS) and Postsecondary (PS) graduation rates have increased, but the USP North Star Goal of ensuring our education systems drive equitable economic mobility remains at risk because: 1) The cost to earn degrees increased by 56% (constant dollars) over the last 20 years; 2) The percentage of HS graduates immediately enrolling in a PS institution has decreased from 70% in 2018 to 62% in 2022; and 3) Challenges to PS persistence occur in the freshman year, partially due to factors prior to enrolling (e.g., insufficient financial aid, lack of academic momentum, etc.). These are exacerbated for our focus students where systemic failures have led to lower PS enrollment (7+ pp delta) and grad rates (22+ pp delta) for Black, Latino, and Indigenous students.
Our USP Pathways strategy was approved in 2021 as a unique approach to address immediate PS enrollment challenges and early momentum toward a credential of value for all students, while closing opportunity gaps for our focus students. The strategy built on work in the K-12, PS, Data, and NAT strategies (e.g., early college high schools, PS advising, community college Guided Pathways) and focused on “quality pathways,” a student experience that includes:
High-quality advising (support to define college and career goals, create plans, navigate PS application, financial, and enrollment processes, etc.)
Accelerated course sequences aligned to and transferable to a PS program of study (emphasizing math given its role in PS admissions and progression) to accelerate progress to PS completion
Career-connected learning (at a minimum, incorporating career exploration into advising, which can increase learner motivation and engagement)
Experiences that build relationships and college-going and growth mindsets via above solutions 
…and results in increases in the following momentum metrics (range based on existing research):
Accumulation of 12-15 early college course credits (e.g., dual enrollment) saving $3-$7K in college 
College and FAFSA application (2-3pp increase for each)
Immediate PS enrollment (5-20 pp increase) 
…that, together should result in increased PS completion at a faster time and lower cost, which drives increased educational value for students (~20% reduction in debt for students who would have otherwise attended college without a pathways experience) and, when executed thoughtfully, can be a more efficient investment ($ per degree) for taxpayers (through reduced taxpayer subsidies per credit and by creating a larger tax base due to greater PS completion and higher earnings).
A look-back across all BoW is in Appendix A; highlights that suggest we are on the right path include:
Tech and data enabled solutions show promise: 
ASU’s Credit Mobility Technology (CMT) projects $1,200 savings per transfer student. We see increased opportunity for students and institutions through AI, with Berkeley CAHL’s use of AI in credit evaluation improving the equivalency algorithm’s accuracy from 46% (2019) to 94% (2024). 
Quality advising partner OneGoal has seen increases of 20pp in immediate PS enrollment, 16pp in year-to-year PS persistence, and 8pp in college graduation. 
Similarly, our partners previously shared with you outcomes related to dual enrollment (e.g., increased PS enrollment (+9.8 and +8.6 pp increase for Black and Latino students); AA completion rates (+9.2 and +6.5 pp for Black and Latino students) (CCRC, 2020), and our support for Community College Research Center’s Dual Enrollment Equity Pathways approach projects strong improvements (+12 pp increase in PS enrollment for our focus students when implemented well) and greater equity of access (expanding the existing base of 2.5M annual DE students).
Additionally, early proof point work demonstrated interest in combining solutions into an integrated pathways framework (supporting field convergence


--- 090320 USP Pathways Roundtable Cover Note.docx ---
Bill, Melinda, and Mark:
We are excited for our time with you on September 3rd for a roundtable discussion with thought leaders on a critical component of the education space – education to employment pathways. This is one of the priorities of the US Program. Our guest experts will frame the challenges for low-income and students of color to successfully transition from high school to a postsecondary pathway that leads to economic mobility. The inequity in current outcomes is profound – instead of functioning to lift people out of poverty, our education system is largely reinforcing and occasionally exacerbating income differences, which are also stratified by race. For example, students with bottom-half math scores – but the highest socioeconomic status – are more likely to complete college degrees than the lowest socioeconomic status students with top-half math scores. Black students who do complete degrees experience the longest time to completion, borrow significantly more than other students, and receive the lowest pay after graduation. The experts will share their views on how we can have systemic impact that address these challenges – sharing successful models and practices that provide personalized advising supports to our target population to get on and persist in a postsecondary path. Finally, they will highlight how the pandemic has increased the risk of students losing momentum, but also accelerated efforts to leverage digital delivery platforms for these supports in ways that can dramatically expand their reach. Without an increased focus on supporting the high school to college transition, we as a country could face a lost generation of millions of young people who struggle to get on a postsecondary and upwardly mobile career path.
We will be joined by Dr. Nicole Smith, Chief Economist, Georgetown Center for Education and the Workforce, Melissa Connelly, CEO, OneGoal, and Dr. Nicole Hurd, CEO, College Advising Corps. Dr. Smith will provide an overview of historical high school to labor market trajectories of Black, Latinx and students from under-resourced communities, and highlight the risks of these students “undermatching” with institutions that have low graduation rates. Ms. Connelly and Dr. Hurd will describe key aspects of the models they built that seek to transform the existing paradigm for college advising by leveraging high-quality curricula, strategic use of human capital, predictive analytics using postsecondary outcomes data, and digital delivery platforms to catalyze school and district leaders to prioritize this work and reach larger numbers of students. 
Our discussion on September 3 will give you an opportunity to understand how these advising models can overcome resource and delivery constraints to dramatically improve the availability of these student supports critical to success for recent and high school graduates in a shifting education and labor market. Some of the themes our guests will be ready to discuss in more detail include:
The emerging post-pandemic labor market picture for Black and Latinx young people – historical trends and current/future projections for those with varying education credentials, including barriers to labor market outcomes and wealth creation, even for degree holders.
What young people – specifically Black and Latinx students – need to successfully navigate the postsecondary transition in this environment, and to overcome these barriers.
Ways that these particular models combine a set of elements that have the potential to change outcomes at scale and change the current postsecondary advising paradigm.
Mechanisms to drive awareness and adoption of these practices, including the role of philanthropy in creating sustainable, publicly funded models.
We have enclosed an overview memo, presentation deck, and two appendices as pre-reads. 
Sara Allan will lead the discussion, and Allan Golston, June Davis, Bob Hughes and Patrick Methvin will also join the meeting.  Bill T


--- 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Cover Note.docx ---
We look forward to discussing the proposed Pathways strategy with Bill and Mark on February 12, and over email follow-ups as needed with Melinda. This work has grown out of an ongoing foundation focus on supporting student momentum from high school graduation to postsecondary attainment, which originally manifested in our K-12 investments in early college high schools. Over time our understanding of what it takes for education systems – both K-12 and postsecondary – to support young people to succeed in required preparatory coursework and develop the skills, agency, and confidence to persist and complete postsecondary programs that position them for mobility in the workforce has deepened. The development of a focused pathways strategy that is designed to bridge and connect K-12, postsecondary, and employment systems represents an evolution from a set of loosely coordinated K-12 and postsecondary bodies of work (postsecondary advising, math pathways, early college learning opportunities, education to employment pathways, guided pathways) focused on key transitions along the 11-16 continuum (and already approved in existing strategies), to an integrated framework with a shared vision and clear, accountable goals around the aligned policies, delivery structures, programs and student supports that are necessary to boost student momentum towards credential attainment and strong labor market outcomes. As outlined in the materials, we are seeking your approval of an updated programmatic portfolio which comprises a set of mutually reinforcing bodies of work aligned to a more clearly defined set of goals, representing a commitment of $132M over four years. This coherence and focus are timely given the momentum in states to increase and improve pathways for students.
Because this work sits across and between K-12 and postsecondary, execution requires ongoing management of key intersections with those PSTs, as well as with the USP Data team. We have called out those intersections and begun to define a set of internal coordinating mechanisms that will drive collaboration in a functional operating model, including some cross reporting of staff and coordinated relationship management of key partners. Externally, we see opportunities to play a connective role between funders (such as Walton and Lumina) that traditionally focus on K-12 or postsecondary but are increasingly interested in the concept of pathways to create leverage.
The proposed strategy integrates three proposed USP opportunities that we reviewed with you in November with the current, ongoing work in Pathways: 1) an expansion of our investments in postsecondary advising solutions to drive improvements in their scalability through technology-focused R&D and to foster greater adoption, 2) a learning initiative focused on testing and validating the potential for credit mobility platforms to shift the incentives and behaviors of postsecondary institutions around credit transfer between institutions to deliver improved ROI to students and taxpayers; and 3) a set of innovation investments to support K-12 districts, CMOs and postsecondary institutions to develop and pilot accelerated early college pathways to AA degrees within a year of high school graduation. These investment areas complement and deepen our focus on the student-facing interventions needed to support momentum towards degrees, and on the underlying delivery system coordinating mechanisms that are needed to better connect K-12 and postsecondary programs and align them with labor market demand. We look forward to exploring the proposed work in detail and responding to any ongoing questions you have.

USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation – Cover Note
Overview:  On March 13 you will be joining a 2-hour facilitated conversation with six field leaders to discuss the evolution and future of grades 6-12 mathematics content, pedagogy, and technology, and the efforts to align high school courses to the national scaling of postsecondary math pathways. The discussion will provide additional perspectives on the ongoing changes in high school math course pathways that we discussed with you on June 21, 2022; and on curricular innovations we are exploring to transform grades 6-12 math instruction as part of the K-12 strategy. This meeting will also provide relevant background information to inform your fireside chat focused on building broader awareness of the elements of the K-12 math strategy at the ASU-GSV Conference on April 18, 2023, in San Diego.
Objectives: The presentation and discussion will cover:
Needed changes in what grades 6-12 mathematics content is taught, including how new high school math pathways align to postsecondary programs of study and the debates around these changes
How changes in math content and tools students use to do math, along with advances in AI, technology, learning science, and understanding of student engagement, create opportunities for changes in how grades 6-12 mathematics is taught, with a focus on our target population of Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds
Current efforts to shift math content and instruction, and the opportunities and challenges for enacting change in education systems

Context (10 min)
Advances in WHAT math is taught (40 min)
Advances in HOW math is taught (60 min)
Our guests include:

They represent examples of the kinds of instructional approaches we will support in the research, development and rapid commercialization of a set of exemplars in the next phase of the K-12 Math strategy.  Dr. Koedinger’s lab at CMU is currently funded by the foundation for R&D work on motivation, learning, and self-regulation. The PLUS tutoring project is not currently funded by us but is being considered for future R&D funding. Amplify has been a grantee in multiple projects in the past including the Balance The Equation Grand Challenge. Amplify’s Desmos product has not been a subject of those prior grants but is being considered for future R&D funding.

--- 021921 USP Pathways Strategy Review Meeting Notes.docx ---
Pathways Strategy Review
Co-chair Meeting Notes 
February 19, 2021
Attendees: Allan Golston, Bill Gates, Bill Tucker, Bob Hughes, Christine Marson, Dan Kaufman, Emily Lockwood, Este Griffith, Gargee Ghosh, Hien Tran, Isa Ellis, Jen Engle, June Davis, Mark Suzman, Nate Simpson, Nicole Ifill, Patrick Methvin, Phoebe Lipkis (notetaker), Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Rob Nabors, Sara Allan, Shweta Suresh, Sidney Hacker, Sue Liu
Follow-ups 
Provide additional information on:
Evidence & Momentum in the Field: Share current evidence and describe current state of existing models of dual enrollment, early college pathways and advising. 
Case studies of how standardization of specific career pathways coursework across high school, two year and four-year institutions minimizes credit loss and accelerates time to credential.
Policy & Finance: Prepare primer on pathways policy and funding streams.
Final Approval:  Engage Melinda’s office in appropriate steps for her approval. Send a follow-up email formally requesting approval of the Pathways strategy.
Key takeaways from co-chairs 
Drive coherence across the whole strategy. As work progresses, critical to demonstrate how discrete bodies of work - credit mobility, dual enrollment, guided pathways – work as an integrated set of interventions.
Focus on driving high-quality exemplars in new models for grades 11-14 that are affordable and accessible, attractive to and motivate students, and lead to student success.
Advising is a key anchor of the strategy. Develop platforms that optimize staffing models/ratios and are digitally enabled; be clear on specific elements that drive efficacy, affordability and are implementable by schools.
Be crisp on critical metrics. For example, success in credit mobility should not simply be about the volume of credits transferred - students need guidance to take relevant transferable courses aligned to program of study.  
Other discussion items
We discussed uptake of dual enrollment programs in the past ten years. Our follow-up material will provide a more comprehensive view of dual enrollment, but we highlight here a few key metrics that help us anchor our understanding of current uptake in the field: 
About 1.4 million students took dual enrollment courses in 2010-11, up from 813,000 students in 2002-03, nearly doubling participation in 8 years. 
Availability of the program varies significantly across states with about half of schools nationally offering dual enrollment courses, but with state-level rates varying from 17% to 82% in 2017-18.
In Texas, more than 133,000 high school students (9% of all enrolled 9th-12th graders, but most DE courses are taken in grades 11 and 12) took dual enrollment courses in 2015 compared to 42,000 in 2005—an increase of 316 percent. Hispanic students are enrolled in dual credit at higher rates compared to the overall percentage of Hispanic students in Texas (44% of dual enrollees versus 35% of all students), while Black students are underrepresented (7% and 12%, respectively).
In California,12.6% of high school graduates from the class of 2016-17 took a community college course while in high school. Participation rates are not proportionate across demographics, with only 8.8% of Black graduates, 10.9% of Latino graduates, and 10.8% of students experiencing poverty taking a college course versus 18.5% of Asian-American high school graduates and 13.9% of white graduates. Access is highly inequitable - dual enrollment is concentrated in roughly one in five high schools, with 82% of high schools having no graduates completing a college course.

Noting that private sector incentives are challenging to implement in public sector contexts, will be interested to see if the bonus structure will work in incentivizing districts to use data and improve student outcomes through quality pathways such as advising, dual enrollment, and accelerated path to associates degree, despite the lagged nature of the bonus.
Important to track how bonus is earned to ensure that incentives do not get skewed and districts try to lower the bar to satisfy requirements, e.g., through lower-quality associates’ degree or industry certificates.
A strong longitudinal data system is critical to the success of this effort to ensure that we have relevant data on the intermediate measures (e.g., FAFSA completion, college applications, immediate postsecondary enrollment) and longer-term measures (credit accumulation, credential attainment, and income) to assess impact. Regional data tools will also help in providing more timely data to districts/schools, pulling from state and national systems as needed.
Even if districts do not earn a large proportion of the possible bonus funding, the structure has the potential to change mindsets related to data sharing and district focus on postsecondary transition rather than just high school graduation and is helping drive demand for quality advising solutions. It will be politically difficult to fully roll back the bonus structure, which enjoys bipartisan support.

Pathways Strategy Review
Co-chair Meeting Notes 
10-6-22
Attendees: Bill Gates, Thomas Tierney, Mark Suzman, Allan Golston, Sara Allan, Andy Cook, Angela Jones, Ankur Vora, Archie Cubarrubia, Becky Frank, Bill Tucker, Bob Hughes, Brooke Anderson, Caira Woods, Carolyn Ainslie, Chris Elias, Christine Marson, Connie Collingsworth, Dale Whittaker, Dan Kaufman, Gargee Ghosh, Hien Tran, Isa Ellis, Jacklyn Altuna Willard, Jamie McKee, Jen Engle, June Davis, Larry Cohen, Leslie Mays, Mark Lloyd, Nate Simpson, Nicole Ifill, Patrick Methvin, Phoebe Lipkis, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Rob Nabors, Rodger Voorhies, Sarah Buhayar, Sonia Vora, Sue Liu, Susan Byrnes, Sylvia Symonds
Follow-ups 
Provide an analysis of economics of different dual enrollment models (i.e., traditional dual enrollment, comprehensive accelerated postsecondary programs), including sources and uses of funding for different models; undertake and share additional research on impact of dual enrollment, at various levels of costs and on different segments of students, on student outcomes such as postsecondary credit accumulation, time to degree, PS enrollment, and degree attainment.
Provide analysis of FAFSA completion and immediate postsecondary enrollment data, including analysis of trends and impact on student outcomes, centered on focus states and analyzed by income, race, and if available, gender. Will also explore drivers of FAFSA completion and impact of immediate postsecondary enrollment on students segmented by time between high school graduation and postsecondary enrollment. 
Provide historical and current trends of college-seeking perception among 18-24-year-olds by race and income, exploring impact of labor market on those perceptions. This will include quantitative and qualitative baselines across multiple existing surveys.
Provide demos of high-quality advising models and new credit mobility platforms. These demos will display the components, features, and underlying data required for a quality user experience. For advising, demo will also show use of tech to supplement and / or enhance human interaction and to create digital advising curriculum.  
Key takeaways from co-chairs 
Overall, co-chair is aligned with strategy. The Pathways team should deepen its research agenda and evaluation efforts to strengthen the evidence base underpinning its solutions, specifically for dual enrollment and accelerated postsecondary pathways models. The evidence needs to control for selection bias, segment students to understand impacts particular to specified groupings (e.g., time from graduation to postsecondary enrollment), clearly map sources and uses of funding required to sustain implementation, and articulate baseline data so that we can discern value-add of specific solutions and models of integrated implementation over time.
Would like to better understand economics of dual enrollment, including funding flows and benefits to education systems, students, and families. The team noted that investments in California, Florida and within the Accelerate[ED] initiative communities, including our partnership with Arizona State University to pilot a national platform with select school districts to offer accelerated postsecondary pathways, will enable evaluation and learning that deepens field understanding of cost / benefit of dual enrollment.
Would like a clearer image of implementation contexts and models for postsecondary advising solutions: what data is being shared with students; what human versus AI or other tech engagement looks like; how technology can enhance these solutions; and the contours of a quality user experience.  


--- 031323 USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation Meeting Notes.docx ---
USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation
Co-chair Meeting Notes 
13 March 2023
Attendees:  
Internal: Allan Golston, Andy Cook, Archie Cubarrubia, Bill Gates, Bill Tucker, Bob Hughes, Chris Rush, Elizabeth Moore, Francesca Mazzola, Gargee Ghosh, Hien Tran, June Davis, Lewis Leiboh, Mark Suzman, Patrick Methvin, Rebecca Hartzler, Sara Allan
External: Daniel Meyer, Dashiell Young-Saver, David Kung, Kenneth Koedinger, Ji Yun Son, Pamela Burdman
Follow-ups 
Send Skew The Script quadratic equations lessons.  Note: Links to lessons and a tester account sent to Bill on 15 March 2023.
Key takeaways from co-chairs 
Overall, co-chair found the demonstrated products compelling in how they integrated algebra and data science content, used topics relevant to students, allowed for real-time student-teacher interaction, and were user-friendly.
Discussion made clear that there is no boundary between algebra I and statistics / data science. The ability to teach real world skills around both, as with CourseKata, represents a double win.
Boundary between statistics, data science, and simple programming doesn’t really exist and we need to make sure we don’t place statistics / data science on one path and calculus on another without any integration across those courses.
We may need to take out less relevant topics in algebra I, geometry, and algebra II to make room for data science and simple programming.   
Dilemma with personalized learning because it does not allow for social interaction. The products demonstrated show how the interaction between teachers and students and among students is critical to learning.
There is a concern that the ability to drive national standards on what math must be learned  has weakened when the SAT is no longer required. While a national framework on math course sequences and math content isn’t possible at this point, it is important to hone in on a smaller number of strong frameworks for states to use, as our partners are doing.
Equity and access issues for calculus and other advanced math remain significant.
Other discussion items 
While several of these new products show promise, they are still in early stages. None have been scaled in a way that can show student outcomes across an entire school district.


--- 031323 USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation Appendix A.docx ---
USP Roundtable on Math Pathways & Instructional Transformation – Appendix A
June 21 2022 Memo – Aligning High School and Postsecondary Math Pathways
Success in relevant, rigorous math courses in high school and postsecondary education is essential to students’ ability to achieve their educational and career aspirations and to meeting workforce and societal needs. But the current K-12 math education system serves neither students nor the workforce well. Many students do not get the foundational knowledge in K-8 math to enter math successfully in high school. High schools continue to guide students to an algebra-heavy curriculum aligned to a perceived singular higher ed calculus pathway, even though the labor market increasingly requires skills such as statistics, data science, and quantitative analysis. Many students struggle with the traditional course sequence, and this impacts their postsecondary options and success – longitudinal data from Texas shows that of the students who complete Algebra II as their highest math course, only around 35% persist in a second year of college. While many postsecondary systems have evolved to offer additional math gateway courses, elite college admission requirements still preference calculus, in practice and perception, driving many students to accelerate through middle school math and take high school courses with limited relevance to their college and career goals. At the same time, inequitable access to calculus and other advanced math courses by race and income means that many Black and Latino students and students from low-income backgrounds are unable to take the calculus path even if it aligns with their goals. And even where schools do offer a calculus pathway, our target students are often relegated to low-rigor, dead-end courses that do not align to college entry requirements. 
But the math landscape is changing. Many postsecondary systems now offer non-calculus pathways. Today, nearly twenty states have started a process to shift K-12 policies to align with reformed postsecondary math pathways. They have begun to adopt new advanced math course sequences or to implement new courses – although implementation remains early, and the leading states do not share a single approach. Some key universities have followed suit: In 2020, the University of California system allowed data science, statistics, and certain quantitative reasoning courses to satisfy UC admissions requirements. Just this May, Harvard declared that data science, statistics, and mathematical modeling are given equal consideration to calculus in the application process, acknowledging that “many students have no intention to pursue college coursework that requires a knowledge of calculus” and not all “have the same opportunities to take certain math classes in high school, including calculus.”
Our partners – led by the Dana Center at UT Austin – have been the leading voices and technical experts driving these changes, supported by over a decade of investments by the Postsecondary Success team to reform developmental math in higher ed, and three years of investments by the K-12 and Pathways teams to foster adoption by states and districts of high school math course sequences aligned with these postsecondary changes. As we build on the changes already made by postsecondary systems, our experience and our ability to work on both demand (policies and practices) and supply (instructional materials) positions the K-12 and Pathways teams well to accelerate these changes in the K-12 space. 
The objectives of this memo and our June 21 discussion on high school math pathways are to summarize the key drivers of the current state of high school math, describe field momentum towards a new vision for high school math pathways, and update you on USP’s existing work in high school math. In contrast with our work in K-8 math, which is focused on optimizing curricular solutions and teaching methods aligned to settled content standards, th

--- 102824 Pathways Strategy Update_Appendix E.docx ---
Appendix E: Pathways Incremental Request Memo (2025-28) 
Executive Summary								
The Pathways team is requesting an incremental $124M to fund the next phase of work that builds on solutions and policy momentum to set the stage for national-level adoption by 2028. With a baseline budget of $124.9M, a $14M reallocation from Postsecondary Success (PS), and a $7M envelope from PS, this request brings the total proposed four-year budget to $270M. The request includes one additional FTE, bringing the total to 16 FTE and significantly increasing our $ per FTE ratio. We will wind down two current BoW and reallocate $18.8M to next phase priorities. This memo outlines the Core, High, and Low scenarios, and proposes two performance-based funding unlocks (included in all scenarios) to support resource allocation and tradeoffs. In the core proposal, 59% of funding is aligned to the Foundation’s comparative advantages (Solution R&D and Data) and ~78% aligned to USP division priorities (compared to 65% of overall USP budget allocated to division priorities).
This incremental funding helps ensure that by 2028, the field has the exemplars, solutions, policies, and public goods to support a nationwide field of states and regions implementing quality pathways for students. Without the funds, we risk missing the opportunity to leverage private and public investment earmarked for pathways today and shaping the definition and delivery of quality pathways, which could in turn improve postsecondary outcomes nationally – and the ROI of postsecondary experience for students and taxpayers. Please refer to the Pathways Strategy Memo for more detail.
Costing Methodology & Tradeoffs							
We developed our proposed budget by aligning on high-level strategic goals and outcomes, drilling into key workstreams to achieve impact, then further scoping specific activities to support our bottom-up cost analysis. This process included an audit of current BoW outcomes and funding allocations to determine the work to continue, stop, or start. We have organized our work into four core portfolios – Pathways Integrated Framework & Solutions, Proof Point Delivery, Enablers, and Strategy & Learning, and restructured our BoWs to sharpen our focus on the Foundation’s comparative advantage in tech-enabled solutions to support delivery.
This process led us to sunset three BoW, freeing up $39M to invest in toward 2028 outcomes:
Two learning BoW from the previous strategy are winding down - Pathways Intermediary Function ($12.8M) and Narratives & Knowledge Mobilization ($6M). Lessons on how intermediaries drive collaboration, shape community narratives, and data use for improvement are informing how we fund delivery and build a strong pathways field in this next phase.
The work embedded in the Accelerated Postsecondary Pathways BoW ($20.2M), including the equitable dual enrollment research and AccelerateED Network, is at the center of our next strategy. We will divide work and funding into focused BoW to ensure rigorous solutions practice for the Integrated Pathways Framework and Dual Enrollment, while AccelerateED sites are supported as part of a cohesive proof point portfolio, and the broader AccelerateED network becomes the base for momentum in the Strong National Pathways Field BoW.
To ensure efficient resource allocation for the work that we propose, we partnered with internal and external experts to benchmark estimated budgets:
Pathways Integrated Framework & Solutions: Partnered with Boston Consulting Group (BCG) to scope and cost tech-enabled advising play. Credit mobility estimates are based directly on developer costs and were benchmarked with the field. Remaining public goods-focused BoW benchmarked on Postsecondary Success public goods investments.
Proof Point Delivery: Benchmarked delivery costs against previous regional initiatives (AccelerateED, PS Intermediaries for Scale). Engaged USP Data to estimate infrastructure costs.
Enablers: Benchmarked field-building


--- 102824 Pathways Strategy Update_Summary for Appendix D.docx ---
Summary for Appendix D. Advising
The appendix provides an overview of the Advising BoW proposed in the next phase, with an emphasis on describing the tech- and AI-focused approach. 
The field is broadly aligned on the essential components of the "desired student experience" (DSE) for holistic advising – high-quality, data-informed conversations with caring adults, identity formation & exploration, options that span both college & career, visibility into financial tradeoffs, and frictionless application processes. These experiences can lead to meaningful improvements in PS enrollment outcomes when deployed with fidelity (1.1-1.4X PS enrollment vs. baseline for our priority students) and can also act as an enabler for delivery of the integrated framework writ large (supporting uptake of dual enrollment, etc.).
However, these experiences are not available for students at scale – and while we have seen (and invested in) examples of how tech has helped to deliver at greater scale in past (e.g., BottomLine's BluPrint), we see a potential step-change on the horizon with fully AI-centric advising – i.e.,  More personalized postsecondary option exploration and decision support, enabled by a range of multi-modal conversational interfaces
Deeper integration of advising into real-time choices, with more accessible supports (both 'on-demand' tools for students & adult-mediated touchpoints)
Improved ability for advisors & district leaders to monitor, measure, and reallocate which students receive which supports
Yet, there are many "known unknowns" about the future of AI and how the technology will evolve (as well as how rapidly People and Processes adapt to these new opportunities). To balance the need to impact the market today, while also avoiding exacerbating potential technical obsolescence in the future, we propose an investment approach that gives us option value across two potential technology-enabled horizons. 
Horizon 1 | In the near-term, we propose to pursue a place-based play that seeks to raise the table-stakes functionality for the CCR market in line with our vision of quality, by using our influence as a "power user" (i.e., district or state-level buyer) to influence multiple CCR platforms' R&D agendas (e.g., NYCPS has 3 CCR players, ~20%+ of market). This will allow us to influence the current state market, drive outcomes today, and preserve optionality for when & how to pursue our Horizon 2 play (as CCR players are one of several scenarios for who could lead supply-side dev't in an AI-centric future) 
Horizon 2 | We will also invest in a learning agenda about AI-centric advising, exploring key areas of clarity (e.g., ideal tasks for direct-to-student functionality vs. through an adult, need for bias mitigation, understanding 'who will win' in supply-side market) that will inform the role we play in its realization (i.e., will our innovation agenda focus on the tech directly, or on the related people/process innovation? How much will we drive R&D directly vs. focus on enablers such as data, benchmarks?). Based on the findings from these learnings & pace of change, we will evaluate in ~18-24 months whether to unlock a $25M contingent budget to pursue Horizon 2 at greater speed.
In parallel, we will continue to invest in "no regrets" enablers (e.g., definition of advising, mechanisms for measurement) that will support the field at large.

USP Pathways Roundtable
September 3, 2020 | 1:45-2:45 pm PDT
Meeting Participant Bios
June Davis 
Director, Strategy Planning & Management and Chief of Staff, U.S. Program 
June supports the overall strategic and operational work of our United States Program, bringing over 15 years of experience in the education and retail industries. Her background includes leadership roles in strategy, digital commerce and merchandising at Nike and Target Corporation, as well as in the for-profit K-12 and higher education sectors. She launched LearnNow’s first public charter school, an elementary school in St. Paul focused on the Hmong community. June also served as the Senior Managing Director for Strategy and Operations at Teach For America for their People Development team. In her role as Global Inclusion & Diversity Lead at Cargill, she identified the best practices, policies, processes and systems needed to embed inclusion and diversity into the organization spanning 150,000+ employees across 70+ countries in sustainable and scalable ways, and partnered with business teams and leaders to support and accelerate their work in creating inclusive work environments.  
Sara Allan 
Director, Early Learning & Pathways, U.S. Program 
Sara, director of Early Learning and Education Pathways in the United States Program, oversees efforts to  increase the quality of pre-K systems and programs and to ensure that students effectively transition between Pre-K and K-12 systems, the K-12 system and the Postsecondary levels, and between education after high school and the workforce.  She joined the foundation in 2011 as the deputy director of Strategy, Planning and Management for the K-12 team. Most recently, she led the development and implementation of program strategies targeted at improving the college and career readiness of U.S. students. Her work focused on catalyzing innovative models in education that aim to dramatically accelerate student learning, and on expanding access to high impact education-to-employment pathways that help students of color and those experiencing poverty to build skills and attain credentials that enable upward mobility in the workforce. Previously, Sara was a district level leader at Portland Public Schools, where she led reform initiatives including restructuring the portfolio of schools, redesign of core human capital processes and systems, the development of school results frameworks, and directed resource allocation strategy, research, evaluation, data policy & analysis. Prior to working in education, Sara was a strategy consultant with the Boston Consulting Group, where she worked with private and public sector clients on issues of strategy, organizational development, and growth in North and South America.   Sara grew up in Canada and holds a B.Sc in mathematics and economics from the University of Toronto and a MBA from the Ivey School of Business at the University of Western Ontario.
Patrick Methvin	
 Director, Postsecondary Success, U.S. Program 
Patrick Methvin, Director of Postsecondary Success in the U.S. Program, leads the foundation’s team that is responsible for scaling the adoption of innovations, including wholly new education and business models that have been proven at enterprise scale to help achieve a more student-centric, effective, efficient, and value-driven, approach to higher education.
Prior to the foundation, Patrick served as a Principal in the Boston Consulting Group's Social Impact and Consumer Goods Practice Areas. In the Social Impact Practice, Patrick supported higher education institutions in managing operating model changes necessitated by their rapidly changing funding environments. He also supported K-12 transformation at the state, district and charter school organization levels. In Patrick's Consumer Goods work, he focused on consumer insights and marketing, leading efforts at both local and national scale using multiple communication vehicles. 
Patrick holds an MBA from the Wharton

	EXTERNAL PARTICIPANT Bios & Organizational profiles
Dan Meyer 
Director of Research, Desmos/Amplify
Dr. Meyer taught high school math to students who didn’t like high school math. He has advocated widely for better math instruction and is the author of the Mathworlds Substack. He earned his doctorate from Stanford University in math education and is the Director of Research at Desmos/Amplify and former Chief Academic Officer at Desmos, which was founded with a goal of helping everyone learn math, love math, and grow with math. Dan speaks internationally and was named one of Tech & Learning’s 30 Leaders of the Future. He lives in Oakland, CA.
Amplify is dedicated to collaborating with educators to create learning experiences that are rigorous and riveting for all students. Amplify creates K-12 core and supplemental curriculum, assessment, and intervention programs for today’s students. A pioneer in K-12 education since 2000, Amplify is leading the way in next-generation curriculum and formative assessment. Today, Amplify serves 15 million students in all 50 states, where their captivating core and supplemental programs in ELA, math, and science support teachers in engaging all their students in rigorous learning and inspiring them to think deeply, creatively, and for themselves, while their formative assessment products help teachers provide the targeted instruction students need to build a strong foundation in early reading and math. In 2022, the Desmos Classroom division became part of Amplify Education and released a comprehensive middle school math curriculum, operating under the belief that that everyone has an inner mathematician and that some people haven’t been given the opportunity, encouragement, or tools to discover theirs’. Desmos’ free suite of math tools is now used annually by over 75 million people around the world. 
Dashiell (Dash) Young-Saver
High School AP Statistics Teacher and Founder & CEO of Skew The Script
While teaching at a high-poverty school on the southside of San Antonio, Dash Young-Saver threw out his traditional AP Stats curriculum and created lessons on topics that his students cared about: voter power, food deserts, the Spurs’ chance at winning the NBA title, online dating, and more. That year, more students at the school took and passed the AP exam than in the previous 16 years combined. During the pandemic, Dash put his lessons online, where they went viral. His website became Skew The Script – a nonprofit that provides free, socially relevant math lessons. Dash still teaches part-time in San Antonio and writes math lessons for The New York Times. He holds a BA and master’s in data science from Harvard. 
Skew The Script launched as a non-profit in 2020, based on Dash’s online curriculum to make statistics more accessible and relevant to students. The lessons tackle authentic problems with real data, and the material are aligned with state standards, allowing teachers to use Skew the Script lessons in their unit plans. Skew the Script has a collection of AP Statistics, Algebra I, and Algebra II lessons with animations and instructive simulations using the Desmos platform. The curriculum is used by more than 20,000 teachers and 400,000 students nationwide. 
Dave Kung, PhD	
Director of Policy, Charles A. Dana Center 
As Director of Policy, Dr. Kung enacts the Center’s mission of ensuring that all students—particularly those from groups historically underserved—have equitable access to an excellent, modern, math and science education. He supports collaborations with state agencies and professional associations of mathematicians to ensure that the next generation of STEM professionals reflects the full diversity of our society.
