--- WSI Strategy Memo 04152024.docx ---
The Washington State Initiative (WSI) strategy was approved on September 15, 2022, with a vision that every Washington (WA) student -- especially Black, Latino, Indigenous students, and students from low-income communities -- has the opportunity to pursue and attain a credential of value. Currently, 50% of high school graduates in WA do not enroll directly in any form of post-secondary (a 10-percentage point drop since strategy approval). By 2035, our goal is to increase postsecondary (PS) enrollment in WA State to 70%, above the national average of 69%. The four levers of our Theory of Action will drive these outcomes: 1) statewide infrastructure & systems support, 2) regional partnerships that drive alignment among regional actors & influence state decisions, 3) adoption of effective pathways structures & solutions, and 4) an enabling data ecosystem at the state & regional levels. 
Our early momentum indicators (EMIs) are, by 2026, to increase FAFSA completion, dual credit attainment, and access to quality advising. This will be powered by an Education Roundtable focused on Equitable Value and a Funders’ Table that connects system components into state-wide strategic plans, policies, and funding for PS enrollment. Four strong regional partnerships will be seen as exemplars, using state-wide data and high-quality solutions within their regions to customize pathways for their students, as they focus on our shared goal. We will also set the stage for scaling what we learn.  
Three environmental factors introduced headwinds to our short and long-term goals in these first 18 months. First, we based our strategy goal on 2019 data that showed WA State PS enrollment at 61%. More recent data shows the class of 2021 at a PS enrollment rate of 50%. Latino enrollment declined dramatically from 54% to 40% in the same time period and was the only focus population whose decline accelerated rather than slowed or leveled off between 2020 and 2021. While we do not believe we should adjust our 70% goal, this new data will be a key factor in setting targets for our EMI progress in regions in Q2 2024. Two critical research studies: the first to understand the mindsets and paths of the 50% of students not enrolling and the second to evaluate the effectiveness of a PS enrollment Targeted Universalism advising model will inform regional interventions. We will have initial findings in 2025 and final findings from both in Q2 2026. 
Second, the “Better FAFSA” (Free Application for Federal Student Aid), a streamlined student aid form released by the US Department of Education, is hindering rather than helping students with FAFSA completion. As of March 30, 2024, WA’s FAFSA completion rate was 26.8% (44th in the nation), as compared to 37.5% this time last year and below the 33.7% national average. While we influenced colleges and universities to extend their enrollment deadlines to accommodate graduating seniors, we will not know the full impact of these challenges until Fall 2024 enrollment numbers are published in early 2025. 
Third, WA Student Achievement Council (WSAC), the state’s higher education entity, launched a round of regional grants in Q1 2023, in parallel with our planned timeline for selecting regional partnerships. Given WSAC’s leadership role, we delayed our regional partner selection to Q4 2023. Our intention with this change was to be a good partner, reduce confusion in the field, and align to the education system’s calendar for budgeting & planning. Despite this delay, strong partnerships are now in place to help us close this short-term gap in our progress. The delay also allowed us (per stakeholder feedback) to launch a state-wide learning network for practitioner leaders, where we can experiment with scaling and knowledge-mobilizing mechanisms. 
Despite the headwinds, we have made significant progress across the four levers in our theory of action and the corresponding bodies of work.
Statewide Infrastructure: Policy, Advocacy & 


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Memo.docx ---
WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review
May 22, 2019
This memo summarizes our assessment of the charter sector in WA and outlines a proposed strategy for the next three years. We recommend a decision in 2022 to refresh the strategy or exit the sector. We conclude by highlighting critical risks of this strategy.
Assessment of WA Outcomes: How is the sector performing?
As the scorecard for WA on slide 5 shows, we are seeing mixed but promising results on Academic Quality, promising results on Access & Equity and unfavorable results on Financial Sustainability. On Innovation and Influence on Ecosystem, we have limited data due to the early stage of the sector and can only provide a qualitative assessment. Thus, we focus on the first three outcomes:
Academic Quality: We define a high-quality charter school as one where students demonstrate greater growth than students in the comparison district. We use the state’s student growth percentiles (SGP) and the CREDO analysis to assess.  
Results are preliminary given small samples and limited years of data and we consider the 2018 CREDO report as a baseline. For the sector, CREDO found “no statistically different academic growth in reading and math when compared to their exact-match counterpart in nearby district schools.” However, the performance of schools is varied, and we see early signs of quality outcomes for students, with some schools as significant positive outliers.
Access & Equity: Access is defined as equal or higher enrollment shares than traditional public schools (TPS) of students of color, low-income students, English language learners (ELL), and students with special education (SpEd) needs. Equity is defined as higher reading and math learning gains than TPS for these student populations. We assess access & equity using data from the Office of Superintendent for Public Instruction and the CREDO results.
WA charter schools show strong results, serving high proportions of above student populations. ELL significantly outperformed TPS in both reading and math. Black and Hispanic students had improved math learning gains over TPS, but not at a statistically significant level. 
Financial Sustainability: We define financial sustainability as a school being able to fully fund its costs with public revenue, once the school is fully built out (i.e., all grades have opened and are enrolled). We assess using publicly available audited financials and data provided by CMO grantees and Washington State Charter School Association (WA Charters).
We see unfavorable results sector-wide with two drivers. First, factors that impact all charter schools, e.g. limited public funding for SpEd, no access to local levies, and teacher compensation increases. Second, there are specific factors related to school tenure and management which we detail in Appendix E. 
Assessment of Enablers: What are the drivers of sustainability?
We focus on Enablers that are most critical to Financial Sustainability outcomes above. Other enablers are currently deprioritized until the sector is successfully incubated.
Financial Supports
Facilities Support: Charters do not have access to public funds for facilities and therefore currently use per-pupil operating funds or philanthropic funds to finance facility lease, renovation, purchase, and maintenance. (For reference, 29 states and DC have authorized policy options to assist with charter facilities funding.) This is an area where philanthropy has played a critical role in other states, such as the Walton Family Foundation’s Charter Impact Fund ($200M for long-term loans) and Facilities Investment Fund ($100M for short-term, for high percentage of project costs), and Charter School Growth Fund’s facilities technical assistance and facilities financing assistance.
Special Ed Support: The state caps SpEd funding based on average SpEd enrollment statewide (13.5 percent). This leaves schools with high percentages of SpEd students (e.g., Olympus), or schools that ser


--- 09152022_ WSI Strategy Approval_ Memo.docx ---
Executive Summary & Goals
During my 27 years serving in Washington state’s education system, whether in the classroom or as an executive leader, one thing was consistent: students I engaged with had hopes and dreams far beyond the schoolhouse walls. Despite what they have faced over the past few years, Washington students maintain high aspirations for their futures, but many are on a crash course with the economy. Forty percent of high school graduates in Washington do not enroll directly in any form of postsecondary education (2-yr, 4-yr, registered apprenticeships), while in Washington, nearly 70 percent of all projected job openings require at least some education beyond high school. While challenges are complex, we have optimism and a focused sense of urgency to break down barriers and co-create solutions to ensure students successfully move through pathways to credentials of value and meaningful employment. Our vision is that every Washington student, and especially Black, Latino, Indigenous students, and students from low-income communities, have the opportunity to pursue and attain a credential of value with a path to meaningful employment. With the strong relationships we’ve built in Washington State, the assets across the US Program and the experience from past investments, we deeply believe that we can create change so more students in Washington, especially under-served students, can thrive. 
By 2035, our goal is to increase the direct high school (HS) to postsecondary (PS) enrollment to 70%, beyond the state average of 61% and the national average of 69%. We believe that we will also see some impact on key PS metrics that lead to increased PS completion rates given the predictive relationship between direct HS to PS enrollment and the key PS metrics. We made four strategic choices to reach this goal: 1) close the transition gap between HS and PS, an acute problem in the education-to-workforce (E-W) continuum, through a focus on grade 9-13 interventions; 2) leverage regional partnerships to solve system fragmentation and drive local impact; 3) leverage state-level policy, data, funding, and eco-system alignment as an enabler of regional partnerships and multi-regional scale; and 4) generate exemplars in Washington and share lessons learned that inform national strategies, especially US Program Data, Pathways, Postsecondary and K-12 strategies. Our place-based strategy (Appendix A: Placed Based Framework) depends on engagement across government, the private sector, civil service, and community in Washington. We have deeply engaged with stakeholders throughout the strategy development process (Appendix B: Stakeholder Engagement Update and Ongoing Engagement Plan) and will continue to embed this practice over the four years of our strategy.
Theory of Change & Scale
Our Theory of Change (TOC) asserts that in Washington’s highly decentralized system, strengthening regional partnerships is the critical unit of change to increase the direct student transition between HS to PS (slide 5). Regional partnerships are strengthened through critical enablers at the state-level, and regions with strong data capacity can play a critical role driving change statewide. These mutually reinforcing mechanisms drive adoption of regional solutions while state enablers support delivery of equitable services. Our TOC is informed by understanding exemplars that have achieved meaningful progress on student outcomes both in Washington and other decentralized states. We will rigorously test this theory through support to cross-sectoral regional partnerships, bolstered by state-level policies and data infrastructure that reduce barriers to PS enrollment. For the 4-year strategy period, we will focus on 3-5 regions. Success in these focus regions along with improvements in statewide policies, data infrastructure and funding will serve as exemplars as we consider longer-term implications for scaling improvements to increase direct student enrollmen


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Cover Note.docx ---
TO		Bill Gates and Sue Desmond-Hellmann
FROM		David Bley
DATE		May 14, 2019
SUBJECT	Pre-read: 05222019 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review
Dear Bill and Sue,
We look forward to meeting with you on May 22, 2019 to seek your feedback on the next phase of the Washington Charter School Initiative (WACSI). In the five years since the charter law passed, significant legal hurdles have slowed progress. However, in October 2018, after the legislature approved a major overhaul to comply with the initial court decision, charter schools were declared constitutional by the WA State Supreme Court – a critical milestone for the sector. We considered this an opportune time to take stock of what we have learned and align with you on our role in the sector going forward.
Our investment strategy to date has sought to incubate a new charter sector in WA, with incubation defined as a sector with schools that are high quality and financially sustainable. We define quality as students demonstrating greater growth than students in a comparison district, and we define sustainability as being able to fully fund costs with public revenue once a school is fully built out (i.e., all grades have opened and are enrolled).
Thirteen charter schools currently operate in WA, enrolling 3,187 students. The existing schools serve students with Special Education at rates higher than the state average, a notable fact in the charter sector. We are seeing early signs of academic quality, though this is uneven across the sector and it is too early to draw conclusions. The sector has a strong Charter Support Organization and support among policymakers is growing.
Despite this progress, our financial analysis shows that the sector is far from being financially sustainable. Eleven of the schools are not projected to reach financial sustainability on current public funding levels. While enrollment and cost drivers can decrease operating gaps, schools need increased state funding to be financially sustainable.  Thus, our recommended strategy shifts are more modest than might be expected in a typical refresh, and they rely on advocacy as a critical lever for increasing public funding. We outline in the attached memo a strategy approach for the next three years, at which point we recommend a decision about whether to exit the sector or make a more significant strategy shift. 
Our recommended strategy continues to manage considerable risk. We are currently subsidizing an unsustainable sector, and our recommended 2022 decision hinges on a policy fix that brings public revenue to an adequate level. We are optimistic about a policy fix – the climate around charters is as positive as it has ever been among policymakers, and we have secured policy wins in more hostile climates. However, there are no guarantees and we would estimate our chances at better than 50/50.
The proposed strategy would cost $54M over three years, approximately $18M/year, which is in-line with our annualized spend since 2013. The current budget allocation is $14M/year. If you are aligned with our direction, we will make an incremental request of ~$4M/year for 2020-2022 via the annual planning process later this year. By 2022, we expect to double the number of students served by charter schools in WA across 19 schools.
We intend to use our time together to brief you on the current state of the sector and discuss our proposed approach for the next three years. We seek your feedback and alignment on the critical risks associated with this strategy and the proposed 2022 decision. 
Orientation to the Charter Sector Scorecard
We developed a scorecard to learn from exemplar sectors, assess the WA charter sector, and guide future investment. Because we use the scorecard to organize the memo and supporting materials, we include here a reference for how to read and interpret the scorecard.
We believe an exemplar sector is one that has high-quality, financially sustainable schools serving diverse populations,

Washington State Initiative: Strategy Approval Presentation
Cover Note
September 8, 2022
The Washington state team is looking forward to our third strategy meeting with you on September 15 where we will seek your approval of our new WSI strategy. 
Sine our last meeting with you in June, we have focused our energy in a few key areas, and we plan to spend the meeting time sharing and discussing those: our ToS and MLE plan (slides 7-12); our regional and state levers including cohort tracking (slides 13-18); and execution planning including our results framework (slides 19-22).
Our materials are structured to present a comprehensive view of our strategy so you will see some familiar content especially in the memo and deck. We want to point you to materials that represent new work, including areas you asked about in June, and remind you about what you’ve seen before. 
We heard your ask for a better understanding (including examples) of what drove change in Washington in regions and at the state level and have captured key insights on our ToS in our presentation deck (slides 8-12), a ToS appendix (Appendix F: Theory of Scale) and case studies, including one on Chehalis (in Appendix D: Lessons learned from Washington and Other States). 
We have provided some additional details on our regional partnership approach (Appendix C: Regional Partnerships) and illustrated the role we anticipate playing in regions (Appendix E: Hypothetical Investment Scenario).
We have shared some details of early execution thinking and planning (Appendix H: Execution Planning).
We have also included appendices you have seen before with some updates (Appendix A: Place-Based Framework, Appendix: B: Stakeholder Engagement Update and Ongoing Engagement Plan and Appendix G: Risk and Mitigation Strategy). 
For this team, Washington is not just our focus state, it is our home. We are deeply excited about the impact potential of the strategy we share in these materials, and we recognize that, especially as a place-based strategy, the details will continue to be refined and the work will come to life through execution planning and implementation in collaboration with local partners. We are eager to advance this through the Year 1 execution priorities we have identified and to share with foundation colleagues what we learn as we go about how to best support impact and scale in the context of local partnerships.
As we seek your approval of the WSI strategy, we have a few things we ask you consider as you read the material and engage with us during the presentation:
Do we have your approval for our Washington State strategy?
Are you aligned with our theory of scale and how we will design and invest for state-wide impact in the context of our regional focus strategy?
Are you supportive of our approach to policy reform and do you have any advice?
Do you have any reflections on the strategic questions to inform our execution planning? 


--- 05222019 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Meeting Notes.docx ---
Washington Charter Schools Initiative (WACSI)
Strategy Refresh Meeting Notes
05/22/2019 
Attendees: Allan Golston, Bill Gates, Buck Child, David Bley, Edna Garrett, Grant Nguyen, Jill Nishi, Lindsay Hunsicker, Jean-Claude Brizard, Kate Stivers, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Sarah Buhayar, Shweta Suresh, Sue Desmond Hellmann, Telca Porras, VK Vu, Sam White 
Follow Ups:
No formal actions or deliverables were highlighted for follow up with Bill and Sue. 
The team will keep Bill and Sue posted on developments around school closures, levy equalization, and potential unionization of charter school teachers that would have significant impacts for the strategy.  
The team will come back to Bill and Sue with any specific asks where the foundation’s voice can help to advance our goals.
Key takeaways from co-chairs 
Levy equalization is the highest priority for the WA State Charters Initiative. WACSI will direct the bulk of its advocacy efforts to support this goal over the next year. 
Ensuring that our charter school partners provide the highest possible quality of education is crucial. Assuming that we can get levy equalization, we need to focus on ensuring consistently high-quality education that meets the needs of low-income students in WA.  
Marketing is an important lever toward which we should direct additional resources. We currently spend approximately $3m on marketing. We will direct more money toward digital advertising, school supports, parent outreach and organizing through the summer before we reassess our funding levels over the next few years.
WACSI will continue to monitor efforts to unionize charter school teachers and the potential implications for our work. 
Overall agreement on our path forward
Overall, Bill and Sue are supportive of the direction put forward by the WACSI team and the proposed timeline for a decision on continuation of WA charters work in 2022.
WACSI will submit an incremental funding request, as outlined in the meeting docs, during fall annual planning.

--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix B.docx ---
Appendix B: Look Back and Key Lessons Learned
As part of this refresh process, we have looked back at our initial hypotheses and our lessons learned in order to inform actions moving forward. We share our reflections below, based on an examination of those initial hypotheses as well as some difficult choices made during the period of legal instability.
A focus on quality can drive early student outcomes. From the start, we had non-negotiable criteria that charter schools in Washington (WA) would be high quality and we focused on building the systems required to support this. We’ve learned that the leadership academies and technical assistance have been essential for the schools. We’ve also seen that supplementing the Washington State Charter School Commission’s accountability framework with more frequent formative data and a rigorous nationally-benchmarked evaluation (CREDO) are important to validate progress and support improvement efforts. We do not yet have conclusive evidence of which school models are producing the best results; we will learn more about this in the coming years.
Our initial hypotheses about CMOs and independent charter schools have not been borne out. Based on early exemplar research, we hypothesized that CMOs were more likely to bring high-quality and cost-effective models to the state. Independent charter schools in other markets have a mixed track record, so we wanted to ensure they remained no more than half of the portfolio here. However, the results in WA have been different. The CMOs have a mixed record on quality and are operating with significant funding gaps (average of $2,300 per pupil). Some independent charter schools have achieved higher quality, and they are all running leaner models with smaller gaps (average of $700 per pupil). CMO challenges appear to be driven by (1) slow adaptation to rapidly shifting conditions, (2) overreliance on brand value more than direct marketing and sustained parent engagement, and (3) inability to realize economies of scale in this sector. Going forward, we are supporting the CMOs to address these challenges and/or transition schools to new operators, and we will fund new school growth in the sector via independent charter schools.
It is possible for charters to meet a need in the market. Charter schools in some sectors are rightly criticized for “creaming” or under-enrolling students with disabilities, dual-language learners, and low-income students. We were determined that this would not be the case for charter schools in WA, and the sector has been successful in enrolling these students at high rates and supporting them to achieve equitable outcomes. Our market research suggests that word-of-mouth is an important channel for parents of students with special needs, and that many of these parents are dissatisfied with district special education services. We have provided funding for technical assistance through Seneca Family of Agencies to ensure schools have the support needed to serve these students, which has turned out to be critical.
However, WA does not adequately fund schools to educate students with special needs. The success of the charter schools in enrolling high numbers of special education students is contributing to funding gaps that we seek to remedy through advocacy. 
Advocacy remains a critical lever. Our education work in WA has long included a focus on state policy, and WACSI is no different. Advocates effectively mobilized parents and students at key moments, such as reinstatement of the charter law in 2016 and at the courthouse for the second round of Supreme Court oral arguments, helping to secure critical wins. Similarly, we saw advocates advance charter issues farther than expected in the 2019 legislative session, building momentum for subsequent sessions. Advocates have consistently demonstrated an ability to turn a meaningful number of Democratic legislators to publicly express support, despite intense opposition. In general, the di


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix C.docx ---
Appendix C: Scorecard and Exemplars
Charter Sector Scorecard
The objective of the scorecard on slide 5 is to define the attributes of an exemplar charter sector in order to track progress and make strategic decisions. We believe an exemplar charter sector is one that has high-quality, financially sustainable schools reaching diverse populations, and developing innovative solutions that can be replicated beyond the charter sector by influencing the overall ecosystem. Thus, on the left panel, we identify necessary Academic Quality, Financial Sustainability, Access & Equity, Innovation, and Influence on Ecosystem Outcomes that we can routinely track Washington State’s (WA) progress against. On the right panel, we identify key Enablers, spanning financial, organizational and ecosystem supports, that can potentially influence these Outcomes. Understanding these Enablers allows us to diagnose current issues with the sector and update our strategy. For example, the WA scorecard shows poor outcomes for Financial Sustainability that are explained by the limited financial support present in the sector. Thus, our strategy is focused proving startup capital and ongoing operations support to schools and advocating for additional state funding.
The scorecard indicators are results for the WA charter sector and thresholds are defined based on national frameworks and exemplar research.  As indicators for Quantitative Outcomes are diverse, threshold definitions for Excellent, Acceptable and Limited vary as well. For example, in exemplar states, 30-40 percent of charter schools perform better than the district on reading and math. Thus, our threshold for Excellent for Academic Quality indicators 1 and 2 are defined at 50% or higher. Similarly, the threshold for Excellent for Access & Equity achievement growth indicators 9-12 is 30 percent or higher because exemplars show similar outcomes. For Qualitative Outcomes, we rely entirely on qualitative evidence given the difficulty measuring Innovation and Influence. For Enablers, we rely on a mix of quantitative and qualitative evidence. For example, to rate Technical Assistance we rely on a qualitative assessment of the services provided, especially by the Charter Support Organization, in the sector. For Parent Demand, we rely on waitlist data and public polling results.
The scorecard is limited by a reliance on qualitative assessments for some Outcome and Enabler categories and lack of data systems to routinely collect necessary data. Our investment in the CREDO report and our ability to access school financial statements provides us with robust data to inform Outcome indicators. We also use data reported by WA Charters to measure Enablers. However, Outcome categories like Innovation and Influence on Ecosystem and Enabler categories like Accountability System are defined qualitatively as it challenging to find robust, quantitative data to measure them. We continue to include them in the scorecard as they are important attributes of an exemplar sector and a qualitative assessment can still provide a reasonable indicator of progress on them. Additionally, because the WA charter sector is young and small, its scorecard results, especially on Academic Quality and Access & Equity, are highly variable and are expected to shift significantly as existing schools build out and new schools enter the sector. 
Exemplars
While there is significant charter activity underway in most states, no state stands out as a clear exemplar in all outcome areas. California (CA), Colorado (CO), Massachusetts (MA), and Texas (TX) are a few states that stand out against specific scorecard outcomes.  We also see exemplar activity in relation to specific outcomes in urban locales such as Boston and Denver. 
Our primary references for Academic Quality and Access & Equity indicators are state-level CREDO reports. Given the lack of access to school-level financial data outside of WA (and the variable nature of education funding env


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix D.docx ---
Appendix D: CREDO Summary
Overview
In January 2019, the Center for Research on Education Outcomes (CREDO) at Stanford University released an analysis of Washington State charter schools’ student performance from the 2014-15 school year through 2016-17. Given the early state of the sector, this report was intended primarily as a baseline for subsequent CREDO studies. Using student-level demographics and test results, CREDO evaluated learning gains in charter schools against traditional public schools (TPS). 
Methodology
CREDO’s primary methodological component is the virtual control record (VCR), commonly referred to as a “virtual twin.” To isolate the effect of attending a specific school on student learning gains on the state standardized Smarter Balanced Assessment (SBA) and regular end-of-course exams (e.g., Algebra I, Geometry I, Algebra II), CREDO matches each charter student with a TPS virtual twin using similar demographic characteristics and past test performance. These matched pairs are then pooled into a regression using grade, demographics, previous test scores, and other metrics.
Results
Overall, charter schools showed promising but mixed results on quality. 
Math: Of five charter schools, two significantly outperformed TPS, two were not significantly different, and one significantly underperformed.
Reading: Of seven charter schools, four significantly outperformed TPS, two were not significantly different, and one significantly underperformed.
For the overall sector, CREDO found “no statistically different academic growth in reading and math” compared to TPS.
In both subject areas, Rainier Prep significantly outperformed TPS and Destiny significantly underperformed.
English language learner charter students significantly outperformed TPS in both reading and math gains. Black and Hispanic charter students had improved but statistically insignificant math gains over TPS. 
Caveats and Assumptions
As anticipated by CREDO given the early state of the sector, the small number of schools and available school-years hinder the stability of the results. For example, one of the highest rated schools in the CREDO report backslid significantly on its 2017-18 Student Growth Percentile (SGP), while one of the lower-performing schools in the CREDO report improved by a similar margin.. Another issue is the grade-level constraints of the SBA, which is administered in grades 3-8 and 10. Because of this, elementary schools are not able to be included in the study until they reach grade 4 and the results of high schools, two of which were the highest-scorers in reading gains, may have survival bias due to the two-year testing gap. (To contextualize potential survival bias issues, particularly in schools serving 10th grade, CREDO will supplement future reports with student attrition data and consider adding an intent-to-treat analysis.)
Next Steps
Student Growth Data Sources
Going forward, we will continue to use CREDO as an annual sector-level performance report. Because it controls for student characteristics, it is a rigorous and valuable comparison. It also offers national benchmarks to other charter sectors, which we anticipate being valuable over time. CREDO helps us to answer the question, “is the WA charter sector performing better than the traditional districts?”
We will monitor the annual SGP results because they are relatively timely and used as the standard metric for student growth in most state-level reports. In addition, while MAP Growth is optional and isn’t reported by school districts for direct comparison, its comprehensive grade coverage is a major benefit to evaluating elementary and high schools. We will continue to monitor this as well, since the schools themselves use this data most frequently.  SGP and MAP help us answer the question, “is a given school improving year over year? And are individual students improving?” 


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix E.docx ---
Appendix E: Analysis of WA Charter School Financial Sustainability
Since the McCleary fix passed in 2017, the WASCI team has been tracking self-reported school operating deficits across the sector. The impact of McCleary was promising for schools and post-McCleary financials showed five of 12 schools projecting financial sustainability, with the largest operating deficit at $1,700 per pupil. However, when schools submitted contingency budgets after the start of the 2018/19 school year (SY), there were large variances from previous year projections, primarily driven by missed enrollment targets for some schools and sector-wide increases in teacher salaries. Given the fluctuation in projections, we recognized the need to tighten our assumptions, verify data sources, and perform sensitivity analyses. Based on these, we calculate the current and projected operating deficits on slide 7.
Thus, only one out of 12 schools is projecting financial sustainability. While the proximate causes for the recent decline are missed enrollment and teacher salary increases, we see variation in financial sustainability across the sector that can be explained by three key drivers:
We unpack these drivers further to illustrate both the variety and complexity of factors associated with charter school sustainability.
Choice of school model
School operator and geographic location: Schools receive different levels of funding depending on the grades served and operating location. Elementary schools and schools located in the greater Seattle area receive higher state funding of $1,400 and $1,300 per pupil on average, respectively. However, it is less expensive to operate outside of the greater Seattle and Tacoma regions, with costs lower by $2,600 per pupil on average. Thus, choosing what grades to serve and where has a meaningful impact on how close to sustainable the school can be.
School size and enrollment: Another key factor is school size, which is determined by the number of students a school either chooses or succeeds in enrolling. Higher enrollment results in fixed costs being spread across more students. School that are closest to sustainability are fully enrolled with greater than 500 pupils (Atlas, Impact, SIA and Pride Prep). Rainier Prep is an interesting example of a school that is fully enrolled at 337 students but is still facing a ~$2,300-$3,300 shortfall in operating income because its facility cannot accommodate any more students and renovations are not permissible. Sierra is also an interesting example in that they are near full enrollment, but experiencing revenue losses due to Running Start participation, which effectively lowers their measurable enrollment for state revenue. Thus, although we have limited data to test this hypothesis, 500 students may be a critical threshold in WA to reach financial sustainability. This is in line with data from Charter School Growth Fund—they recommend a school size of 500 or more unless it is a high funding market. 
Underlying financial and operating assumptions: In WA, there are three type of schools based on tenure and type of management that share financial and operating assumptions.
National Charter Management Organizations (Summit, Green Dot): These schools opened between 2015 and 2018 and share similar sustainability challenges. Both Green Dot and Summit were recruited to bring their California models to WA. Both charter management organizations (CMOs) based their initial models on CA, but it has become clear that WA is different in terms of per-pupil funding and parent awareness/demand. They have since both struggled to adjust to these conditions (for example, they have high administrative overhead costs from initial plans for larger networks in WA). This is a key driver to their specific sustainability challenges. Enrollment remains a major challenge at all Green Dot schools and Summit Olympus. 
Old Independents (PRIDE Prep, Rainier Prep, SIA): These three independent schools were part the


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix F.docx ---
Appendix F: District and Other State Financial Benchmarks
As Appendix E discusses, charters in WA have been struggling with financial sustainability. However, if we look across the sector and at other states, an emerging hypothesis is that charters in WA are just under the school size needed to be successful in a tightly-funded sector. Key drivers of differences are 1) access to higher levels of per-pupil revenue and 2) lower percentages of “other” expenses, which are primarily fixed costs, e.g. commission fees, that schools can spread across larger student populations. 
Public education finance 101
Public education finance in WA is similar to what we see across the country: 
On the revenue side, WA public schools (districts and charters) receive a combination of state, federal, and local funding to cover expenses. Some schools also privately fundraise for supplemental programming. On average public schools in WA receive $12,900 of per-pupil funding to cover basic education costs (slightly lower than the national average of $13,900). State funding is driven by enrollment, region and student need. Federal funding is special purpose and generally program specific and reimbursement driven. Local funding varies depending on access to local levies and fundraising initiatives. 
On the expense side, public schools use per-pupil funding to cover the same general categories of expenses: teacher salaries and benefits, student services, and administrative/overhead expenses. However, traditional public schools (TPS) may aggregate some fixed costs (for example facilities costs such as lease, debt service, and/or maintenance costs) at the district level and may receive additional state funding support for these. 
Shifting funding landscape in WA
The landscape for state funding of public education in WA has shifted over the past two years. The waterfall chart below summarizes these changes for CMOs.
McCleary Impact: Districts’ over reliance on local levy funding created inequitable funding across districts (at the time, they raised on average 34 percent of total funding from local levies). The McCleary lawsuit attempted to reduce inequity and resulted in a change to the state funding formula which allocated more dollars through the state’s basic education rate (an average increase of $1,900 for all Washington charter schools, $2,100 for CMOs). In return, the levy authority was capped at $2,500 per pupil for traditional public schools (TPSs), decreasing the TPS-charter funding gap to ~13 percent or ~$1,700 less per-pupil revenue for charters. However, in 2017, McCleary gains for charter schools were offset by: higher than average special education populations, resulting in unfunded expenses; missed enrollment targets, resulting in fixed costs spread across less students; and higher than funded transportation expenses. 
SpEd Revenue Changes: Since McCleary, all schools received a minor increase in special education funding in 2018, and again in 2019, amounting to $60 total per pupil.
Teacher Salary Increases: Additionally, since McCleary, teachers’ unions have negotiated new teacher contracts with higher salaries, causing large funding gaps to materialize for TPS. These gaps are similarly felt by charters, who responded to these raises with salary increases of their own. TPS argue that the increased state funding is not fully covering their losses from levies, and thus, the 2019 legislative session saw the approval of a levy lid lift for TPS, providing them with some relief. Unfortunately, for charters, this widens the gap in district-charter funding. 
Benchmarks- Expenses as % of Revenue
Emerging hypothesis: New WA charters need >500 school size for financial sustainability
A preliminary analysis of charters in WA with comparison TPS, shows that charters have lower per pupil revenue and higher per pupil costs. 
 As explained in the call out box, on the revenue side, differences in local levy funding drive funding gaps between TPS and charters

--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix H.docx ---
Appendix H – CMO Challenges and Path Forward 
We invest in seven charter management organization (CMO) schools in the Seattle-Tacoma region. Currently, all seven face issues with financial sustainability and one school in the Green Dot (GD) network is facing issues with quality.
Financial issues
All CMO schools are projected to be financially unsustainable in school year (SY) 22-23. GD currently operates four schools, serving approximately 600 students and Summit operates three schools, serving 900 students. Both CMOs are significantly below their respective targets of 1,475 and 1,267 students they intended to serve by this time. They are therefore not yet covering costs, and current projections indicate they will still be running operating gaps of $500-$3,900 per pupil in SY 22-23 (totaling between $6M and $10M/year for the WA networks) even at full enrollment. (See slide 7)
There are multiple cost and revenue drivers at play, as shown in the example for Summit Sierra on slide 8. The key cost drivers are:
Staff salaries & benefits: Teacher salaries have increased by up to 14 percent for all WA schools after a long period of stagnation. 
Special education (SpEd) costs: SpEd students make up 11-23 percent of all students at charter schools in WA, and 13.75-23 percent at CMOs. Since the state caps the amount of SpEd funding at 13.5 percent of total enrollment, this results in significant funding gaps (~11-21 percent of operating income).
Facilities expenses: Given low enrollment, fixed facilities costs are carried by fewer pupils (~10 percent of expenses). 
Network fees: CMO network fees are an additional fixed cost (~10 percent of expenses). 
On the revenue side, key drivers are:
Policy fixes: The state’s policies and process for SpEd reimbursement are woefully inadequate. Potential policy fixes, e.g. levy equalization, could be a significant benefit here. 
Enrollment: Student enrollment continues to miss targets, and for GD Destiny, decline. 
Quality issues
CREDO and supplementary data show results that are average to slightly promising for Summit but mixed for GD. We have a first baseline report from CREDO showing performance of students at WA charter schools as compared to their district counterparts. As CREDO uses SY 2016-17 data, its sample is limited to two of three Summit schools and two of four GD schools. We also have supplemental data from SY 2017-18 from MAP Growth and SBA/SGP testing that provide more current context, as well as initial information for Summit Atlas and GD RVLA. Overall, we expect fluctuations in outcomes due to changes in student enrollment patterns and school quality as schools build out and mature. GD Destiny, however, is not following a normal trend and is seeing declining enrollment.
While the CREDO results are preliminary, Summit’s two high schools showed high quality in reading. However, while Summit Sierra and Olympus’ MAP Growth scores for SY 2016-17 generally align with CREDO’s positive findings, their MAP Growth results in SY 2017-18 showed significant drops in student growth, with 10-20 percent declines in the percentage of students who met their growth targets, falling close to national averages. Summit Atlas’s MAP Growth and SGP results, while limited to one year of data, are at the top of the charter sector in both ELA and math student growth. 
The CREDO report found that GD performed worse than traditional public schools (TPS); however, the most recent MAP Growth and SGP results show improvement. In the CREDO report, GD Destiny showed the lowest performance of all charter schools in WA, and GD Excel showed positive results in reading only. However, if their rate of improvement on MAP Growth and SGP continue over the next few years, these two schools may catch up to district averages. RVLA, while limited to one year of data, showed the third-highest math learning gains but below-average ELA learning gains.
Path forward
The path forward for CMO support includes improving financial susta


--- 052219 WA Charter School Initiative Progress Review Appendix I.docx ---
Appendix I: Student Recruitment and Enrollment Pilot Update 
Helping charter schools achieve full enrollment is a vital step to institutionalize current players and pave the way for additional schools. Lack of awareness, misperception and simply understanding the application process have been headwinds on school enrollment. To that end Gates Ventures and the foundation are collaborating with Summit and Green Dot to test how outreach and recruiting via digital media can help “widen the funnel” on lead generation and increase applications to schools. Our hypothesis is that the costs to increase enrollment are far less than the revenue generated by additional student enrollment. Increasing enrollment by one student attracts an additional $10,000-$12,000 in total revenue. However, there are subsequent cost impacts, excluding marketing, between $4,000 and $8,000. Assuming no additional staff needs, each additional pupil results in a net impact between $2,000 and $8,000.  
Our goal is both to support schools to positively impact enrollment numbers by delivering high-quality viable leads, determine support needed for schools to “close” on those leads to convert from application to enrollment, and develop a playbook or replicable model that other schools can adopt. We know more about lead generation at this stage than we do about conversion, which is the ultimate goal. While the final results of the pilot are still pending, there are encouraging signs: 
Direct media costs to land a qualified lead are coming in <$50 per which is relatively low cost, suggesting that digital can and should play a part of each school’s and network’s recruiting mix and can be increased as needed relatively quickly.
Fully burdened acquisition costs per lead, including core positioning, messaging development, back-end systems support, is coming in <$300 which is initially daunting but many of these costs are one-time start up fees that should be amortized over the longer campaign. 
As an example of continued supports for processes and structures to support immediate next steps after lead generation, additional funding could support schools needing core customer relationship management (CRM) systems and staff.Most schools are not resourced for this, but we see an opportunity to support a shared resources model through WA Charters that would reduce the capacity required by an individual school. Given that none of the schools are directly competitive, centralizing many of these functions, even to human outreach on behalf of schools, could be a highly efficient and impactful way to help the sector. 
Wave 1 – Summer 2018 (complete)
Wave 2 – Spring 2019 (in process)
To immediately apply learnings and position future activities, Gates Ventures is narrowing down to the best strategies in which to double-down on lead generation and conversion at select schools in the pilot to impact the SY19-20 recruitment season. Conversion approaches will be modeled on successful strategies at current schools like Impact Public Schools’ Community Outreach Ambassador program, centered on engaging parents in long-term, sustainable community outreach, which has resulted in wait lists at Impact Public Schools in Tukwila. We plan to invest an additional $125K to increase strategies on both lead generation for SY19-20 (e.g. deepening content, continuing digital media, enhancing collateral, supporting events, increasing translation materials) and to protype increased conversion activities (e.g. building out engagement support by hiring recruiters, and increasing staff capacity for planning and prioritization for conversion) for upcoming recruitment seasons.
WAVE 3 – TBD
Enrollment results from the Wave 2 pilot will inform our plans for Wave 3.  We expect to have final enrollment data by the beginning of October 2019, and we will finalize plans then. Our hypothesis is that the costs of a more robust digital marketing infrastructure will be significantly less than the costs we are currently


--- 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix F _Theory of Scale.docx ---
Our approach to scale (slide 10): We will invest against two pathways to impact-at-scale. The first pathway focuses on integrating known scaling mechanisms into our regional partnerships to expand progress within and across regions. The second pathway is pursuing statewide policies to unlock public funding, sector coordination, and technical guidance for solutions, particularly related to FAFSA and dual credit. We believe this not only drives scale opportunistically in the short-term but sets the scaling conditions in regions from the start to create resilient scaling. We will incorporate metrics to capture early evidence on our scaling theory so we can adjust when necessary to stay on track with our goals. 
Pathway A: Integrating known scaling mechanisms into regional bets: Evidence suggests that regional bright spots in WA scale to other regions through 3 main mechanisms, in order of reliability: statewide agency expansion of regional bright spots; multi-region networks (e.g., Career Connect WA) scaling bright spots across their networks; or PS institutions influencing other PS institutions (slide 11). This mechanism will spread solutions to other regions through investing in knowledge mobilization, building implementation capacity in intermediaries that can take solutions to scale, and informing policy based on learnings from regional successes. This approach enables local adaptation creating sustainable solutions and closing equity gaps but may be slower than state-level reform and result in slower student-level impact in focus regions by ~3-4 years. We will consider which of the 3 mechanisms on slide 11 are relevant for each regional investment, since regional capabilities, connections to legislative champions, and presence of network intermediaries vary. 
Pathway B: Pursue state-wide reform informed by national exemplars: The second lever of scale is state-wide policy reform (slide 12). This pathway focuses on advocating for policy reforms known to drive PS transition from national exemplars in FAFSA completion (like in TN and LA) and dual credit completion (like in CO). This approach is best used for addressing systemwide challenges across WA with potential for scale in the first 4 years. Focusing on increasing PS enrollment and credential attainment continues to be a priority for policymakers, advocates, and business. For example, the legislature passed bills related to increasing financial supports to students (Senate Bill 5789) and to expanding the data reported on dual credit programs to include indicators such as awarding of HS and PS credits (House Bill 1867). The Foundation’s role can include informing and supporting the acceleration of policy reforms already receiving support by advocates and/or being considered by policymakers. 
Our scaling assumptions have significant implications for our regional selection. While some challenges are common statewide, many are specific to regional characteristics, including student mix and geography (e.g., urban vs. rural). Bright spots tailored to these characteristics are more likely to scale to regions with similar characteristics. We will therefore take a portfolio approach to regional selection, and ensure the portfolio represents broad geographic reach / type and is inclusive of our focus students. 


--- 09152022_WSI Strategy Approval _ Appendix G _Risk and Mitigation Strategy.docx ---
Associated risks and mitigation strategy
As we move forward, we have recognized and considered risks along with a plan to mitigate them:
Washington Charters – there have been significant questions raised recently about the WA charter sector’s accountability measures and issues at the two largest networks that serve almost half of charters students. While we believe the sector still holds promise, these real concerns will have implications for our WA Charters strategy and create reputational and advocacy risks for WSI. While charters are separate strategically from our strategy, external partners and the community don’t view it this way. We see three risks: 1) a risk to the WA State strategy policy goals because of the potential for legislators’ focus to be taken up on charters policy issues 2) reputational risk to the foundation and WSI given the over-sized funding we provide to the sector and the amount we have allocated to Charters in WA over this 4-year period ($76.2M) compared to WSI ($72.5M) and; 3) risk to both strategies and the effectiveness of the education system long-term if stakeholders continue to see Charters and the K-12 systems as competitors. We will continue to work closely with the US Charters and communications team to ensure a unified narrative and coherent policy and advocacy strategy as we support all facets of the education system in WA. 
Washington Student Achievement Council (WSAC) Challenge Grant –WSAC is taking a near identical approach to increasing PS enrollment via regional strengthening, identification, and approach. We are considering what a partnership might look like with WSAC. Partnership with WSAC has a history of poor follow-through which might create execution risks for us, but not partnering could result in confusion and overlapping funding in the field. We are discussing with WSAC leadership the potential to collaborate in regions to mitigate these risks and create more opportunity.
BMGF “big-footing around” in communities that do not have high degrees of trust in us- We heard through our stakeholder engagement process that there are some negative perceptions of BMGF and stakeholders expressed a desire for the us to engage in more inclusive, open, and relationship-focused ways. ​Our place-based approach has resulted in choices in both our strategy and execution processes. For example, we are committed to integrating feedback mechanisms and in our execution model and to use a co-convener model (especially with funders). 
Leadership turnover- All levels of education are seeing higher turnover in leadership.  Sixty five out of the 296 superintendents are new to their jobs, compared to the average of 40 per year.  Similarly, higher education has seen new leaders emerge in the 2-year system, a quarter of the 4-year public system, and 2 new agency leads at the state level.  While new leadership can bring fresh perspective and innovation, WA also runs the risk of becoming increasingly fragmented as we work to build new relationships and new leaders assert their new vision.
Impact of COVID-19 on learning loss- A COVID impact report released by WSAC in 2021 showed a 42% increase in students receiving a F grade, no credit, or an incomplete.  According to Brookings Institute research from January 2022, math and reading assessment scores experienced a drop much greater during COVID-19 than after other large scale education disruptions like Hurricane Katrina.  There is also concern from education leaders in WA about the likely funding cliff after receiving an influx of federal support and the ability to continue interventions instituted over the past 2 years.

--- USP Data Investment Narrative 10062022.docx ---
USP Data 2022 Strategy Review | FY23-26 Investment Narrative
The system-generated Investment Plan accounts for the proposed 4-year outlook ($72.1M) for the USP Data Strategy. The table below includes additional funding envelopes from the Postsecondary Success ($36M) and Economic Mobility & Opportunity ($7.7M) teams which the USP Data team manages for data investments in support of those USP teams’ goals in addition to our own. These envelopes account for approximately 38% of our total investment portfolio ($115.9M) and provide a more accurate depiction of relative weighting of resource allocations. 
As our strategy and collaborations have grown over the past two years, we have adjusted our operational processes to better manage and communicate any shifts in our resource plan. We have restructured our portfolio utilizing the Group function in DSA, a change from our 2022-25 Resource Plan that housed our programmatic bodies of work into a single “USP Data” group.
2023-2026 Resource Plan- Not Including Proposed Incremental Requests 
Strategic Shifts
There are no significant strategic resource shifts being proposed compared to last year’s annual planning submission. We have made one small change to our BOW structure to add clear BOW Groups to manage the work more effectively. You will see that small change represented in our Investment Plan but does not change the financial allocations across individual BOWs. Lastly, we will be submitting two incremental requests as a part of the Annual Planning process which, if approved, would result in a net portfolio increase of ~$20M for 2023-24. These additional dollars will produce key deliverables that will unlock the next iteration of our strategy in 2024. 
Accelerating Innovation: As we look to pivot the Innovate with R&D learning BOW to execution, we believe we can strengthen the education-to-workforce ecosystem by investing an additional ~$17M over the course of two years (2023-24) plus the ~$3M in available headroom. We hypothesize that increased investments in improving interoperability, privacy and security, and quality of longitudinal data will improve learner mobility and interventions at key moments of transition to equitably improve outcomes for Black and Latino learners and learners from low-income backgrounds. The proposed investment plan is actively being refined based on leadership feedback and will be submitted as a part of the Annual Planning process.  
Leveraging Cross-Cutting Enablers. The E-W indicators framework launched in early 2022 and has been well-received both internally and externally, creating demand for additional dissemination and implementation support beyond the initial funding scoped ($2.5M) as part of the 2020 USP Data strategy ask. Given USLT’s desire to prioritize the framework across the division, we recently launched a cross-division working group to fully scope the next phase of this work and an investment plan proposal for Annual Planning, which will likely increase the more modest incremental request ($3M over two years) we scoped earlier this year.


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion USP Data Strategy Proposal Memo.docx ---
OVERVIEW
One of the most vexing challenges in US education is that decisions are not consistently driven by data and analysis, with our system struggling to understand drivers of performance and to identify exemplars to inform policy and practice solutions that enable scaled improvements in student outcomes. There are major gaps in the current education-to-workforce data ecosystem that constrain effective use: disconnected, incomplete, and outdated systems; privacy concerns that chill access; and difficulty translating data into actionable evidence and use. The USP Data Strategy will focus on addressing critical gaps by investing in a limited set of levers and places where we can play a unique role. Our goal is to drive improvements that will provide practitioners and policymakers with the information they need to answer critical questions about student progress and to implement evidence-based reforms that equitably improve education outcomes, especially for Black, Latinx, and low-income students and other underserved populations. Our strategy is built around several consequential assumptions:
Data should reflect all students’ experiences in the education system: The gaps in the current education data ecosystem provide an inaccurate and incomplete rendering of students’ experiences, especially about students of color and low-income students whose outcomes are obscured when data are not disaggregated, leaving equity gaps ignored.  
Scaled data systems are critical to scaling education reform: Solving “the data problem” within rather than across our strategies leads to bespoke solutions that don’t scale across the education pipeline and fail to address the transition points that are often critical gaps for students. An integrated point of view and plan for improving and scaling data systems and their use is critical to the success of our reform efforts across USP. 
Improvements in education data must help the field answer a set of “critical questions” about system performance: The field is starting to converge around key indicators that are predictive of student success along the education-to-workforce pipeline such as 9th grade on track, college readiness, and postsecondary success (see Appendix A for our draft framework). Aligning improvements in data systems to these measures will focus the field on tracking and disaggregating performance to identify and address equity gaps.
States can demonstrate the value of the full education-to-workforce data ecosystem in place: Statewide P20W systems connect data from across the education pipeline and are managed by state agencies accountable for education policy and funding. States are also key adopters of national postsecondary and workforce data systems tracking students and workers across state lines, and support local data use by schools and districts.
Private industry partnerships and platforms are important for innovation and scale with public education data systems: States need industry-grade technology (e.g., cloud-based systems with data lakes) and privacy techniques, and we can best improve data in the public sector by leveraging the technical capacity and scale of private providers in the market.
An ecosystem approach is necessary to enable the data feedback loop in education: Building tools and upgrading technology is not enough to drive adoption. We need to holistically address the problem by:
Channeling unmet need for data from key stakeholders by engaging them as partners to improve data systems to help solve real-world problems in education, particularly for students of color and low-income students (Demand).
Modernizing data systems to ensure comprehensive, interoperable infrastructure is available to readily connect supply and demand for data from stakeholders (Systems).
Enabling open yet secure access to data by lowering impedance and cost while rigorously preserving privacy and security (Governance).
Facilitating adoption of data systems through analytical tools and 


--- USP Data Strategy Review Memo 10062022.docx ---
INTRODUCTION
The Data strategy aims to enable work across U.S. Programs by modernizing and using education-to-workforce (E-W) data systems to identify and close disparities so that race and socioeconomic status are no longer predictors of education attainment and economic mobility. Our vision for a national E-W data ecosystem includes localities using real-time, interoperable data linked with longitudinal outcomes to support students; states linking data across education, workforce, and social service systems to track student progress against key milestones and monitor system conditions that help or hinder success; and national postsecondary-to-workforce systems that measure whether all students earn credentials of value that lead to economic mobility. But the current ecosystem has critical gaps that we are addressing through our theory of change to build DEMAND from key stakeholders for improved SYSTEMS and to establish GOVERNANCE structures that enable policymakers, researchers, and practitioners to access and USE data to improve education programs and outcomes. 
To ensure data systems are fit to this purpose, the field must be aligned on which data are most critical for systems to collect and report so policymakers, educators, researchers, and students and parents can answer essential questions about students’ progress along their E-W journeys and understand the system conditions that promote success. As shared with leadership this spring, we engaged with experts across the country to develop and release an Education-to-Workforce Indicators Framework that identifies those essential questions and the metrics necessary to answer them (Slide 7). The framework provides a foundation for work across our theory of action (Slide 4) to scale national postsecondary-to-workforce data systems, build statewide P20W data system exemplars, and innovate with R&D to unlock longitudinal data availability and use. State agencies in Texas and Washington are eager to map the indicators to their existing data systems to identify gaps, which we will support in follow-up grants. The E-W Framework also informs our collaborations with other USP teams to develop data infrastructure and tools that allow their partners to use these metrics to assess and improve outcomes. For example, with the Pathways team, we examined whether our focus states collect and publicly provide transition-related “momentum metrics” so we could understand strengths and gaps in data collection and quality to help us determine the best leverage points for engaging states (See Slide 7 for more use cases). The Data team will partner with every USP team over the next two years to align these metrics to internal MLE plans and select external projects.
Our objectives in this first strategy review are to update leadership on our progress, share examples of how data made more accessible and usable through our investments have been used to improve decision-making, and highlight next steps to operationalize the E-W Indicators Framework.
SUMMARY OF STRATEGY PROGRESS
Overall, our strategy is largely meeting expectations (Slide 6), but our state-focused work to modernize data systems and enable use is slightly below expectations, given delays with state agencies due to competing COVID priorities and an increasingly challenging political environment. We recognize that work in states, particularly those where we take a holistic approach like Texas, require a longer-term view, so remain cautiously optimistic that as we continue to build trust and show incremental wins, state agencies will continue to advance the work. Our vision remains that progress in our partner states (e.g., TX, CA, WA) will motivate action by others by providing modernization roadmaps in partnership with private sector providers including Google and Microsoft and demonstrating actionable use through research portals and data visualizations. We describe our progress below, highlighting work with significant learning, prog


--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update Memo.docx ---
USP Data Strategy Update Memo
I. Overview
Artificial Intelligence is rapidly reshaping the role of education in providing pathways to prosperity. AI is simultaneously transforming how we learn by scaling personalized instruction and advising, while upending the relative value of what we learn by changing the fundamental economics of knowledge work. Popular perception of AI in education is split between panacea and plague, but most agree that if left to the market alone, both the gains and losses will be large and unevenly distributed. As we’ve discussed in recent meetings and learning sessions, we have a strengthening conviction in USP that under the right conditions, AI presents a penicillin moment for education – a technology capable of giving every student personalized support and reducing historical disparities by doubling learning rates for the bottom quartile of students. To help realize this future, USP is prioritizing AI-enabled solutions across its portfolio, with the goal of reaching transformational impact on educational outcomes within the next 20 years. This AI-centric shift also requires a fundamental rethinking of the role of a highly technical functional team capable of meeting the needs of the division and accelerating our impact in the field. While the USP Data strategy has made significant progress in building the data infrastructure to support U.S. education-to-workforce pathways, continuing a strategy centered on administrative data would be out of alignment with the evolving needs of the division, and risks missing the critical window of opportunity to bend the arc of AI development toward improved learning and life outcomes for our nation’s most underserved kids.
To meet this moment, we are evolving to an integrated Data & AI Strategy focused on accelerating and scaling AI-enabled solutions across the division through public goods infrastructure, trusted evidence, and faster feedback loops. This updated strategy positions us to support near-term divisional priorities, while opening new possibilities for transformational impacts and scale across PSTs beyond 2030. We have anchored to Bill’s vision: that all learners – especially those historically underserved – have access to a lifelong AI companion that provides personalized, trustworthy guidance and support across institutions and time, helping learners achieve their full potential. By investing in core public goods infrastructure for AI agents, building trusted evidence of AI efficacy and safety, speeding up system feedback loops direct to practitioners, and coordinating with hyperscalers, this strategy will position the division to design, scale, and sustain the most effective, equitable, and holistic AI-enabled solutions in instruction, tutoring, and navigation – supporting learners continuously across their educational journeys.
To achieve this vision, we will shift from general data ecosystem-building to catalytic investments and action across three pillars and three cross-cutting enablers. The pillars are:
Enable AI Solutions for Learning and Navigation: Investing in open, portable agentic infrastructure and standards powering PST solutions so AI systems can retain and apply learner context across time, models, and institutions – replicating the best of human instruction, tutoring, and navigation.
Build Trusted Evidence for AI in Education: Establishing rigorous, pre-market evaluation standards, benchmarks, and simulated testing environments to ensure AI-enabled tools are effective, equitable, and safe before reaching classrooms, informing PST investments and accelerating adoption of the most effective solutions.
Accelerate System Feedback Loops: Harnessing AI-native data systems for more rapid transformative changes in education by cutting the time-to-insight by half for districts and postsecondary institutions and enabling cross-sector understanding of longitudinal outcomes for better regional coordination.
These cross-cutting enablers will under


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Cover Note and Acronym List.docx ---
Dear Bill,
We look forward to speaking with you about the USP Data Strategy on October 20, 2020. In this meeting we will share our proposed strategy, answer your questions about our recommendations, and seek your approval to begin final planning and start execution.
This meeting follows our discussion of the topic in June where we aligned on the strategy direction and next steps to clarify the strategy’s design and anticipated time to impact. At that meeting, you gave us your support to proceed. Since then, we have developed a clearer point-of-view on how industry-grade technology can be applied to solve problems in the public sector through state P20W upgrades as well as research and development projects to unlock data in SIS/LMS platforms. We also have a better-articulated theory of action describing how our state-specific investments in Texas would complement necessary work on national postsecondary and workforce data systems, and how we can use our role to build broader alignment in the field around a shared agenda to improve the education-to-workforce data ecosystem in the US. Finally, we have anchored in on the Critical Questions framework (Appendix A) so that our collective work is accountable to answering key education system performance questions that are necessary to improve outcomes. 
As such, our proposed strategy consists of six investment areas over the next three years: Investment Area 1 focuses on developing an exemplar statewide P20W data ecosystem to demonstrate core use cases, learn to aid scaling, and spur a healthy market of private-sector players with a focus on TX. Investment Areas 2 and 3 complement the state work by supporting the development and use of data systems at other levels, national or multi-state data systems and local data in SIS/LMS systems, respectively. Investment Area 4 extends the work in the first three areas by developing public goods to support replication and scale. Investment Area 5 consists of the policy, advocacy, and communication work that is critical to enabling the strategy and Investment Area 6 focuses on building strategic partnerships with philanthropy and industry. With this strategy, we aim to address major gaps in the education-to-workforce data ecosystem in the US so practitioners and policymakers have the information they need to answer critical questions to implement evidence-based reforms to equitably improve education outcomes. We prepared three case studies to show how our priority investment areas will come together concretely and how our investments will drive that work. You will find these in Appendices B, C, and D.
You will see that we are recommending that we invest $60M in this new portfolio over the coming three-year period. These resources will be allocated from USP budget holds that I have created for strategic shifts to ensure best and highest use of Foundation resources across our USP portfolios during this period of unprecedented and dynamic change in US Education. We will also reallocate the needed FTEs out of existing division headcount. Finally, we have worked across the division to assure that these resources support our PST data spending and that the total approach is coherent and has the synergy we expect to deliver impact.
We recommend focusing your review of our documents on the memo, presentation deck, and appendices A-D. We propose the following agenda:
Introduction and regrounding in our goal, TOA, and TOC (5 minutes): Allan Golston and 
Jennifer Engle
Share our proposed approach for the strategy’s first three years (30 minutes): Jennifer Engle
Closing discussion (15 minutes): Allan Golston and Jennifer Engle
We look forward to discussing our proposal and engaging with your feedback. If we receive your approval, we will proceed with final planning, incorporate our proposed budget into this year’s annual planning process, and transition to full execution in Q1 2020. --- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update Cover Note.docx ---
Dear Bill and Mark,
I look forward to our September 17th meeting on USP Data, building on our earlier discussions and the June learning session. We are at a pivotal moment: AI is advancing faster than education systems can adapt, and the choices we make now will determine whether learners, especially those historically underserved, benefit equitably from these tools. 
In this session, we will present our proposed 2026-2030 USP Data Strategy for your input and approval. Our strategy is designed to seize this moment and position the foundation as a leader in shaping AI for learning, not simply reacting to market forces. 
We see transformational opportunities ahead: convening hyperscalers to influence the direction of open infrastructure, establishing trusted evaluation standards for AI in education, accelerating system feedback loops to cut time-to-insight, and creating the conditions for breakthrough solutions to scale equitably. By investing now in public-good infrastructure and trusted evidence, we can ensure AI accelerates – not fragments – the path to equitable learning outcomes.
We believe this strategy reflects the ambitions you’ve envisioned and that we’ve discussed over the past year. For learners, it supports both near-term choices – like a 9th grader setting aspirations, choosing courses, or staying motivated – and longer-term guidance that builds awareness, motivation, and identity over time. This is enabled by our work on context, memory, and trusted AI tools, and it’s reinforced through data feedback loops that support continuous improvement in practice and policy. Importantly, this strategy can get started quickly. By 2028, we expect to see clear signals on whether we are on track for scaled impact, along with a path toward even more transformational use cases in learning and navigation.
Your guidance at this stage is critical to sharpen our path forward. In our discussion, we hope to hear your direction on:
Alignment: Do you agree with our proposed path forward, and are you comfortable with us advancing these priorities within our core budget while the LDA request is pending?
Partners: Are there any additional partners (philanthropic, government, or tech) you believe we must bring to the table early to maximize our influence?
Clarity: Are there areas you would like us to expand on in a follow-up?
We look forward to your input on how to sharpen our strategy, accelerate action, and ensure we are positioned to lead in shaping AI for equitable learning outcomes.
Thank you,
Matt on behalf of the USP Data team

Introduced by Allan Golston, Matt Gee, Nicole Ifill, Vic Vuchic, and Natasha Fedo presented the updated USP Data strategy, outlining big bets for enabling AI solutions, building trusted evidence for AI in education, and accelerating system feedback loops.
