Below are key Chair reflections and takeaways: 
Overall reaction
Found the strategy direction exciting and encouraged the focus on memory, interoperability, and concrete learner-facing scenarios.
Expressed enthusiasm for the shift in USP Data’s agenda toward AI and upstream innovation, provided the team narrows in on the must-deliver use cases.
Standards and interoperability with AI: Acknowledged potential gaps in standards and interoperability issues in education; drew parallels to FHIR in health, asking whether AI needs rigid schemas or new frameworks for transcription and semantics. Intrigued and supportive and urged the team to sharpen the focus. The team explained that while rigid schemas proved effective in health, AI requires a different approach – emphasizing field-level aligned semantics rather than rigid structures. Aligned semantics both reduce LLM error rates and expand the range of tools that can be effectively exposed via MCP. In effect, a data standard acts as a form of compression: less context is needed because meaning is pre-aligned across parties, yielding the greatest gains for AI. This represents a departure from how standards work has traditionally been approached.
Learner memory: Asked whether memory & context focus would span all subject areas. The team clarified that while their work with standards bodies is subject-agnostic, the initial emphasis will be on math.  
Advising and career pathways: Raised concerns about for-profit companies monetizing AI-driven career advice. Suggested the foundation could play a role in ensuring college and career data remain publicly available, with value-add layers built on top of open AI data infrastructure.
Data investments: Noted that perfectly measuring outcomes (e.g., 8th grade achievement) and then waiting years for long-term impacts creates slow feedback cycles. Pushed for strategies that generate near-term impact and actionable data, while acknowledging the value of longer-term longitudinal data.
Common Core State Standards: Reflected on the potential to build continuity with past investments in the Common Core State Standards by embedding them into AI-enabled instruction and assessments. Suggested that aligning modules and components to these standards could extend the value of prior work while ensuring greater coherence across emerging AI tools.

Appendix A: Additional Details on Strategy Plays 
This document is intended to complement the memo and presentation deck by outlining additional approach and implementation details for strategy plays within the 2026-2030 USP Data Strategy. The team continues to develop further detail on these areas and others, and early progress is already underway. For details on costing, please see Appendix B: USP Data Incremental Request Memo. 
ENABLE AI SOLUTIONS FOR LEARNING	
Portable Memory & Context for AI-Enabled Learning
Description & Approach: We will invest in the foundational infrastructure that enables AI systems to retain and apply learner context over time, supporting more adaptive and personalized experiences. This includes core efforts: (1) developing definitions for the data model and structure for portable semantic memory (including skills, evidence, and instructional design), potentially through NSEs and CA Pathways (CareerLadder); and (2) demonstrating how linking curriculum-aligned knowledge graphs (e.g., CZI KG) to learning platforms (e.g., Kiddom, Khan using Illustrative Math) and discovering essential rulesets through R&D (e.g. optimal forgetting) can improve personalization and outcomes.
Based on our Medium Resourcing scenario, implementation will focus on 4 key components/phases: 
Develop structure for memory framework: Develop data specifications to support the building / scaling of learner context (e.g., ongoing investments at national scale enterprises like ASU, WGU; state/national system initiatives like CA CareerLadder with WestEd, C2C).
Test and validate: Fund the creation of ecosystem for portable semantic memory and context by engaging curriculum providers (e.g., Khan, Kiddom) to connect KGs (e.g., CZI's Illustrative Math) data to context; and prototyping memory rule templates in tutoring (e.g., K-12 Khanmigo) and advising tools (Pathways) with AI labs (e.g., Anthropic).
Create and integrate guardrails to keep learner data safe and age-appropriate: Co-develop open-source protocols and guardrails that can be built into LLMs and AI Agents to ensure that data governance requirements are embedded in solutions (e.g., age-based guardrails for ‘parental control mode’) in partnership with partners like Khanmigo and hyperscalers like Anthropic.
Create conditions to accelerate adoption of portable memory and context for learning: Support communities of AI developers, researchers, and grantees through open sandboxes and builder communities – spaces where new tools can be co-developed, tested, and improved together with corresponding guardrails. These environments will promote knowledge sharing, training, and collaboration around common infrastructure, accelerating progress across the field.
In the Medium scenario, impact and adoption would be limited to 1-2 prioritized use cases, to be determined (from: K-12 Instruction & Tutoring; Pathways Advising; or Credit Mobility). 
With additional funding (High Scenario), we would be able to drive adoption into solutions in all 3 priority use case markets (K-12 Instruction & Tutoring; Pathways Advising; Credit Mobility).
Sustainability Plan: We will develop domain-specific open-source memory structures and guardrails and build awareness through domain-aligned communities of practice (e.g., standards and OS communities).  Common infrastructure such as the structure for memory framework will be co-developed with hyperscalers and domain experts. We anticipate governance of this framework to transition to a consortium of institutional partners, including hyperscalers and builder communities, to maintain and drive awareness and use. Key anticipated milestones include:
By 2028: Research conducted, including minimum context for memory and onboarding, developed structure for memory framework, and identified critical guardrails needed and potential partners. With domain partners, prototype enriched solutions and test hypotheses about e.g. motivation and efficacy improvements. Testing may 


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix B.docx ---
Powering Regional P16W Initiatives with State Data: 
A Case Study from our Statewide Exemplars BOW
Overview
Over the next 3-5 years, we plan to work with Texas to develop its education-to-workforce data ecosystem into an exemplar. Texas already has a number of organizations making good use of state data in spite of current limitations, and we believe our planned investments will enable these organizations to dramatically improve their use of data by spending less time on data collection and cleaning and more time on building data capacity and literacy in partner schools. This appendix focuses on the Commit Partnership in Dallas (one of our P16 Community Investments) as an illustration, describing the data-related challenges they face in their work to get more Black, Latinx, and low-income students to and through postsecondary education, and how our anticipated investments to improve the P20W data system in Texas will address these challenges to accelerate their work. The Commit Partnership and similar regional efforts in Texas anchor their work to key metrics in our Critical Questions framework, especially outcome measures related to high school completion, college readiness, and postsecondary enrollment, progress, and completion. Commit works to equip K12 schools and postsecondary institutions with the data needed to drive improvement on these indicators in their region. 
How does the Commit Partnership Use Data?
Founded in 2011, Commit works with over 200 partners (K-12 schools, PS institutions, foundations, businesses, and nonprofits) to address systemic education challenges in North Texas, a region that educates more than 10% of Texas’s preK-12 enrollment. Commit’s work is focused across the education pipeline and organized through three main initiatives, including Dallas County Promise, a partnership of school districts, colleges and universities, and other regional stakeholders to increase postsecondary attainment. In its work, Commit makes use of a variety of individual-level and aggregate data for three primary purposes: (1) identifying and calling attention to equity and performance gaps, (2) supporting schools in improving their delivery, and (3) advocating for better education policy.
(1) Identifying and calling attention to equity and performance gaps: An important part of Commit’s work is aggregating and analyzing data from across the education pipeline to identify equity and performance gaps in schools and districts in North Texas, identifying where the education system falls short in serving Black, Latinx, and low-income students. Commit makes many of these findings public through a variety of tools and often shares more detailed analysis directly with school and district administrators. Commit’s public-facing tools include: an online dashboard of key student indicators, interactive data visualizations (e.g., school-level data on assessment performance vs. economically-disadvantaged student enrollment), and an annually published “scorecard” for Dallas County that tracks progress since 2011 on 11 indicators of student success that are well-aligned with our Critical Questions framework (e.g., Kindergarten readiness, 3rd grade reading proficiency, high school graduation, postsecondary enrollment). These tools are intended to enable community stakeholders and advocates to hold legislators and school leaders accountable for improvement, as well as to support school and district administrators with making those improvements, often at their request since limitations in state agency tools and their internal capacity means they often turn to Commit for this data and analysis.
To generate the data for these tools and insights, Commit pulls upwards of 20 separate publicly-available data files (housed in different virtual locations) from TEA and pairs this with postsecondary enrollment and completion data from National Student Clearinghouse reports that it must get directly from each school district because those data are n


--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update_Appendix B.docx ---
Appendix B: USP Data Incremental Request Memo
Executive Summary
The U.S. Program Data team is proposing an incremental request of $142M (medium) and $174M (high) over 2026-2029. The request focuses on harnessing AI’s transformative potential in U.S. education through three core pillars: Enable AI Solutions for Learning, Build Trusted AI Evidence, and Accelerate System Feedback Loops. In addition, our work will be supported by three cross-cutting enablers: Integrate Data & AI in Place to amplify impact in focus geographies, a Data & AI Enablement Hub to directly support program teams with technical expertise, and through Leverage Key Partnerships to coordinate with the field. This strategy marks a shift from foundation-building to field-shaping – moving from general ecosystem building to enabling AI infrastructure, accelerating actionable feedback loops, scaling adoption pathways, and formalizing USP Data’s role as a division-wide hub for AI and data insights.
This request focuses resources where USP Data has a distinct comparative advantage and demonstrated momentum – building on successes from the prior strategy and on promising investments already underway in other U.S. Program (USP) strategies. The purpose of this appendix is to provide a high-level overview of the proposed requests and resourcing levels across these pillars. For further details on the strategies and programmatic work underlying these requests, please see the strategy proposal summary memo.
Summary of Trade-offs
USP Data’s current baseline core budget of ~$15M per year represents a substantial reduction from recent investment levels of ~$35M per year. The initial phase of the USP Data strategy was largely funded through internal division trade-offs during the 2021 Annual Planning cycle, with USP continuing to supplement emergent needs since then.
In addition to the core budget, USP Data receives ~$12M per year in envelope funding from PS and Pathways. While portions of this funding have been realigned with the new strategy, the majority remains committed to PST-specific priorities and cannot be redirected to cover the division’s broader data and AI infrastructure without PST agreement.
Because PST allocations are necessarily tied to delivering on specific partner priorities, they cannot fund the core set of flexible, non-PST dependent resources that USP Data requires. These resources are critical for enabling the team to act with agility, take calculated risks, and deliver faster turnaround times – capabilities that PST-directed funding cannot replicate. This incremental request for central resources is therefore essential to ensuring USP Data can maximize adaptability and drive impact across the division.
In the medium and high funding scenarios, the team’s resource estimates exceed initial LDA projections. This reflects the criticality and central role of data in enabling the division’s success in the AI era – providing the infrastructure, evidence, and insights that power transformational moves across K-12, Postsecondary, Pathways, and other USP strategies. Given the division’s ambitious scope, USP cannot reallocate enough resources to fully cover this work without undermining other priorities. However, USP can contribute approximately one-third of division-level reserves to help close the current gap if the medium or high scenario is approved (roughly $8M in the medium case and ~$10M in the high case).
USP Data Request Details
The following reflects our current best thinking on funding scenarios. These projections will continue to evolve as we execute the strategy. Given the rapid pace of change, we will need to remain nimble – adopting investments in response to rapidly evolving technologies, while carefully weighing trade-offs across pillars.

The USP Data team aims to invest in open, connected agentic infrastructure (e.g., stan


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix C.docx ---
Scaling PS and Workforce Data for Scaled Impact:
Case Studies from our National Systems BOW
The Scale National Systems Body of Work has two main components: 
Improving data infrastructure for institutions of higher education by building awareness and availability of the Postsecondary Metrics Framework through adoption and use of the National Student Clearinghouse’s Postsecondary Data Partnership (PDP) to support equitable college access and completion efforts.
Linking education and workforce data across states through the Coleridge Initiative’s Administrative Data Research Facility to support efforts to improve pathways from education into the workforce for Black, Latinx, and low-income students.
Overview: What is the Postsecondary Data Problem? 
With the ban on collecting student-level postsecondary data at the federal level, institutions of higher education are forced to rely on aggregated IPEDS data for comparing their outcomes to peer institutions. These data are limited in scope to only a subset of today’s students, not timely with at least a two-year reporting lag, and cannot be fully disaggregated. For example, federal graduation rates focus solely on first-time, full-time, fall-enrolled students. Those missing from this definition (e.g., students who began part-time, transferred from another institution, or started in the spring term) can represent over 60 percent of students at a typical community college. This means that community colleges using IPEDS data as a benchmark for measuring the efficacy of their completion initiatives are making policy and programmatic changes with only half the relevant information. Our investments in postsecondary data infrastructure, including the National Student Clearinghouse’s Postsecondary Data Partnership, are designed to ensure colleges and universities have the information they need to implement interventions relevant to their institutional context while measuring progress for a diverse array of students in order to close equity gaps in completion. These investments are designed to improve the field’s ability to track several metrics in our Critical Questions framework at scale, including outcome measures related to postsecondary enrollment, progress, and completion as well as equip K12 schools and postsecondary institutions with the data needed to drive improvement on these indicators. 
What Will We Do to Address the Postsecondary Data Problem in this BOW?
The Postsecondary Data Partnership is a national data analytics and visualization platform designed to help colleges and universities gain a fuller picture of student progress and outcomes and identify where to focus resources to improve completion. With our funding over the past three years, PDP has steadily grown in adoption and now serves over 300 institutions of higher education, representing 15 percent of 2- and 4-year student enrollment. Built on the Postsecondary Metrics Framework developed by the Institute for Higher Education Policy, the PDP offers a more comprehensive understanding of college student success than existing federal IPEDS data, providing disaggregated outcomes by race/ethnicity, Pell Grant recipient status, age, academic preparation, and other key student demographics. This year, PDP launched benchmarking capabilities, allowing schools to compare their own outcomes with peer institutions. Rather than using ratings data that privileges large endowments and other non-student-focused outcomes, the PDP encourages schools to consider their institutional context to identify peer institutions with similar student populations. This functionality allows schools - and affiliated completion initiatives like Achieving the Dream - to find and learn from other institutions that are successfully closing gaps in completion for Black, Latinx, Indigenous, and low-income students. 
More comprehensive graduation rates are necessary but not sufficient to keep students on a path to completion. Institutions also need data on 


--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update_Appendix C.docx ---
Appendix C: Investment-Level Lookback
USP Data has accomplished a great deal in the 5 years we have operated as a strategy. Overall, we met expectations on strategy execution, delivering significant progress across a range of initiatives: supporting states such as Texas and Washington in linking siloed systems across K-12, postsecondary, and workforce data; scaling national data platforms such as PDP and ADRF, which enabled voluntary participation from a significant number of states and institutions to share data on these platforms; developing public goods like the Education-to-Workforce Framework and Learner Information Framework; driving policy change at the state and federal level; and advancing novel data-sharing technologies through Privacy Enhancing Technologies. We also strengthened connectivity across the division by working with colleagues in PS, WSI, EMO, Pathways, and K-12 to amplify and advance the collective impact of the division.
However, strategy impact was slightly below expectations. While demand and governance made steady progress, systems modernization and practical data use among key stakeholders lagged behind – constrained by widespread data sharing agreement challenges, limited practitioner-oriented design, persistent privacy hurdles, and uneven adoption of tools like PDP and Equitable Value Explorer (EVE). Private-sector partnerships often failed to align with public-sector priorities, and longstanding issues with the National Student Clearinghouse – driven by misaligned incentives – further limited traction. And while frameworks like Education-to-Workforce Framework (EWF) gained traction, uptake requires significant support to drive state and local decision-making. At the same time, continued fragmentation at the federal level continues to slow opportunities for scalable, systemic impact.
Our updated strategy pivots from general data ecosystem-building to an expanded role focused on enabling AI solutions and accelerating system feedback loops for decision-makers. At the same time, it builds on areas of continuity from the previous strategy, including a continued role for national data collaboratives (e.g., NSC, Coleridge, NLx), growing relevance of wage linkage efforts, and building on past work in standards and privacy-enhancing technologies.
Case Study: Education-to-Workforce Indicators Framework (EWF)
Looking Back: Between 2021 and 2024, USP Data invested $4.2M to design and launch the Education-to-Workforce Indicators Framework, a set of 20 critical questions intended to help states build equity-centered data systems. The framework was disseminated widely through a dedicated website, field publications, and practitioner convenings. It has since been applied in 12 states to design research agendas, identify system gaps, and prioritize new data collection. Awareness of the framework among K-12 and postsecondary decision makers grew from 30% to 48% between 2024 and 2025, and usage increased from 15% to 27%; nearly all users (99%) said it prompted changes to their data practices or policy. The number of community-based organizations leveraging the framework rose from 2 to 32, with 91% indicating they plan to implement its metrics. Internally, USP Data supported 13 implementation projects across eight program strategy teams, fostering stronger cohesion across the division.
Lessons Learned: Despite strong traction, the framework still requires contextual adaptation. Partners frequently ask for tailored entry points and clearer use cases, with implementation complexity reported as a top challenge. Adoption was most successful when technical assistance, co-design, or partner onboarding accompanied the framework, highlighting a need for implementation guides and toolkits customized for specific use cases. While there has been some progress on funder coordination, alignment remains a challenge during leadership transitions and in evolving political contexts.
Path Forward: As part of EDU-Net’s interoperable e


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix D.docx ---
Leveraging SIS and LMS Data for Insights and Impact:
A Case Study from our R&D BOW
Overview: What is the Data Problem?
Rich, real-time data exist in SIS and LMS platforms, but they are vastly under-utilized to understand the drivers of student performance and to develop and evaluate approaches and tools to equitably improve student outcomes. This is due both to a lack of tools for practitioners to use the data to deliver insights as well as lack of researcher access to these data.  Given nascent work in this area, we are proposing research and development into opening access to analyze SIS and LMS data as a core part of the USP data strategy. This investment area will be anchored by several demonstration projects with SIS/LMS solution providers. In this appendix, we share an example of one such demonstration project, outlining our work with Infinite Campus to use their data to assess how K12 schools and districts can best design their courses and course sequences to prepare students for college. This work focuses on college readiness—one of our critical education-to-workforce indicators—as the relevant outcome measure, with optimizing high school course-taking and counseling as the mechanism by which the work will improve progress on this indicator.
Research shows that Black, Latinx, and low-income students often have limited access to college-prep coursework because teachers and counselors misidentify the courses that would best prepare them for postsecondary work and/or misidentify which students would benefit from college-prep courses due to implicit bias. Also, the relevant courses may not be offered by the schools they attend. A 2014 report by the Education Trust found that high-achieving students of color and students from low-socioeconomic backgrounds (i.e., top 25% in reading and math) often “leave high school with lower AP exam rates, lower SAT/ACT scores, and lower GPAs than their high-achieving white and more advantaged peers” and that “lower performance on these outcomes indicate that these initial high performers may not be privy to the same type of instruction and support in their high schools.”
While SIS and LMS systems contain the data needed to match students to appropriate college-prep coursework, these platforms are currently not configured in a way that enables use of that data. Schools are using these systems to record this information, but do not have the tools needed to identify enrollment and achievement patterns, consider alternate course placement or sequences, or evaluate new strategies to improve college readiness. This is particularly pertinent to schools in Texas, where the new K12 funding model in recently passed HB3 rewards post-secondary readiness, offering $5,000 for each economically disadvantaged student who graduates “College, Career, or Military Ready.” Yet, local SIS and LMS are ill-equipped to integrate, process, and report the variables that school and district leaders need to understand whether students are ready for postsecondary work and how to respond with interventions when students are off-track.   
What Will We Do to Solve this Data Problem in the R&D BOW?
We aim to address the challenge of better understanding pathways for college preparedness through a series of pilot projects. Initially, we plan to work with Infinite Campus (the second largest K12 SIS/LMS in the US with 75 million unique student records), placing data scientists and product engineers within their organization. We are already in the process of funding the Infinite Campus work, which will include: (1) updating their platform to readily produce the data needed by researchers, (2) conducting studies on optimal learning pathways for college-preparatory coursework, and (3) using the insights from the studies to embed efficacious pathways into the company’s course-scheduling algorithms and course-recommender platforms. If successful with Infinite Campus, future pilots will target other large players in the K-12 and P


--- 102020 USP Data Strategy Discussion Appendix E.docx ---
Activate PAC Context to Ensure Supportive Enabling Conditions 
for Data Reform (Investment Area 5 Details)
$5M over three years, 2021-2023 (~8% of core budget, 6% of total budget); Managed through existing NAT FTEs
Overview
The USP Data strategy will require close coordination between programmatic activities and policy, advocacy, and communications to ensure success, especially related to our state and national data work. There are both specific needs aligned with individual investment areas (e.g., establishing the right enabling policies and funding to transform the P20W data system in Texas), as well as needs that cut across the strategy (e.g., ensuring privacy in all data investments, managing the foundation’s reputation). While the work led by the NAT team will be closely integrated with programmatic work described in the strategy proposal memo document, we enumerate it here for clarity.
Our Work 
(1) Supporting the data policy and funding environment in Texas (and other focus states): Building state exemplars (Investment Area 1) will require both funding and policy action at the state level (e.g., authorization of governance structures, reporting requirements for LEAs, approvals for inter-agency data sharing) and at the federal level (e.g., funding for future SLDS grant competitions, Regional Educational Labs). Our primary lever of influence on both policy and funding will be through supporting national and state-based advocacy organizations (e.g. Data Quality Campaign, Texas 2036), offering both financial support and specific technical assistance (e.g., support from a communication firm to more effectively message data). In Texas, we will partner with NAT to grow the emerging data advocacy coalitions there while also building playbooks, communities of practice, and collaborations to scale to other states. We will also work directly with state agencies, providing technical assistance to help them define and adopt best-practice enabling policies (e.g., DQC on governance, Future of Privacy Forum on privacy). To drive demand from the public more broadly, we will support local media outlets to write about education with a data-oriented perspective in Texas and beyond. While our initial state policy and funding work will be centered on Texas, we will monitor, support, and advance data improvements in other states as opportunities arise given ongoing work by the North America Team to enable data work in all of our focus states (except Florida, where conditions are not supportive at this time).
(2) Supporting the national data policy and funding environment: Our vision to scale national systems for postsecondary and workforce data (Investment Area 2) requires changes to federal data systems and federal policy. As noted, we are supporting our partners through the Postsecondary Data Collaborative to advocate for a comprehensive federal postsecondary student-level data system, which will require Congressional authorization and funding. Additionally, our partners are advocating for expanded access and use of federal financial aid data from the U.S. Department of Education as well as federal employment and earnings data from the U.S. Census. We will continue to work with advocacy partners to build alignment around these priorities among diverse coalitions of organizations in the postsecondary and workforce spaces (e.g., Association for Public Land-Grant Universities, U.S. Chamber of Commerce Foundation). To create a supportive environment for data advocacy at all levels, we will also continue to support national media outlets with the capacity to build awareness of the need for improved data access and use through their reporting (e.g., Education Writers Association). 
(3) Supporting privacy technical assistance for our data investments: Proactive management of privacy is important across investment areas to ensure student data is protected and that stakeholders have confidence in the privacy safeguards. We will address this need in

--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update_Appendix D.docx ---
Appendix D: Results Framework Methodology
Introduction
Our results framework is designed to guide and strengthen how we measure progress, ensuring focus on the factors that matter most to achieving our long-term goal: 
By 2045 all learners—especially those historically underserved—and the adults who support them are empowered by safe, evidence-based, AI-enabled solutions that deliver personalized experiences. Connected, actionable data ensures universally recognized skills and credentials, enabling learners to navigate pathways, move across institutions, and thrive in meaningful work.
The framework drives toward five interconnected field goals that collectively define the conditions necessary to support connected, personalized, equitable, and data-informed education systems at scale. They represent the cumulative impact of our programmatic investments and the cross-cutting enablers that support them.
Enable AI Solutions: 40% of learners reached by solutions embedding open-source memory and context
Build Trusted Evidence for AI in Education: 50% of learners reached by solutions that have adopted evidence-based benchmarks and evaluation technology that improve safety and quality
Accelerate Actionable Feedback Loops: 40% reduction in average time to insight on key Essential Questions for district leaders and postsecondary administrators
Integrate Data & AI in Place: 50% of districts in Pathways and WSI regions have strategic institutional adoption of AI-enabled solutions and 100% of regional cross-sector data tools in Pathways and WSI regions hit thresholds for regular use
Amplify Coordination and Impact: 2-3x ($415-$540M) leverage on USP Data investment through key partnerships
(see more details on Field Goals including model visuals in Pre-Read section of Strategy deck)
We have designed our initial results framework and targets using the best available information. Over the next six months, we will further refine our measurement approach, key assumptions, and goals to address several forces that introduce market uncertainty:
Division-Wide Goal Refinement: The broader USP division is re-examining ambitions with the 2045 winddown. As PST strategies shift, we may need to refine our goals in response.
Efficacy: Current research on AI’s impact on student learning and development is growing but remains limited, particularly in K-12 settings and in understanding longer-term learning and socioemotional outcomes. We will monitor this space closely to ensure our work both draws from and contributes to the emerging evidence base.
AI Dynamics In Flux: Since the public release of ChatGPT in November 2022, more than 100 newly launched AI companies have reached valuations of $1 billion or higher. With AI market dynamics evolving rapidly, we’re committed to staying nimble and adapting to field developments and external shifts as they happen.
For each goal, we aim not just to raise the baseline, but to accelerate the trajectory of improvement through targeted investments that lay the technical, institutional, and relational groundwork for catalytic change.
Field Goal Overview
Enable AI Solutions for Learning and Navigation: By 2030: 40% of learners reached by solutions embedding open-source memory and context
Why It’s Important: Learners need access to impactful and personalized experiences that provide trustworthy guidance and support continuously across their educational journeys. Recent research from Khan and Zearn on the impact of personalized, tech-enabled supports on learning, reinforces historic evidence on the importance of memory and context in supporting learner engagement and outcomes. Yet, weak supply side incentives hinder innovation around the necessary open protocols and infrastructure to drive adoption at scale. 
Key Assumptions: We see a critical role to play in closing these incentive gaps by focusing our efforts on driving development and encouraging adoption of open-source memory and context components. Our goal hinges on th


--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update_Appendix E.docx ---
Appendix E: USP Division Data-Related Spend Analysis 
I. Summary 
In partnership with the IDM team, we used AI-enabled tagging to estimate divisional data-related spend, identify its primary goals, and determine the main user groups being targeted. We defined data-related spend as investments primarily aimed at gathering, evaluating, or disseminating data – including for internal use. While results should be interpreted as directional – given potential inaccuracies in AI tagging – this analysis provided valuable insights into how PST data needs are evolving and where future investments may be required. We anticipated – and this analysis confirmed – that data spend has been growing and will likely continue to grow across the division because of how essential data is to AI-powered solutions. However, the scope of this analysis is solely focused on historical spend and we have not sought to project forward future spend. 
What we found: In 2024, approximately 34% of USP division spend ($275M) went to data-related activities – up from 17% ($108M) in 2020. The largest growth was in investments focused on using data to power and improve interventions [Figure 1]. This signals a shift in PST needs, broadening from system-level insights to solution-driven implementation support. 
Why this matters: Much of PST spending is tied to vertical strategies and solution-specific requirements. To complement these, USP Data’s role is to provide shared infrastructure and capacity that make vertical investments more effective and scalable. USP teams themselves have reinforced the need for Data to continue enabling divisional infrastructure while expanding into AI and advisory supports that help them deliver against their priorities. 
Looking ahead: Over the next four years, we anticipate that USP Data’s core investments will complement PST-driven spend – laying the foundation for more transformational outcomes in PST use cases. Our pillars on Enabling AI Solutions for Learning, Building Trusted Evidence for AI, and Increasing Speed & Efficacy of System Feedback Loops will strengthen the conditions for impact and unlock greater value from PST solutions. 
Figure : Estimated USP Spend on Data-Related Activities by Use Case
II. Methods 
In close partnership with IDM, we used ChatGPT’s API to tag all USP divisional investments initiated between 2020 and 2024. The analysis covered only investments beginning in 2020 or later, with ChatGPT accessing system-level information, not underlying documents. 
Investments were tagged across three categories: 
Data-related investments: defined as an investment whose primary purpose was to either collect, analyze or disseminate data. 
Primary use case of the investment: defined across six key use cases, determined in collaboration with PSTs: 
Understand needs and context – investing in data to understand the context and trends shaping the education and workforce landscape
Affect policy and practice – support better public decision-making by equipping state, district and ecosystem-level actors with data and infrastructure 
Power interventions with better data – use data to design, test and improve tools
Measure intervention efficacy and impacts – use data to evaluate intervention effectiveness 
Enable internal strategy and performance tracking – use data to inform program and divisional resource allocation and track against strategy goals 
Advocacy and communications – leverage data to shape public narratives 
Validation was done through a multi-step process. First, we manually tagged a 100-investment sample to evaluate a subset of AI outputs, assess accuracy, and refine prompting. Second, we manually checked $5M+ investments for accuracy. Third, USP teams reviewed $1M+ investments for accuracy.  However, even with this rigorous approach, results should still be interpreted as directional. Two offsetting considerations are important: a) the analysis tags the entire investment as data-related, even though all portions o


--- 09172025 USP Data Strategy Update_Appendix F.docx ---
Appendix F: Funding Ecosystem Methodology
The funding landscape for AI/data in education is marked by significant complexity: state budgets lack the granularity needed to isolate data-related spending, districts differ in how they categorize funding, and multiple federal sources contribute in ways that are fragmented and uncertain. When combined with the constant evolution and proprietary battles in the private sector, this becomes an even more complex funding environment. The analysis and methodology detailed below is designed to provide a directional overview of this landscape. For this analysis, we consider AI/data spend to include expenditures explicitly for data systems, IT functions tied to data, or AI/data-related initiatives in education. In the public sector, where AI funding remains limited, we only assessed data spend; in the private sector, we also estimated AI spend. Below are the segment overviews, with detailed methodology following: 
Federal baseline: Funding going towards education at the national level was approximated using estimate spend towards data-focused federal programs based on a combination of FY 2024 appropriations numbers for programs like the State Longitudinal Data System funding from IES and portions of the 2021 allocation for some larger grant programs based upon an analysis conducted by the Data Quality Campaign in that year. This estimate includes allowable uses of federal funds which could mean actual spending is lower. We estimate total federal funding towards data to be ~$1.5B. 
Range: ~$500M as a lower range reflects funding for which it must be spent on federal data initiatives. The upper range estimate of $1.5B reflects additional program funding for which data is noted as allowable use but actual spend may be lower. 
State baseline: State funding for education and data is not centrally collected and compiled beyond each state agency’s budget. A sample of 39 State Education Agency (SEA) budgets were pulled for FY2024 to identify information technology (IT) spend. In the instance where IT spend was unable to be disaggregated from other line items (e.g., instructional materials), only 50% of the funds were counted. Within our sample, this resulted in $2.2B in IT spend. Adjusting for the remaining states, this results in an expected ED State IT spend of $2.8B across all 50 states.
Data funding: Expert reviews determined ~30% of IT spend is likely towards data-related initiatives. Therefore, 30% * $2.8B results in ~$0.8B from SEA data spend. To approximate additional data spend on workforce initiatives, we assumed a similar amount was spent on data at each state’s Department of Labor and Department of Higher Education. Therefore, we estimate 3 * $0.8B = $2.5B in state data funds. 
Ranges: It is difficult to isolate and locate data spend in state budgets, particularly across multiple departments and line items. We maintained a lower boundary of $1B as our bottom-up build supports this floor for SEAs. Due to the flexibility in funding strategies across departments, we applied a larger assumption to the top end of the range, accounting for areas where education or workforce data use may be applicable: 125% * $2.5B. This results in a range of $1-3B for the state segment. 
Local Education Agencies (LEAs): Multiple sources and assumptions were utilized to address two main streams going to LEAs: i) state-controlled funds (i.e., K-12 formula allocation dollars), and ii) local-controlled funds (e.g., county taxes). We know from the National Center for Education Statistics (NCES) the US for public elementary and secondary education had $792.7B in spend in 2023. Of this, $468.6B was instructional spend, with $209.3B under state control and $199.7B under local control. In total, this represents ~$409B in LEA K-12 funds. 
Data funding: A sample of focus district budgets was collected, revealing <2.5% of budgets are spent on data initiatives in “data processing services” or related categories. 2.5% * $40

Matt Gee
Director, US Program Data
Matt Gee leads the Data strategy team in the foundation’s U.S. Program, which focuses on data demand, access, governance, and use to support decision-makers across the education-to-workforce continuum. At BrightHive, he pioneered the use of autonomous teams of AI agents that complete complex data tasks for organizations without data engineers or data scientists. He also led the development of innovative governance models, including data collaboratives and data trusts, enabling responsible data sharing for AI training and other applications.
Matt has a rich history of leadership in data and AI for public benefit. He co-founded Data Science for Social Good and has worked extensively with government agencies and nongovernmental organizations to apply data-driven solutions to societal challenges. In the areas of education and workforce, Matt has helped lead multiple data innovation networks, including the Workforce Data Initiative, the T3 Innovation Network, the Open Skills Network, and the Job Data Exchange.
Matt previously worked at the U.S. Treasury and founded several companies that use data to improve society. He has served as an advisor to Code for America, DataKind, and the World Bank.

--- USPAC Strategy Review Memo 03042019.docx ---
U.S. Policy, Advocacy and Communications
Strategy Review Memo – March 4, 2019
Introduction
The foundation’s policy, advocacy and communications approach in the United States and Canada has shifted over the past two years. The decision to consolidate these activities internally, expanding the U.S. Policy, Advocacy and Communications (USPAC) team, has been a key evolution across the foundation’s work in specific geographies, or in place. This integration effort recognized the foundation’s external influence as one of our most critical assets in advancing our priorities. It has also centralized trade-off and prioritization decisions within distinct geographic markets.
USPAC’s objective within the foundation, and GPA’s Theory of Influence, is twofold: 
Funding: Maximize federal and state level funding across foundation priorities. 
Policy: Influence government decision makers and align policy priorities with foundation objectives. 
To achieve these objectives in the United States and Canada, our strategy relies on three functions and bodies of work: 
Defining the Landscape: Monitor political impacts and trends and analyze relevant political movements. 
Shaping the Environment: Cultivate targeted constituencies (veterans, faith-based communities, etc.), build political will, and develop suggestions for policy solutions. 
Driving Priorities in Place: Advance primary advocacy and communications goals across USPAC specific to the geographies where we work. 
This memo outlines our work to date and focuses on our priorities in place moving forward as USPAC seeks to advance the foundation’s goals. Additionally, this memo details the environmental and political challenges facing the foundation moving forward.
Federal Advocacy in the United States
Progress and Lessons Learned 
The political landscape in the United States continues to shift following the 2018 midterm elections and the 2016 presidential election. However, many of the underlying trends have been developing for some time. This includes a growing mistrust of major institutions, including government, university systems and the media. Our political system continues to be deeply divided. 
The Trump Administration’s lack of focus, and often antagonistic approach toward our priorities has undoubtedly affected USPAC’s work to achieve maximized federal funding and aligned national policy. Our approach has been primarily defensive to avoid major cuts proposed by the Administration. As a result, we pivoted our advocacy efforts almost entirely to Congress. This has proved relatively successful, and while we are still awaiting final FY19 appropriations for many of our global priorities, we are confident funding levels will remain similar to FY18. Foundation priorities funded through the Labor-HHS-Ed bill fared well: our education issues were sustained, and the National Institutes of Health (NIH) saw a record $2 billion increase. 
Our approach over the past two years has led us to the following conclusions and lessons learned across our core functions and tactics: 
Defining the Landscape: We have lost champions faster than we can replace them. For example, since Congress passed PEPFAR, a milestone in the development community, 79 of the 100 Senators who passed the legislation are no longer in Congress. Although there are still foreign aid champions, the numbers are not as large as in the past. This leaves our priorities vulnerable, particularly as many of our long-standing allies near the end of their tenures.
Shaping the Environment: While there is general support for foreign aid, there is not n significant passion for the issue among many members of Congress.  There are not many lawmakers who would prioritize our issues above other pressing domestic and international concerns. This was particularly apparent during the FY19 budget process when we saw the largest increase to non-defense discretionary (NDD) spending in modern history, and foreign aid received the smallest increase of any sp


--- USPAC Meeting Notes 03042019.docx ---
US Policy, Advocacy & Communications
Co-chair Meeting Notes 
March 4, 2019
Attendees:  Bill Gates, Sue Desmond-Hellman, Mark Suzman, Allan Golston, Trevor Mundel, Chris Elias, Rodger Voorhies, Carolyn Ainslie, Connie Collingsworth, Hari Menon, Rob Nabors, Ryan McMaster, Gaby Gomez, Maeve Ward, Max Espinoza, Jeff Hall, Greg Ferrante, Elisa Keller, Ollie Babson, Jill Nishi, Bob Hughes, Patrick Methvin, David Bley, Susan Byrnes, Sam White, Rhett Dornbach-Bender, Lynn Eisenhart, Larry Cohen, Katie Ford   Phone: Joe Cerrell, Gargee Ghosh, Anja Langenbucher
Follow ups
Data Use and Privacy: We will work through the USP Data Working Group to craft an update that 1) analyzes and summarizes the key challenges in the external environment around data use and privacy 2) how these challenges impact our policy and advocacy strategies 3) how we are leveraging USPAC assets to support and advance USP data priorities.
Policy: We are in the process of refining a policy agenda that pulls together the highest leverage policies to advance the overall USP goals, including policies critical for supporting and advancing our EL, K12, PS and Pathways programmatic goals. 
Budget and Finance: Over the next several months we will 1) develop a body of work around state budget/financing, 2) more clearly define the finance pools at the federal, state and local level that are feeding the education system 3) understand the points of influence within these pools, and 4) chart a path forward to begin influencing resources and alignment across these pools.
Key Takeaways
Co-chair support for directional engagement at the US and Canada federal levels including continuing to explore new tactics and engagement with different constituencies to grow support for foundation issues
State work remains on track, but additional clarity and alignment is needed to establish and communicate our policy positions related to US Education (more below)
Other issues:
Data Use and Privacy: There are two main challenges vis a vis the external environment; data privacy and security and data governance across states. With regards to data use and privacy, we face political barriers created by privacy concerns and as a result we face a headwind that makes driving toward our data-related strategy objectives challenging. On governance, our focus states have different levels of strength of state longitudinal data systems (SLDS) and governance around those systems. This is a result of a range of factors, including political factors and the general tolerance for data use and level of privacy concerns. These factors are inconsistent across states which makes defining and obtaining an ideal state of policy and governance across our focus states extremely challenging.
Policy Priorities: Our strategy around advancing a set of key policies in support of USP objectives is seen as underdeveloped and unclear. We need to find a way to more clearly articulate and communicate our policy priorities and stances to foundation leadership and more effectively incorporate this into our advocacy and government relations strategies at the federal, state and local levels.
Budget and Finance: Historically, our work to advance US education priorities has been focused on mobilizing key audiences and influencing decisionmakers to drive systems change at the state and local levels. This has meant a limited focus on federal budget and finance issues, and virtually zero focus on mobilizing or aligning resources at the state and local level. This has been flagged as a gap in our current strategy and identified as a scope of work to focus on moving forward.

--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Memo.docx ---
April 15 Postsecondary Federal Policy Memo
Overview 
During our February 26 update on ongoing policy development related to our US education work, we described the four key pillars of our policy agenda: Data, Finance, Pathways, and Instructional Supports. As the first in a series of deeper-dive sessions, on April 15 we had planned to discuss how this framework applies to our postsecondary federal policy agenda and demonstrate how U.S. Program (USP) strategy and the North America Team (NAT) coordinate to advance shared goals. We began preparing a set of materials to address your expressed interest in and questions about our points of view on a wide variety of federal higher education policy issues. However, the COVID-19 pandemic and response has changed the federal policy landscape. In some cases, previous opportunities to enact our agenda such as through Higher Education Act (HEA) reauthorization are on hold, while new windows for policy change have opened around successive stimulus bills. We will need to adjust our priorities accordingly. We present the below memo as information about the work leading up to the pandemic, our view of federal policy in that prior context, and the progress we made against our agenda. During our meeting we hope to have a conversation with you about the pivots we will make in response to the new normal.  
Through 2025, the Postsecondary Success Team (PS) is focused on demonstrating that 300-400 colleges and universities can increase postsecondary attainment rates and close gaps by race and income in the economic return to a degree. In the process the team will create public goods that other institutions then use to implement what works and achieve similar outcomes. Achieving these goals points directly to the need for policy interventions at the federal level. The federal government’s $130+ billion yearly investment in higher education through Pell Grants, student loans, the G.I. Bill and other forms of student aid account for over half of all higher education revenue (excluding research).
The following memo: 1) briefly describes the postsecondary federal policy landscape prior to COVID-19; 2) reviews our current policy agenda and points of view on some key issues within the four pillars framework; 3) identifies key partners  and shows how they fit into our strategy, and 4) evaluates our progress to-date. The appendices provide some supporting detail for all the above. 
Postsecondary Federal Policy Landscape
Federal policy vehicles provide an opportunity for systemic changes that support our postsecondary programmatic work. These vehicles typically include the Higher Education Act; the Perkins Career and Technical Education Act (recently reauthorized); other separate bills with connections to higher education; annual appropriations; and administrative guidance and regulations. Prior to COVID-19 absorbing virtually all of Congress’ focus, the Congressional education committees were working to advance HEA reauthorization, while the Trump Administration focused on regulations and guidance around data, student loans, and Title IX campus sexual assault.  
As we reported earlier, on December 19, 2019 the President signed the FUTURE Act, which provided permanent funding for Minority Serving Institutions and simplified the FAFSA by allowing for IRS tax data to be automatically imported into the form for all students. However, HEA reauthorization remained outstanding due to election politics and partisan policy differences on funding levels, campus sexual assault, accountability for higher education institutions, and the federal role in college affordability. The COVID-19 crisis has almost certainly closed the narrow window for reauthorization before 2021. 
In the absence of a reauthorization, we and our partners had pursued reform through executive branch agencies, the Congressional appropriations process, and independent bills such as the FUTURE Act noted above. A series of additional emergency stimulus 


--- 2020-21 USP Trustee Memo.docx ---
U.S. Program (USP) 2021 Trustee Memo  
April 2, 2021
I. Executive Summary 
Our overarching objectives in the U.S. Program (USP) are: 1) ensuring that all students – especially Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty – have an opportunity to earn a degree or certificate that prepares them for a successful career and life; and 2) supporting work by public officials and community groups to better understand and advance economic mobility, leading to permanent ladders out of poverty for families and communities, such that race, ethnicity, and income are no longer predictors of outcomes. 
Not surprisingly, the COVID-19 context (which led to temporary school closures, quick pivots to remote instruction, standardized testing waivers, declines in postsecondary enrollment, increases in unemployment, and more) had a disproportionate impact on Black and Latino students and students experiencing poverty and created challenges for our work. Nonetheless, we made progress in a number of areas—including the identification of strategic opportunities presented by the COVID context. 
As a division: last spring, we assessed the viability of all USP Bodies of Work (BoWs) in light of the COVID pandemic. The COVID context, along with increased national focus on the need for racial justice, led us to identify and recommend eight new strategic opportunities for investment that complement existing strategy and, if pursued, have the potential to dramatically influence the opportunities that are available to our priority students and increase their agency and ability to choose education pathways that lead to upward mobility. The new opportunities we identified touch on three core themes, or keystones: (1) Accelerating next generation digital learning, (2) Enhancing digital & data-enabled student supports, and (3) Changing the paradigm of high school to college, and beyond. Identifying and prioritizing opportunities in each of these areas was a major effort that involved teams throughout the division, with particular emphasis in K-12, Postsecondary Success, and Pathways. We have reallocated roughly $300M to fund eight specific opportunities as well as the newly approved USP Data strategy – for more on this, see “Allocation Changes” below. In addition, we have reallocated a number of FTEs to ensure that our core capabilities align with these shifts, with more reallocations to occur over the coming months.
K-12 Education: 2020-21 progress: the COVID context allowed for acceleration of some K-12 R&D efforts – this was particularly true with middle years math investments focused on tutoring. The team launched a Grand Challenge, the first for U.S. Education, around Algebra I and rethinking the traditional math classroom to better support students who have historically been marginalized in math. The Advanced Education R&D Fund (AERDF) also concluded a two-year test of its first concept and secured additional funding from the Chan Zuckerberg Initiative and Walton to expand the pilot and develop two additional concepts. 
The COVID context allowed for steady progress on existing and emerging solutions, particularly in the case of Coherent Instructional Systems. A $200M expansion was approved for this work in early 2020. In response to COVID, the team accelerated work around digital materials and as remote learning led to quick adoption of those materials, the team was positioned to support adoption of EdReports “green” curricula in the COVID context. EdReports continues to spur greater availability of standards-aligned curricula and efficacy studies are underway, including some focused on specific titles. K-12 is working with the North America Team to incentivize adoption of standards-aligned materials and professional development. However, the majority of teachers modify curricular materials so implementation remains an area of focus. To better understand and address this, the team is launching an Effective Implementation Cohort that will test 


--- 4a. USP Global Good_ Cover Memo_011420.docx ---
Memorandum 
This year’s U.S. Program Global Good session on January 14th will focus on examining the factors that affect the availability of researcher access to student-level, longitudinal data that can explain why and how programs and interventions are improving education and economic outcomes. We will explore possible levers and solution ideas that can increase data access and availability for research on a local, state, and national level to unleash education data as a public good.
While the ideal education to workforce data ecosystem must eventually address the needs of a variety of stakeholders, our current focus is on solving for researcher access given the potential for research to create impact at all levels. The complexity of education systems, and the skills required to navigate and translate its various data outputs, means that policymakers, practitioners, and families cannot, on their own, determine what is working at scale. We depend on researchers to surface these insights and make them accessible to the public to support adoption of evidence-driven reform in education.
This complex problem space is nuanced and multi-layered. We have classified the barriers and levers, and potential solutions into three areas: Demand, Systems, and Governance. Demand gets at the availability and use of data for research that solves real problems facing students and parents, practitioners, or policymakers in a relevant, applicable way. Systems are the technical infrastructure that enable data sets to be seamlessly linked, accessed and analyzed by researchers. Governance entails the stewardship rules, including policies, processes and people, necessary for data access that are transparent, easy to navigate, and allow for broad use by researchers while safeguarding privacy. At the intersection of all three is better evidence, which can be generated by researchers to support action by policymakers, practitioners, and even students and families.
The pre-read for the meeting will prepare us for both the exemplar case studies we’ll see during the session and for a discussion about potential options for foundation investment, including some no-regrets options, in advance of a full strategy proposal to be developed with support from SI^2 and an expert advisory group in 2020. Please note that some of these investment options are already in our investment portfolio (e.g., ADRF, NSC, TERA) but could be amplified to more robustly address the researcher use case. 
This session is part of a learning journey we began last summer when we launched the USP Data Initiative to examine the role of the foundation in the education data space. In the first phase of the work, we looked across our strategies to: 
Map our current data-related investments to the foundation data taxonomy;
Develop and align on key use cases for education data with a focus on longitudinal data at key transition points; and
Consider opportunities for greater synergy and impact across the program that would enable us to build or support public data goods. 
As a result, we are pursuing two cross-USP data initiatives this year: 
Improving data linkages between postsecondary education and workforce at regional and/or national scale.
Developing and advocating for solutions to ensure data privacy and security that support our key data use cases.
In the second phase of the work, we are engaging in deeper learning on education data to ground our collective understanding and to align on the problems, opportunities, and potential role for the foundation that would have us work beyond our approved USP strategies. The core question driving this effort is based on the researcher use case: What are the options for increasing the availability of student-level, longitudinal data from national, state, and other sources, sufficiently anonymized to protect privacy, in order for researchers to generate rigorous evidence about whether and why our reforms are effectively improving education outcomes,


--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_PS Courseware_Memo.docx ---
USP Deep Dive: PS Courseware 
In 2021, the Postsecondary Success team is launching a wave of new investments in our digital courseware portfolio based on detailed strategy discussions and email conversations we had with you in July through September of 2020. It was through those discussions and conversations that we aligned on a detailed vision and set of outcomes for this work. Our goal is to close equity gaps in course completion rates for Black, Latinx, and Indigenous students and students experiencing poverty in the top 20 gateway courses at focus institutions while also increasing institutional and student ROI from greater efficiency and student retention. The additional resources allocated to our work in this portfolio enable us to pursue a more comprehensive courseware R&D strategy to develop and scale high-quality courseware that is not only superior at conveying content, but also helps faculty (largely adjuncts) redirect their instructional time to the highest value activities tied to student success. As part of our near-term R&D plan, we are fast-tracking the development of an exemplar courseware product for one gateway course (in advance of the remainder of the courseware development portfolio) to serve as a benchmark for future courseware investments. We’ve scheduled this action-oriented Deep Dive with you to evaluate specific exemplar courseware products and narrow in on which courseware features and functionality should inform the exemplar product roadmap that we develop together – similar to your work in Big History. As pre-work in December 2020 leading up to this Deep Dive, we aligned with you on our target faculty and student profiles and pain points. In this next session, we are looking to confirm more specific details about the courseware’s design, functionality, and use cases.
After this meeting, and as we advance our exemplar courseware development in 2021, we will provide you with periodic updates on the progress and decisions we’re making, such as: 
The courseware developer with whom we plan to partner, along with an evaluation of their timeline and ability to deliver across the prioritized features and critical courseware design elements we’ve identified
The high-level product specifications and prioritized use cases we’re tasking the provider to build toward for this exemplar course
Our progress in negotiating with incumbent providers as part of the “courseware upgrade” efforts that leverage insights and directional guidance from this “exemplar course” development process 
Emerging innovations in the courseware space
An update on our Quality Access Program initiative (see Appendix A) which is designed to help faculty identify high quality courseware
As background for our January Deep Dive session, the remainder of this document starts with a brief overview of the higher education curriculum market and our shared north star vision for courseware. Next, we’ll go deeper on the seven priority use cases, which is where we want to focus our time with you on January 25th. For each of these discussions we’ve included the key design consideration we’re facing, as well as relevant research that’s guiding our thinking. Finally, we’ll give you a sense of what to expect in the session by highlighting the tool we’ll use and ways in which we will engage with you.
U.S. Higher Education Curriculum Market Overview
The U.S. higher education curriculum market is comprised of textbooks, courseware, and instructional tools. 


Courseware is defined as “instructional content that is scoped and sequenced to support delivery of an entire course through software built specifically for educational purposes. It includes assessment to inform personalization of instruction and is equipped for adoption across a range of institutional types and learning environments.”i 
College faculty adopt core and supplemental course-level materials, including courseware, to support delivery of instruction and improve the course learning experienc


--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_Student Outcomes & Racial Equity_Memo.docx ---
Improving student outcomes: Racial equity & targeted solutions
The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation’s U.S. Program seeks to improve outcomes for Black and Latino students and students who are experiencing poverty. Ongoing demographic shifts, research that continues to identify gaps in outcomes and opportunities between Black and Latino students and white students, and a recently heightened national awareness of racial injustice make this mission more urgent and valued than ever before. In this document, we’ll look at how the education market is shifting toward products and pedagogy that affirm racial and cultural identity, and demonstrate both the need for and viability of education solutions targeted to close the opportunity gaps for Black and Latino students. 
Changing markets, enduring inequities
In this first section, we’ll look at three major transitions in the education landscape: from a student population that is majority white to majority students of color; from research that too often conflated socioeconomic status and race to research disaggregating between those two factors; and a shift in demand from both teachers and administrators for learning materials and professional development focused specifically on better supporting students of color.
New majority
The face of public education in the United States is changing. Between 2000 and 2017, students of color went from being the minority to the majority of the U.S. public school population. This trend is predicted to continue over the next decade, with students of color expected to comprise 56% of the public school population by 2029 (see Table 1 in Appendix C).i
This shift has been even more significant in the country’s largest school districts. Districts like Chicago and Miami-Dade comprise well over 60% students of color. In Houston, white, non-Hispanic students make up only 15% of the student population.ii 
Workforce demographics in schools come nowhere close to reflecting the racial and ethnic diversity of their student body. In 2017–18, roughly 79% of public school teachers were white, 9% were Hispanic, 7% were Black, 2% were Asian, 2% were of two or more races, and 1% were American Indian/Alaska Native.iii The percentage of administrative leadership of color was found to be equally small: in 2017-18, 77.7% of public school principals were white, 8.9% were Hispanic of any race, 10.5% were Black non-Hispanic, and 2.9% identified as “other.”iv
The demographic shift in the student population increases both the relevance and potential market viability of education solutions designed specifically to ensure equitable opportunities and outcomes for students of color.


Enduring inequities, growing disaggregated evidence
At the same time that demographics of U.S. classrooms have been changing, student outcomes have continued to demonstrate gaps in access to quality education along lines of race and income level.
Since Brown v. Board of Education, the U.S. has allegedly pursued the ideal that all students should have access to the same quality of education, regardless of their race. But well over half a century after that landmark decision, it is evident our education system has fallen far short of its promise. Despite certain improvements in outcomes for all students, gaps persist between the outcomes of students of color and white students, particularly for Black and Latino youth.
Take NAEP math scores as an example (see Table 2 in Appendix C for reference). When looking at average composite scores for all 8th grade students in the U.S. from 1990 to 2011, scores generally improved for students of all races. However, with the persistent gap between students of color and white students, this merely put students of color roughly on par with where white students had been twenty years prior. And while the gap between scores of students of color and white 8th graders decreased by a couple of percentage points, there remained a 30+ point gap between Black and White students.

During our last learning session with you on February 26th, we provided an overview of how we organize our policy work, how priorities are selected and developed, what “good” policy looks like, and how we work with our USP colleagues to ensure alignment and coordination. This new reality, in turn, has reaffirmed the federal government’s already critical role in this area and is driving postsecondary federal policy, starting with stimulus funding but extending well beyond. This new environment is prompting us to think through what a revised federal policy agenda for higher education might look like and where the foundation can have the greatest impact.

We are happy to craft similar papers on other topics in the near future and to answer any questions you may have related to any of the materials. Our plan, however, is to focus our April 15th presentation and discussion around how the evolving US landscape will likely impact our own federal postsecondary policy priorities and the broader federal policy agenda around higher education in the months and years ahead.
To help lay the foundation for and extend our collective thinking around postsecondary federal strategic priorities and points of view, we will be joined during the session by Ben Miller, Vice President for Postsecondary Education with one of our key partners, the Center for American Progress.

--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_Student Outcomes & Racial Equity_Cover Note.docx ---
Bill,  
We look forward to meeting on January 25, when we will have a “Deep Dive” into two critical topics for USP. In the second half of the meeting, we will engage with you in a learning session focused on the potential for targeted solutions to improve racial equity in student outcomes. This is a follow-up to the discussions and questions you have raised, and I see it as the first in a series of engagements focused on promoting greater equity in outcomes. We will structure subsequent discussions based on feedback and questions that result from this first discussion.
Over time, we plan to discuss the roles that products, programs, pedagogy, administrative practices and structures, and policy can play in increasing equity. We wish to begin this conversation grounded in something concrete that has a strong anchor point in our existing strategies: specifically, we will start by focusing the upcoming meeting on ways that products and programs—things we typically think of as solutions—can improve outcomes and close gaps for our priority students.  
Across our education strategies in the U.S. Program, we believe that data and technology-enabled solutions, when combined with effective programs, pedagogy, practices/structures, and policy, have the power to drive system change, closing opportunity and outcome gaps for Black and Latino students and students who are experiencing poverty and leading to tangible progress on specific metrics. Outcomes have been improving for these students over the past 30 years, but they continue to trail outcomes for white students and students from wealthier backgrounds. These gaps must be closed in order for our educational systems to live up to their promise.  
There is a growing body of evidence, including research from the fields of neuroscience, cognitive science, psychology, and sociology, that learning is social, emotional, and cultural. There is also a strong research base that shows that our priority students, especially students of color, face many challenges in the learning environments they have access to that negatively impact their ability to learn. In the pre-read and the session itself, we will share examples of products and programs that are specifically designed to affirm the identities of our priority students, helping create the conditions for learning. Some products and programs do this in explicit ways—for example, the Kingmakers program developed by Chris Chatmon focuses on creating a healthy, affirming learning environment for Black boys in the public school system. Other products and programs build in more subtle supports for students of color, embedding them into scaled academic offerings with the intent of making those offerings more effective for specific subgroups—Zearn, led by Shalinee Sharma, is one example of a comprehensive mathematics solution designed to address diverse learning needs and develop students’ sense of themselves as mathematicians. As part of the session, we’ll have an opportunity to discuss the impact that these solutions (which we refer to as “targeted solutions,” a term that is defined in the pre-read) are having.  
This is a particularly important topic right now, given both activity in the field, exacerbated opportunity gaps due to the impact of COVID-19 on public schools, and the focus of our solutions work in K-12 and PS – and there are three important things to keep in mind. First, as the pre-read will discuss, there is increasing demand for curricula and other tools that are “culturally relevant” or “anti-racist,” and for tools that foster social emotional learning (which aligns to racial equity work in many contexts). We expect this demand will continue to grow, given shifts in student demographics and added awareness on the part of educators about racial injustice and the need for more equitable solutions.  Second, we are already seeing the market response – some with quality/impactful products and supports and some with stunningly poor product
