--- 062823_USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence_Cover Note.docx ---
supporting Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence
Bill,
The cross-USP working group on Motivation, Engagement, and Persistence looks forward to continuing our conversation about how we can improve students’ motivation, engagement, and persistence (MEP) to increase learning. This deep dive session builds on our discussions in January and March that:
Established BMGF definitions for terms that do not have universal field consensus (but require us to be more concrete to support our product development, measurement and market guidance approaches)
Shared an emerging set of quantitative and qualitative measures for M, E, and P
Highlighted three areas where we can increase M, E, P in the student’s experience; via products/content, teacher/faculty practices, and (to a lesser extent) the learning environment. We also discussed hypotheses about where technology can do some of the heavy lifting on this, where humans are uniquely situated to do the work, and where technology can support humans to do their work.
Began to unpack the role of personal and systemic context in how they impact student motivation, engagement and persistence (and therefore should impact our solutions)
Signaled our ultimate objectives for this focused work on M, E, P (including accelerating learning across the division and strengthening the market for products and approaches that enhance M, E, P)
Next week’s deep dive will build on our shared enthusiasm for this effort by showcasing how key elements of our investment approach come together. Partner demonstrations will show what effective, MEP-focused pedagogy looks like and how products can encourage it. Throughout the demos, we will discuss creative and ongoing measurement approaches, and then end by previewing what lies ahead for this promising and impactful cross-USP effort. 
Attached are ~20 slides that serve as a pre-read for our conversation. You will experience more robust video content live during the demonstrations, so these materials are meant as background material to: 
Reground us on progress to date while exploring Motivation, Engagement and the role of personal and systemic Context at greater depth (slides 3-9): In our past conversations we have aligned on the benefits of increased MEP to drive student learning. We have continued to take an analytical approach to refine and deepen our framework for each construct and their interaction based on research and expert engagement. For instance, we’ve decomposed “motivation” into two contributing concepts of expectancy and value, while differentiating the dominant forms of “engagement” (behavioral, cognitive, and emotional) and clarifying how each shows up in our work. We aim to be specific enough so we understand what M, E, and P add up to and how to measure components of them effectively, while also presenting a framework that is sufficiently simple, broad, and relatable that partners can act on it and test what works (and what doesn’t), especially for focus students. 
We have also attempted to clarify the personal and systemic contexts we think have the most impact on a student’s motivation, engagement, and persistence and highlighted implications for our investment approaches. Although R&D focused specifically on context is limited, research is clear on certain contextual factors that cause variation in student MEP and learning – especially for our focus students. We’ve shared key contextual factors that are central to our strategies and that we believe we can help address through investments in product and practice improvements (e.g., variation in grade-level readiness, English language fluency, teacher racial bias). Session demonstrations will use student personas to illustrate how solutions we support are designed to account for focus students’ contexts while also driving MEP for all students. 
Show how early progress reflects our theory of action for MEP (slides 10-14): We have a unique ability to make an impact on MEP by bridging the research-to-de

--- 5b USP Global Good 2020 Appendix B Educational Data Stakeholders 011420.docx ---
Appendix B: Educational Data Stakeholders 
It’s important to acknowledge and define all of the stakeholders involved in a researcher’s data journey, as each one plays a vital role in the educational data ecosystem. 
Researchers can be academic (within a university), institutional (at a school district or public agency), commercial (nonprofit or for-profit research organization), or a member of the data science community, which includes independent researchers, nonprofits, government agencies, and for-profit companies. They devise questions, gather data, and conduct queries and analyses of that data using research methodologies designed to generate credible claims of evidence. 
Practitioners are those individuals working in early learning, K12, postsecondary, and workforce institutions and programs to support students and other opportunity seekers. They are the day-to-day generators and users of the data that is translated into research insights about what works to improve outcomes. 
Agency staff consists of individuals working at national-, state-, and local-level education, workforce, and social service agencies. They are a step removed from practitioners, but they often house the various silos of data generated at the practitioner level. They also control the parameters for researcher access to data and some agencies also have their own research agenda and capacity.
Policymakers are the individuals and groups working within federal, state, and local governing bodies. Policymakers are not directly involved in the storage and use of data; however, they can mandate an agency’s ability to grant or deny access to data. They are also a key consumer of the insights produced from research.
Students & families are both generators of data and the ultimate beneficiaries of research, once the insights researchers produce are applied by practitioners and policymakers. They can approve or deny participation in some types of research, which impacts a researcher’s ability to collect comprehensive data. 
Each of these stakeholders plays a key role in the educational data ecosystem, and each has distinct needs that are not always addressed by the same solution. For example, a solution that integrates data sources at the local level may meet practitioner needs for timely, comprehensive data that enables more effective interventions. However, it may do very little for policymakers, because it provides no additional visibility into which programs are working well at scale and why, especially if researchers are not able to leverage that data to test interventions.


--- 5c USP Global Good 2020 Appendix C Data Privacy and Security 011420.docx ---
Appendix C: Data Privacy and Security
Closely related to the governance issues of data stewardship and safeguarding are data privacy and data security.
Data security includes the technical methods to protect data in all its forms. It means making sure that confidential/protected information is only available to those who are allowed to access it for authorized purposes, and that the data is protected from unauthorized disclosures (either through inadvertent or malicious breaches). Data security includes technical approaches used by researchers and others to protect data, the availability of that data, and the integrity of the data while it is processed.
Data privacy concerns the governance rules about who gets to see what data. For governmental agencies, protecting information privacy means that data is collected, used, and stored in accordance with an understood set of privacy principles and in accordance with legal requirements. It also means thinking about why certain data needs to be collected, that the data are collected from individuals in a privacy-protected manner, and that downstream use of data is consistent with privacy principles. 
Today we see evidence of public skepticism and sensitivity around nearly anything related to data. That skepticism has roots in actual data failures, which the media covers quickly. For example, the 2019 exposure of records held by title company First American Financial Corp.: 
            “The digitized records — including bank account numbers and statements, mortgage and tax records, Social Security numbers, wire transaction receipts, and driver's license images — were available without authentication to anyone with a Web browser.” 
It’s essential that we find ways to balance risk and utility with respect to the safe use of data for research. Strict laws must be in place to safeguard data, while also accommodating research to improve practice and policy as an acceptable and safe use of the data.
Data privacy: who gets to decide the limits of data access
Data privacy is primarily a governance issue—who gets to see what data. Governance rules can come from federal and state policies or from informal political influences. The fear that exists around more open access to data results in strict regulations and policing of data, which impacts research. That fear is reasonable considering the vast amounts of data that are collected and stored by academic institutions, software applications, and governments, and that there can be unintended consequences of data analysis, even when they are collected and analyzed for seemingly “good” purposes.
Trust is a key factor 
People are resistant to data collection for purposes they don't understand or don't agree are helpful to them. They might also resist simply because they believe that entities such as the government should respect their privacy when requested. And because we generally understand so little about the data that is collected about us and how it is used, there is general skepticism about data collection. People are more likely to support data collection when they can see that they will receive something of value from their participation. 
Most researchers do not need personally identifiable information (PII) to conduct longitudinal research. However, PII and de-identified data sets so small that individual identities could be determined with reasonable certainty are usually what trigger privacy concerns. “Student data” as a concept is enough of a flag to stakeholders who fear improper use of data, that drawing a distinction between identified and de-identified data seems like splitting hairs. Even if the best governance and technical systems were in place, mistrust about data use binds up access to research data, requiring a robust outreach strategy. 
Formal data privacy policies
It is important to understand the variety of federal and state policies that regulate access to student data and how each one affects the work of researchers.
Fa


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 1.docx ---
Appendix. Biographies and priorities of key federal policymakers on postsecondary policy issues and reauthorization of the Higher Education Act (HEA). 
Senator Lamar Alexander (R-TN)
Chairman of Senate Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions (HELP) Committee 
Biography
Lamar Alexander, a seventh-generation Tennessean born and raised in Maryville, was twice elected governor of Tennessee. He also previously served as president of the University of Tennessee and as U.S. Secretary of Education under George H.W. Bush.
Alexander was first elected to the Senate in 2002 and has been re-elected twice. His Republican colleagues elected him three times to be chairman of the Senate Republican Conference. He has served as chairman of the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions committee since 2015.
He has always believed that in most cases the best decisions are made by those closest to the people -i.e. state and local policymakers. Alexander is also chairman of the Senate Energy and Water Development Appropriations Subcommittee, where he works to boost funding for basic energy research and invest in our inland waterways and harbors.
Priorities for HEA Reauthorization 
Chairman Alexander has been focused on HEA reauthorization to address issues of affordability and accessibility, while also limiting the role of the federal government. To accomplish this, the Chairman has prioritized the following in negotiations to date: 
Simplifying the Free Application for Federal Student Aid (FAFSA) by reducing the number of questions from 108 to a “couple dozen.” The Chairman was able to include simplification measures in the “FUTURE” Act, which was passed earlier this year to extend mandatory funding for HBCUs, but there is still room to further reduce the number of questions and simplify. 
Simplifying loan repayment options, especially income-based repayment plans. The Chairman has previously proposed eliminating the multiple options for repayment and to maintain one income-based repayment plan. 
Simplifying and expanding the gainful-employment rule to ensure that all colleges and universities, not just career college programs, are included in the rule. 
Increased accountability for all institutions, largely through the use of a measure of student outcomes, including a percentage of students repaying their student loans. 
Reducing burden on institutions through deregulation, with the intent that this could also reduce cost of attendance and allow for greater institutional investments. 
Senator Patty Murray (D-WA) 
Ranking Member of Senate HELP Committee
Biography
Patty Murray has served as a member of Senate Democratic leadership since 2007, and education has been a legislative priority for her since the beginning of her public service. In addition to being the first female Senator from Washington state, Murray served as the first female Chair of the Senate Veterans’ Affairs Committee during the 112th Congress and served as the first female Chair of the Senate Budget Committee during the 113th Congress.  
She currently serves as the Ranking Member of the Senate HELP Committee and the Ranking Member of the Appropriations Subcommittee on Labor, Health and Human Services, and Education.  She continues to serve on the Budget Committee and the Veterans’ Affairs Committee as well.
Murray was born and raised in Bothell, Washington. Her dad ran a five and dime store on Main Street, where she and her siblings all worked growing up. In 1992, she ran for the United States Senate as a voice for Washington families who were not being heard. Dramatically outspent, Murray ran a grassroots campaign of family, friends, supporters, and public interest groups to beat a 10-year veteran of the U.S. House of Representatives. Murray was re-elected in 1998, 2004, and 2010, and 2016.
Priorities for HEA Reauthorization 
Ranking Member Murray has been focused on HEA reauthorization in order to address issues of affordability and accessibility.  She has prioritized the foll


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 2.docx ---
Appendix 2
Federal Postsecondary Policy Status and Implications
This section organizes federal policy priorities and issues into our four policy pillars and provides a summary of the status of each relative to some key federal policy vehicles—Higher Education Act (HEA) reauthorization, U.S. Department of Education (USED) action, and U.S. presidential campaign themes.  While campaign themes are not themselves a federal policy vehicle, they can be indicative of major priorities for incoming or second-term presidents that may be addressed legislatively or through executive branch action.  
*NOTE: The HEA reauthorization updates below are based on the status of negotiations directly before COVID-19 developed. These negotiations are now on hold.  
Data
Data systems for consistent, transparent data
Current HEA reauthorization:
Based on increasing bipartisan support, the Senate bill could include the College Transparency Act (CTA) which would improve and add consistency to postsecondary data collected and reported across institutions nationwide.  However, some Republicans in both the Senate and House remain opposed based on privacy concerns and/or their views on the role of the federal government in data collection.
The House Democrats’ bill includes CTA fully.
U.S. Department of Education action:
USED updated the College Scorecard website this year to include information on student loan repayment rates at the program level, a significant step forward in public data transparency.  
Presidential campaign themes:
N/A – issue has not substantively been raised 
Finance
Affordability for target populations: 
There are gradations of college affordability and aid proposals being currently considered for national policy.  These include: 
Federal-State Partnerships to encourage states to fund college affordability,  
“Free college” (which has its own gradations), 
Student loan debt forgiveness (forgiving all existing student loan debt), 
Increases in Pell Grants, affordable student loans, and campus-based aid.  
Federal-State Partnerships are one innovative policy mechanism being considered to address college costs and completion.  Our policy grantees have led much of the policy research and development work on federal-state partnerships.  The policy would require states to fund affordability measures at the state level, in exchange for receiving new federal funds (federal cost: potentially several billion dollars).  Before COVID-19, Democrats in Congress viewed this as a way to lay the groundwork for a larger, much more costly proposal around free college should a Democrat win the presidency.  Massive new money in reauthorization is unlikely, due to budget rules that each provision must be paid for, so Democrats have been eyeing budget reconciliation in 2020-21 as an option to fund free college. However, now the appetite for new mandatory money in 2020-21 (after the presidential election) remains unclear. A narrower federal-state partnership that falls short of fully funding free college could be seen as a more reasonable alternative, depending on the state of the economy.    
HEA Reauthorization:  
House: Provides grants to states that agree to waive community college tuition and fees and allow Pell and other aid to be used for non-tuition costs. States must provide at least 25 percent of the total funding and must maintain their funding effort.  Provides up to sixty credits free for low-income students who attend a Minority Serving Institution, through direct grants to students.
Senate: The Senate is currently negotiating a set of federal-state partnership provisions.
USED action: No substantive new action on incentives for state investment in postsecondary affordability/financing.

--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4 Summary.docx ---
Appendix 4 Summary: Our Points of View on Federal Postsecondary Policy Issues
This appendix delivers a series of brief position papers explaining our point of view on a variety of federal higher education policy issues. As we explain in the cover note, midway through our preparation it became clear we needed to reprioritize our work in response to the COVID crisis and discuss our new assumptions and new plans during our time together. We decided to provide a streamlined version of our points of view focusing on areas closer to our strategy except for federal data policy and FAFSA simplification where we have already published extensive white papers. Should you require position papers on those issues, or more peripheral areas we do not cover here, or greater depth of analysis on any of them, we can provide them as follow up to the meeting.
Appendix 4a: Our point of view on federal accountability in higher education includes our thinking on the types of metrics needed to determine eligibility for federal Pell Grants and student loans, financial incentives for schools that provide value and mobility for Black and Latinx students, and students from low-income families, and financial support for institutions to implement evidence backed solutions. 
Appendix 4b: Position paper on debt forgiveness detailing current examples in laws and regulations through income-based repayment, public service loan forgiveness and borrower defense. We do not believe blanket forgiveness proposals are a good idea, but believe some circumstances justify forgiveness for low-income borrowers and those who took the risk to attend a college but were not served well. 
Appendix 4c: Federal-state partnership policies propose greater federal investment in higher education to incentivize or match greater state financial support. The idea initially came about after the Great Recession to encourage states to make up for some of the budget cuts that resulted in tuition spikes. More recently, several Presidential candidates proposed federal free college proposals implemented through federal state partnership. We believe the idea has merit now to head off tuition increases that would harm Black, Latinx, and low-income students.
Appendix 4d: Our point of view on free college proposals. Our preference is for a combination of Pell Grant increases and other federal financial support to make college effectively free in a targeted way. We support only targeted free college programs that cover costs beyond tuition, prioritize low-income students, and include supports for evidence-backed solutions shown to increase graduation rates. 
Appendix 4e: We support increasing the maximum Pell Grant size, including financial incentives for students who take more credits, and schools with strong graduation rates among Pell-eligible students to receive additional resources to spend on evidence-backed student supports. 
Appendix 4f: Our position paper on student loan repayment plans lays out a few reforms to streamline and better target the student loan repayment system. 


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4a.docx ---
Our Point of View on Federal Accountability in Higher Education
Overview
The federal government distributes over $130 billion in federal financial aid each year. In order to protect this significant investment, lawmakers are tasked with ensuring those dollars go toward programs of quality that provide students value. The federal government shares this responsibility with states and accreditors, all together known as the triad, to oversee, enforce, and monitor program integrity in higher education. Currently, there are shortfalls in the system that have led to a string of sudden school closures, left thousands of students in need of debt relief, and put billions of taxpayer dollars at risk. These issues have prompted policymakers to consider strengthening the accountability system and implement new measures of quality. Several of our partners have informed our thinking through different approaches that strengthen the triad, implement new accountability measures, and ensure the federal investment in higher education is protected. 
Primary Accountability Metrics Currently in Law
Accountability policies fall into 3 broad buckets; gatekeeping, monitoring, and outcomes.  
Gatekeeping Measures: 
Accreditation- Primary gatekeeper. Third-party review of institution to ensure they are of sufficient quality. 
Withdrawal rates- For institutions first entering the federal aid system, must not have a student withdrawal rate above 33% for the most recently completed school year. 
Ongoing monitoring: 
Financial responsibility scores- Evaluates private institutions based on financial reserves, equity, and net income. 
90/10 rates- Private for-profits must receive at least 10% of revenue from sources other than the U.S. Department of Education. 
Program reviews- All institutions must comply with federal aid rules. 
Financial audits- All institutions can be subject to financial review, for-profits also evaluated on compliance with federal aid rules. 
Post-school measures:  
Cohort Default Rate (CDR)- Measures the share of borrowers who default on their loans within three years of entering repayment. Lose access to Title IV after default rate exceed 30% for 3 consecutive years.
Gainful employment- Evaluates programs at for-profits and non-degree programs based on the share of graduates’ income that goes toward loan payments. The current Department of Education ceased its implementation of the rule, and currently institutions report data with no enforcement.  
Policy Design Considerations 
The above policies set a threshold for the bare minimum standards institutions should meet. Unfortunately, due to inconsistent enforcement, lax oversight, and multiple loopholes, too many poor-performing institutions slip through the cracks. Below are some considerations for implementing improved minimum eligibility requirements for federal financial aid.     
Thresholds for eligibility:  At what point should the threshold trigger a loss of federal aid for students? If the threshold is too low it could risk not capturing problematic institutions quickly enough, if too high it could prompt the loss of federal aid for too many institutions without giving them the opportunity to improve. 
Scope of Measures: Should measures apply in the same way to all institutions or adjust with the mission and type of institutions. 
Consequences for metrics: What should the consequences be of missing the mark on a metric? Should institutions lose access to federal financial aid? Should they have time to adjust? Should metrics be used mainly to promote transparency with no financial consequences? 
Should institutions receive financial support to meet federal standards? 
Our Points of View on Accountability Measures and Accreditation 
To protect federal aid dollars before they go out the door, we should support minimum eligibility standards for Title IV funds including adequate completion rates for students, satisfactory repayment rates for an institution’s borrowers, and evidence a


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4b.docx ---
Our Point of View on Debt Forgiveness
Overview
As total student debt has crossed the $1.5 trillion mark, lawmakers and political candidates increasingly have focused on the plight of borrowers trying to repay student debt. Current policy already provides for some forgiveness through income-based repayment plans, borrower defense, and public service loan forgiveness. A variety of proposals seek to expand avenues for loan forgiveness that would reach a wide range of borrowers.
Debt Forgiveness Already in Law
Income-driven repayment: Multiple repayment plans currently in law allow borrowers to pay back their loans based on 10 or 15 percent of their discretionary income. After 20 or 25 years, depending on the program, all remaining debt is forgiven. As of now, that debt cancelation counts as income on a tax return. 
Public Service Loan Forgiveness (PSLF): After 10 years of payments while working for a qualifying nonprofit organization or government agency at any level, borrowers can have all of their debt forgiven. 
Borrower Defense Rule: Provides debt relief to those students that were defrauded or attended institutions that shut down. The Trump Administration recently conducted an overhaul of the rule that would severely limit the number of students that could seek and receive debt relief. These changes have been subject to opposition in the courts and in Congress. The fate of the rule is unknown.
The CARES Act Stimulus Bill: While not strictly debt forgiveness, it is worth noting that the bill suspended payments and interest on all federally held student debt for the next six months. 
Policy Design Questions
Should forgiveness be applied broadly or targeted to borrowers who most need it? For example, targeting based on income. 
Should the quality of school attended impact who receives forgiveness? For example, the Borrower defense rule noted above. 
How much debt is forgiven?  
Should debt payments be reduced through interest rate reductions or eliminations? Although not strictly debt forgiveness, interest rate reductions reduce effective amounts paid back.
Democratic Candidate Proposals
Senator Sanders: Proposes cancelling all student loan debt immediately, regardless of income. 
Senator Warren: During her Presidential campaign, she proposed cancelling $50K in loans for people making under $100K a year. During the recent stimulus debate, she has proposed a blanket $10K in cancelation for all student loan borrowers. 
Vice President Biden: Proposed reforming public service loan forgiveness so that people working in public service jobs receive up to $10K in forgiveness each year up to five years. He does not support broad-based cancellation. 
Our Point of View
We oppose blanket debt forgiveness proposals: The regressive impacts of plans circulated by some presidential candidates are far out of line with our policy goals. Even the more targeted plans such as the Warren proposal would have spent billions on upper middle-income borrowers, many of whom could have repaid their debt. We would put the recent proposal to include blanket debt forgiveness in the recent stimulus package in the same category. However, there are some forms of forgiveness we think are a good idea: 
Debt forgiveness for income-driven repayment plans: If you’re paying student loans in small increments for many years with little hope of finishing, eliminating debt after two decades or more is a worthwhile insurance policy against a career that does not pan out. 
Public Service Loan Forgiveness: While PSLF likely gives disproportionate resources to high-income borrowers who graduated with law or medical degrees, the political support for the program is also quite strong. We support new proposals that would give smaller levels of forgiveness earlier than 10 years that would likely help more undergraduate and lower-income borrowers. We would also support income limits, above which borrowers would no longer receive any forgiveness to address the issue of high-income 


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4c.docx ---
Our Point of View on Federal-State Partnerships
Overview
In 2018, overall state funding for public two- and four-year institutions was still more than $7 billion less than 2008, pre-recession.  Currently, because of the unprecedented COVID-19 pandemic and resulting economic downturn, it is likely state governments will again struggle to fund competing economic priorities, which could result in increased costs for students.  Tuition hikes disproportionately impact low-income students and students of color and create accessibility issues across the board. 
Our partners such as the Urban Institute, Center for American Progress, the Century Foundation, and The Institute for College Access and Success have advocated for the creation of a “federal-state partnership.” Originally, the idea was to create a federal financial incentive for states to at a minimum close the funding gap left by the Great Recession, and possibly increase their commitment to higher education through equitable funding of institutions. The work will take on new meaning in the new economic crisis, where a federal-state partnership is one way the federal government can supplement (but not supplant) state funding for higher education and try to head off another spike in college costs. 
Policy Design Considerations 
Typically the idea is put forward as a new federal funding stream that matches new state funding. The below issues are primary design considerations when crafting a federal-state partnership. 
Scope and Structure of Federal Role: What’s the mechanism for federal funding? E.g. should it run through an existing program like the Pell Grant or a new funding stream? How much should the federal government fund? This includes total dollars as well as the proportion of funding relative to state dollars.
What requirements should attach to federal dollars? For example, should the federal dollars come with affordability guarantees from states, incentives for states to enroll more Pell students, support evidence-based programs, or achieve student outcomes like completion rates? 
Our Points of View on Federal-State Partnership
Overall, we support the implementation of a voluntary federal-state partnership that incentivizes actions that bring down attendance costs for students and ensures all institutions have the resources they need to serve students well. We believe there should be a new funding stream, overseen by the Department of Education. 
Ensure state investment. Ideally additional federal investment to states should come with a maintenance-of-effort provision that would compel states to first increase their investment to match federal funds over time and then subsequently maintain their investment in postsecondary education. The federal government can cover the majority of new costs (e.g. 70% - 80%) and require a state match that varies based on the state’s wealth, eligible population, and number of low-income students served. This approach is similar to other federal-state matching programs such as Medicaid, WIC, CHIP, and ESSA. 
Contain costs. A well-designed federal-state partnership will require affordability benchmarks, set to inflation, that include both tuition and non-tuition costs. These benchmarks would align the federal-state match to enable states to cover a percentage of average costs of attendance at public institutions, so attending college will remain affordable for students, and costs will not increase.  
Account for the current and future economic downturn.  Federal-State partnerships should also contain safety net measures that will temper or offset any budgetary constraints felt by states or institutions during times of economic downturn. This should come in the form of a “counter-cyclical” matching formula in which the federal share is increased during times of state or national economic downturn so states can maintain their current funding level in higher education. This would ensure college remains affordable and accessible for all stu


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4d.docx ---
Our Point of View on Free College
Proposals for free college have sprung up in a variety of forms from policymakers across the political spectrum, with increasingly ambitious plans coming from the left. All of the ideas aim to address concerns about higher education affordability. Covering college costs is expensive, however, so policy design often limits who is eligible and for how much aid. Policymakers also adjust features to please key constituencies or achieve policy goals like greater completion. 
Most Common Design Features of a Free College Plan
Eligibility: Plans can be means-tested to target low income students, or require a minimum HS GPA, age, or citizenship to limit enrollment or gain political support.
Requirements: Standards to remain in the program such as maintaining a minimum GPA, attending full-time, or a community service requirement to limit enrollment or increase completion rates.
Costs covered: The main driver of program costs and impact. Programs may be only tuition-free, debt-free, or cover the full costs of attendance. Last dollar programs that apply other aid first cost less and assist middle- and high-income students.
Institutions: May cover public 2-year, public 4-year, and/or private schools. 
Funder: So far states are the primary funders while Democrats see a larger federal role. 
Student supports and marketing: Some programs envision outreach, advising and pathways programs to enroll more students and help them complete. 
Outcomes: Free College plans may complement outcomes focused policies either through state funding formulas or federal funding incentives. 
Current Implemented State Examples
Presidential Candidate Proposals (Current Administration Does Not Support Free College)
Our Point of View
Evidence demonstrably shows that more financial aid and lower costs increase enrollment and likely increase completion among some low-income students. Last dollar programs like in TN send added dollars mainly to high-income students, but emerging evidence suggests that the clear free college message helps enroll more people. While TN Promise increased enrollment, both OR and TN showed some worrying signs of more students choosing 2-year rather than 4-year schools. However, TN Promise also showed higher graduation rates, though that may have resulted from statewide developmental education and guided pathways reforms. 
Based on the evidence we should publicly support free college programs that include key design features that improve affordability, access, and value for low-income students and student of color. 
Our preference should be for first dollar programs that minimize eligibility requirements, include all public schools, and especially include resources for student success programs such as advising and incentives to improve outcomes. We should also support mean- testing to limit resources and structure any federal program to reward states that spend more money and in an equitable manner.
Previously, we would have assumed the costs of these features would be too expensive at the federal level in the current political climate to gather the necessary support. The size of the recent stimulus packages and history of our response to crisis changing what is considered politically possible suggests there may be more of an opening. Further, the debate itself over the most recent package – the CARES Act – suggest more aggressive affordability policies are possible.
We would recommend continuing to advocate for expanding Pell Grants, targeting additional resources toward low-income students through Pell, and continuing to advance accountability reforms. And while we don’t need to drive the free college conversation, we should communicate what a good free college plan looks like should the opportunity present itself. 
As we note in the memo, we have modestly invested in the Roosevelt Institute to shift the debate on the progressive left to target resources to students who need it most. We have also worked wi


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4e.docx ---
Our Point of View on Pell Grants
Three decades ago, the federal Pell Grant covered a substantial portion of the total cost of attendance at a four-year public college. Tuition and other living expenses have outpaced inflation and the growth of the program for some time, resulting in a maximum grant that covers just 30 percent of the necessary costs at the typical 4-year public school. A review of the literature suggests that for every $1,000 of additional aid, enrollment among low-income students rises between 3 and 5 percentage points. Recent evidence on the Pell Grant program also suggests that relying on aid likely increases completion rates and earnings for first time four-year students. Based on the available data, there are several adjustments we and our partners support making to this foundation of federal financial aid. 
Policy Design Questions
Who’s eligible? Pell eligibility is determined by a combination of the maximum Pell Grant and expected family contribution (EFC) formula. The EFC formula estimates what a student or their family can contribute to the student’s educational costs. The total cost of attendance minus the EFC equals the student’s financial need. If that need is larger than the maximum Pell Grant, the student receives the maximum Pell amount. 
Which programs are eligible? Most implications for this question we will cover as part of the position paper on accountability; however, there is active debate over whether short-term non-credit programs down to 8 weeks and 150 hours of instruction should be eligible for Pell. Currently the minimum program length is 15 weeks with 600 hours of instruction students must receive credit. 
Points of View
Increase the Maximum Pell Grant. The evidence is clear this would likely increase enrollment, completion, and value and would disproportionately benefit low income students and students of color.
Target the Expected Family Contribution Formula. The structure of the expected family contribution formula means that increases in the maximum Pell Grant end up sending small Pell Grants to middle-income families. Depending on the size of the increase, the formula might need to be adjusted to ensure it remains well targeted at students with the lowest incomes. 
Make Pell Grants a Mandatory Program and Index the Maximum Award to Inflation. Because most Pell Grant funding must be appropriated each year, in economic downturns the need for Pell often outpaces the appropriated dollars from the previous year. Our grantees end up spending a lot of time protecting this funding. By ensuring the dollars are automatically sent out the way that social security is, we can focus our efforts on other policy priorities. 
Provide a Bonus to Students Who Are Meeting Certain Targets. Randomized controlled trials suggest that students respond to financial incentives to take more credits and maintain a minimum GPA, thereby increasing the likelihood of graduating. While not all students can add more credits to their calendar – particularly not student parents or those who have to work – a significant bonus for students who can take 15 credits would likely increase graduation rates. 
Provide a Bonus to Institutions who Enroll and Graduate Large Numbers of Pell Students. This would create a financial reward for institutions that serve the students we care about the best, providing them additional resources to further improve and expand their programs. Those additional resources should be accompanied by requirements that they go toward evidence-based student success programs geared toward closing gaps by race and income. 
Extend the time limit on Pell Grant eligibility. Currently students can qualify for up to 12 semesters of Pell Grants. This should be extended to allow working students who may transfer from 2-year colleges to complete their degrees. Because these students often attend part-time, they will sometimes require more than 12 semesters to complete a bachelor’s degree.
Re Short-Term Pell:


--- 041520 USP Strategy Update Appendix 4f.docx ---
Our Point of View on Student Loan Repayment Plans
Last year, about 10 percent of borrowers defaulted on their federal student loans within three years of leaving school. That number rises over longer time frames. Not completing a degree is the single biggest factor in predicting default, but policy design matters too. With the availability of many income-based repayment plans on federal student loans, we might expect the default rate to be lower because even borrowers with low incomes might be able to secure low or zero-dollar monthly payments. The fact that so many continue to default suggests streamlining the structure of student loans may help borrowers take advantage of the options available to them. 
Key Policy Design Questions
How many federal student loan plans should there be? Currently there are up to eight federal student loan repayment plans depending on how you count them.
For each plan remaining, what should the terms of repayment be?
Should the payment be fixed or vary with income? 
For income-based plans: 
How long should the repayment term be? 
Should there be forgiveness for whatever is unpaid at the end of the term if the plan is income based? 
What percentage of income should borrowers repay? 
How much income should be exempted from repayment to protect low-income borrowers 
Should undergraduate and graduate students face different terms? 
Our Policy Position
We think there should be two repayment plans, one standard plan with payments amortized over 10 to 15 years and an income-based plan. 
The income-based plan should have the following characteristics:
For those with less than $57,500 in student loan debt, borrowers can pay back on the current REPAYE plan, at 10% of their income, forgiving whatever is left after 20 years. 
For those with more than $57,500 in principal borrowers should pay back between 12.5% and 15% of their income over 25 years. We know all these borrowers attended graduate school because undergraduate borrowing limit is 57,500 for independent students. The additional time and income requirements ensure graduate students who are disproportionately better off end up fully repaying loans. 
Borrowers who are late on their payments should automatically enter income-based repayment using their previous year tax information – unless that income level would lead to a higher payment. Borrowers could opt-out, should they choose and re-enter the 10-year plan. 
Implementation of an effective and efficient repayment system would also obviate the need for more significant reforms in the form of income share agreements (ISAs). ISAs allow students to attend school up front in exchange for paying back a percentage of their future income over a set period of years. Income driven plans already offer most of the perceived benefits of ISAs so we see little need for a federal program.

--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_PS Courseware_Appendix.docx ---
Appendix A: Quality Access Program (QAP) and Decision Tool Update
As part of the digital learning strategy refresh, we cited the need to proactively steer demand toward choosing quality courseware. The QAP is designed to address this market inefficiency. It is targeted for a public beta launch in March of 2021.
Description and Goals 
To increase awareness and better selection and adoption of high-quality courseware among decision makers teaching the top 20 gateway courses, the foundation is supporting a robust, user-focused update of the courseware-in-context (CWiC) framework. In a market where information about the efficacy, capabilities, user experience, and affordability of courseware is opaque to buyers, we believe that a set of targeted decision support resources, tentatively called the Quality Access Program and Decision Tools, will create transparency and help improve the quality of selection decisions. 
Today, faculty rely on word-of-mouth and publisher marketing to influence their curriculum choices, which has been a significant barrier to courseware adoption. The QAP will include a web-based comparison tool that enables faculty, instructional designers, and administrators to search, discover, and compare courseware that has been rigorously evaluated against a set of 20 criteria by a third-party consortium of institutional users and experts. There will be accompanying resources that highlight EBT implementation using the courseware in the top 20, encouraging an environment where products can improve with visible user feedback and accountability.
Principles Guiding Design of the QAP
In order to influence demand and supply side actors, the resources must be designed to ensure usability and credibility among both decision makers and courseware providers. Key design principles include:
Faculty and instructional designer friendly
Current and refreshed regularly in response to market dynamics/solution availability
Convey equity-, evidence- and research-based quality criteria, which can and will evolve over time
Signal a measure/level of quality and support with defensible criteria and data
Managed and governed in a transparent manner that establishes trust and credibility with relevant stakeholders across the postsecondary ecosystem
Be a primary resource that all faculty and institutional decision makers turn to – and that suppliers support
Product Evaluation Rubric
The quality of digital learning products is evaluated by a panel of expert users within the postsecondary space. Currently, the evaluation rubric covers four dimensions of quality and 20 criteria for assessment. The rubric was developed to prioritize the user perspective, a product focus, and equity. In future iterations (2022), we expect the rubrics to include not only platform functionality, but all content learning outcomes reviews.
The four broad categories of quality assessed include:
Equity-minded focus: considerations prioritize pedagogical and evaluative approaches that promote equitable outcomes for Black, Latinx, and Indigenous students and for those experiencing poverty (e.g., first-generation students, Pell-grant eligible students). For example, evaluating whether the courseware includes culturally relevant content, is supportive of inclusive teaching practices, or helps increase engagement and belonging for these specific student populations.
Efficacy: highlight the learning outcomes, quality and types of evidence that are available for a particular tool, and to what level of sophistication the courseware enables analysis and disaggregation of outcomes data at the individual student level.
Functionality: relative strengths of tools across capabilities necessary for delivering a learning experience including collaboration, student-centered and outcomes-driven instructional design, enablement of evidence-based teaching and interventions, data-driven insights, etc.
Systems capabilities: requirements for adoption and integration into institutional techn


--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_Student Outcomes & Racial Equity_Appendix.docx ---
Appendix A: Case studies
Case study I: Kingmakers of Oakland
Date started: 2010
Fiscal data: $1.4M operating budget in FY2019-2020
Brief description and organization history: Kingmakers started in the Oakland Unified School District (OUSD) in the Office of African American Male Achievement (AAMA). They have since become an independent nonprofit, while AAMA has continued as a part of OUSD.
Kingmakers works with districts across seven main areas: 
Implement a rigorous, culturally-affirming curriculum 
Recruit, train, and retain Black male teachers
Develop student leaders
Systems change through district collaboration
Reverse harmful policies
Engage families and communities
Drive narrative change
After more than ten years in the district, the independent non-profit now supports school districts across the country to improve the educational and life outcomes of Black boys and create a healthy, affirming learning environment for Black boys in the public school system.
Outcomes: One evaluation from Thomas Dee at Stanford concluded that OUSD’s AAMA program led to a "significant reduction in the number of Black males who dropped out, as well as smaller reductions among Black females, particularly in 9th grade."cviii AAMA was also associated with an increase in the graduation rate for Black males from 46 to 69 percent."cix
Additional outcomes include: 
Reduced the one-year event dropout rate of Black male students from roughly 8.5% to 4.8%, implying the graduation rate for Black males increased by at least 3.2 percentage points.
The GPAs of students in AAMA’s program are 25% higher than those of their Black male peers in OUSD who aren’t in that program.
Over the course of two years, 8% of participating students increased from below grade level reading to grade level or above.
The number of AAMA students receiving 1+ suspension decreased by 50%, from 18% in 2011-12 to 9% in 2017-18.
82% of middle and high school participants surveyed reported that MDP activities make them feel proud to be a young Black male.cx
Equity Pillars:
Get to know the students. Because of their work with liberatory design (see Appendix H), Kingmakers has worked closely with Black students from the beginning to inform how they design their supports. They continued this close work with their target students as they quickly pivoted their programming in response to COVID-19.
Promote adult introspection & cultural competence. Kingmakers includes a Learning Collaborative that works with cohorts of school district staff and teachers to identify the conditions in their district that can help sustain and support Black male students.
Affirm identity & culture. The Khepera curriculum, developed by Kingmakers’ Chief of Content in Training Bayaan Bakari, is explicitly designed to promote cultural pride and positive racial identity for African American men, with accredited course names like “African American Power in the U.S.,” “World’s Great Women & Men of Color,” and “Mastering Our Cultural Identity.”
Account for adult behavior/implementation. Kingmakers tackles the lack of Black male teachers of color, working with districts on both recruitment and retention.
Build community, build relationships. Kingmakers’ includes programs in student leadership, supportive cohorts, and counseling to build meaningful relationships between students and with counselors who help advocate for, plan, and work for academic success.
Case study II: Puente Project 
Date started: 1981
Students served to date: ~300,000  
Fiscal data: Puente Project is funded via three revenue streams, two of which are through the California State Legislature’s SAPEP program and the University of California. The third source is through private grants.cxi
Brief description and organization history: Puente Project was founded at Chabot Community College in Hayward, California.cxii Puente Project is currently integrated into six middle schools, 38 high schools, and 65 community colleges throughout the state of California

--- 4b. USP Global Good 2020 INTERNAL Pre-read_011420.docx ---
USP Global Good 2020 Pre-read
Data for the Public Good: Landscape and Exemplars
Table of Contents
1. Increasing access to data for robust research on education and economic outcomes
As part of our USP Data Strategy Initiative launched last year, U.S. Program teams are continuing to engage in deep learning on education and economic data to ground our collective understanding and to align on the problems, opportunities, and potential role for the foundation to have a greater impact. We are approaching this in a way that will have us work more effectively both within and beyond our approved USP strategies. This phase of the initiative is focused on learning more about options for improving the national data ecosystem that would increase researcher access and use of education and economic data to answer critical questions about the effectiveness of our reform work in the field—and this will inform our wider data strategy development in 2020.
We’re well into an age where big data is helping us understand problems and identify effective solutions. Yet, researchers still struggle to make the most use of administrative and other data sets in education to understand whether interventions are working for students. For this Global Good session, we’ll explore factors that affect the availability of and access to student-level, longitudinal data that can explain why and how education interventions are improving student outcomes, including the current state of the fragmented national education to workforce data ecosystem. We will also review case studies that are working to increase data access and availability for research on local, state, and national levels. And we will discuss potential investment options that we are considering to affect an exponential change in researcher access and use of education and economic data for greater insights and impact.
While some researchers can more readily access data to explore trends and gaps in education and economic outcomes, it is more difficult to access data that can test whether, why, and how interventions and/or reform efforts are working for students. To know if an intervention or program works, you need clean and robust data on the activities of education alongside the outcomes of those activities. Instead, researchers encounter incomplete and disconnected data systems, complex policies and procedures that govern access to data, and pressure to use more readily accessible data that may not support conducting studies about effectiveness. Addressing the many barriers impeding robust education research has the potential to make an enormous impact. In particular, we must address real consumer fear of big data and a growing mistrust of government having access to personally-identifiable information (PII), as well as reluctance by governance agencies to release data that might show negative results, which creates obstacles to demand for and access to data. At the same time, access to better data does not automatically lead to use of that data and appropriate changes in policy and practice. We need people who can translate research insights into action so that policymakers and practitioners are equipped and supported to implement evidence-based solutions. 
By focusing on the needs of researchers (e.g., academic, institutional, commercial, data science), we can zero in on the people with the expertise to help the field understand what works and why, so that we can work towards better implementing effective reforms and interventions at scale. Education systems are complex; we cannot rely on practitioners to have the information, technical skills, or the time to understand the full system of education that surrounds them. In this context, we depend on the research community to discover how to make education better at a systemic level. As such, ensuring researchers can easily, cheaply, and quickly decrypt and diagnose opportunities for improvement in education systems is a critical and unique opportuni

--- 012521 USP Deep Dive_PS Courseware_Participant Bios.docx ---
Meeting Title: USP Deep Dive: PS Courseware
Date | Time of Meeting: January 25, 2021 | 1:30PM-3:10PM PT
Participant Bios
U.S. Program Leadership Team
Bob Hughes 
Director, K-12 Education
Bob oversees the work to ensure that we prepare all students for success in college and career.
Before joining the foundation, Bob was president of New Visions for Public Schools, an New York City school network of 70 district schools serving approximately 45,000 students. Under his leadership, New Visions created 99 district and 7 charter public schools, provided mentoring services to hundreds of new principals, developed school-based certification programs for teachers and principals, and created suites of data and system tools to streamline school operations and track student progress toward graduation and college.
Prior board affiliations include Board of the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching, Advocates for Children of New York, Fund For Teachers Board of Directors and Projects in Education, the nonprofit publisher of Education Week.
Hugh Chang
Interim Director, Economic Mobility & Opportunity
Hugh Chang is currently serving as the Interim Director for Economic Mobility and Opportunity (EMO) in the US Program Division at the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation. The EMO program focuses on increasing mobility from poverty by emphasizing power and autonomy and a sense of belonging in addition to the traditional measure of income.
Previously, Hugh served as a Director in the Global Development Program at the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation from 2012-2018. Most recently, he led the Maternal Newborn and Child Health (MNCH) team to create a comprehensive strategy to improve the health of women and children in the developing world. Prior to his MNCH role, he served as the Director of Strategy, Planning & Management for Global Development. He worked closely with leaders across the Gates Foundation’s Global Programs on strategic planning, portfolio management, and coordination across the foundation’s global activities. He was also responsible for the Global Libraries program and the development of data and analytics to support work across the Gates Foundation’s global programs.
Before joining the foundation in 2012, Hugh was Director of Special Initiatives at PATH, and led development of its strategic framework, including measurement and evaluation, annual planning meetings and processes for continuous improvement. Before his shift into Global Health, Hugh spent 15 years in the Tech industry in computer design, product management and program management, including 9 years at Microsoft.
Hugh received his Bachelor of Science in electrical engineering from UC Davis, his Masters of Business Administration from UCLA, and Masters of Science in Bioengineering from the University of Washington. He currently serves on the Board of Advisors for the Department of Bioengineering for the University of Washington and is a former board member of the Fred Hutchinson Cancer Research Center, the Washington Biotech and Biomedical Association, and the Overlake School.
Jennifer Engle 
Acting Director, USP Data
Dr. Jennifer Engle leads the foundation’s team responsible for improving the quality and use of data across the education to workforce pipeline. This work aims to empower practitioners and policymakers with the critical information they need to implement evidence-based reforms to equitably improve educational outcomes.
Jennifer is a noted national expert and leader in the field. From the Pell Institute to the Education Trust to the Institute for Higher Education Policy, she has focused on improving college access, affordability, completion, and value for underserved students throughout her career. For her, it is a professional passion that comes from her personal experience as the first in her family to go to college. As a researcher and an advocate, Jennifer has focused her work on using data and evidence to call attention to and close equity gaps in

--- 062823_USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence_Participant Bios.docx ---
Meeting Title: USP Innovation Session on Motivation, Engagement, Persistence 
Date | Time of Meeting: June 28, 2023 | 2:15-4:45pm PT
Participant Bios
External Guests (Presenters and Discussants) 
Barbara Means 
Principal Learning Sciences Researcher, Digital Promise
Dr. Barbara Means studies the effectiveness of innovative education approaches supported by digital technology. Currently, she is working with colleges and universities to redesign high-enrollment introductory courses to incorporate equity-minded pedagogy and digital learning technology in ways that enhance student engagement and course outcomes for low-income students and students of color. She also has been studying the equity impacts of the shift to remote instruction caused by the COVID-19 epidemic. Dr. Means has authored or edited more than a half dozen books related to learning and technology, including Learning Online: What Research Tells Us About Whether, When, and How. She has served on many study panels related to science education for the National Academies of Sciences, Engineering and Medicine, including the panels that produced How People Learn I and How People Learn II. Dr. Means earned her undergraduate degree in psychology from Stanford University and her Ph.D. in educational psychology from the University of California, Berkeley.
Dan Meyer 
Director of Research, Amplify Desmos Math
Dan Meyer taught high school math to students who didn’t like high school math. He has advocated for better math instruction on CNN, Good Morning America, Everyday With Rachel Ray, and TED.com. He earned his doctorate from Stanford University in math education and is currently the Director of Research at Amplify, where he explores the future of math, technology, and learning. Dan has worked with teachers internationally and in all 50 United States and was named one of Tech & Learning’s 30 Leaders of the Future.
Kim Thanos
CEO and Co-Founder, Lumen Learning
Kim Thanos is the CEO and co-founder of Lumen Learning. Lumen provides well-designed courseware for gateway courses, as well as professional development to guide faculty members in the use of evidence-based teaching practices. Lumen has saved students over $100 million in textbook costs while improving outcomes for students.
Kim began this work through a Next Generation Learning Challenge grant in 2011 addressing the challenge, “Can we improve success for at risk students using open content?” She led the Kaleidoscope Project, which was among the earliest programs to implement open educational resources (OER) as a complete textbook replacement in order to eliminate access and cost barriers for college students. Kim founded Lumen Learning to sustain and scale this work. Kim holds a Bachelor of Arts degree in International Relations from Boston University and an MBA.
Kyla Haimovitz 
Director of Education Technology, Digital Promise
Kyla Haimovitz is the Director of Education Technology of the Learner Variability Project at Digital Promise Global, a nonprofit that brings together educational research, practice, and technology to accelerate more equitable innovations in learning. In this role, she helps other organizations integrate research on how people learn and develop into educational products in more inclusive and equitable ways through training, consulting, and grant-making. Prior to Digital Promise, she held similar roles as a Senior Learning Engineer at the Chan Zuckerberg Initiative, a Scientist in Residence at the nonprofit Character Lab, and Postdoctoral Faculty at the University of Pennsylvania. She received her BA from Reed College and PhD from Stanford University, where she conducted research on how to create motivating mindsets and learning environments.
